Provisioning itself remains NOT IMPLEMENTED (`campaigns.provisioning.PROVISIONING_IMPLEMENTED = False`) — the gate exists, the vendor account does not. | D-34 listed "number provisioning gated behind KYC" among R-11's non-negotiable mitigations and it was the only one with nothing behind it — no table, no column, no blocker, no route — while SURFACES §2b, FLOWS §2 and BRD §245 all described the control as though it existed. **The research decided the shape** (sources cited in `apps/api/compliance/kyc.py`): DoT replaced bulk connections with **business connections** (31 Aug 2023, expanded May 2024) requiring the entity's CIN/licence, address, GST where applicable and an end-user list; a **June 2025 circular** extends the same protocols to internet telephony, so a cloud number is not a lesser thing than a SIM; **Telecommunications Act 2023 s.3(7)** obliges authorised entities to identify their users, with three years and ₹50 lakh for obtaining an identifier on another person's identity — which is R-11's exposure exactly, because on a self-serve motion the applicant is a stranger; and **Exotel, our own D-05 pick, blocks outgoing calls until KYC clears**, so a product that let a client "buy" a number without a record would be writing a cheque the TSP will bounce. **DLT PE registration overlaps and does not subsume it** — same entity documents, but held by an access provider for headers and templates, with no address tied to the number's city, no end-user list and no CAF — so this record stores a REFERENCE and never a second copy of `pe_id`. **The tier split is the load-bearing part, and it is two questions rather than one.** A tier-blind DIAL gate would halt every existing client over a data-entry backlog without closing a risk: a managed tenant is not anonymous — we contracted with them, we executed their PE registration, and their dialling is already gated by `pe_registration_*` and `number_not_registered` — and this repo has already paid that price once with `tm_registration_missing`. A tier-blind PROVISIONING gate, by contrast, is the only version that holds: the DoT obligation attaches to the CONNECTION regardless of who pays us, and `plan_tier` is an admin-settable column, so a legal control keyed on it alone is one support ticket away from being switched off — the "bypass for testing" hard rule 5 forbids. It blocks no existing client because it can only ever refuse a NEW request. **Never the document itself**: `document_kind` names entity registries only (CIN/LLPIN/GSTIN/Udyam/shop-establishment/trade-licence), `evidence_ref` is a reference to where the pack is filed, and a CHECK refuses a bare 12-digit `document_ref` — an Aadhaar pasted into a business field is unstorable rather than merely discouraged. `signatory_name` is a name and deliberately carries no identity-document number. **Two things left open on purpose.** Whether a non-licensee reseller must itself hold the CAF, or whether furnishing the entity's documents to the licensed operator discharges us, is NOT settled by any source found — so no CAF, form workflow or document store is modelled. And the residual risk is stated rather than hidden: a managed tenant that somehow holds a number without KYC keeps dialling; that closes with an ops sweep requiring a verification for every tenant holding a number, which is a `platform_state`-shaped surface, not a change to this predicate. |

| D-48 | **"Leave no half-wired feature" becomes a build gate with a named baseline** (extends D-29's pack; changes none of its four) | `scripts/check_wiring.py`, in `make guardrails`. Four framework-aware questions, each asked against a live REGISTRY rather than against symbol references: (1) every module that declares an `APIRouter` is mounted by the running app — including `apps/voice-runtime`, which import-linter structurally cannot see (D-18's hyphen is not a legal module name, so grimp's package walk misses the service entirely); (2) no revision sits on a second head, so nothing `alembic upgrade head` would skip is in the tree (alembic's own `ScriptDirectory` walks the map, so this is not a second revision graph that can disagree with the real one); (3) every ORM column is named by some executable line; (4) the baseline itself still holds. Shape (3) carries `UNWIRED_BASELINE`, a **per-column** dict of deliberate deferrals each of which must state its reason and what closes it — a model-level exemption would silently cover the next column somebody adds — and shape (4) is what keeps that dict a deferral list rather than an exemption file: an entry naming a column that has since been wired, or a column that no longer exists, FAILS the gate. **The baseline may only shrink, and only by wiring something.** What it does NOT check is written down too: not read-vs-write (most access here is raw `text()` SQL, so `agents.business_hours` — a writer with no reader — is out of reach), not enum-member reachability (a member is legitimately produced by a DB row or a vendor payload), and not ARQ crons (`cron()` takes the coroutine by reference, so the failure cannot be expressed; `tests/job_registration_test.py` covers the real one). | D-29's argument is that a rule enforced only by human vigilance gets violated exactly when the codebase grows fastest, and this rule had just proved it four times over: `apps/api/agents/publishing_routes.py` sat complete, tested and mounted by nothing. **An unmounted router is not merely inert — it is unaudited.** Every authorization sweep this repo relies on enumerates the routes of the LIVE app (`assert_policy_registry_complete` at boot, `impersonation_reads_test`, `authz_audit_test`, `check_redaction_exposure`), so a router nothing mounts is in none of them, and it can carry a D-22 violation, an undeclared permission or a raw-PII response for as long as it stays unmounted — with every check reporting green, right up to the day somebody mounts it and the violation arrives with it. **The off-the-shelf tools were evaluated and rejected on the record** (research note at the foot of the file): `vulture`/`deadcode` answer "is this symbol referenced", which on FastAPI/SQLAlchemy/Pydantic is a false-positive machine whose own remedy is a whitelist simulating usage — the exemption treadmill this design forbids; `ruff`'s F401/F841 see one file at a time; `import-linter` constrains which modules may import which, and a module imported by nothing breaks no contract. Alembic's own `ScriptDirectory.get_heads()` is ADOPTED rather than re-implemented, because parsing `down_revision` by hand is a second revision map that can disagree with the real one. **The baseline is the feature, not a compromise**: it turned the standing list of "declared but not yet wired" from folklore into a diffable artefact, and the effective-dating and halt-reason work in this same wave closed three of its entries (`Plan.effective_from`, `Plan.effective_to`, `PlatformState.halt_reason`) by deleting them — which is what a baseline is for. |
| D-49 | **An alert is not an alert until it leaves the process — and observability config with no client is deleted, not kept** | `apps/api/core/alerting.py` gains a delivery path. `alert()` still writes its structured ERROR log FIRST and unconditionally (that is the durable record; delivery is best-effort on top of it), then enqueues to a bounded in-process queue drained by one daemon thread, which sends through the SAME transport as hot-lead notifications (`apps/workers/transport.py`: SMTP / console / null). Two bounds, both named: per-fingerprint repeat suppression on `stage:code` at `ALERT_REPEAT_INTERVAL_S = 900`, and a global token bucket (`ALERT_BURST = 6`, `ALERT_BUDGET_PER_HOUR = 20`); what either drops is counted and reported in the next delivered body, and a FAILED delivery clears the dedupe stamp. Recipient is `ALERTS_EMAIL`; a non-local deployment without it logs `alert_delivery_unconfigured` at boot. **`LANGFUSE_PUBLIC_KEY`, `LANGFUSE_SECRET_KEY` and `POSTHOG_KEY` are REMOVED from `Settings` and `.env.example`**, with the restore steps written beside `SENTRY_DSN` and a test pinning that they stay gone; `redact_trace_payload` is kept as hard rule 6's hook shape with a docstring saying nothing calls it. | OPERATIONS §4 promised alerts to a person and §8 makes "alerts firing to Sri's phone" a pre-launch gate; `alert()` wrote a log line and stopped, in a deployment with one operator and no log search — so the post-call stall, the outbox exhaustion, the unkeyable engine payload and the bad payment signature all fired into a file nobody reads at 3am. **Two deliberate departures, both stated because the documents they depart from are binding.** First, this path does NOT go through the transactional outbox (BACKEND-PATTERNS §4), which every other side effect in this repo does: the alarms that matter most are `outbox_dispatch_exhausted`, `pipeline_stalled` and `enqueue_failed`, and an alert routed through the broken component is an alert nobody gets. It therefore touches no database and no Redis, and the honest cost is that a process dying with alerts queued loses those SENDS, never the log lines. Second, what runs inline is bounded to three non-blocking operations — one log record, one dict lookup under a NON-BLOCKING lock, one `put_nowait` — because `alert()` is reachable from voice-runtime's 500ms ack path (hard rule 3), from the global exception handler and from a SIGTERM handler; the lock is non-blocking specifically because a signal handler runs on the main thread that may already hold it, and deadlocking a drain to gain one deduplication is the wrong trade. **The storm shape is adopted, not invented**: per-fingerprint suppression is Alertmanager's `repeat_interval` and PagerDuty's caller-supplied `dedup_key` — which is why every call site carries a stable code rather than a formatted string — and the bucket exists because dedupe alone does not survive a bad deploy, where 500 distinct codes are 500 distinct fingerprints. Fifteen minutes against Alertmanager's 4h default, and no batching, because there is ONE operator and no incident console: first-signal latency beats grouping. **One transport, not two**: a second mechanism for alerts is a second thing to configure and a second thing to be broken on the night it is needed, and the import happens inside the delivery thread so voice-runtime's forbidden `apps.workers` import surface stays clean. **The removal is the other half of the same principle.** Three settings that were no-ops EVEN WITH CREDENTIALS are worse than absent: absent prompts a question, present tells the next reader that traces are being recorded. Restoring Langfuse needs a project nobody holds and a decision-log entry choosing a second tracing pipeline beside the OTel one already shipped; PostHog is browser analytics that never belonged in a backend `Settings` and restores as `NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY` in `apps/web`. TRD §2 and ROADMAP §2 are corrected rather than left describing the old inventory. |
| D-50 | **Continuous WAL archiving is built, unapplied, and its retention window is 35 days on both chains** | `infra/backup/`: wal-g PITR to a Cloudflare R2 bucket that is NOT the recordings bucket (chain A), plus a nightly encrypted `pg_dump` to a NON-Cloudflare provider (chain B), a nightly base backup, a four-check health watchdog, and systemd timers (`OnFailure=` so a dead run has somewhere to go, `Persistent=true` so a missed night runs at reboot rather than being skipped in silence). `archive_timeout = 300`. **Both chains retain 35 days**, and that number IS the erasure SLA for backups. `FailureStage` gains `HOST_BACKUP` for the one alarm that says the database is unrecoverable. It DOES travel D-49's email path, by default and without configuration: `notify.sh` defaults `BACKUP_ALERT_COMMAND` to the relay beside it, which subprocesses into `python -m scripts.host_alert` → `alert()` → the SMTP transport. An override remains, and stays ONE command, because two delivery paths are two dedupe windows and the day one stops nobody notices. **Nothing is applied**: every secret is a `<<SECRET:…>>` reference, no bucket, endpoint, account id or key appears in the tree, no wal-g command has ever been run, and `runbooks/database-restore.md` carries an UNVALIDATED banner instructing its first user to remove it. "Backups verified" on the OPERATIONS §8 checklist is ticked by `runbooks/backup-restore-drill.md` PASSING once with the record committed to `docs/evidence/`, never by this tree existing. | D-26 chose host-PostgreSQL over managed and closed by ACCEPTING a consequence — nightly dumps alone break OPERATIONS §5's 15-minute RPO, so continuous WAL archiving is REQUIRED, plus an offsite dump and quarterly drills. None of it existed while DEPLOYMENT §9 already assumed wal-g was there, so the RPO the runbooks promised was fiction and nobody would have found out until they needed it. **`archive_timeout` is the line that makes the RPO true**: without it a partial 16MB segment is never shipped, so on a quiet SMB night "continuous archiving" ships nothing between 23:00 and 07:00 — the design fails silently exactly when the promise is being made. **Four checks because one is not enough**: PostgreSQL does not record an archiver killed by a signal or exiting above 125 in `pg_stat_archiver`, and `command not found` is 127, so deleting the wal-g binary leaves the obvious check green; freshness, a `wal-verify` against the BUCKET (the only check that catches "exited 0, the object is not there") and `pg_wal` growth each cover the previous one's blind spot, and the drill induces the exact failure `pg_stat_archiver` cannot see. **Two chains with independent credentials** because our edge and our R2 archive are one Cloudflare account, so collapsing chain B into R2 "to simplify" would put every copy behind one compromise. **Why 35 and not 7 or 365**: the floor is the longest routine reason to reach back into a backup, the ceiling is SEC-COMP §4 — every extra day is a day in which an erasure cannot fully reach our data — so 35 is the smallest window that covers the first and the smallest exposure that satisfies the second. The R2 bucket lock is set at 30 days on purpose, BELOW the retention window, because a lock that outlives the retention rule turns every prune into a failure nobody reads. **A restore un-erases people**, and that is a mandatory numbered step rather than a footnote: anyone whose DPDP erasure completed after the recovery target returns holding a certificate that says otherwise, and the authoritative list must come from the PRESERVED pre-restore cluster, because requests raised after the target do not exist in the restored one — which is why the runbook forbids deleting the broken PGDATA. **The largest unverified assumption is named**: wal-g against R2 is documented-compatible and not confirmed here — R2's multipart implementation has open rejection issues across several clients and wal-g #1639 records `backup-push` hanging after an S3 409 — so the first hand-run push is a watched test and the honest fallback is that chain A moves providers. Two questions this raises are deliberately NOT decided here. The external dead-man heartbeat, now narrowed to exactly three residuals that no in-repo check can cover because each removes the observer along with the observed: the host off or off-network (no timer fires, and absence of alerts is indistinguishable from health), systemd itself not running (which takes the schedule and heartbeat checks with it), and the alert path broken beyond us (wrong recipient, provider refusing, spam rule — every alert 'succeeds' locally and lands nowhere). Only an observer outside the failure domain turns SILENCE into a page; the shape is one line and it is unbuilt because it adds a vendor. And whether `ERASURE_LIMITATIONS` gains a backup clause, which is a legal call recorded as open in SEC-COMP §4. **Partly superseded twice, and both are recorded here rather than left for a reader to discover.** (1) The external dead-man heartbeat named as open here is BUILT (D-54) — success-only ping from `backup-health.sh` to a hosted check, so silence is what pages. (2) **The `ERASURE_LIMITATIONS` question is DECIDED (D-164), not open**: the clause ships, with `BACKUP_WINDOW_DAYS`, an index-aligned `backup` exception and a test pinning the number across the runbook, the DPA and the certificate. This sentence still said "stays open" after the entry had already taken one supersession marker — an entry that can carry one and not the second is the drift `check_docs_drift` is structurally blind to, and it is why the 17 Aug docs pass looked for exactly this shape. |
| D-51 | **The first-campaign manual review is a hold on the ACCOUNT, and absence of a decision means held** | `first_campaign_reviews` (migration `c4d9e18a72b6`; one mutable row per tenant, standard §1 RLS, FORCEd): `status` in (`approved`,`rejected`), `decision_note` NOT NULL with a length CHECK, `decision_source` in (`operator`,`migration_backfill`) with a CHECK that an operator decision names its operator, and `reviewed_campaign_id` → `campaigns` `ON DELETE SET NULL` as EVIDENCE of what was read. `compliance.service.first_campaign_hold_blocker` returns `first_campaign_review_pending` / `first_campaign_review_rejected` for `self_serve`/`trial` tenants only, and is asked by **both** `campaigns.service.launch_blockers` and `dispatch_blockers` — deliberately NOT by `check_dispatch`. The client reads their own state at `GET /v1/compliance/first-campaign-review` (`org:read`); ops decides at `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{tenant_id}/first-campaign-review` (`admin:tenants`, audited, tenant in the PATH). There is no client-realm write and no request path. | D-34 lists "manual review of each account's first campaign" among R-11's non-negotiable mitigations, FLOWS §2 and BRD §245 describe it as though it existed, and `tenancy/signup.py` named it in prose — it was the last of the six with nothing behind it. **"First" had to be a property of the account, and the obvious alternative loses twice in a minute.** A `campaigns.review_required` flag on the first row is defeated by launching a SECOND campaign, which dials unreviewed, and by DELETING the flagged one, which lets a DELETE decide what "first" means — neither an attack, both ordinary client behaviour. Account scope removes both: while held, every campaign is refused, and the campaign an operator actually read is kept as evidence rather than as the thing the hold hangs on, which is why losing that pointer to a delete changes nothing. **Absence means held**, with no `pending` row and no request surface: a stored `pending` would be a second representation of one fact that can disagree with the absence, written on a path being refused anyway, and the read fails CLOSED to held on an unscoped session — the same shape `kyc.NOT_RECORDED` uses. **Enforced at dispatch as well as at launch**, so a release withdrawn after complaints arrive stops a RUNNING campaign at the next tick instead of only blocking new launches. **Released once, released for good on this rule**: the requirement is review of the FIRST campaign, not a signature before every one, and the ordinary gates (DNC, DLT, consent provenance, caps, KYC) carry the rest. **Out of `check_dispatch` on purpose** — that gate is also D-21's single-lead button and the instant callback, and neither is a campaign; every document that asks for this control scopes it to the first campaign. The migration grandfathers self-serve tenants that had already launched, stamped `decision_source = 'migration_backfill'` so a NULL decider is self-describing rather than an anonymous release. **Consequence:** all six R-11 mitigations now hold in code, so the build-side objection to `self_serve_signup_enabled` is retired and the switch becomes a business decision. **Residual, reported rather than papered over:** the hold has no cross-tenant queue, so an operator must be TOLD an account is waiting; `kyc_records` has the identical gap, and both close with ONE change to the admin tenant directory rather than two. That change was in flight as this entry was written — check `apps/api/admin/` before rebuilding it. |

| D-52 | **`calls.latency` is dropped, and per-call latency storage is re-opened by a measurement, not by a schema** | Migration `f1a7c39d5be2` drops the column; the ORM attribute goes with it (or autogenerate re-proposes it), the `UNWIRED_BASELINE` entry is deleted, and `tests/call_latency_column_test.py` guards all four ways it returns — the SQL column, the model attribute, the baseline entry, and prose in `apps/`/`packages/` naming it. The numbers TRD §4 argues from now come from OPERATIONS §2 gate 4, which additionally captures Bolna's `latency_data` as an adapter fixture and records whether it agrees with the stopwatch. | The column had never been written and TRD §4 said every call logs stage timings, so the rule stood while the mechanism did not exist. **The case for filling it was real and was answered, not skipped**: a column is queryable in SQL beside its call, survives sampling — the trace ratio is 0.1, so nine calls in ten have NO span — and outlives trace retention. But every span this repo opens is on OUR side of the call (the post-call pipeline serving the two-minute lead SLO); the in-call audio path runs inside the rented engine, in a process we do not run. Filling a column called `latency` from pipeline stages would have named the engine's two-to-three-minute wait for `completed` as a caller-perceived latency, which is worse than either honest option. Confirmed at the adapter rather than assumed: `ExecutionSnapshot` carries nothing finer than `duration_s`, and the transcript arrives as prefix-tagged plain text, so `TranscriptTurn.start_ms`/`end_ms` are NULL for every Bolna turn and no turn latency is derivable from what we ingest. **Supersedes the adapter's standing claim that per-turn timings are not exposed**: Bolna documents a `latency_data` object on Get Execution (`time_to_first_audio` plus per-component blocks), corroborated by their OSS types. It still cannot fill this column, for three independently sufficient reasons — it is a DIFFERENT set of numbers (voice-to-voice turn latency would be our own arithmetic aligning three components by sequence id); nothing validates that arithmetic, and D-39(b) is explicit that we hold zero real-PSTN measurements, so measure → budget → optimise; and its documented `transcriber.turns` entries carry recognised TEXT, which a naive mapper would land in a JSONB column with no redacted counterpart (hard rules 5/6). Their doc pages are egress-blocked from this environment, so the reading rests on search-surfaced text plus their OSS source — one more reason to capture the payload before writing a parser against it. **A column that always reads NULL is worse than none**, because the next reader builds a dashboard on it. |

| D-53 | **`apps/web` gets a behavioural test suite, and the toolchain is chosen for dependency count as much as for ergonomics** | Vitest 4 + jsdom + `@testing-library/react@16` (the React 19 line, where `@testing-library/dom` is an explicit peer that must be installed separately). 40 tests across 6 files, wired into a new `make web-check` (typecheck → lint → test) that joins `make check`'s prerequisites, and into the CI frontend job — which also gained `pnpm -C apps/web lint`, previously defined and wired into nothing. `next build` stays CI-only. **Three conventional packages deliberately omitted**, each argued in `vitest.config.mts`: `@vitejs/plugin-react` (its only job here is overriding `jsx: "preserve"`, which one `oxc` line does without pulling a Babel toolchain in), `vite-tsconfig-paths` (one alias, spelled out) and `@testing-library/jest-dom` (matcher sugar, where these assertions are about presence AND count). | Every frontend guarantee rested on `tsc`, ESLint, a successful build and human reading — while the frontend had accumulated decisions a type checker structurally cannot see: `firstCampaignState` fails CLOSED and `holdRule` fails VISIBLE (opposite directions, both deliberate); verdicts key on the server's `is_verified`/`messageable`/`held` and never on `status`, so a lapsed consent cannot render green; money arrives as a string that must never be parsed; a null worst-case renders "we cannot say", never ₹0. Several of those had been bugs in earlier drafts caught by reading, which is not a control. **The suite earned its keep on its first run by finding two live bugs of one kind**: `holdRule()` and five sites in `kyc.ts` tested membership with `in`, which walks the prototype chain — so `holdRule("constructor")` returned `Object` typed as a hold rule and the next line blanked the entire ops queue with a TypeError, and `isKnownKycStatus("constructor")` answered true, rendering a verdict box with no headline and an undefined tone. A screen that goes white is the one way to fail an operator cannot see past, and the documented contract was fail-VISIBLE. Fixed with `Object.hasOwn` in both, one way per problem. The same shape survives in six `Record`-with-fallback lookups fed by our own enums — lower risk, recorded for a sweep. **Hard rule 9 was the gate on this entry, not a footnote**: 50 new packages, the full added list read, `pnpm-workspace.yaml` byte-for-byte unchanged with `allowBuilds` still only `sharp`, no build script blocked, `pnpm audit` identical before and after (9 findings, all pre-existing paths, zero entering through the new dependencies) verified by restoring the baseline lockfile and re-running rather than by eyeballing, and D-19's pins (`next@15.5.21`, `react@19.2.4`) confirmed untouched. One unfamiliar transitive — `obug`, a `debug`-adjacent name of exactly the shape the July 2025 campaign exploited — was chased to a zero-dependency fork by a Vite core maintainer **published via npm OIDC trusted publishing rather than a laptop token**, which is the specific failure mode that incident turned on. |

| D-54 | **The backup dead man is EXTERNAL, it is fed only by success, and silence is the alarm** | `scripts/backup/backup-health.sh` gains one final step, reached only when `failures == 0`: `heartbeat.sh` → `python -m scripts.host_heartbeat` → one GET at `BACKUP_HEARTBEAT_URL`, a hosted **Healthchecks.io** check configured at 15-minute period / 1-hour grace. No new deployable, no infrastructure we run, no second scheduler — the emitter is the check that already computes the verdict. The URL is a `Settings` field (`.env.example` + `check_env_parity`), injected from the secrets manager, treated as a CREDENTIAL (anyone holding it can silence the alarm) and never logged: operator output names a 12-char digest. Unset = a stated no-op, announced once per state transition in journald, never a silent pass. An undelivered ping is `backup_heartbeat_undelivered` in the journal and does NOT touch the failure count, the exit status or the mail path. `app-python.sh` is extracted so the alert relay and the heartbeat share ONE implementation of "run a repo module as `postgres`, unattended". `tests/backup_heartbeat_test.py` (12 tests) drives the real script with stubbed `psql`/`wal-g`/`systemctl` against a local HTTP monitor; `runbooks/backup-heartbeat-silent.md` is the triage, drill §7.8 is the proof. | **Closes D-50's named residual**, which was the last alarm in the system with nowhere to go. Every other alarm here is a MESSAGE and therefore dies with its sender: the host off or off-network, systemd not running, or the mail path broken beyond us each remove the observer along with the observed, and absence of alerts is indistinguishable from health. Only an observer outside the failure domain turns SILENCE into a page. **The asymmetry is the mechanism, and the tests are load-bearing on the half that is easy to "improve" away**: a failing run must ping NOTHING, or the check silently degrades into "this script ran" and sits green through a broken chain — so the vendor's `/fail` and `/start` endpoints go deliberately unused (failure already has a delivery path, and a second one is two dedupe windows on one fact — `notify.sh`'s own argument). **Vendor chosen against three real alternatives, argued with citations in `scripts/host_heartbeat.py`.** Self-hosting the same BSD-licensed software was the strongest-looking option and loses twice: new infrastructure we run, and an observer inside the failure domain. **Sentry Crons was the serious contender** — already a vendor here, also a plain check-in URL — rejected because `SENTRY_DSN` is optional and unset by default (the last alarm standing would depend on a credential error reporting may not have), because that credential is the platform project key rather than a single-purpose bearer secret we would be putting on the database host, and because a missed check-in rides Sentry's quota and alert rules, so an unrelated error storm could silence backup monitoring. Dead Man's Snitch (1 free check, no self-host path) and Cronitor / Better Stack / UptimeRobot (heartbeat as a side feature, priced with a bundle) are dominated for one check and one operator. The exit door matters more than the price: the protocol is a GET at a URL, so migrating providers — or self-hosting under duress — is a config change. **Accepted residual, stated rather than solved:** the monitoring vendor being down tells nobody anything; a monitor watching the monitor is a regress that costs more than it buys at this size, and the quarterly drill re-proves the check still turns red instead of assuming it. Doc pages were egress-blocked from the build environment (as Bolna's were for D-52), so the ping semantics rest on search-surfaced documentation and are re-verified by hand at the first drill. |
| D-55 | **The webhook ack budget is a per-process CAPACITY rule, and the only defect behind the convoy was a connection pool that churned** | Diagnosed by measurement, then split into the two things it actually is. **(a) The code fix**, `apps/api/db/session.py`: the shared async engine now runs `pool_size=DB_POOL_SIZE` (new `Settings` field, default 16), `max_overflow=0` and `pool_timeout=5s`, replacing SQLAlchemy's defaults of 5 + 10 overflow and an unbounded-in-practice 30s wait. **(b) The sizing rule**, DEPLOYMENT §2a: `ack_p50 ≈ in-flight ÷ acks-per-second-per-process`, so `max in-flight per process = 0.5 x T`; at the measured T≈250/s that is ~125, giving 2 uvicorn workers for a 100-concurrent pilot and 4 for 250+ production, with a connection budget table that needs `max_connections = 200` on the VPS. OPERATIONS §5 gains the triage (flat distribution = add processes; `webhook_claim_timeout` = incident). **(c) The enforcement**, `tests/voice_runtime_ack_budget_test.py` §1b: three DETERMINISTIC assertions — a warm receiver opens ZERO new Postgres connections under a storm twice the pool's width, the engine has no overflow and takes its ceiling from config with a bounded `pool_timeout` above the durable deadline, and the connection is checked in BEFORE the post-commit Redis write. No millisecond bound was added. | The storm suite (commit 7c9f143) recorded `X-Ack-Ms` p50 going 110ms at 1 concurrent → 669ms at 96 → 1389ms at 384 with a FLAT distribution, breaching hard rule 3 at roughly 100 in flight per process — against D-32's Bolna numbers of 100 concurrent on Pilots and 250+ in production. **Four plausible causes were measured and three were eliminated.** Pool exhaustion: NO — the connection is acquired lazily at first execute, and instrumented checkout wait was p50 0.1ms at 192 concurrent; pools of 8, 16 and 32 performed identically. The enqueue inside the transaction: NO — 26ms p50 at 192 concurrent, and a bare-Redis control ran 384 concurrent GETs in 44ms. Lock contention on the unique index: NO — the distinct-key storm, which contends for nothing, was as slow as the same-key herd. CPU work on the ack path (json, hashing, pydantic): NO as an isolated hotspot — accept-to-first-await was 0.5ms p50. **What it IS: the process is CPU-saturated on ONE core.** Measured out of process against real uvicorn, `utime+stime` sat at 92–99% of a single core from 8 concurrent upward, ~3.5ms of CPU per delivery, and throughput pinned at ~250 acks/s while latency tracked `in-flight ÷ throughput` to within a few percent at every width — Little's Law, i.e. a queue, which is exactly why the distribution is flat. A cProfile attribution found no hotspot to remove: psycopg 14%, asyncio 13%, SQLAlchemy 10%, Starlette/FastAPI 9%, redis-py 7%, our own code 2.8%. **That makes it a sizing question, and a sizing rule that is not written down is not a rule** — hence §2a rather than a code change nobody can point at. **The one genuine defect** was found in the same profile: connections above `pool_size` are SINGLE USE (SQLAlchemy closes them on return — sqlalchemy/sqlalchemy#11707, where the maintainer's answer is "avoid the overflow and make the pool size bigger"), so at 100 concurrent the receiver burned **186 fresh Postgres backends for 1448 requests, 34 per second**, while never exceeding 15 concurrent. Each costs ~6ms of CPU in this process — `password_encryption = scram-sha-256` means PBKDF2 on every connect — so ~20% of the single core was being spent re-authenticating connections it had just discarded. A same-process A/B at 128 in flight, with the old configuration re-run afterwards as a control, measured 200 → 232 acks/s (+20%) and p50 628 → 542ms, and churn falling from 5.9–8.0 to 1.2 new connections per 100 requests; against real uvicorn the backend count fell 186 → 18. **`pool_pre_ping` was measured and KEPT**: turning it off bought a further 12% (232 → 265 acks/s), and it is declined, because the receiver's traffic is bursty by nature and a connection killed by an idle NAT between campaigns would surface as a failed ack at an endpoint whose vendor never retries (D-31) — a lost call is not worth 12% of a throughput number we can buy back with a `--workers` flag. `pool_recycle` was the alternative and guesses an interval instead of asking. **Deliberately NOT done, with the arithmetic:** folding the claim and the forensic INSERT into one round trip would cut 1 of the 4 statements per ack (~10–15% throughput) at the cost of complicating a durable claim shared by four callers — a quarter of what one extra process buys, on the code that decides exactly-once. **Why the enforcement is a connection COUNT and not a millisecond bound**: a wall-clock assertion under concurrency measures the CI runner, which both the storm file and `voice_runtime_security_test.py` already refuse on the grounds that a flaky latency test gets deleted along with the guarantee it carried. A count of connections opened is exact on any machine at any speed and is the mechanism the latency was made of; it was seen to fail by restoring `max_overflow=10` — 8 connections opened where 0 are allowed — and the sibling assertions were seen to fail by moving the fast-path write back inside the transaction and by re-defaulting the pool. Correctness was untouched throughout — every assertion in the storm suite still holds unchanged. |
| D-56 | **An in-call opt-out is caught in TWO layers with ONE write path, and the detector is a phrase list that says so** | `apps/api/compliance/optout.py` is the whole seam: a curated Telugu/Hindi/English phrase list (`detect_opt_out`, romanised **and** native script, caller turns only) plus `record_call_optout`, which suppresses through the existing `compliance.service.add_to_dnc` (`source='call_optout'`, `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`), appends ONE `consent_ledger` withdrawal (`purpose='marketing'`, `consent_source='inbound_call_verbal'`, evidence = detector, rule, language, turn index and the caller's matched words) and writes one `audit_log` entry. Two callers reach it and nothing else does: **(a)** the post-call pipeline, as step 2b — before extraction, because extraction is a 30s model round trip and the deadline is 30s; **(b)** an in-call tool, `POST /tools/v1/{engine}/opt-out` in voice-runtime (source-IP verified, bounded body, no DB writes, ack measured) → the `record_in_call_optout` ARQ job, which resolves tenant, number and direction from the authenticated Get Execution rather than from the payload. Dedupe is on `(tenant, phone, purpose, status, call_id)`, so a replay, a double tool call and both layers firing on one call produce ONE record. The xfail pin `eval_known_gaps_test::test_the_post_call_pipeline_writes_an_in_call_opt_out_to_the_dnc_list` is promoted to `tests/call_optout_test.py`, whose proof is the real dispatcher answering `dnc_blocked`; `scripts/eval.py` now scores the detector against every fixture in both directions. | **The hole:** `dnc.SOURCES` carried `call_optout` from the day the module shipped and only TESTS wrote it — a caller could opt out, the agent could confirm, and the next tick dialled them again. **Why both layers rather than the one the xfail proposed:** they fail differently. The tool depends on the model invoking it and on a vendor behaviour that is UNVERIFIED (OPERATIONS §2 gate 8 — Bolna custom functions, no documented tool timeout; D-31/D-32 say that is a gate or a marked assumption, never a silent premise), so it cannot be the only layer; the transcript pass runs on every completed call but is late by the length of the call, so a 40-minute call leaves 38 minutes in which the number is dialable. The union is what makes hard rule 5 true for the population. **Why a phrase list and not a model:** an LLM call on a compliance path adds a per-call cost, a second dependency and the failure mode 'the extractor was down, so nobody was suppressed'. The engine's own tool call IS the comprehension layer; this is the deterministic floor under it, and the file says out loud that it will miss phrasings nobody wrote down. **The failure direction is chosen:** a false positive suppresses a caller who did not ask (annoying, lawful, recoverable); a false negative dials someone who did (a TCCCPR violation with the CLIENT's registration on the line, 5+ complaints in 10 days ⇒ TSP action). So the patterns favour recall — except where the negation carries the meaning (`call cheyandi` = 'please call' vs `cheyakandi` = 'don't call'), which would suppress the caller who asked for a callback. **Regulatory basis, researched not recalled** (TCCCPR 2018 Reg. 6, the Second Amendment of 12 Feb 2025, PIB PRID 2102413; gazette PDFs are cited in the module because this environment's proxy blocks trai.gov.in): preferences must be given effect **in near real time and in no case beyond twenty-four hours** — our 30-second tick deadline is stricter and stays that way; revocation must be recorded **immutably and non-repudiably** (hence the append-only ledger row rather than a flag); a sender may not re-solicit an opted-out customer for **90 days** (hence `call_optout` staying outside `dnc.REMOVABLE_SOURCES`); and the obligation sits with the **Principal Entity — the client — regardless of using a third-party telemarketer**, which is why the suppression is tenant-scoped and the evidence has to be legible to them. What this is NOT: a DND/preference registration. That is an Access Provider function (1909/DLT) we do not hold, and the module says so. **Known residual:** consent withdrawal and erasure demands (`rt_cl_consent_withdrawn_midcall`, `rt_re_erasure_request_midcall`) are deliberately out of scope here — they are DPDP §12 requests with their own surface, and folding them in would file a suppression nobody asked for while still leaving the erasure unserved. |
| D-57 | **The dispatch tick's screening loop moved INTO Postgres, and the tick became single-flight** | `dispatch_scan(active_statuses, active_horizon)` (migration `a8d4f21c9b06`) — **SECURITY INVOKER** plpgsql that walks `engine_agent_routes` and, per tenant, sets `app.tenant_id` and reads that tenant's live outbound lines and running-campaign flag under that tenant's own policies, returning ONLY the tenants that hold a line or have a campaign. `apps/workers/campaign_dispatch.py` therefore opens tenant sessions for WORK instead of for existence, and takes a Redis `SET NX PX` lease (`_tick_lease`) so two ticks can never both spend the shared line pool. Two partial indexes (`ix_calls_outbound_live`, `ix_campaigns_running`) keep each probe index-only. No new table, so no new RLS policy. | **Measured, not estimated:** 12,070 dispatchable tenants cost 12,070 sessions and 22.9s on a job scheduled every 30s — and the split was 48% session setup / 30% query / 21% commit, i.e. two thirds session machinery, 80% of it CPU inside the worker, which is why parallelising measured only 22.9s→17.2s at 8-way. Deleting the sessions gives 0.25s (1M call rows, warm). **Rejected:** `SECURITY DEFINER` owned by a `BYPASSRLS` role (faster still, and hard rule 1 in a wig); a GUC widening of `campaigns`/`calls` (a `calls` row carries `to_e164` — a platform-wide read of it is a PII leak, not a refactor); a trigger-maintained global (tenant, lines, campaigns) index (a new table, a new RLS exemption and write amplification on the busiest table, to answer a question Postgres already answers in 0.25s); bounded-parallel tenant sessions (the cost is client CPU, and `max_overflow=0` means the pool queues rather than bursts — sqlalchemy#11707). **Overlap was a real bug:** an arq cron id embeds its intended execution time, so consecutive ticks are different jobs and DO overlap; the claim CAS already prevented a double DIAL, but the platform pool was a read-then-act (BACKEND-PATTERNS §5), and arq's own remedy — a fixed `cron(job_id=...)` — would turn a 30s tick into a 60s one (`keep_cronjob_progress`), which hard rule 5's DNC deadline forbids. |
| D-58 | **Every WRITE on the ops router is step-up confirmed, and a confirmation that cannot be SIZED is not one** | `POST /v1/ops/outbox/replay` was the one mutation on that router with no `X-Confirm-Action` check, and the console collected a typed word it never sent — a confirmation dialog that confirmed nothing to the server. It now takes `replay_dead_letters`, or `replay_dead_letters:<job>` when scoped, refused in BOTH directions so a header can never authorise a wider act than the request performs; `GET /v1/ops/audit/verify` is the router's one deliberate exception. `GET /v1/ops/platform` additionally publishes `outbox_dead_letters` (depth, per-`job` breakdown, oldest age) from the SAME grouped aggregate that feeds the `outbox_dlq_depth` metric. | It is the widest-blast-radius control in the product and it was the least guarded: replaying does not flip rows, it RE-SENDS — real HMAC-signed webhooks into clients' own CRMs, real Sheets appends, real emails, across every tenant at once, from one unconfirmed POST. Scoping follows from the 100-row cap rather than from taste: unscoped, an operator recovering one client's CRM webhooks out of a backlog of dead-lettered emails replays 100 emails, reads `replayed: 100` as success, and leaves every webhook parked. The depth is on the existing route because `ops:manage` is a MUTATING permission, so a NEW GET declaring it would have to be added to `ADMIN_CONSOLE_GETS` — an allowlist whose own test warns that entries are how it becomes a hole. **Breaking, not grandfathered** (D-45's precedent): the runbook's curl and the console moved in the same change, and the runbook had twice stated something true when written and false when read. Per-TENANT scoping is refused rather than approximated — `outbox_messages` has no `tenant_id` and the ids sit unindexed inside the JSONB payload, so it is a migration under hard rule 8, and the console says so instead of letting an operator assume the scope bounds whose data moves. |
| D-59 | **A chain that appends inside a transaction is serialized BY that transaction, not by a lease** | The `audit_log` hash chain moves from BACKEND-PATTERNS §5's Redis `SET NX PX` mutex to `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtextextended('audit:chain', 0))`, and the Redis head cache is **deleted** — the head's only home is the last row of `audit_log`. `at` is stamped `clock_timestamp()` rather than `now()`, `(at, id)` is indexed (`ix_audit_log_chain`, built CONCURRENTLY), and `GET /v1/ops/audit/verify` walks the WHOLE log by default, returning `entries_checked` / `complete` / the `at` range beside the verdict. §5 and §7 are amended in place; `credit_ledger` already worked this way (`billing/service.py::lock_tenant_credits`), so this is one way per problem rather than a new one. | The Redis lock was not merely mis-tuned, it was **not consulted**: the `SET NX` result went into a variable read only by the `finally` that released it, so a writer that lost the race proceeded anyway. Repairing that would not have been enough. Correctness needs no second writer to read the head between our read and our COMMIT, and the entry is appended in the CALLER's transaction (§7's own choice) — a window of unbounded length, so any TTL is either too short (a 4s commit outlives a 3s lease and the next writer chains onto the same head) or long enough that a crashed holder parks every audit write behind it. The cached head fails the same way one level down: published inside the transaction a ROLLBACK erases it, published after COMMIT a crash in the gap loses it, and either leaves the next writer forking off a head that does not exist — a **durable** break in the one artefact whose entire value is being unbreakable. Validating the cache against the table is the query the cache existed to avoid. An advisory xact lock is released by COMMIT *or* ROLLBACK, the two events that decide whether the row exists, so its lifetime is exactly the window needing protection; there is no TTL, and no second system whose outage forks the chain. `now()` was the same class of bug in the timestamp: it returns TRANSACTION START, so two overlapping transactions could write entries whose `at` ordering contradicted their chain order and `verify_chain` would report tamper on an untampered log. The scope change is the visible half: `limit=1000` on `ORDER BY at ASC` verified the OLDEST thousand, so on any real log the green tick covered the entries nobody was worried about and said nothing about last night — and the console rendered it as an unqualified pass. **Cost, stated plainly:** audit writers now queue for the REMAINDER of the holder's transaction, not just its INSERT, which is inherent (releasing earlier lets the next writer read an uncommitted head) and is why `write_audit` belongs late in a transaction; a holder that then blocks on another audit writer deadlocks, which Postgres detects and raises — loud and recoverable, versus a silent fork. |
| D-60 | **Inbound A/B attribution is a question of fact, not a bucket we may draw afterwards** | `workers/pipeline.py::_record_arm_the_engine_ran` attributes a call to a script arm if and only if `ExecutionSnapshot.engine_agent_ref` names an ARM's own engine agent — the ref `service.publish_variant` minted for it — and attributes nothing when it names the agent's. `agents/assignment.py::arm_of_engine_ref` is that lookup, and it writes through the same `record` (same transaction as the `calls` row, `ON CONFLICT (call_id) DO NOTHING`) as the dial path, so there is still exactly one kind of assignment row and no call can change arms. `experiments.results_for` stops asserting `attributed_directions = ("outbound",)` and READS the directions off the assigned calls, and a two-way agent's coverage note now prints how many completed inbound calls in the window are in neither arm. `start` still refuses an inbound-only agent. No new table, so no migration. | The ROADMAP called this "an assignment lookup at inbound call creation", and that framing is the trap: the lookup it implies is `assign()`, which DRAWS a bucket. Outbound may draw because we choose to dial and can dial the arm we drew. An inbound caller dialled the client's number, which answers with the agent (FLOWS §3), and heard whatever the live prompt pointer said — so a bucket drawn when the call row is created names an arm nobody spoke, and reports the caller's real conversion under it with nothing in the data to show what happened. **The two-speed publishing machinery does not rescue it either**: the live version often EQUALS the control's, but attributing inbound on that basis puts 100% of a non-randomised traffic stream into one arm, and Newcombe's interval — which assumes two randomised binomials — would then be describing the direction mix rather than the script. What IS available is unambiguous: every arm is its own engine agent with its own ref, so the engine's answer to "which agent object ran this call?" is the arm, with no inference at any step. That fact is recorded wherever it appears, in both directions, which is why the same change repairs an OUTBOUND defect nobody had noticed: `dispatch_call` records only when its own INSERT wins the `engine_call_id` conflict, so an engine webhook arriving before our transaction committed created the `calls` row first and left a call that demonstrably ran arm A carrying no arm — a silent under-count of one side of the comparison. **Deliberately NOT built:** inbound randomisation via the engine's incoming-call webhook (selecting the agent per call is an UNVERIFIED vendor capability — D-31/D-32 discipline says that is a gate in OPERATIONS §2, not a premise), and a `unattributed_inbound` API field (the count reaches the reader through `coverage_note`, which the prompt screen already renders; a field no screen reads is the half-wiring this entry exists to remove). |
| D-61 | **Hard rule 6 is enforced at the SPAN EXPORTER, and the hand-called hook D-49 kept is deleted** | `observability.redact_trace_payload` — the hook CLAUDE.md's hard rule 6 names, which D-49 deliberately KEPT as a shape after Langfuse config was removed — is gone. Every span now leaves through `_RedactingSpanExporter`: attributes re-sanitized, exception events reduced to `exception.type` + `exception.escaped`, `Status.description` replaced by the exception type, span name through `redact_text`, and a span whose redaction RAISES is dropped rather than exported. Sentry loses `traces_sample_rate` and gains `before_breadcrumb=scrub_breadcrumb`. The OTel/error-tracker correlation the old hook justified moves to a real caller: `scrub_event` stamps `tags.otel_trace_id`. | **D-49's premise stopped being true, and the audit that checked it found a LIVE leak rather than a tidy-up.** D-49 kept the hook because no real enforcement point existed; it recorded a shape, and a shape enforces nothing. Every call site in this repo was already clean — no `set_attribute` / `add_event` / `record_exception` exists outside `observability.py` — which is exactly why this survived. The PII came from three fields the OTel SDK writes ITSELF: `start_as_current_span` defaults to `record_exception=True` and `set_status_on_exception=True`, so any exception escaping any span writes `exception.message`, `exception.stacktrace` (a span EVENT, which `sanitize_attributes` never sees) and a status description. Reproduced against the live in-memory exporter with a transcript in full; the production vector is `str(IntegrityError)`, which embeds `[parameters: {'p': '+91…'}]`, reached by a duplicate-lead insert in `pipeline.lead_upsert`. Fixed at the exporter and not the call sites because `record_exception=False` at four sites fails at the fifth — a guard you must remember is the one that fails when the tree grows fastest (D-29's own words). Message and stacktrace are DROPPED rather than `redact_text`-ed, on the same fail-closed doctrine as the attribute allowlist: `redact_text` catches phone-shaped runs and length, not a caller's name or a Telugu sentence; span, ids, ERROR status and exception type all survive, so the signal does. Two Sentry gaps came with it: `traces_sample_rate` was running a SECOND, unfiltered tracing pipeline whose transaction events `before_send` never sees (sentry-python#1226), and breadcrumbs are built by the logging integration from `record.getMessage()` BEFORE our formatter redacts. **Latency (hard rule 3) is zero on the ack path by construction** — the exporter runs inside `BatchSpanProcessor`'s own thread, never the request thread; measured at 9.12us/span anyway, with voice-runtime's disabled-tracing cost unchanged at 2.07us. The hook is deleted rather than left beside the automatic one because two ways to redact one thing is the accumulation CLAUDE.md forbids, and the hand-called one is the way that gets forgotten. **A Langfuse restoration therefore inherits a requirement, not a hook**: it must export through this pipeline, because a direct SDK client would be a second unfiltered path — which is precisely what `traces_sample_rate` turned out to be. |
| D-62 | **An A/B arm's counts are resolved BY DIRECTION, because one of them can hold calls nobody dialled** | `VariantResult.dialled` / `VariantOut.dialled` are replaced — no alias, D-45/D-58 precedent — by `outbound_dialled` (calls we PLACED into the arm, any status) and `inbound_attributed` (how many of `attributed` arrived inbound). `attributed` keeps its meaning and stays the denominator of `rate` and of Newcombe's interval; the statistics are untouched. `_counts_sql` filters each count on `calls.direction`, and the prompt screen renames the column to "Dialled (outbound)" and folds the mixture into `rateReading` itself, so no rendering of a rate can drop the sentence saying part of its denominator was never split. | D-60 gave `call_variant_assignments` a second writer, and `dialled` was `count(*)` over that table — so an inbound call an arm's own line answered was published to a client under a column meaning "calls we placed for you". A direction-neutral RENAME would have made the label true and left both numbers it feeds wrong. **The connect-rate diagnostic is an outbound question**: the gap between placed and completed is how a telephony fault is told apart from a script fault, and an inbound call arrives already connected, so folding it into both sides moves the ratio towards 1 — a diagnostic that fails in the direction of looking healthy. **And the count that can actually be a MIXTURE is `attributed`, not `dialled`**: outbound calls are randomised by `assignment.bucket_of`, inbound ones are not drawn at all (D-60), so two arms mixing the populations in different proportions are not the two randomised binomials Newcombe assumes. `coverage_note` already said so in prose; a number is what lets the screen qualify the specific rate it is rendering, and what a future decision to restrict the analysis set would be made on. **Deliberately NOT done: restricting the comparison to outbound.** That is the statistically cleaner estimator and it is a change to what the product measures — it would silently discard conversions D-60 chose to record as facts, and it should be made when this field shows the mixture is non-zero in practice, not ahead of the evidence. Today's provisioning answers inbound with the agent, so `inbound_attributed` is 0 on every live experiment. |
| D-63 | **The onboarding setup fee is billed through the invoice, and "once" is a unique index rather than a reader's `if`** | `plans.setup_fee` stops being a column nothing reads. New append-only, RLS'd `one_time_charges` table (migration c7e1a4b90d63) with `UNIQUE (tenant_id, kind, ref)`; `billing/charges.py` writes the setup charge with an unconditional `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` and `billing/invoice.py` reads the month's charges as ordinary lines. The charge is stamped to the tenant's ONBOARDING month (the IST month of `organizations.created_at`) and carries the amount AS BILLED plus the `plan_id` that quoted it. A NULL, zero or negative `setup_fee` writes nothing and prints nothing. The `UNWIRED_BASELINE` entry is deleted; `tests/setup_fee_test.py` holds the four ways it could double-charge. | An invoice here is a DERIVED statement (D-46's whole premise), so "charged on the first invoice and never again" cannot mean "the first time this code runs" — regeneration is normal. It has to be a durable fact the render re-reads, which is a ledger row, and the ledger is where the idempotency belongs: `WHERE NOT EXISTS` in front of an insert is the check-then-write hole that put duplicate pairs on `credit_ledger` (f9c2b41a8e57), so the guard goes IN the write (BACKEND-PATTERNS §5) and the second of two concurrent generations blocks on the index entry and writes nothing. **The advisory lock the credit ledger uses was considered and rejected**: that ledger must READ its predecessor to compute `balance_after`, so only a lock covers its critical section; here there is nothing to read and a unique index also binds a future writer who forgets the lock. **The month is the onboarding month, not "whichever statement was opened first"** — the latter makes July's invoice depend on whether August's was rendered before it, which is the same defect D-46 closed one level up. Its cost is stated rather than hidden: a tenant whose onboarding month is never rendered is never billed (the row is written whenever that month IS rendered), and there is no proration to split a fee across a plan change because D-46 has none to build on. **The amount is frozen in the row** because `plans` is mutable and a re-derived fee could change a statement a client already paid. **Deliberately NOT built:** an `invoices` table (still only needed when a statement acquires state we cannot derive — sent/paid/overdue), a signup-time side effect (nothing in the product creates a plan row, so there is no such moment, and a fee recorded at signup and rendered elsewhere is two sources for one number), and any reversal SURFACE — a waiver is a compensating row under its own `ref`, which the invoice already prints. |
| D-64 | **The setup fee is issued by a scheduled job, and rendering an invoice writes nothing** | `billing/charges.py` splits into `issue_setup_fee` (the only writer) and `one_time_charge_lines` (a pure read); `billing/invoice.py` no longer writes, and no longer reads `organizations.created_at`. New `apps/workers/billing.py::issue_one_time_charges`, a DAILY arq cron registered in `workers/settings.py` at 02:05 worker-local with an explicit `max_tries`, issues every owed-but-unrecorded fee. Migration e3f9c2a71d84 adds `unbilled_setup_fees(charge_kind, charge_ref)`, a SECURITY INVOKER plpgsql scan modelled on `dispatch_scan` (D-57): it loops organizations and `set_config('app.tenant_id', ...)` per tenant, so `plans` and `one_time_charges` answer under that tenant's own policies. The unconditional `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` and the unique index of D-63 are untouched. | D-63 named its own gap and this closes it: the fee was recorded when a statement was RENDERED, and nothing in this product renders statements on a schedule, so a tenant whose first invoice nobody opened was never charged — and the only caller was a GET, i.e. a read with a side effect. **`POST .../issue` was considered and rejected**: a button is still a human, which is the same defect one layer down, and it would add admin API surface with no `apps/web` caller. Moving the write into `issue_setup_fee` buys the read/write split without an endpoint. **Daily rather than monthly** because the fee is owed the moment an operator attaches a plan quoting one, and a monthly run would leave a tenant's own in-progress statement without its setup line for up to 31 days and then grow one. Crucially THE SCHEDULE DOES NOT PICK THE MONTH — `issue_setup_fee` derives it from the tenant's `organizations.created_at` via `ist_billing_month`, so the stamp is identical whichever night the tick runs; that matters because arq evaluates `cron()` against the worker's LOCAL clock and a billing decision must never depend on a container's TZ. **`arq.cron()` defaults `max_tries` to 1 and `WorkerSettings.max_tries` does not apply to a function carrying its own**, so the retry ladder CLAUDE.md mandates would have been silently absent; it is passed explicitly and verified against a real `arq.worker.Worker` schedule. **The judgement call worth a reviewer's attention:** the cross-tenant scan runs under `admin_session()`. `engine_agent_routes` — what `campaign_dispatch` and `retention` use to avoid it — is a SUBSET of this population (a tenant can owe an onboarding fee having never published an agent), and c7e4b19d3f52's rule is "a superset here and a subset never"; the alternative was a third `RLS_EXEMPT_TENANT_COLUMNS` entry making every client's commercial terms globally readable. `app.admin` widens `USING` on `organizations` only, `plans` and `one_time_charges` stay fenced inside the loop, and every write happens in an ordinary `tenant_session`. The fencing is load-bearing and tested, not asserted: dropping the per-iteration `set_config` turns 6 of 22 tests red. |
| D-65 | **A state transition answers three questions, not one** | New `apps/api/db/transition.py::transition_status` — CAS first and unconditionally, then, only on the losing path, a write-free SELECT to name which zero-row fact it was: row absent = 404, already in the target state = SUCCESS with no second write, any other state = 409 `invalid_status_transition` NAMING the state actually found. `campaigns/service.py::set_campaign_status` and `kb/service.py::approve_source`/`reject_source` delegate to it and return whether THIS call moved the row; `admin/routes.py` writes its audit row only on a real transition. No new error codes. | All three cases previously collapsed into one 409, which lied in two directions. It told an operator reviewing a queue that an already-approved source "is not awaiting approval" when the outcome they wanted had already happened — and a repeat click wrote a SECOND audit row. And it answered 409 for an id belonging to ANOTHER TENANT, i.e. reported that a row exists; RLS makes those rows invisible, so 404 is both the correct answer and the one that does not confirm the id. Already-in-state is a success per RFC 9110 §9.2.2, and the repeat deliberately writes NOTHING so `approved_by`/`approved_at`/`rejection_reason` stay the FIRST reviewer's — an approval is attributable, and a double-clicked button must not rewrite who signed off. The shape is not new: `ingest/service.py::set_active` and `integrations/service.py::deactivate_endpoint` already did exactly this for BOOLEAN flags (which have no "different state" branch); this generalises that one to a status column rather than inventing a second way. `set_campaign_status` also stopped INTERPOLATING its from-statuses into the SQL as string literals — they are bound parameters now; the values are internal constants, so this was one refactor away from mattering rather than exploitable today. **No compliance gate was added or removed**, on the resume path or any other: a campaign can sit paused for a week, so a gate at THAT moment proves nothing about the moment it dials, and the dial-time check is the enforcement (`campaign_dispatch_audit_test.py` pins it). **Left for its own slice:** `launch_campaign`'s own CAS still raises a vague `frm`, campaign pause/resume still write no audit row at all, and lead status / DLT template status / experiment conclude were not audited for this pattern. |
| D-66 | **A tenant's commercial terms are RECORDED, never edited — and the absence of them is a state an operator must clear, not a row we invent** | New `billing/terms.py` writes `plans` INSERT-only, resolving what a row supersedes through the EXISTING `plan_in_effect_sql` rather than a second ordering. `GET/POST /v1/admin/tenants/{id}/commercial-terms` and the Commercials screen; onboarding still writes no plan row, and the GET returns a named `state` (`none` / `unpriced` / `lapsed` / `set`) the screen renders as a refusal. Loosening a hard cap additionally requires `ops:manage` AND the `X-Confirm-Action` header. Migration a1c4f70b9e28 adds `ck_plans_window_ordered` (`NOT VALID` then `VALIDATE`, per b1d5c8e73f04). | Nothing in this product wrote a plan row: the only writer of `plans` was the client cap upsert, so invoices, margin, caps and D-64's setup-fee cron all sat on a row an operator inserted BY HAND. **Seeding a default row was rejected** for two reasons that are stronger than the convenience: inventing prices is forbidden, so a seeded row could only carry NULLs — and an all-NULL row is EXACTLY equivalent to no row for every reader (`read_caps`, `usage_summary`, the setup-fee cron), so it buys the appearance of configuration and nothing else; and it would destroy the distinction `warn_no_plan_in_effect` depends on, which logs only when rows exist and none is in effect, making every new tenant look permanently misconfigured. **Recorded-never-edited** follows D-46: an in-place UPDATE re-prices a statement a client already paid, and the sabotage that proved the test suite holds this made next month's price start charging today. **Backdating is refused** for the same reason. **Churn deliberately does NOT close the plan window** — a window closed at churn leaves the final month unpriced and renders the last invoice at ₹0.00. `overage_rate_value` stays settable and NULL, because the retail number is a founder decision and the SURFACE was never what blocked it. |
| D-67 | **A suspended tenant stops dialling, which it did not before** | `compliance/service.py::account_stopped_blocker` refuses `suspended` / `churned` / soft-deleted, wired into `check_dispatch` immediately after the big red switch, and into `launch_blockers` under the same rule name. `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{id}/status` and the Lifecycle screen give the column its first writer, through the shared `db/transition.py` discriminator (D-65). Inbound is untouched. | `organizations.status` had a five-value CHECK constraint, was READ by the health board's ended-account filter, and was written by nothing — no suspend, reactivate or churn path existed anywhere. The part that made this a compliance defect rather than a missing button: **`check_dispatch` never read it.** That is the one gate every outbound path calls, so an account an operator believed suspended would have kept placing calls — and there was no way to suspend one to find out. Wiring it at the gate rather than per-caller covers the campaign tick, the call-this-lead button, the lead-callback webhook and WhatsApp escalation in one place, and fails CLOSED on an unreadable row. Inbound stays live because a suspended account's customers still dial the client's number and hanging up on them is a different decision. |
| D-68 | **Admin-realm MFA is enforced in `verify_token` from Clerk's `fva` claim, and `X-Confirm-Action` is KEPT beside it** | Clerk's session token carries `fva` = `[firstFactorAgeMinutes, secondFactorAgeMinutes]`, where `-1` means the factor was never verified for this session. `core/auth.py` refuses an admin-realm token whose `fva[1]` is `-1` (`mfa_required`) or whose claim is unreadable (`mfa_claim_missing`); absent fails CLOSED. The frontend `AdminMfaGate` explains the refusal and is keyed on the problem CODE, never the status. TRD §2 and SEC-COMP §5 now describe what exists. | Both docs called admin MFA MANDATORY and no MFA code existed anywhere in the tree — a documentation claim guarding the platform halt and the outbox replay. **Placement is the load-bearing choice**: in `verify_token`, not `current_admin` and not per-route, so that "an admin token" and "an admin token that passed MFA" are the same object — there is no `VerifiedToken(realm="admin")` in existence that skipped it, which covers ~60 route declarations and every future dependency by the act of calling the verifier at all. **The sabotage is the evidence that this is not theatre**: with the backend check removed and the UI gate fully intact, the frontend suite stayed 4/4 GREEN while four backend tests went red, including the big red switch actually going through. **`X-Confirm-Action` is retained, not superseded**: MFA answers WHO holds this session, proved once for 12h; the header answers WHICH ACT they meant on WHICH TARGET, proved per request. A fully MFA'd session is exactly what a tab left open on an unlocked laptop is. **Clerk reverification (a FRESH second factor bound to high-risk actions) is deferred** — it needs a browser reverification flow this repo does not have, and gating the 3am halt lever on a prompt nobody can answer is how a control gets switched off. Named in OPERATIONS §8, not silently skipped. Citation honesty: `clerk.com` is blocked by this environment's egress proxy, so the reference that could actually be READ is the Clerk Go SDK declaring `FactorVerificationAge [2]int64 \`json:"fva"\``. **An operator must still enable and require a second factor in the admin Clerk application** — the API can only refuse, it cannot make Clerk offer a factor. |
| D-69 | **Deploy path: one image, component-scoped `vps-deploy.sh`, expand-then-swap migrations** | `Dockerfile` (one image, three services), `compose.prod.yml`, `scripts/vps-deploy.sh`, `infra/nginx/` (including the `rate-zones.conf.template` the drift guard had been DEFERRING), a gated `.github/workflows/deploy.yml`, and `runbooks/deploy-failed.md`. | `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` made `scripts/vps-deploy.sh` THE deploy mechanism and the file did not exist; there was no nginx config, no Dockerfile and no deploy job — everything in this repo was undeployable. **Migrate → then swap, never auto-downgrade**: hard rule 8 (no drop in the release that stops writing) IS the statement "old code runs against new schema", so the migrate→swap window is safe by construction while the reverse window is not. An automatic downgrade could drop a column already written to, turning a failed deploy into data loss. **Hard rule 3 is honoured by a reviewable path→component map plus `--no-deps`**: `apps/api/crm/**` deploys api and workers and does not touch voice-runtime; `core/**` and `packages/shared/**` DO deploy it, because it imports them — stated rather than hidden. Swap order is workers → api → voice-runtime last. Migrations run FROM THE DEPLOYED IMAGE, resolving a real self-contradiction in the doc (§2 "Python is NOT needed on the host" vs §4.7 running `uv run alembic` on the host) and guaranteeing the revision set that runs is the one in the artifact being shipped. **Blue/green was rejected for now**: a third-party rollout CLI plugin on the production host is a supply-chain surface (hard rule 9) and needs its own decision; the recreate gap is named and the reconciliation poller (D-31) is what absorbs it. Zero third-party GitHub Actions in the deploy workflow, deliberately — the runner IS the target host. Four independent gates, and `VPS_DEPLOY_ENABLED` unset means merging this changes nothing. **Closing the drift deferral ARMED a comparator that had never been able to fire**: it now refuses a rate-zone disagreement between the doc and the template, naming both and stating that the edge enforces the template. |
| D-70 | **voice-runtime derives the client IP from ONE header written by ONE trusted hop, and refuses when it cannot** | `engine_intake.client_ip` returns `str | None`. Outside `APP_ENV=local` it reads exactly one header — `CF-Connecting-IP` — and only when the socket peer is a trusted-proxy CIDR; `X-Forwarded-For` is no longer read at all. Absent, blank, a list, a `host:port`, or an untrusted peer all yield `None`, which is a 401 plus `webhook_source_rejected`. nginx now WRITES `CF-Connecting-IP` from `$remote_addr` (`calevate-proxy.conf`). | The previous code preferred `cf-connecting-ip` and otherwise took the **leftmost** `X-Forwarded-For` entry. Leftmost is caller-controlled by construction — MDN's XFF page says a security use must only trust addresses added by a trusted proxy, and RFC 7239 §8.1 says the same of `Forwarded` — and Cloudflare APPENDS rather than replaces. This is the ENTIRE authenticity control for an unsigned engine (hard rule 3, TRD §5), so it was one misconfiguration away from accepting a forged source. It was safe only because the origin lock made `CF-Connecting-IP` always present, which is a property of the edge rather than of the code. **The fix also closed a hole nobody had named:** the origin lock allows `127.0.0.1` for deploy health polls, so an on-box process could POST a forged `CF-Connecting-IP` at the hooks vhost — nginx was passing the header through untouched. Having nginx write it means the value can only come from the hop we trust. **What the deployment must now guarantee**, stated because the code depends on it: `real_ip_header CF-Connecting-IP` + `real_ip_recursive on` + the CF ranges; the `proxy_set_header`; and nothing reaching the container except through that nginx. Adding a second proxy changes the hop count and is a CODE change in the same commit. Failure is now a visible 401 storm plus poller catch-up rather than silent acceptance. XFF splitting was removed, so the ack path got cheaper, not dearer. |
| D-71 | **The subject-access export is MODELLED, and a guardrail exception is now per-FIELD rather than per-route** | `POST /v1/compliance/subject-export` returns `SubjectExportOut` (six nested models, all `extra="forbid"`), validated BEFORE the audit write so a document that fails validation is never disclosed. `scripts/check_redaction_exposure.py`'s `ALLOWED_ROUTES` values became `RawDisclosure(reason, fields)`: `fields=None` keeps the old whole-path skip for the three existing entries, a named set keeps the model walk ON. New `GET /v1/compliance/deletion-requests` returns the tenant's erasure register — ids, status, timestamps, `subject_ref`, and no phone numbers. | The export returned `dict[str, Any]`, which meant it was not a field the guardrail judged safe — it was a field the guardrail **could not see at all**, on the one endpoint in this product whose payload is an entire named human being. `deletion_routes.py` had already made this argument about its own `proof`; this applies it where it mattered most. Making the exception field-scoped is the part worth keeping: a whole-path skip means the next field somebody adds to that response is unexamined forever, which is how a guardrail quietly stops guarding. Proven rather than asserted — adding `to_e164` to a call model is reported, and narrowing the field set makes both phone fields reappear. The register exists because a filed request was reachable only by its opaque id, so a client who closed the tab lost the handle on an in-flight legal obligation with a statutory clock on it. `subject_ref` (a hash) is included and the number is not, because a register of erasure requests that lists everyone's phone number is the opposite of data minimisation. |
| D-72 | **Lead lenses: one column registry for screen AND export; unknown columns degrade, unknown filters are REFUSED** | `crm/columns.py` is the single registry, resolved by both `list_leads_page` and `export_leads_csv` — there is no longer a header constant in the export. Facets are built from the agent's `extraction_schemas.fields` (enum fields only, `MAX_FACET_FIELDS = 8`), counts follow every other filter and ignore the facet's own. New `lead_saved_views` table (a7e2c40d9b53), private per user, resolved against the live schema on read. | SURFACES §2 calls this the parity floor and there were ZERO hits for `saved_view` or `bulk_` in the tree. The registry matters beyond tidiness: the screen and the file each had a hand-maintained column list and **they disagreed** — the file carried `source`/`created_at`, the screen carried `owner`/`updated_at`. Mirroring is now structural rather than promised. **The asymmetry is the safety decision:** an unknown COLUMN key is dropped and reported (it narrows, and screen and file drop it identically), but an unknown FILTER key is a 422 — dropping it would WIDEN the result set, on the one route that emits unmasked numbers. Saved views prune stale filters visibly at read time so a client is never silently handed a wider set. **Shared views are deferred with the real question named**: the hard part is who may edit a view three colleagues have open, not storing a flag, and the table has no `visibility` column so there is no shared row to get a permission wrong on. Jira's answer to stale field references is that filters BREAK plus an integrity checker; resolving against the current schema on read and saying what was pruned beats that for free. **Deliberately NOT built:** bulk actions and further inline edit — a different question with its own guardrails, and half of it would have been the half-wired defect. |
| D-73 | **The QA report is computed ONCE and rendered twice; the 5% sample is a keyed hash with its seed and frame stored** | `scripts/qa_report.summarize()` produces `calevate_shared.qa_report.QaReport`; the CLI's Markdown, the stored row and the client-realm API response are all renderings of that one object, and `apps/api/quality/service.py` counts nothing. New `qa_reports` + `qa_call_samples` (d5b8a2c60e17). The weekly draw orders by `md5(seed || call_id)` with `seed = "<tenant_id>:<week_start>"`, storing seed, rank, `population` and `target`; `cron(draw_qa_samples, weekday={0}, ...)` with an explicit `max_tries`. | SURFACES §2 promised the report in-app and it existed only as `make qa-report`; SURFACES §1's sampling queue did not exist at all. **The anti-fork rule is the whole point**: a QA report that disagrees with itself is worse than none, so the harness (~110 model calls, unrunnable in a request handler) stays the one computation and the route renders a stored object. The test parses the numbers back OUT of the CLI's Markdown and compares them with the live route — the two documents a client can hold, not the API against a function it calls. **A sample nobody can re-derive is not evidence.** `random()` and `TABLESAMPLE` are both rejected in-code (unauditable; page-sampling correlates with arrival time); the stored seed and rank mean anyone can re-run one expression and get the same list, and storing the FRAME is what makes "we sample 5%" falsifiable rather than a slogan. The seed changes weekly so no call is permanently unlucky, and `UNIQUE(tenant_id, call_id)` means a re-run inserts nothing. Realms are split deliberately: the report is CLIENT-realm (its value is that the client reads it) and the queue is ADMIN-realm (it holds OUR verdict about THEIR call). The reviewer sees `get_call(raw=False)` — the same function and argument the client's own screen uses — with no raw variant on the admin router at all, asserted by a test that enumerates the router's paths. |
**OPEN, surfaced by D-74 and deliberately NOT decided there:** `docs/SURFACES.md` §2b lists the
voice on the IMMEDIATE lane and `LANES` says so, but `set_agent_voice` does not publish — so no
agent obeys the lane table, and the client screen renders "voice" under "Applies straight away".
Reconciling it means either auto-republishing (which re-voices a live line on an unrun ear test,
against OPERATIONS §2 gate 3) or amending an authoritative doc. Separately, `publish_variant`
sends the agent's CONFIGURED voice to experiment arms, so starting an experiment can move part of
the traffic while the mirror still reports "republish required" — over-reporting, which is the
safe direction, and pre-existing. Both need a decision; neither blocks anything today.
