| D-74 | **A voice has TWO answers — configured and live — and `republish_required` is now measured rather than assumed** | Migration c8b3f14e7a29 adds `agents.live_tts_voice` / `live_tts_provider`, mirroring `live_prompt_id` (a4e7b2c95d18). `publish_agent` is their single writer and records what it ACTUALLY SENT, in the same UPDATE as `engine_agent_ref` and after the vendor call, so a vendor failure rolls the mirror back with it. `GET /v1/agents/{id}/pending` gains `voice.configured` / `voice.live` / `republish_required` / `headline`; the admin picker pre-selects the configured voice and renders **Callers hear now** beside **Configured**; the client agents card shows the voice callers hear. | The picker shipped able to SET a voice and unable to DISPLAY one, and closing that naively would have replaced a missing fact with a wrong one. `set_agent_voice` writes our row and deliberately never touches the engine, so a published agent is configured for one voice and SPEAKING another until the next publish — and `SetVoiceOut.republish_required` was computed as `published`, an assumption that is right the first time and wrong every time after, including when an operator re-selects the voice the engine is already running. **Exposed on `pending`, not on `AgentOut`**: `AgentOut` has nowhere to put the second answer, a single `tts_voice` there renders as "the voice" and is wrong on exactly the agents where it matters, and putting both there would move two-speed publishing onto a read that knows nothing else about it. `pending` already answers configured-vs-live for the script and the call cap; the voice is the third instance of one question, not a new one. It is also the read the admin screen already makes, so pre-selection costs no extra request. **Client-visible, and that was checked rather than assumed**: `list_voices` is already client-realm, and D-36's ladder is a PRICE ladder — `overage_rate` vs `overage_rate_value` bill the rungs differently and `usage_events.meta.tts_tier` records which rung each call ran on, so a client billed by rung must be able to read the rung. **No backfill, deliberately**: `live_tts_voice := tts_voice` would tell a client their callers already hear the new voice, which is the exact false claim the column exists to prevent. NULL means "nothing recorded as sent" and resolves to `republish_required` — one harmless extra republish, self-healing on the first publish. **Voice is NOT added to the `pending` change list**: that list is what Apply and Undo act on, and `undo_staged` does not touch these columns — listing it would put an Undo next to a change it cannot undo. |
| D-75 | **`GET /v1/integrations/events` is a MODEL, and it carries the Sheets capability** | New `EndpointOptionsOut { events: list[str], sheets_delivery_available: bool }` replaces `dict[str, list[str]]` on the same path. The screen reads both from one request; the Sheets form is not offered at all when the capability is false, and the frontend's `lookup()` walk is deleted. | An untyped response generates a bare index signature, so `events` was the one read in that module TypeScript could not check — the same defect class as D-71's `dict[str, Any]`, and fixed the same way. **`events` stays `list[str]` rather than narrowing to the `EventName` literal**, which is the call worth recording: the union is what THIS BUILD can put in a request body, the server's list is what the RUNNING DEPLOYMENT offers, and those are different facts. Narrowing makes the gap unrepresentable, deletes the screen's "this account can also receive X, which this console cannot subscribe to yet" branch, and turns a deployment that adds an event into a 500 out of response validation. **The capability is a rendering hint and never the check.** It follows `KycRecordOut.number_purchase_available` exactly, and the route's own `sheets_delivery_available()` remains the authority — pinned by a stale-capability test that reads the capability as true, flips the setting, and asserts the create is still refused with zero rows written. The screen's optimism is allowed to be wrong; it is not allowed to be the gate. **The hard failure on a malformed body was KEPT** and re-documented as a §52 boundary rather than deleted as a typing workaround: a 200 missing `sheets_delivery_available` would otherwise render "Sheets is not switched on for your account" — our ignorance printed as one of the server's two answers. |
| D-76 | **Bulk lead actions ship TWO named selection scopes, and a partial failure is never a green tick** | `POST /v1/leads/bulk` (`leads:write`, audited, `MAX_BULK_LEADS = 500`). The header checkbox selects THE PAGE; an escape hatch — offered only when rows are off-screen — selects every lead the filters match, and the request carries `scope: "ids" | "filter"` with NO default (omitting it is a 422). The response echoes the scope and returns `requested / changed / unchanged / failures[]` where `changed + unchanged + len(failures) == requested`. `unchanged` is a SUCCESS bucket. Above 25 rows the confirmation is typed and WHAT YOU TYPE IS THE COUNT. | Z deferred this deliberately, and the reason it was worth its own slice is that the facets it built made "select all" genuinely ambiguous: the filtered query can be far larger than the page. The research settles it as two NAMED scopes rather than one guessed meaning (PatternFly's page-scoped header checkbox, Helios' "where the selection is made implies a different scope", Gmail's banner as the learned interaction), and the confirmation must describe the set that will ACTUALLY be acted on — hence `expected_count`, and a cap that REFUSES rather than truncates. **Typing the count rather than a fixed word is a deliberate departure** from this repo's `ERASE`/`HALT`: a fixed word proves you meant the button, but the risk here is the NUMBER, and you cannot type "140" without reading it. **Partial success is 200 with per-item results, not 207**: this repo already answers "processed, and the answer is a refusal" with 200 (`CallLeadOut.status == "blocked"`), and problem+json is reserved for "the request failed". **Bulk delete was considered and REJECTED**: `deleted_at` only hides a row while DPDP erasure runs through `compliance/deletion.py` (request row, outbox job, worker reaching calls, turns, extractions, storage and the engine, then a certificate). A Delete button here would teach a client they had answered an erasure request when they had not. **Undo rejected too**: both actions are their own undo, and an undo token would be a second stateful path to the same write. |
| D-77 | **Lead status and assignment go through the shared discriminator, and `transition_status` learns `visible_where`** | `update_lead` ran a blanket `UPDATE … SET status`; it now uses `db/transition.py::transition_status` (D-65), and assignment gets the same three-answer shape with an `IS DISTINCT FROM` CAS. The primitive gains `visible_where`, applied to BOTH the CAS and the discriminating SELECT. | Three real defects, not a tidy-up: a second click wrote a second `status_change` TIMELINE ROW claiming a change that never happened; a no-op edit bumped `updated_at`, which is this table's sort key, so the client's list re-ordered under them for nothing; and a soft-deleted lead's 404 was a COINCIDENCE of the row not matching rather than a statement about visibility. `visible_where` has to cover both halves and that is the subtle part: applied only to the CAS, a soft-deleted row would fall to the discriminator and answer **409 naming a status the caller may not know it has** — which both leaks the row's state and contradicts the 404 the same id gets everywhere else. The bulk path inherits all of it, which is what makes "3 of 10 were already hot" report as `unchanged` rather than as failure. |
| D-78 | **Per-tenant feature flags are plain config rows; the default lives in code and absence IS the default** | New `apps/api/flags/` and `tenant_feature_flags` (3a91c7e04d58, RLS + policy in the same migration). Resolution is platform default (code, `FLAGS[name].default`) → per-tenant override (row); NOTHING seeds a row for any tenant. Write is `admin:tenants` with audit-on-real-change; read is `org:read`. The cache is THE REQUEST — memoised on `Session.info`, keyed by tenant id — so a flip takes effect on the very next request everywhere, with no window. | CLAUDE.md mandates the shape ("feature flags via plain config rows, not a flag SaaS") and SURFACES §1 asks for the surface; there were zero hits for `feature_flag` in the tree. **What it does NOT replace, argued rather than assumed, because this repo already has four flag-shaped things:** platform config like `self_serve_signup_enabled` (one value per deployment, read where no tenant exists); the big red switch (an incident control, globally scoped, step-up confirmed, with a three-layer cache tuned under a dispatch tick); plan tiers (dated, invoice-derived, contractual — collapsing them would let a beta toggle change a bill); and **the build-time constants `PROVIDER_CREATES_ORDERS` / `LEAD_RETRIEVAL_IMPLEMENTED` / `PROVISIONING_IMPLEMENTED`, where migrating would actively cause an incident** — a row cannot make unwritten code exist, so flipping one would enable a lie rather than a feature. The recommendation is that none of the four move. **A flag may never gate a compliance control**, stated in the registry and pinned by a test — hard rule 5's "no bypass for testing" with better manners. **No cross-request cache, deliberately**: a flag that takes thirty minutes to turn off is a different product from one that takes effect now, and if a flag ever gated something safety-relevant, staleness would be a safety question rather than a performance one. **No step-up**, because releasing an account for outbound dialling takes none and a flag is a smaller act; a flag that needs a step-up header is the wrong mechanism for what it is doing. Nothing is gated on a flag in this change — introducing a mechanism and rewiring behaviour onto it are two changes — and the console prints **"Nothing reads this flag yet"** beside any flag whose `consumed_by` is null. |
| D-79 | **A missed campaign occurrence is SKIPPED, never caught up; a repeat repeats the START, not the dialling** | `schedule_recurrence` stores weekday + IST wall-clock time under the existing `start_at` key, so no migration is owed. An occurrence fires only within `RECURRENCE_CATCHUP = 1h` of its own instant; past that it is abandoned, audited (`campaign.recurrence_skipped`), alerted, and the schedule advances to the next occurrence AFTER NOW. `_claim_occurrence` locks on the occurrence key, so the identity is the occurrence and not the clock. `complete_or_rearm` returns the campaign to `scheduled` WITHOUT resetting contacts. | This is Kubernetes CronJob's `startingDeadlineSeconds` answer, and the argument is stronger here because the jobs ring strangers' phones: a worker down for three days must come back to ONE upcoming run, not three at once, or the recovery is itself a compliance incident. The asymmetry with the one-time `GRACE = 24h` is deliberate — a lost one-time start is lost outright, a lost occurrence is followed by another. **A repeat repeats the START, not the dialling**: re-dialling subscribers who were already reached would be an unrequested repeat call, which is a different act from the one the client scheduled. **Weekday + time, never day-of-month**, so month-end ambiguity and the RFC 5545 skip/clamp divergence cannot arise. **A recurrence outside platform calling hours is REFUSED at creation** (unlike a one-time 22:00 start, which is a lawful one-off intent deferred to 09:00): a standing instruction silently reinterpreted is a schedule the client cannot read. **The gate is on every occurrence path** — `_fire_recurrence` reaches `running` only through `launch_campaign`, which opens with `launch_blockers`, and two tests pin it including a structural one. **`launch_campaign` keeps its own CAS and borrows only the DISCRIMINATOR**: `transition_status` treats already-in-target as success, and launch is NOT idempotent — it scrubs DNC, stamps `launched_at`, writes an append-only `campaign.launched` row and counts a start. Pause and resume now write audit rows, only on a real transition. |
| D-80 | **`conclude` names the experiment it is ending, and is never redirected onto another one** | `ConcludeExperimentIn.experiment_id` is REQUIRED; `_LATEST_SQL` is gone from the write path (it remains the READ's header). `transition_status(visible_where="agent_id = :aid")` scopes both the CAS and the discriminating SELECT, so a cross-agent or cross-tenant id is 404 rather than a 409 naming a status the caller is not entitled to. | The endpoint concluded "whatever is running on this agent", so **a retry arriving after a LATER test had started ended the later test** — losing a running experiment and promoting an arm nobody chose. **A body field, not a path segment**: the mutation is AGENT-shaped (it promotes a version onto the agent, moves the agent's disclosure line, republishes it, and the audit row's `object_id` is the agent), so `.../experiments/{id}/conclude` would name the experiment as the object of an agent-shaped act. The id rides as a PRECONDITION, which is the shape `ApplyIn.expected_version` already established. **`If-Match` was rejected on a fact about the resource**: the results representation changes with every completed call, so an ETag over it would refuse a good conclude because a phone rang while the operator was reading — and a precondition that fails for reasons unrelated to the hazard trains people to retry blindly. **Optional-with-a-default was rejected outright**: the only caller that would take the default is the stale request. A mismatched id is answered ABOUT THE TEST IT NAMED — 200 `changed:false` if that test already ended the way asked, 409 naming the ending if it ended another way, 404 if it names nothing visible. The existence of a later test is not a fact about this request. **The sabotage is what shows the D-65 branches were never enough**: restoring the agent-fallback turned exactly one test red while all 27 others passed. |
| D-81 | **A control the server permits is not hidden by the screen; a control the screen exposes states what the server will do with it** | The schedule and repeat forms move out of the launch panel's `ready` branch to a sibling guarded on `check.data` (a verdict answered, either way). Three stated consequences replace the hidden control: beside the form while arming, on the armed card BEFORE the first attempt, and from `schedule_blocked_rules` after it. | Scheduling deliberately runs NO gate at arm time — `schedule_campaign` and `schedule_recurrence` each do one CAS and import nothing from compliance — because the gate runs at FIRE time, every time, which is what makes a lapsed registration weeks later still refuse (D-79). The screen was therefore inventing a rule the server does not have: a client whose blockers are outstanding TODAY could not arm a start for next Tuesday, by which time they may well have cleared them. **The forms stay unreachable while the check is LOADING or FAILED**, deliberately: a consequence stated from a verdict we do not have is §52's defect rather than its remedy. **A gap closed on the way past**: an armed-but-unattempted schedule previously said only "Starts Monday, 10:00 IST", so a doomed schedule's first evidence would have been calls that never happened. The forecast is guarded on `status == "scheduled"` specifically, because a RUNNING campaign's `launch_blockers` reports its own status as a blocker — true, and not a statement about the next occurrence. **Cancel was checked rather than assumed** and did NOT have the same defect: both cancel controls already live outside the launch panel. That is now pinned by tests, because a schedule you cannot cancel BECAUSE you acquired a blocker would be the worse version of this defect. |
| D-82 | **Money reaches a wallet through a screen, and the confirmation is the payment reference itself** | New `/admin/tenants/{id}/credits` over the existing `POST|GET .../credits` — no backend change; the routes were already idempotent-by-UTR under an advisory lock with the audit row in the same transaction. The ledger renders above the form, the reference is typed TWICE, and a replay renders a neutral "Already recorded — nothing was credited" panel naming the entry that already exists. | `runbooks/topup-payments.md` instructed an operator to hand-construct an authenticated, MFA'd admin API call off a bank statement, and that was the ONLY way money reached an account. **The typed confirmation is the reference, not a fixed word** — the house idiom is a typed word naming the act (`HALT`, the tenant id in `spendCapConfirmation`), but a fixed word fails twice here: it becomes muscle memory, and it does not check the one field where the error is unrecoverable. The reference is different every time AND is the double-keying check. The amount is echoed in grouped INR in the button label, so a misplaced decimal is the last thing read before the click. **The duplicate-reference warning is one-directional**: a match warns, an absence never reassures — the screen holds the newest 50 entries while the server checks the whole ledger, and a console that said "not a duplicate" would be asserting something it cannot know. **`credit_ledger` is append-only (hard rule 4), so the screen surfaces the compensating-entry path rather than letting an operator discover there is no undo.** It also states plainly what has no tool: `reconcile_credit_ledger` handles DUPLICATES only, so a credit to the wrong tenant or the wrong amount has no remedy in code and must be escalated. **Found and NOT fixed** (reported): SURFACES §1 promises "credit adjustments (compensating entries, never edits)" and nothing implements it — `POST .../credits` refuses a negative amount with a remediation pointing at a tool that does not exist. |
| D-83 | **The invoice's supplier identity is CONFIG, and the document refuses the tax-invoice framing rather than printing an invalid one** | New `billing/gst.py` (Rule 46 particulars, place of supply per IGST s.12(2), the CGST+SGST / CGST+UTGST / IGST split); four `GST_SUPPLIER_*` keys plus `GST_SUPPLY_SAC`, no defaults and no placeholder GSTIN. With any of them absent the document renders **PROFORMA INVOICE**, says it cannot support input credit, and names the exact env vars blocking it. New client-realm `GET /v1/billing/invoice` — a second ROUTE, never a second computation. | The document charged 18% GST while carrying no supplier or recipient GSTIN, no HSN/SAC, no place of supply and no entity address, so a B2B client could not claim input credit against it. **The flat 18% was structurally wrong, not cosmetically**: CGST, SGST and IGST are three separate credit ledgers, so tax charged without naming the head cannot be claimed AT ALL — and the old shape could not express the Union-Territory-without-a-legislature case (CGST+UTGST) at all. **The refusal is a legal position**: CGST s.32 prohibits an unregistered person from collecting tax, so with no GSTIN there is no tax invoice to issue. **Zeroing the tax was considered and REJECTED** despite being the literal reading of s.32: one forgotten env var would then silently under-bill every client by 18%. A missing config key changes what the document CLAIMS, never what the client OWES. **Recipient GSTIN comes from the VERIFIED `kyc_records` row**, not a new `organizations.gstin` column, because that column's only writer lives in a module this slice could not touch — it would have shipped as a column nobody fills. A human having checked it against the registry is also the stronger fact. **Open and pinned rather than faked**: the serial `CAL-202608-0192f0aa` is 19 characters against Rule 46(b)'s 16-character cap and is deterministic rather than consecutive. A consecutive series needs a stored issued-invoice registry, which conflicts with D-46's derived statement, and truncating trades a length breach for a collision. A test fails the day the scheme changes. |
| D-84 | **The untyped-2xx sweep, third time: a model where the response MEANS something, a 204 where it never did** | `POST /v1/lead-sources/{id}/test` gains `LeadSourceDryRunOut`; the two intake receivers and the unschedule route gain models; four constant acks (`pause`, `resume`, `consent-provenance`, `DELETE /v1/dnc/{id}`) become **204**; `/v1/numbers/purchase` becomes `NoReturn`. | Same defect as D-71 and D-75 one level down, and this time on a handler with a NORMALISED CALLER PHONE in scope — not a field the guardrail judged safe, a handler it could not see. **A constant ack is not a candidate for a model**: four of these returned a fixed string saying only what the URL already said, and modelling a constant satisfies both tools while teaching the next reader nothing. 204 is what this repo already does for idempotent transitions. **`NoReturn` on the purchase route** because the handler always raises — the old `dict[str,str]` was the shape of a contract for a body that cannot exist, and `NoReturn` makes mypy force the author to declare a real one the day provisioning lands. **`IngestAckOut` on a duplicate returns nulls, not `dispatched: false`** — `false` beside `duplicate` reads as "nobody was called", the one thing it must not mean. Meta's handshake stays `text/plain` because it echoes `hub.challenge` verbatim and a JSON model would break the subscription. Two sabotages are now permanent detection tests, and the second proved the guardrail's walk reaches `/hooks` routes and not only `/v1`. |
| D-85 | **Impersonation mints a short-lived DELEGATION grant, and the grant is not a credential** | `POST /v1/admin/impersonation-grants` mints an HS256 JWT: `aud="calevate:impersonation"`, `sub`=tenant id, `act={"sub": admin id}`, `jti`, 15-minute expiry, presented as `X-Impersonation-Grant` beside `X-Impersonate-Org`. `verify_grant()` checks presence, signature, audience, shape, expiry, actor and subject in ONE call. Five refusal codes, all 403, none degrading to a plain admin session and none widening one. New `IMPERSONATION_GRANT_SECRET`, failing closed outside `local`. | D-22's "session start audit-logged" was decorative: the endpoint minted nothing, the console never called it, and `X-Impersonate-Org` alone opened a tenant — so `admin.impersonation_started` was absent for every real session. Reads WERE audited, so D-22 was not violated; the session-start half simply did not exist. **RFC 8693's `act` claim is the exact shape**, and the reason is precise: a token CARRYING `act` has delegation semantics while one without it has impersonation semantics — which is D-22's own rule written by someone else first, since D-22 forbids acting-as to avoid dual attribution. So the feature called impersonation deliberately gets a delegation-shaped credential. Token exchange, `may_act` and nested `act` chains were read and refused; chaining is refused in code (an impersonating session may not mint). **No revocation list and no grants table, because the grant is NOT a credential**: it never travels in `Authorization` and does nothing alone, so every request still carries the operator's own MFA-gated admin token and still re-reads `admin_users`. Revocation lag is ONE REQUEST, not one token lifetime — and the docstring names what would invalidate that argument. The TTL is sized against audit-ledger volume, not against revocation. **A dedicated secret rather than a subkey derived from `audit_chain_secret`** (NIST SP 800-108 was read and rejected): that secret rotates with a drill, and coupling a routine credential rotation to a drill means it never happens. **Found and NOT fixed**: `audit_chain_secret` still falls back to the guessable constant `local-dev:{app_env}` in EVERY environment including prod, so a deploy that forgot it has an unverifiable chain. |
| D-86 | **Every HMAC key resolves through one ladder, and a present-but-short key is refused exactly like an absent one** | `resolve_hmac_key()` in `core/settings.py` with `MIN_HMAC_KEY_BYTES = 32`, used by the audit chain, the idempotency scope fingerprint and the D-85 grant. Staging/prod refuse to sign or verify without a real key and `/healthz/ready` is red until it is set; the derived constant survives only under `local`. `AUDIT_CHAIN_SECRET_RETIRED` verifies (never signs) the pre-rotation era, and `ChainVerifyOut.entries_under_retired_key` publishes how much of the log rests on it. | D-85 found and did not fix it: `audit_chain_secret` fell back to `local-dev:{app_env}` in EVERY environment, so a prod deploy that forgot the variable signed its append-only ledger with a constant printed in this repository — anyone who could read the source could forge a row that verifies, and the console said "chain intact". **Length is part of being configured**: to a caller, a 6-byte secret and no secret are one condition ("no usable key here"), so failing closed on absence while accepting a weak key would guard the easier half of one mistake. 32 bytes because three authorities converge there and the strictest governs — RFC 2104 §3 (a key under the hash length "is strongly discouraged"), NIST SP 800-107 Rev. 1 §5.3.4 (128-bit absolute floor), RFC 7518 §3.2 (HS256 key ≥ hash size). **The retired key is a verification-only input, never a signing one**, so a rotation cannot be quietly un-done by an operator who sets the wrong variable. **The weakly-attested count is NOT a component of `ok`** and is rendered beside both verdicts: those rows are intact, and what they lack is attestation STRENGTH — a distinction that only matters when the log is exported as evidence, which is exactly when a caveat living in a runbook reaches nobody. **Idempotency got its own secret rather than sharing the chain's**: the chain's rotates on a drill, and a rotation of the fingerprint key makes in-flight `Idempotency-Key`s miss their stored record, so a client retry re-executes. |
| D-87 | **A credit adjustment is a compensating entry against a NAMED ledger row, keyed on (entry, amount) and gated on the direction** | `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{id}/credits/adjustments`. Idempotency key `adjust:<corrects_entry_id>:<amount>`, content-addressed and enforced by the EXISTING `ux_credit_ledger_tenant_reason_ref` (an index, not a reader's `if` — D-63). The ceiling is the target entry's remaining magnitude, cumulative across corrections (`reversed_amounts`, published as `LedgerEntryOut.reversible_inr`). `allow_negative=True`. Step-up `X-Confirm-Action: adjust_credits:<entry_id>` on the DEBIT direction only. | Hard rule 4 says the wrong row stays where it is because it IS the evidence, so the only correction an append-only ledger allows is a new row with the opposite sign — and the product promised adjustments (`credit_ledger.reason = 'adjustment'` since M1) with no way to make one. **A caller-minted key was rejected**: the failure this must survive is a SECOND CLICK, which mints a second key. The cost is stated in the code — two genuinely distinct corrections of the same amount against the same entry collapse to one key, and the second reads as "already corrected, nothing moved", which is the safe direction when money is leaving. **The balance may go negative, and refusing would be the worse answer**: a wrong credit that was partly spent cannot be fully reversed otherwise, and a ledger that keeps claiming credit the client never had is the condition this route exists to end. Because that must not be silent, `stops_dialling` carries the DIAL GATE's own predicate, evaluated inside the write's transaction rather than re-derived — a second copy of "does an empty wallet stop calling" is how the console and the gate tell a client two different stories. **No superadmin gate**: `core/rbac.py` reserves that for unbounded switches, and this act is bounded (≤ what the named entry moved) and itself correctable — making the operator who erred wait for a superadmin keeps a wrong ledger wrong for longer than the risk justifies. **Known residual, pinned by a test**: `reversible_inr` nets one level, so correcting a correction does not restore the original entry's ceiling. Netting properly is an alternating sum over a chain with a cycle guard on a money path, and it buys nothing — the chain never dead-ends, because the next correction goes against the newest entry. **Still absent**: under-crediting (₹5,000 recorded for a ₹50,000 UTR) has no path, since reversing the entry does not free the reference. |
| D-88 | **Cartesia is rejected as an ORCHESTRATOR and retained as a speech-vendor candidate; D-31 stands, and its Cartesia rationale was stale** | TRD §10 row rewritten from evidence: Cartesia Line's LLM is fully BYOK (LiteLLM, 100+ providers, `model=` + `api_key=`) while **STT and TTS are not** — Ink 2 and Sonic 3.5 are the product and the SDK exposes no swap interface. So Line cannot host D-36's Sarvam stack, and its $0.06/min is a BUNDLED rate that is not comparable to Bolna's BYOK platform fee. New TRD §10.4 prices both paths at four volumes. | The teardown's "low-latency English-first TTS" is now false and had to be corrected rather than left: Sonic 3 covers the top 9 Indic languages including Telugu, the Blue Machines AI partnership (~Feb 2026) targets India-RESIDENT processing for BFSI/healthcare, there is a Bangalore office, and deployment spans cloud-regional, on-prem/VPC and air-gapped with SOC 2 Type II / HIPAA / PCI L1. A stale rationale is how a settled decision gets re-litigated badly. **The decisive criterion is not price**: our compliance layer assumes an Indian carrier relationship (PE/TM, DLT templates, 140/160 series, DNC before dispatch), and Line's telephony is Cartesia numbers, imported Twilio, or Voximplant — none of which yields a DLT-registered Indian number. **Where Cartesia WOULD win is recorded honestly**: its Scale tier ($0.014/min, models included) beats Bolna+Sarvam on variable cost at S4 volumes, gated on the India telephony gap and a Telugu ear test against Sonic we have not run. ⚠ **This row originally added "an unpublished enterprise commitment" as a third gate — that was WRONG and is withdrawn; Scale is published and self-serve at $239/mo annual. See D-94, which supersedes the economics here.** **The useful residue**: Bolna is BYOK on speech, so Ink/Sonic are a candidate INSIDE Bolna against Bulbul — a config change at our adapter boundary, and an option D-36 did not list. **Sources are secondary**: `docs.cartesia.ai` and `www.cartesia.ai` are unreachable from our build environment; the BYOK finding rests on Cartesia's own Line SDK README on GitHub, which is first-party. |
| D-94 ⚠SUPERSEDES the economics in D-88 | **Build for the orchestrator switch; do not build the switch. Three pre-committed triggers, and telephony is the one that decides it** | TRD §10.5. We build the capability descriptor on the `VoiceEngine` port, make the conformance suite PROVE a capability claim rather than trust it, and run the system in tests against a capability-restricted engine that dictates speech and has no built-in KB — the Cartesia shape — without writing a Cartesia adapter. Re-evaluate only when ALL THREE hold: sustained volume >12,500 min/month, Line BYOC SIP from an Indian DLT-registered carrier confirmed **in writing**, and Sonic Telugu passing the same ear test Bulbul passed. | **Two corrections to D-88, both in Cartesia's favour, recorded because a comparison that only flatters the incumbent is not evidence.** (1) Scale is **not** an unpublished enterprise negotiation — it is published and self-serve at **$239/mo annual** ($2,868/yr ≈ ₹252,384) or $299 monthly, dropping Line to **$0.014/min** with models included, 8M model credits and $299 prepaid Line minutes. The commitment is a known ₹21,032/month. (2) That moves the crossover to **≈12,300 min/month** (₹1.71/min variable advantage ÷ the plan fee), earlier still — ≈6,450 — once the Bolna path needs Sarvam Pro for rate limits, which the Cartesia path does not because it buys no Sarvam speech. At S3 (20,000 min) Cartesia Scale is **₹3.79/min against our ₹4.30, a saving of ₹23,168/month**. **There is also an upper bound nobody quotes**: Scale caps concurrency at 10 slots ≈ 26,400 min/month, so the window is ~12,300→26,400 and above it the price is Enterprise and genuinely unknown again. **The founder's BYOK instinct is HALF right and the halves matter**: our Sarvam LLM key does work on Line (LiteLLM lists Sarvam as a provider; Sarvam serves an OpenAI-compatible endpoint at `api.sarvam.ai/v1`), so D-36's free-per-token LLM leg survives a move — but Line's TTS config takes a Cartesia `voice_id` and a Cartesia `model` with **no provider field**, so Saaras and Bulbul do not, and those are the two legs carrying D-36's residency argument. **The decisive unknown is not price**: whether Line accepts BYOC SIP from an arbitrary carrier. If it does, an Exotel/Vobiz-class Indian carrier can front it and the exit is live; if it does not, the whole window is unreachable and the price analysis is moot. It is cheap to ask and it gates everything, so it is asked first. **No Cartesia adapter is written**, on this repo's own doctrine that an adapter written against an imagined API is worse than none because it looks finished — and their docs are unreachable from our build environment, so anything written today would be invention with a version number on it. |
| D-95 | **Core config and secrets are managed from `admin.calevate.tech/ops`, in Postgres, under envelope encryption — and the KEK never enters the database** | Full spec: `docs/PLATFORM-CONFIG.md`. Two tables, not one: `platform_settings` (plaintext, readable, revertible) and `platform_secrets` (AES-256-GCM ciphertext + wrapped per-version DEK, append-only, joins the hard-rule-4 family). `PLATFORM_KEK` in the environment; `PLATFORM_KEK_RETIRED` unwraps but never wraps, mirroring D-86. Resolution order `env → db → default → refuse`, with six bootstrap keys (`APP_ENV`, `DATABASE_URL`, `ALEMBIC_DATABASE_URL`, `PLATFORM_KEK`, `PLATFORM_KEK_RETIRED`, `REDIS_URL`) that a new guardrail forbids ever reading from the store. Sentinel-key polling propagates a change to all four processes in <10s with no restart and no DB read on voice-runtime's request path. | The founder's requirement is legitimate — rotating a vendor key must not need an SSH session — and CLAUDE.md's "no secrets in the database" is equally binding. Envelope encryption satisfies both: the database holds ciphertext and wrapped keys, and the key that opens them is somewhere else. **Two tables rather than one `is_secret` boolean**, because that boolean is one bad default away from writing an API key in plaintext and nothing would notice. **`env` beats the database deliberately** — it breaks the bootstrap deadlock, keeps a broken store from bricking the deployment, and is the 3am override when the console itself is what is broken. **No read-back route exists or will**: a console that can display a credential leaks every credential through one screenshot, so a compromised session buys write access, not exfiltration. **A `/test` route dry-runs a candidate value against the vendor before storing it**, which is what separates this from a new outage source — a wrong key is refused at the screen instead of at the next call. **Vault, OpenBao, Infisical and Doppler were read and rejected**: Vault's BSL plus a seal/unseal lockout risk for a solo operator; OpenBao and Infisical are second stateful deployables on a one-VPS estate (CLAUDE.md prefers Postgres first); Doppler and cloud KMS give up the India residency D-36 was chosen for. The KEK provider is isolated in one module so OpenBao or a KMS is a later swap, not a rewrite. **The trade is recorded rather than discovered**: today stealing every vendor credential needs VPS access, and afterwards one compromised admin session is enough — accepted against mandatory admin MFA (D-68), a distinct `platform:secrets` permission, no plaintext on any route, hash-chained audit rows, and the env override as the way to take control back. **Residual and unmitigated**: an admin session can REPLACE a key with an attacker's own, pointing our engine at their account; detection (an alert on every `platform.secret_set` in production), not prevention, is the answer. **Six ENV-ONLY bootstrap keys, and that is a count of `check_bootstrap_keys.BOOTSTRAP_KEYS` rather than a count of lines in a file** — `.env.example` carries eight, and the two extra ones are not a leak (D-185). `OBJECT_STORE_ENDPOINT` and `OBJECT_STORE_BUCKET` are required `Settings` fields with no default, so `Settings()` cannot construct — and therefore the store cannot be reached — without them; they are console-MANAGED (source `env`, read-only, when set) rather than console-only, and they are locators, not credentials. The object-store CREDENTIALS (`AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`) are deliberately absent from `.env.example` and always were, because botocore reads them itself and nothing here passes them to boto3. `tests/env_example_bootstrap_floor_test.py` boots `Settings` off exactly that key set and off every 7-key subset, so the floor is measured rather than asserted. The earlier wording here — "`.env` drops from ~54 keys to 6" — was a file count, and a file count is the drift class D-103/D-105 exist for. |
| D-93 | **Each voice-engine adapter DECLARES an `EngineCapabilities` descriptor, and the conformance suite makes it impossible for that declaration to lie** | Per-leg `SpeechControl` (`ours`/`engine`) for stt/tts/llm; `campaigns`, `knowledge_base`, `transfer`; `number_series: frozenset` (per SERIES, because the launch gate matches on it); `webhook_auth`. **No field carries a default** — a new adapter answers every question in writing rather than inheriting today's engine's answers by omission. Every site that hard-coded Bolna's answer now asks the one selector; an absent capability raises `EngineCapabilityAbsentError` (a `ProblemError` subclass carrying `capability` as a FIELD, not seven problem types for one condition). Four conformance subjects: `fake`, a capability-restricted `fake`, `bolna`, and a real `cartesia` adapter. | The audit found nine Bolna-shaped assumptions outside the engine package, and three were live defects rather than tidiness: **`voice-runtime/engine_intake.py` hard-coded `if engine == "bolna"` with NO `hmac` branch at all**, so adopting a signing engine meant editing the latency-critical receiver in lockstep with the adapter — exactly what hard rule 3 forbids; **the KB publish path assumed an engine-side KB unconditionally**, discovering otherwise three calls in, *after* withdrawing the live version, so a KB-less engine took knowledge down to report it could not replace it; and **the two shipped adapters silently DISAGREED** — `bolna.transfer`/`provision_number` raised while `fake` cheerfully succeeded and returned a number, with nothing able to detect the divergence. **The suite refuses `campaigns=True` outright** because the port has no campaign method, so the claim is unfalsifiable and would sit among six enforced claims borrowing their credibility. **`transfer` was redefined mid-slice** to mean "will `VoiceEngine.transfer` work" rather than "does the vendor have transfer": Cartesia has a real `transfer_call`, but agent-initiated mid-call, and a `True` would have put an escalation control on the console that refuses every time a caller asks for a human. **The first sabotage run caught only one of two lies** — a TTS claim walked through because `models_readable=False` excused everything; that hole is closed (an adapter claiming a leg is `ours` must read that leg back) and the re-run caught both. **TTS answer**: the voice catalogue stays our Sarvam data and `voice_selection_capability()` decides whether it may be OFFERED — the closed case renders as a product fact, not an error, while still showing what callers hear. |
| D-89 | **An under-credited payment is RESTATED against the same reference: you type the TOTAL the bank moved, never the difference** | `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{id}/credits/restatements`. Ref `restated:<payment_ref>:<corrected TOTAL>`; `meta.payment_ref` links back, and `PAYMENT_REF_SQL` groups on `COALESCE(meta->>'payment_ref', ref)` so the wallet still shows ONE bank transfer. New `PaymentOut` (`credited_inr` summed server-side across every row of that payment, `entries > 1` iff restated). Confirmation header ALWAYS, echoing the amount. | The workaround was a second top-up under an annotated reference (`UTR-123-part2`), which puts two references on one bank transfer and silently breaks the reconciliation the reference exists for. **The operator types the total, not the difference**, because at 2am with a bank statement open the total is the number in front of them and the difference is arithmetic they can get wrong; the amount to credit is derived server-side from what the reference credits today, which the screen shows beside the field. **Confirmation on every restatement, unlike the adjustment's direction-bound rule** — this route has one direction, no ceiling above it, and it credits the client, so there is no safe direction to leave unguarded; the header carries the amount so one captured for ₹50,000 cannot be replayed for ₹500,000. Restating twice credits once; restating to a HIGHER total credits only the new difference. **Found the hard way:** the sabotage that proved the link-back test was left in the tree and reached a commit — the `meta` key had been renamed and never restored, which would have silently unlinked every restatement from its payment. |
| D-90 | **Meta lead retrieval is implemented, so a Facebook form submission can actually be called back** | `LEAD_RETRIEVAL_IMPLEMENTED` flips to `True`. The Graph read `GET /{leadgen_id}?fields=field_data` behind the existing `LeadRetriever` Protocol, with `field_data` → flat-map normalization feeding the EXISTING consent gate rather than bypassing it. | A leadgen webhook is a change notification carrying no name and no phone; without the Graph read every authenticated Meta lead died at `meta_lead_retrieval_unavailable`, so the product's headline promise — someone fills your form, the AI calls them in seconds — could not happen at all. The consent answer arrives inside the very `field_data` we can now read, which is why it flows INTO `require_form_consent` (`no_consent_field_configured` still distinct from `no_form_consent`): a lead we may not call is taken and refused, never dialled. |
| D-91 | **WhatsApp goes direct to Meta Cloud API, not through an Indian BSP** | `apps/workers/whatsapp_cloud.py` implements `whatsapp.WhatsAppTransport`. The seam is unchanged: `whatsapp.py` keeps the messages, the gates, the delivery records and the retry ladders, and nothing about "was this person allowed to be messaged" is redefined in the vendor module. Runs in a worker; no request handler reaches Meta. | ROADMAP §6 carried no decision, so the seam shipped and refused to guess (the module said so). Ranked on what actually DIFFERS for a two-person pre-revenue company with one client (R4), the first question decides it: **can we get a WABA without an enterprise sales process?** Cloud API is self-serve through the Meta Developer Console — Business Verification raises limits, it is not required to send at all — where the BSP layer inserts a sales motion and a margin between us and the platform for a capability we would be reselling anyway. |
| D-92 | **The backup tree stops being a hypothesis: the half that needs no cloud account now runs on every developer's machine** | `scripts/restore_drill.py` — seed, dump exactly as `dump-offsite.sh` dumps, encrypt with the same `age` invocation, into object storage, back out, decrypt, restore into a scratch database, then PROVE the restore rather than trust an exit code. A `SABOTAGE=` mode corrupts the dump, drops an RLS policy or disables an append-only trigger so the drill can be shown going red. | `infra/backup/README.md` §9 and the drill runbook both say the tree is unapplied and no wal-g command has ever run, while OPERATIONS §8 treats "backups verified" as a pre-launch gate that today means a file exists. **The coverage statement is the load-bearing part**: wal-g, R2, the offsite provider, the libsodium key, the secrets-manager age identity, the systemd timers and `rclone` are NOT tested and are each named individually, because a harness that ran the cheap half and printed PASS would manufacture precisely the false confidence `backup-health.sh` was written to prevent. Verification reuses `check_rls_coverage`, `check_ledger_immutability` and the audit-chain verifier rather than writing second implementations — a restore that silently drops policies is a data breach dressed as a recovery. |
| D-96 ⚠AMENDS D-95 §5 | **The KEK label is a FINGERPRINT of the key material, and `get_settings()` itself is the resolution point** | `kek_id` is SHA-256 of the key, domain-separated and truncated to 31 bits — not an operator-maintained counter. Unwrap is trial decryption over the ring (KEK + retired). `get_settings()` keeps its signature and its `@lru_cache` and layers `env → platform_settings → code default` underneath; `core/platform_config.py` owns the IO and the dependency runs one way, so there is no cycle. Sentinel bump is a STATEMENT-level database trigger, not an application-side write. | **The counter fails in the one way that cannot be recovered from.** It is correct only while somebody remembers to bump it on every rotation; forget once and new rows carry the OLD generation, the rewrap job skips exactly the rows that need it, and the NEXT rotation renders them permanently unreadable — silent, unrecoverable loss gated on human memory. A label derived from the key cannot disagree with reality. Sound because it is a LABEL and not a control: GCM's tag is a 128-bit MAC, so trial decryption rejects a wrong key at ~2⁻¹²⁸ and a mislabelled row still opens. **Condition attached at review, and it is what keeps the scheme safe**: the rewrap must never use the fingerprint to decide which rows to process — every row is unwrapped and re-wrapped, or the counter's failure mode returns wearing a hash. **`get_settings()` as the resolution point** means ~200 existing call sites pick up console config with no edit and the read path stays IO-free (hard rule 3) — one way to read a setting rather than two, which is the migrate-don't-accumulate rule applied to the most-called function in the repo. **The sentinel is a database trigger** because an application-side bump is correct only while every writer remembers, and the spec's own acceptance test is a `psql` edit at 3am. **Validation uses `TypeAdapter(field.rebuild_annotation())`, not `.annotation`** — the bare annotation would accept `otel_traces_sample_ratio = 5.0`, store it, and drop the next deploy; constraints must travel with the type. The managed set is DERIVED from the `Settings` model minus env-only keys minus credential-shaped names (36 of 56 managed, 18 credentials excluded automatically), so a new field is governed the day it is added rather than the day someone remembers a second list. **Cold start with no snapshot serves loudly** — `platform_config_never_loaded` as a state distinct from `degraded` — because refusing to boot would put an empty table in front of the phone system, and the bootstrap six are env-only regardless. |
| D-97 ⚠TWO DELIBERATE HARD-RULE DEVIATIONS, both argued | **`platform_secrets` joins the append-only family with a BOUNDED mutation, and `/test` calls a vendor from a request handler** | (a) `platform_secrets_forbid_mutation` permits an UPDATE only when every column except `dek_wrapped`, `dek_nonce`, `kek_version` and `retired_at` is byte-for-byte unchanged, and RAISEs on everything else including every DELETE. One file-scoped `BOUNDED_MUTATIONS` entry in `check_ledger_immutability`, not an exemption; the database trigger stays the enforcement. Seven ledgers now. (b) `POST /v1/ops/secrets/{key}/test` makes one authenticated vendor request from the handler, 8s timeout, reading `response.status_code` and nothing else. | **(a)** The blanket `calevate_forbid_mutation` cannot work here: a KEK rotation must re-wrap HISTORICAL DEKs in place, or the retired key can never leave the environment and "the rotation completed" is never true. **The ciphertext is the evidence and is immutable; the wrapping is operational state and is not.** The rejected alternative — splitting the wrapping into a second table so the blanket trigger could stay — is recorded in the migration: it puts a join on every credential read and splits one fact across two rows, and the trigger instead enumerates four mutable columns POSITIVELY, so a fifth cannot be added without editing both the migration and a test that derives the immutable set from the LIVE schema. **(b)** CLAUDE.md bars calling providers from request handlers, and the whole value of `/test` is an answer BEFORE the value is stored: deferring to a worker means storing the candidate first — the outage this exists to prevent — or inventing a job the console polls. Admin realm, `platform:secrets`, manually triggered, one request. The rule protects tenant-facing and latency-critical paths; this is neither. **Engine isolation survives** because the probe reads a status code and never a body, so no vendor shape escapes `apps/api/engine/`. Four outcomes are never collapsed — `accepted`/`rejected`/`unreachable`/`no_probe` — because "we could not check" is a different sentence from "the vendor said no", and all three probes carry `verified=False` since no live refusal has been observed from this build (OPERATIONS §2 marked assumption, surfaced to the operator rather than hidden behind a tick). **§10's residual risk is now WIRED, not merely accepted**: every secret write fires `platform_secret_set` naming key and actor, in every environment. **Found by the tests, not by review**: `AESGCM.decrypt` raises `ValueError` rather than `InvalidTag` when the nonce is malformed, so one bad row would have escaped the background refresh and frozen every process's configuration instead of refusing one credential. **Three of sixteen sabotages found tests that asserted nothing** — including a confirmation test comparing the header against the function that produced it, and a bootstrap test asserting `local == local`. |
| D-98 | **The Razorpay order-creation adapter exists, and "an adapter exists" stays a different fact from "this box can take a payment"** | `PROVIDER_CREATES_ORDERS` is now True — a claim about CODE. `PaymentCapability.creates_orders` is False on every deployment with its own reason `no_api_secret`, carried separately from `reason` so "why can I not pay" and "why is there no order" stay distinguishable in an alert, and never pulling `available` down. `POST /v1/orders` pinned to `/v1`. Receipt content-addressed over `(tenant, amount, 15-minute window)` per D-87, served through `claim_idempotency` per `call_back`. New `GET /v1/billing/topups/capability` publishing two booleans and no reason code (D-75's shape). | **A money bug found on the way, and it was correct only by accident.** The route converted rupees with `int((amount * 100).to_integral_exact())`, which LOOKS like it refuses a fraction of a paisa — but `to_integral_exact` only *signals* `Inexact`, and the default decimal context has that trap off, so it silently rounded half-to-even. It was safe solely because Pydantic's `decimal_places=2` rejected 3-dp input upstream: one validator away from a missing paisa. `inr_to_paise` now refuses rather than rounds, and is the only conversion in that direction. **The order is created SERVER-SIDE so `notes.calevate_tenant_id` reaches the provider by construction** rather than by hoping a browser checkout attached it — the webhook resolves the tenant from that key and from nothing else. **Idempotency composes the two existing answers rather than inventing a third**: D-87 supplies what the key IS (content-addressed, because a caller-minted key is defeated by a second click), `call_back` supplies how a repeat is SERVED. Two departures are stated in code: a 15-minute window, without which a client who paid ₹2,000 today could never pay ₹2,000 again; and the claim commits BEFORE the network call, so a loser gets `idempotent_request_in_flight` instead of a database lock held across a call to a payment provider. **No checkout widget was built**: `checkout.js` is a third unverified vendor surface and a supply-chain decision (hard rule 9), and the browser callback is never the source of truth — the signed webhook is. No Razorpay SDK entered the lockfile. **A defect on the screen this closed**: `PAYMENT_PROVIDER` is unset by default, so the top-up form was offered on every deployment and could only ever answer `payments_not_configured` — a control whose single possible outcome was a red notice. **Research promoted an existing UNVERIFIED claim**: their published SDK confirms the webhook signing scheme our receiver already implements, read at source; the header NAME stays unverified, because the SDK is handed the signature and never names where it came from. |
| D-99 | **Two coverage floors closed to near zero, and the gate-removal sabotage is the evidence that matters** | `dial-path` 97 → 1 (agents/service 6→0, crm/routes 57→0, ingest/service 2→0, campaign_dispatch 15→1, workers/dispatcher 17→0). 29 tests, four new files, no source touched. | The D-21 button and the callback had **never been exercised over HTTP**, on the surface whose whole rule is that no dial skips the gate. The sabotages are the point: removing `if not decision.allowed` from `call_lead` turns a DNC number into a placed call and two tests red; removing it from `call_back` gives `assert 'queued' == 'blocked'`; dropping `AND a.deleted_at IS NULL` from `_load_agent` means a soft-deleted agent dials, because the vendor's object outlives our row and that predicate is the only thing between it and a caller. Also pinned: a reserve that swallows the pool dials nobody AND alerts; a shared pool is spent once; a campaign paused between the scan and the budget read is not dialled; a campaign cancelled mid-tick is not written back to `completed`. **The one remaining unit is unreachable and argued rather than contorted** — `slots = min(slider, tenant_budget)` cannot be zero given `CHECK (concurrency BETWEEN 1 AND 10)` and the `tenant_budget <= 0` break above it, so a test pins the CONSTRAINT that makes the argument true and fails if it is ever relaxed. |
| D-100 ⚠NUMBER NEVER ALLOCATED | **Reserved and burned — no decision was ever recorded under this number** | Nothing. The log skips from D-99 to D-101; a draft claimed the number, the work landed under D-101, and the number was never reclaimed. Uncited: `grep -rn "D-100"` returns this row and nothing else, so no citation dangles. | **A hole in the sequence is an invitation to a collision, and the collision is the failure mode `duplicate_decision_ids` exists for.** The likeliest way two rows end up sharing a number is two sessions independently picking "the next one"; the second-likeliest is one session spotting a gap and treating it as free. Both readings of a duplicated number look right, which is why the guard calls it worse than a dangling reference. Recording the gap costs a row and removes the ambiguity permanently — the alternative, backfilling D-100 with a decision that was actually made under a different number, would put a lie in the log to make it look tidy. |
| D-101 | **The ops console stops lying about when a change takes effect, and a change in flight can no longer be lost or half-applied** | `applies` becomes five values enumerated per key with its reason, guarded by `check_config_applies`; writes are conditional on a per-key sequence-backed ETag under a per-key advisory lock; an identical value is a true no-op; `settings_scope()` pins `Settings` once per request in both HTTP deployables; every managed field is bounded on the field itself; and the SIGTERM handler stops replacing uvicorn's. | **The finding that outranks the rest was not about the console at all.** `_install_signal_handlers` destroyed the drain it documented: `Server.serve()` enters `capture_signals()` and *then* runs the lifespan, so our handler replaced uvicorn's `handle_exit` with one raising `KeyboardInterrupt`. Measured with a real server and a sleeping request — before: `ESCAPED KeyboardInterrupt`, no in-flight line at all; after: `INFLIGHT 200` then exit 143. The exception escaped `asyncio.run`, so `Server.shutdown()` never closed sockets and waited, and the lifespan's `finally` never flushed. **On `hooks.calevate.tech` that made every deploy an abort of whatever webhook was in flight, on an at-most-once feed with no retry (D-31)**, and `stop_grace_period: 30s` had nothing to give its seconds to. DEPLOYMENT §4b's "in-flight work finishes instead of being killed" is true for the first time. **Sixteen of fifty-five keys were mislabelled**, worst first: `usd_inr_rate` said `live` but `BolnaEngine.__init__` copies it and `get_engine()` caches the adapter, so a new rate reached `get_settings()` in seconds and never reached the conversion stamped into `usage_events` — money, labelled the worst possible way; `bolna_api_key` had no classification at all, so **a rotation from the Secrets panel never reached the code placing calls**, and the symptom reads as "the vendor is rejecting our key", sending an operator to Bolna's dashboard instead of a restart; `db_pool_size` claimed `on_restart`, which is worse than a wrong label because it *promises a restart applies it* — `db/session.get_engine` builds from a bare `Settings()` before the store is reachable, since reading the store needs a connection from the pool it would size, so a stored value can never apply and the write is now refused. A fifth value, `unclassified`, is the fail-safe: a field added tomorrow is managed the day it is added, so it arrives unclassified, uneditable and refused — the safe default is "you cannot edit this yet", never "we assume it works". **The ETag is per-key and sequence-backed, not the fleet sentinel**: the sentinel moves on every key, so with 36 fields two operators on *different* fields is the normal case and B editing `alerts_email` would invalidate A's edit to the price — false conflicts teach people to retry without reading, which is last-write-wins with a round trip in front. A global sequence rather than a per-row counter, because a counter restarts at 1 after a revert and a token read *before* the revert would match the row that replaced it. A version column alone is insufficient — two writers reading revision N both pass — so `set_value` takes a per-key advisory lock before the read. **The idempotency comparison is on typed values, not JSON bytes**: a test the author expected to pass failed and was right, because `9.5` and `"9.50"` are one `Decimal` and two strings, so a byte comparison would bump the whole fleet because a form formatted its number differently. **Torn reads were tested rather than reasoned** — 2,000 races against a flipper found zero mixed pairs, the snapshot does swap atomically — but a unit of work is not one read, and two `get_settings()` calls in one request could straddle a refresh: new rate with old price, which on a money path is a *wrong* number, not a stale one. **A bound had to be taken back and produced a rule**: an exact `min_length=15` on the GSTIN broke a test that feeds 14 characters on purpose, where `parse_gstin` already degrades to a proforma — a model-level bound caps blast radius and must never be stricter than a consumer that already fails gracefully. The migration also found a latent bug: `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` covered tables only, and this schema had never had a sequence, so the first console write on a fresh deploy would have failed `permission denied for sequence`. |
| D-102 | **The greppable-constant device gets its reverse check: prose that STATES a capability constant's value is compared against the constant, and CI fails when the doc understates what was built** | New section 5 of `scripts/check_docs_drift.py`. The registry is DISCOVERED, not listed — every module-level `NAME = <bool literal>` under `apps/`, `packages/`, `scripts/`, `alembic/`, `tests/` found by AST, which today is exactly the five honesty constants (`PROVIDER_CREATES_ORDERS`, `LEAD_RETRIEVAL_IMPLEMENTED`, `PROVISIONING_IMPLEMENTED`, `CLOUD_API_CONFIRMED_AGAINST_LIVE_WABA`, `ENGINE_REPORTS_TTS_MODEL`) and nothing else. Prose is judged **only** where it joins the name to a boolean in the present tense (`= False`, `is False`, `is now`, `is still`, `stays`, `remains`, `flips to`) with nothing between them but backticks and asterisks; a sentence that merely NAMES a constant claims nothing and is not judged. Scanned everywhere the claim actually lives: markdown, Python docstrings and comment runs, and the console's JSDoc. Second half catches the rename hole — a value claim about an UPPER_SNAKE name the tree defines nowhere. Negative controls in `tests/capability_claim_guard_test.py`. | **Every error a readiness audit found ran the same direction: the capability was BUILT and the doc still said missing.** `PROVIDER_CREATES_ORDERS` had been True since D-98 while BUILD-LOG, ROADMAP §5, ROADMAP §7 and OPERATIONS §7 all said no checkout could be opened; `LEAD_RETRIEVAL_IMPLEMENTED` had been True since D-90 while SURFACES §2 said "still NOT built and not to be claimed". The existing device only ever guarded OVERclaiming — `UNWIRED_BASELINE` may only shrink, the pilot scorecard derives its verdict — and understating is not the safe error: it is how a shipped checkout gets rebuilt, and how a session re-derives a decision (`docs/` is authoritative, so a reader believes the doc over the code). **Adjacency, not proximity, is the load-bearing choice.** `whatsapp_cloud.py` writes "the same device `payments.py::PROVIDER_CREATES_ORDERS` and `ingest/meta.py::LEAD_RETRIEVAL_IMPLEMENTED` use — and it stays False", where the "it" is a third constant and both named ones are True: any window-based rule reports that line twice and is wrong twice. **Present tense only**, because BUILD-LOG is a chronological log whose older sections correctly record states that have since changed — "was False" is history, "is False" is a claim about this tree. **A whole class was deliberately given up rather than approximated**: BUILD-LOG's "WhatsApp … no vendor adapter, because no decision picks a BSP" was false in both halves (D-91 picked Meta Cloud API; `whatsapp_cloud.py` is the transport) and names no constant, so no matcher can decide it. A grep-shaped guard over that class was written for the credential rule the same week, flagged three files that merely NAMED a credential, caught neither real offender, and was thrown away — half of a decidable question beats all of an undecidable one. What closes the other half is not a cleverer matcher but a constant: a capability worth a paragraph is worth a greppable boolean, and the moment WhatsApp's landed, the prose about it became checkable here. |
| D-103 ⚠COMPLETED by D-104 (the two defects it reports below are now closed) | **The set of engine names has exactly two definitions, both in `calevate_shared`, and an AST scan fails the build on a third** | `config.EngineName` (the `Literal` pydantic validates `ENGINE=` against) plus the new `config.SELECTABLE_ENGINES = frozenset(get_args(EngineName))` answer *what may be selected*; `engine.WEBHOOK_AUTH_BY_ENGINE` answers *what has an authenticity story*. `apps/voice-runtime/engine_intake.py` loses its own `EngineName` and its character-identical copy of `WebhookAuthMethod`; `KNOWN_ENGINES` becomes `frozenset(WEBHOOK_AUTH_BY_ENGINE)` — the same table `verify_source` authenticates from, so "known to the receiver" and "has an authenticity story" are one answer. The receiver's last hardcoded vendor name goes with it: `if engine == "fake"` becomes `if method == "none"`. `tests/engine_name_drift_test.py` AST-walks `apps/`, `packages/shared/src/` and `scripts/` for any tuple, list, set, dict-key or `Literal[...]` spelling two or more engine names outside the two homes, deriving its vocabulary from the homes themselves, and asserts `SELECTABLE_ENGINES ⊆ WEBHOOK_AUTH_BY_ENGINE`. | **The drifted copy did not cause a security hole; it caused a blind spot, which is why nothing caught it for a wave.** Cartesia deliveries were already refused twice over — `WEBHOOK_AUTH_BY_ENGINE["cartesia"] == "hmac"` takes the branch that returns `ok=False`, and `cartesia.py::verify_webhook` fails closed with `SIGNATURE_UNIMPLEMENTED_REASON` — and neither refusal can be softened by a change to the other. What the stale `Literal["bolna", "fake"]` did was collapse the label: `_refuse` reports anything outside `KNOWN_ENGINES` as `"unknown"`, so on `ENGINE=cartesia` every delivery 401s and every 401 is attributed to a stranger probing the URL rather than to our own unimplemented verifier. That is the exact failure `_refuse`'s own docstring argues against for Bolna ("goes SILENT… indistinguishable from a quiet night"), one step worse, because a misattributed graph sends the incident somewhere else. **`WEBHOOK_AUTH_BY_ENGINE` and not `SELECTABLE_ENGINES` is the receiver's source**, because `fake-restricted` is deliberately unselectable and is the only engine declaring `hmac` — the receiver must answer for it identically or the single test exercising the signature branch would exercise a different code path from production. **The subset assertion runs in the direction that hurts**: a selectable engine missing from the auth table answers `unknown engine` on every delivery it will ever send. **A THIRD copy was found and is reported rather than fixed**: `apps/api/agents/models.py::ENGINES = ("fake", "bolna")` renders `ck_agents_engine_enum`, and `admin/service.py::_default_engine` writes `get_settings().engine` into that column on the tenant-birth path — so `ENGINE=cartesia` fails client creation with a raw `IntegrityError`, not a named refusal. It needs a migration, so it is pinned as a strict `xfail` naming the fix and listed in `KNOWN_OPEN_COPIES`, which is equality-asserted: fixing the constant fails the test and forces the entry's deletion, so the list cannot rot into a permanent exemption. Same shape one layer up, also reported: `core/settings.py::runtime_config_missing_keys` names `BOLNA_API_KEY` under `engine == "bolna"` and has no `cartesia` clause, so `/healthz/ready` is green on a credential-less Cartesia deployment — the fix is to ask `engine_availability()`, which already answers `no_engine_credentials:cartesia`, rather than grow a second per-vendor `if`. **The guard is a scan, not a pin**: pinning the copy we found would have been satisfied by this commit and silent on the next one, and the copy in `agents/models.py` — the one with a database constraint behind it — is precisely the copy nobody thought to look for. |
| D-104 | **The third engine-set copy is gone, and `/healthz/ready` stops answering for Bolna on a deployment that is not running it** | `agents/models.py::ENGINES` becomes `tuple(sorted(SELECTABLE_ENGINES))` and migration `d7b1c48a2e93` widens `ck_agents_engine_enum` to admit it; the strict `xfail` and the `KNOWN_OPEN_COPIES` entry D-103 left are deleted, forced by that dict's equality assertion. `runtime_config_missing_keys` loses `if cfg.engine == "bolna"` and asks the adapter instead, via a new `VoiceEngine.credential_env_keys` and `engine.missing_engine_credential_keys()`. `get_engine` is split: `build_engine(cfg)` is the branch, `get_engine` is the cache over it. The `str` widening and the two `getattr`s in the factory — vestigial since D-93 — are gone with it. | **Two Cartesia-shaped defects, one root: a fact about a vendor written down outside `apps/api/engine/`.** The CHECK constraint was the worse of the two, because a constraint is not advisory: `admin/service.py::_default_engine` writes `get_settings().engine` at tenant birth, so on `ENGINE=cartesia` the first thing a new client does — exist — failed with an `IntegrityError` out of Postgres, naming a constraint whose text disagreed with the setting that produced the value. The second was quieter and reaches further: readiness named `BOLNA_API_KEY` under one hardcoded vendor clause, so a credential-less Cartesia box reported itself fit for traffic on the probe an orchestrator uses to decide whether to send it any. **The obvious patch — a second `if` — is the shape that produced the bug**, and it would also put "Bolna needs BOLNA_API_KEY" in a core module, which hard rule 2 reserves to the engine package. So the adapter answers both halves: `holds_credentials()` for the verdict, already the single authority `engine_availability` derives from, and `credential_env_keys` for the NAME, because "not ready" without the key to set is a red light with no next step. **`credential_env_keys` names only what `holds_credentials` gates on**: `CARTESIA_FROM_NUMBER_ID` is required to dial out and NOT to reach the vendor, so listing it would hold a whole deployment down for a control that refuses cleanly on its own. **`build_engine` had to exist before readiness could be correct** — `get_engine` caches on the engine NAME, so a caller passing its own `Settings` gets an adapter built from a different one, and readiness answering about a configuration that is not deployed is worse than readiness not answering; a test warms the cache and asserts the answer still comes from the settings handed in. **The migration's downgrade refuses rather than reverses when it cannot**: narrowing the CHECK with a `cartesia` row present would either be rejected by Postgres mid-flight or "succeed" by deleting client agents, so it counts first and raises BEFORE dropping the constraint — a refused downgrade must not leave the table unconstrained, which is the one outcome worse than either direction. **The schema half needed its own test and the model half could not have caught it**: `ENGINES` deriving from `SELECTABLE_ENGINES` makes the two agree in Python while the database keeps whatever the last migration wrote, so a fourth engine shipped without a migration would leave the model permissive, the constraint narrow, and the failure deferred to a client's first insert — the new test reads `pg_get_constraintdef` and parses it rather than string-matching, because an equality test on rendered SQL fails on spacing and teaches the next reader to loosen it. Sabotage confirmed all five: hand-retyping `ENGINES` (2 red), restoring the hardcoded clause (3 red, including a grep that fails on any vendor key named in `core/settings.py`), narrowing the live constraint behind the model's back (1 red, and ONLY the schema test saw it), pointing readiness at the cache (3 red), and an adapter that refuses for want of credentials while naming no key (2 red). |
| D-105 | **Post-call extraction was aimed at a Sarvam model Sarvam has retired, and pilot gate 1 would have configured a live agent with it** | `SARVAM_DEFAULT_LLM = "sarvam-105b"` and `SARVAM_RETIRED_LLMS` live beside `ModelConfig` in `calevate_shared/engine.py`; `workers/extraction.py::SarvamExtractor` and `scripts/pilot/gates_api.py`'s pilot `ModelConfig` both import the first. `tests/sarvam_model_identifier_test.py` AST-scans `apps/`, `packages/shared/src/` and `scripts/` for any retired identifier as a string CONSTANT (not prose), asserts the default is not itself retired, and asserts the pilot gate IMPORTS rather than spells. TRD §7's extraction paragraph corrected. | **Two literals, both `"sarvam-m"`, both correct when written, and the vendor moved.** Sarvam's changelog reports that `sarvam-m` is deprecated and a Chat Completions request naming it FAILS — so extraction was pointed at a model that no longer answers, and the pilot gate would have provisioned a real agent on a real telephone number with a dead LLM. **The cost model made it worse than a 400**: TRD §10 has priced the LLM leg at ₹0.00 on "Sarvam 105B, free per token" since D-36, so the code and the unit economics disagreed about which model was running, on the one leg whose entire contribution is that it is free. **This is the third time this repo has paid for a constant it did not have** — D-103's engine set, D-104's credential names, and now a model identifier — and the shape is identical each time: no single home, so the correction has one place to land and two places to be missed. **Evidence standing is REPORTED, NOT READ and says so at the constant**: `docs.sarvam.ai` is refused by this environment's egress proxy, so this is two independent search summaries of the changelog agreeing, never a page fetched here. Shipped rather than gated because a wrong identifier fails LOUD — the vendor 400s and `extraction.py`'s error ladder records it — which is the safe direction. **`sarvam-30b` is in the retired set too**, and that is the detail that argues for the set existing: it was `sarvam-m`'s own migration target and has since been retired in turn, so a fix that had simply moved to the 30B would be broken again already. **The scan reads AST constants, not source text**, because the correction has to be EXPLAINED somewhere and a regex would flag the paragraph explaining it. **The pilot-gate assertion is the one that would have been skipped**: `scripts/pilot/gates_api.py` reads like a fixture and is not — it drives a real Bolna account — and an in-place edit there passes the retired-name scan while reintroducing the defect on the next retirement, so it is asserted separately. |
| D-106 | **The one test that could validate the object-lifecycle policy had never run, and when it ran it failed** | `test_minio_accepts_and_returns_the_policy` becomes three: the rules MinIO implements are round-tripped, the excluded rule's exclusion is RE-MEASURED every run, and MinIO's silent drop of `AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload` when combined with another action is pinned. `MINIO_UNSUPPORTED_RULE_IDS` is asserted from both directions. `infra/object-lifecycle/policy.json` is UNCHANGED. | **It skipped on every run for as long as it has existed** — the guard is `_endpoint_reachable("localhost:9000")` and MinIO was not up, so "1 skipped: local MinIO not running" was the line nobody read. Bringing the container back for an unrelated reason ran it, and it failed: MinIO rejects `PutBucketLifecycle` for our fourth rule with `InvalidArgument`. **The cause is a documented MinIO limitation, not a defect in the policy** — MinIO's own S3-compatibility page and `minio-go`'s lifecycle package both state `AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload` is unsupported there; production is Cloudflare R2 (DEPLOYMENT §1), which implements it. Every shape was tried against `RELEASE.2025-09-07T16-13-09Z` before concluding that — `Filter: {"Prefix": ""}`, `Filter: {}`, no filter, the legacy top-level `Prefix`, a non-empty prefix — and all five are rejected. **So the policy stays and the CLAIM changes.** Deleting a real growth control so a local emulator goes green is weakening the thing to fit the test; what was actually wrong was the test's sentence, "the document survives a real S3 implementation's parser", which was never true of this document and had never been executed to find out. **The finding that outranks the failure is the SILENT one**: fold the abort action into a rule MinIO can implement and it answers 200, stores the rule, and discards the action — measured, and the stored rule comes back carrying `Expiration` alone. That is the obvious way somebody makes this red test go away, and it yields a green suite, a 200 from the store, and a lifecycle policy where the multipart growth control does not exist; a loud rejection is recoverable, a silent drop is discovered by a storage bill. Both are now sabotage-verified: emptying the exclusion set fails the round-trip test, and folding the action into `policy.json` fails the exclusion test. **The exclusion cannot rot**: it is re-measured on every run against the live store and fails the day MinIO implements the action, telling the reader to delete it. **`infra/README.md` §2 predicted exactly this class** — "R2, DO Spaces and MinIO each implement a subset of S3's lifecycle grammar" — which is now a measured instance rather than a warning. **CI could not see any of it, and now can**: the workflow declared only `postgres` and `redis`, so all three checks would have skipped there exactly as they did locally — a guard that skips everywhere is not a guard. MinIO joins CI as a `docker run` STEP rather than a `services:` entry, because it needs `server /data` and GitHub Actions service containers accept no `command`; the step polls `/minio/health/live` and FAILS the job if the store never comes up, since a silent timeout would restore the skip and with it the whole defect. |
| D-107 | **The national DND half of the gate becomes a recorded scrub RUN, not a loaded list, and `dnc_list.scope='global'` gets its first writer** | Migration `a1c8e40f27b9`. `preference_scrub_runs` — tenant-scoped, FORCEd RLS, append-only — records provider, the platform's reference, `scrubbed_at`, `expires_at` (23:59:59 IST of the run's day) and **counts only, never a phone number**. `national_dnd_scrub_missing`/`_expired`/`_incomplete` refuse **promotional** campaigns in `launch_blockers` AND `dispatch_blockers`. `scope='global'` is redefined as an absolute platform-wide suppression, written only by the step-up-confirmed `POST /v1/ops/dnc/global`; the RLS `WITH CHECK` gains one branch requiring the session to carry NO `app.tenant_id`. `campaigns.dnc_scrubbed_at` is stamped in the same statement as the CAS to `running`. | **THE BRIEF WAS WRONG AND THE RESEARCH IS WHAT CHANGED THE DESIGN.** I asked for a loader for a list of "tens of millions of numbers". The NCPR (formerly NDNC) **cannot be downloaded**: every access provider's DLT documentation carries the same sentence — *"the preference and consent database will not be accessible to telemarketers due to secure controls and use of blockchain technology on the DLT platform"*. A Registered Telemarketer SUBMITS a list to the operator's scrub facility and receives a reference, a report of COUNTS rather than numbers, and a verdict valid until 23:59:59 that day. So the producer I specified was a producer for a feed that does not exist, and the right primitive is a RUN with an expiry, not a list. **Loading NCPR rows into `dnc_list` was rejected for a second reason that outranks the first**: preference is category-scoped, so `check_dispatch` would read those rows as absolute and refuse **lawful transactional** traffic — wrong rather than conservative. **The gate is scoped to promotional only**, because under full DND every category is blocked except service-implicit while transactional is delivered regardless, so gating those would suppress calls the subscriber is entitled to receive. **It is not a self-inflicted outage**: performing a scrub needs the same RTM relationship that produces `platform_state.tm_id`, and `tm_registration_missing` already refuses every tenant's campaign until that exists — the blocker's precondition is already a precondition. **The blocker is also on the DISPATCH tick, not only launch**, because the window closes at midnight IST while a campaign keeps dialling. **`UNIQUE(tenant_id, phone_e164)` never constrained global rows at all** — NULLs are distinct in a unique index — so the global scope had no uniqueness for as long as it has existed;
