a partial unique index now supplies it. **The scrub timestamp SEC-COMP §67 has promised all along did not exist**; there are now two, and the "one list or two" distinction was previously invisible from every screen. **Three of seventeen sabotages found defects in the agent's own work**: `submitted` taken from the caller passed first because the fixture had equal counts, a log-line leak passed because `JsonFormatter.redact_text` masked it (strengthened to pin the call site's `extra` field set), and one guard turned out redundant against RLS and was reported honestly rather than quietly kept. **The protocol also caught a real reversibility bug**: the downgrade dropped the trigger but not its function, so `downgrade`→`upgrade` failed `DuplicateFunction` — a downgrade that cannot be followed by an upgrade is not reversible (hard rule 8), fixed and proven over three full cycles. **Recorded, not fixed**: the `scrub_ref` is taken from an operator and never queried back, so the gate proves an accountable assertion rather than a performed scrub — the test asserts no HTTP client is imported into `preference_scrub.py`, so the entry cannot outlive the defect. Closed by R-01, an RTM relationship with an access provider. |
| D-108 | **The money path had one rate card in two places, one rounding rule enforced nowhere, and a screen that called a paying client unpriced** | `MONEY_Q` derives from `models.MONEY.scale` and `rates.ROUNDING` is the one mode (`service.ROUNDING` re-exports it). `terms.PRICING_COLUMNS` + `CEILING_COLUMNS` PARTITION `TERM_COLUMNS` and `states_pricing` reads the former; `_record` reads by name, not position. `margin_for_tenant` sums `_tier_totals` instead of carrying its own copy of the cost expression. New `check_docs_drift` §4b diffs TRD §10.1's TTS rate card against `TTS_INR_PER_10K_CHARS` and against the doc's own second table. Guards: an AST scan for `.quantize()` with no explicit mode, a term-column partition check, a rate-card differ, a margin-definition check — measured at 0 false positives each. | **The live bug was in the WRITE path for money.** `workers/pipeline.py::_unit_price` stamps `usage_events.unit_cost_paid` and called `.quantize()` with no rounding mode, inheriting the process-global `decimal` context — mutable, and ROUND_HALF_EVEN by default. Reachable, not theoretical: a ₹0.0180 telephony leg over a 360-second call is exactly ₹0.00005/second, which half-even stores as `0.0000`, rounding the whole leg out of the margin panel and out of a closed month's `spend_used`. The "pass it EXPLICITLY, never inherit" doctrine existed in two docstrings and was enforced by nothing. **The second defect is the D-102 shape wearing a price**: `PlanRecord.states_pricing` read a hand-written list of price columns that `overage_rate_value` was never added to when D-36's second rung landed — so a plan quoting only the value rate, which is precisely the row a founder writes the day that price is decided, reported as stating no price and the console rendered "No price agreed … They are still invoiced nothing" over a plan billing ₹5.50/min. Verified arithmetic: 100 value minutes → ₹550 billed, screen says ₹0. The same lie survived on the history table, which had one "Rate / min" column. **The lead about `rate_zone_drift` was wrong and the agent said so**: that check reads nginx `limit_req_zone` directives — requests per second, no rupees anywhere — so NOTHING in the tree had ever compared a price against the doc stating it. **A tautology was caught by sabotage, in the agent's own new test**: `assert SERVICE_ROUNDING is ROUNDING` stayed GREEN under the sabotage, because `decimal.ROUND_HALF_UP` is an interned string that compares identical between any two modules naming it; replaced with an AST scan for module-level rounding-mode definitions, with the reason recorded where the next person reaching for `is` on a decimal constant will meet it. **Five ambient-rounding sites outside the slice** (`engine/fake.py` ×2, `scripts/pilot/fidelity.py` ×3) are recorded as an equality assertion so fixing one fails the test and forces the entry out. **A pre-existing coverage gap, found by sabotage rather than by reading**: `commercial_terms_test.py` tests that `overage_rate_value` is settable and unset, and 22 of its tests stayed green while `states_pricing` was blind to it. |
| D-109 | **The one latency budget this repo names is measured for the first time, and it turns out to be a process-count question rather than a handler question** | Server-side ack on the in-call tool endpoint, real handler, real `verify_source`, real ARQ enqueue against real Redis, nothing stubbed: **p50 1.0ms · p95 1.4ms · p99 1.6ms at one call in flight**, 0 database round trips, 1 enqueue, 3 Redis round trips. Under concurrency the distribution is flat at every width — p50 6.3ms at 8 in flight, 48.5ms at 96, **143.0ms at 250** — so `latency ≈ in-flight ÷ ~1,750/s` and the whole 100ms is spent at ~175 concurrent in-flight tool calls per process. Distribution recorded in TRD §6.2 beside the number it feeds; CI asserts the MECHANISM (zero DB round trips, exactly one enqueue) rather than a wall clock. | **`CLAUDE.md` carved out this one exception and attached an instruction — "measure it" — and nothing did.** Three near-misses were checked and rejected: `probe_tool_call_budget` scores the right number but from an evidence file that does not exist and measures the end-to-end round trip instead; `voice_runtime_ack_budget_test` pins the WEBHOOK receiver at 500ms; `call_optout_test` asserts one sample against the wrong budget. **The endpoint the budget names does not exist** — D-33/TRD §6.2 keep T3 inside the engine's KB and a test fails the day one appears in voice-runtime — so what was measured is `POST /tools/v1/{engine}/opt-out`, the engine custom function that IS on the audio path and shares every layer a RAG endpoint would need before retrieving anything. That measures the question that actually decides §6.2's fallback: whether the server half of 100ms is affordable at all. **The verdict has two halves and collapsing them would be the lie**: at one call the budget is not close to blown (p95 is 1.4% of it); at production concurrency it is blown by our own server time alone, and D-32 records Bolna at 100 concurrent on Pilots with 250+ in production. **NOT a CI latency assertion, argued rather than assumed**: the margin at width 1 is 70×, so a bound loose enough to be stable would pass against a handler an order of magnitude slower, and this repo already argues that a wall-clock assertion on a shared runner measures the runner and gets deleted carrying its guarantee with it. **What is excluded is stated**: the network — the dominant term — is gate 8's, because vendor hosts 403 at CONNECT here. **The round-trip ledger is the durable half**: sabotage put a lazy `SELECT 1` in the handler and 42 other tests stayed green, including the import-surface guard. Also fixed on the sweep: KB `kind="url"`/`kind="file"` were ACCEPTED and silently ignored — 201 for a fetch never performed, chunks taken from whatever text happened to be pasted — now a named 422; an admin publish panel rendered NOTHING while its read was in flight, telling an operator nothing was awaiting publish about a question nobody had answered (§52); and FLOWS §7 promised four steps that do not exist, one of which — the regression smoke — CANNOT be built, because `VoiceEngine` exposes attach/detach/list and no way to ask the engine's KB a question. **A correction the agent made against itself**: it first claimed nothing pinned the diagnosis ordering, then found by sabotage that an existing lifecycle test reddens too — by coincidence, not by design — and recorded why the coincidence is not a guarantee. |
| D-110 | **The poller was not the guarantee of record, and a concurrent post-call pair double-billed into an append-only ledger** | `_already_completed` becomes `_pipeline_settled`, which asks whether the artefacts THIS snapshot implies (`_expected_artifacts`) exist rather than whether a `completed` call row does; the verdict is a `Literal` so `reconciliation_repairs` separates `missing_call` (the vendor's at-most-once delivery) from `unfinished_pipeline` (our own drop). `lock_call_writes` — the house `pg_advisory_xact_lock` idiom — wraps all three check-then-writes. `EXTRACTION_OWED_SQL` is the SQL form of `needs_extraction`, imported by both the poller's probe and the stall alarm. A systemic publish failure defers the outbox tick with per-attempt backoff instead of charging every message its poison budget. `dispatcher.STALL_AFTER_MINUTES` now IMPORTS `PIPELINE_STALL_AFTER`. | **D-31 calls the poller the guarantee of record and for one shape it guaranteed a status line and nothing else.** `ingest_engine_event` writes the completed call row and THEN enqueues the pipeline, so when the delivery arrived and the post-call job was lost, `_already_completed` saw a completed row and skipped: measured at **ten consecutive ticks reporting `repaired=0` with artefacts still 0,0,0,0** — no lead, no transcript, no usage row, no bill, unrecoverable forever, because Bolna never redelivers and the execution ages out of the 30-minute window. The only trace was a stall alarm that reports and never repairs. **The snapshot is what makes the question answerable with no new column**: the engine's own record says whether the execution had a transcript and a cost, so an absence stops being ambiguous — which is why this needed no migration, and why it is strictly stronger than the `calls.pipeline_completed_at` marker first considered, since a stage marker reports "the pipeline ran" and a run that silently produced nothing satisfies it. **The concurrency defect is worse than a race because the ledger is append-only**: `asyncio.gather` on two `_meter` calls for one call produced **10 usage rows for a 5-row call and 1.5833 minutes counted into `spend_state` as 3.1666** — a permanent double charge AND a spend cap armed at half the tenant's real allowance, guarded only by an ARQ job id, which is a Redis convention with a finite window rather than a database fact. **A 50-second Redis outage dead-lettered the entire outbox** (10s cron × 5 attempts, no backoff), and recovery was a step-up-confirmed operator replay at whatever hour it happened; tolerated downtime goes 50s → 300s and poison messages still reach the DLQ untouched. **The stall alarm's population was false positives**: a silent call on an agent with no extraction schema owes no extraction, and the alarm counted it twice an hour for a day. **Two of fourteen sabotages found defects in the agent's own tests**, which is the argument for doing them: removing the fan-out locks changed nothing because `_meter` took the same lock four stages earlier and staggered the runs — the race was being run by a different lock — and one assertion read `0 == 0` because the counter it checked was never bumped. **The concurrency suite carries a negative control** that stubs the lock to a no-op and asserts the double-bill reappears, so a future edit that accidentally serialises the harness fails loudly instead of leaving the other tests silently vacuous. **Two vendor assumptions are named rather than assumed**: whether the execution listing paginates (a truncated window is calls the poller never sees at all) and whether `GET /executions` can lag the vendor's own webhooks, which decides whether the 10-minute grace is generous or tight. Both bound the guarantee this row strengthens and both belong to pilot gate 6. |
| D-111 ⚠SECURITY, amends D-107 | **Any tenant could DELETE a global DNC row, because `WITH CHECK` is not evaluated on DELETE** | Migration `e4f2a86b13d7` adds a RESTRICTIVE `FOR DELETE` policy on `dnc_list` whose predicate is the same sentence as the existing `WITH CHECK`. The permissive `FOR ALL` policy is untouched. Asserted with raw SQL at the table, not through the route, plus the opposite direction (an ops session can still lift a suppression). | **Found by writing the coverage the ratchet demanded, not by reading the code.** D-107 raised `compliance-gate` from 9 uncovered units to 21; covering `remove_global_entry`'s "RLS refused the delete" branch meant constructing a tenant session that could see a global row but not delete it — and the test would not go red, because the delete SUCCEEDED. Measured as the app role against the real schema: `SET app.tenant_id = '1111…'; DELETE FROM dnc_list WHERE phone_e164 = '+9198…';` → **`DELETE 1`**. One client lifting a regulator instruction, or our own permanent refusal, for every other client on the platform. **The cause is a Postgres semantic that is easy to read past**: `USING` decides which rows a DELETE may remove and `WITH CHECK` applies only to INSERT and to the NEW row of an UPDATE, so an asymmetric policy that deliberately opens `USING` to `tenant_id IS NULL` — which D-107 had to, or a nationally suppressed number would not be visible to the tenant scrubbing against it — opens DELETE with it. **UPDATE was checked rather than assumed** and is correctly refused (`new row violates row-level security policy`); `dnc_list` is also the only table in the schema whose `USING` mentions `tenant_id IS NULL`, confirmed against `pg_policy`, so the blast radius is one table. **A RESTRICTIVE policy rather than four per-command policies**: the permissive rule is load-bearing for SELECT and correct for INSERT/UPDATE, so rewriting three working rules to fix one would make "can a tenant see a global row" a four-policy question; RESTRICTIVE is ANDed and scoped to the one over-permitted verb. **The application guard was not the fix and could not have been**: `remove_global_entry` already carried a `rowcount != 1` refusal written in the belief that RLS refused the delete, and D-107's own sabotage S9 reported that guard "redundant" on the strength of the SELECT's behaviour — correct about the SELECT, wrong about the DELETE. Hard rule 1 says RLS is the enforcement, and an `if` in one function constrains neither psql, nor the next caller, nor the next function. |
| D-112 | **`usage_events` gets the natural key its two sibling ledgers already had, and an unguarded double-metering aborts instead of double-charging** | Migration `b8d3f47c2a19`: partial unique index on `(tenant_id, call_id, unit_type)`, excluding `unit_type = 'other'` and grandfathered on `created_at >= '2026-08-15 10:14:00+00:00'`. Both `KNOWN_AMBIENT_ROUNDING` (five sites) and `KNOWN_OPEN_RELIABILITY_GAPS` (four entries) are now empty. | **A lock is a convention; an index is a fact.** D-110 closed the double-billing race in-process with `lock_call_writes`, and that was the right first move, but the ledger is append-only so a row that slips past the lock is a permanent wrong number. **The exclusion is the finding**: `billing.service.record_tier_correction` — hard rule 4's compensating-entry mechanism, the ledger's ONLY repair path — appends `unit_type = 'other'` against the same `(tenant, call)`, and two ops refs correcting one call legitimately produce two rows. A key over all unit types would have turned the repair mechanism into a 500, so the predicate enumerates the five types `pipeline._meter` writes, POSITIVELY, following `ux_credit_ledger_tenant_reason_ref`'s precedent. **`ON CONFLICT` is deliberately unused**: `DO UPDATE` fires the append-only trigger, and `DO NOTHING` would hide a partial interleaved write. What a retry does now: the guarded path is untouched, and the unguarded path aborts — and because `_meter` writes the five rows, the wallet charge and the `spend_state` upsert in one transaction, the abort rolls back the whole second metering and ARQ retries into a clean no-op. A permanent double charge becomes one failed attempt. **The grandfather column departs from the sibling and the reason is argued in the migration**: `credit_ledger` keys on `occurred_at`, but `_meter` stamps `occurred_at` from `snapshot.ended_at`, so a poller repair of yesterday's call would land OUTSIDE an `occurred_at` cutoff — precisely the path with the weakest lock discipline — while `created_at` is monotone and actually means "written after this migration". **Sabotage proved the cutoff was necessary rather than cautious**: dropping the index made six tests red, and the index could not then be rebuilt, because the unguarded negative control had left permanent duplicates behind. |
| D-113 | **The outbox stops spending a message's whole retry budget on a receiver that is restarting, and the console stops saying "nothing is dead-lettered" in the middle of an outage** | `mark_outbox_failed`'s retry branch REPLACES the claim lease with the backoff instead of clearing it; `OUTBOX_SYSTEMIC_BACKOFF_*` becomes `OUTBOX_RETRY_BACKOFF_*` because it now governs both failure kinds, with the SQL interval extracted so the two writers cannot drift. `deferred` is published on `DeadLetterQueue`/`DeadLetterQueueOut` from the SAME grouped aggregate as the depth (one instant, `FILTER` clauses). | D-110 fixed the systemic branch and left the per-message one, where clearing `locked_until` made a message with budget left re-claimable instantly — five attempts inside fifty seconds against a receiver that would have been back in sixty. **The console half is the one an operator meets**: it truthfully reported zero DEAD LETTERS while messages sat deferred, which reads as "nothing wrong" at exactly the moment something is. **Two of the agent's own fixes had no test until sabotage asked for one**, and writing them found more: the all-clear lives in TWO places, and only the green notice box had been fixed — `deadReason`, the sentence physically beside the replay button where an operator actually reads, still said "Nothing is dead-lettered" mid-outage. The second, `deferred` hardcoded to `0`, sailed through the existing probe because that probe only checks the FIELD EXISTS; the new test asserts a delta and includes recovery, so the gauge is proven to come back down. **The registry's own equality was restructured**: it required `PROBES == KNOWN_OPEN`, so closing a gap deleted the probe that had just proved it could detect the defect. It is a superset assertion now — entries must have probes, and probes outliving their entries are regression tests that must keep answering False. Three of the four fired during sabotage. |
| D-114 ⚠BILLING CORRECTNESS | **Any two clients onboarded within 65 seconds of each other shared an invoice number, permanently and in every month** | The serial's tenant suffix was `tenant_id.hex[:8]`; it becomes `blake2s(tenant_id.bytes, digest_size=6)`. Rule 46(b)'s remaining gap is pinned by a test asserting its two arms — length and consecutiveness — separately. | **Found by writing a test for something else.** Tenant ids are uuid7, whose first 48 bits are a millisecond timestamp, so `hex[:8]` is `ms >> 16` and advances once every **65.5 seconds**. Not a birthday risk — deterministic collision, and it reproduced on the first two tenants a test created back to back. The old test could not see it because it only ever asserted the serial's LENGTH. The replacement derives from the whole id rather than from a claim about uuid7's layout (~2e-7 at 10k tenants). **On Rule 46(b) itself**: verified by search (REPORTED, NOT READ — `taxinformation.cbic.gov.in` is egress-blocked; five secondaries quote clause (b) identically), and the rule adds a phrase worth having — *"in one or multiple series"*. **Nothing is out of compliance today**, and that is the honest framing: Rule 46 binds a REGISTERED PERSON, there is no GST registration, `supplier.is_registered` is false everywhere and the document says `proforma`, which 46(b) does not govern. What exists is a scheme that would be non-compliant the day registration lands. **The serial was deliberately NOT shortened**, because a 16-character hash satisfies length, still fails consecutiveness, and would have deleted the only record of the gap. **The design is written into `invoice.py` and one founder decision is named**: whether a stored `issued_invoices` registry is accepted against D-46, since a consecutive series is stateful by definition and "the 123rd invoice of this year" cannot be recomputed from ledgers — `build_invoice` cannot mint it behind a GET (D-64). Not a sequence: `nextval` is non-transactional, so a rollback leaves a permanent gap, and there is no financial-year reset short of scheduled DDL at midnight on 1 April; instead `UNIQUE (financial_year, serial_no)` allocated `MAX+1` under an advisory lock on the FY inside the issuance transaction, gapless because a rollback un-allocates, with the year in the key so the boundary needs no cron. |
| D-115 ⚠DPDP, the worst defect found this week | **Filing an erasure request made the recording permanently undeletable, and every test was green** | Migration `9c1d3e7a05f4`: `recording_erasure_holds` (tenant-scoped, FORCEd RLS, cross-tenant zero-rows test, deliberately NOT append-only because `erased_at` is stamped on completion), plus `ttl_days > 0` and `UNIQUE (tenant_id, data_category)` on `retention_policies`. `_erase_recordings` destroys the bytes when past the floor and writes a hold with `erase_after` fixed from the CALL's own clock when inside it; the sweep's hold arm runs OUTSIDE the policy loop. `_sweep_objects_in_batches` replaces a hand-written loop across three callers. The erasure certificate distinguishes destroyed / scheduled-with-a-date / proof-predates-this, and never renders absent as zero. | **`execute_deletion_request` nulled `calls.recording_url` and every existing test asserted that column, so the suite was green on a function that deleted nothing.** The column holds an object KEY, and nothing in the repository had ever deleted a recording object — the sweep's own comment claimed the bucket lifecycle rule did it, while SEC-COMP §4 already recorded that rule as a bucket-wide 2555-day GROWTH CEILING that "CANNOT follow the retention policy". Two consequences, and the second is the one that matters: a caller who never asked to be forgotten had only the POINTER expire on their tenant's policy while the audio survived ~7 years; and a caller who DID ask was left worse off than one who did not, because clearing the pointer destroyed the only handle on the key and the sweep selects `WHERE recording_url IS NOT NULL`. **Filing a DPDP erasure request made the recording permanently unreachable by any deletion path we have.** That is not either side of the open "does erasure beat the retention floor" question — it is the failure to have asked it. **The legal reading is encoded where it is enforced, not in a doc**: DPDP §12(3) lets a retention obligation DEFER an erasure, and §8(7) makes keeping the data past that obligation a breach in its own right, so "we cannot delete this yet" is lawfully a SCHEDULE and a schedule must be a row — which is what the holds table is. The 2025 Rules' Third Schedule and its 48-hour pre-erasure notice were checked and do NOT apply (they ride on e-commerce/gaming/social-media thresholds), recorded in SEC-COMP §4 so nobody re-derives it. **The recording floor's authority does not check out and was NOT unilaterally changed**: TRAI's 90 days is the opt-out cooling period before a sender may seek fresh consent, and the two-year commercial-record archive is Unified Licence clause 39.20 binding LICENSEES — Calevate is a telemarketer, not one. The floor errs towards keeping data, which is safe against destroying evidence and unsafe against §8(7), so it is a registry entry for counsel rather than a guess. **Sabotage caught a defect in the agent's own work of exactly the predicted shape**: its first export fix asserted against a query the test had typed out itself rather than calling `build_subject_export`, so breaking the real code left it green — and the fix was unreachable anyway, because an erasure nulls `from_e164`/`to_e164` in the same statement that creates the hold, so no held call could ever match the export's phone predicate. Rebuilt keyed on `subject_ref`, the handle D-44 preserves for exactly this. **Three constraint sabotages could not be reverted afterwards** — the rows the tests inserted while the constraints were absent are precisely the rows the constraints reject — which is a second proof that they were doing work. |
| D-116 | **Every egress path asserted on the bytes that leave, and the formula guard's fifth surface was the one it missed** | `tests/crm_egress_redaction_test.py` drives ten doors with TWO real-shaped Indian mobiles — the lead's own, and a different one spoken inside the call — asserting in both E.164 and bare-national spelling, each paired with a positive "the surface returned something" guard so an empty page cannot pass. Fixes: `sheet_header` now goes through `_cell`/`_disarm`; `lead.updated` is emitted from the PATCH and the bulk action; `export_leads_csv` returns `LeadExport(csv, row_count)`; a BOM on the browser blob only. | **The Sheets HEADER bypassed the formula guard while the CSV export's own comment cited that same writer as the "EVERY CELL, HEADER INCLUDED" rule it was copying** — the guard held on four surfaces and leaked on the fifth, which is the shape this slice existed to look for. **`lead.updated` was subscribable and had no producer**: it was in `EVENT_TYPES`, in the endpoint form as "A lead's details change", and in `DEFAULT_SHEET_COLUMNS` since D-23, with zero emitters — a client could subscribe and wait forever. Wiring it forced `set_lead_name` to grow a CAS, because the blanket `SET name` always reported success, so re-saving an unedited row would have posted to a client's CRM and re-sorted their table. **The export's audit row counted `csv_body.count("\n") - 1`**, which over-counts by exactly the rows whose name or note contains a newline — `QUOTE_ALL` keeps it in-cell — so the compliance record of how many people's data left was wrong precisely for the awkward rows. **The missing BOM is a Telugu-first product detail**: Excel decodes a markless `.csv` in the legacy code page, so every Telugu name arrived mojibake; added to the browser blob only, keeping the API response clean UTF-8, the same one-hazard-two-renderings split `spreadsheet_safety.py` already makes. **A guardrail blind spot is now documented rather than assumed away**: `check_redaction_exposure` walks OpenAPI, so CSV bytes, webhook bodies and Sheets rows are structurally invisible to it, and the runtime suite that does cover them is named in the script. **Three of the agent's own tests passed for the wrong reason and it caught all three**: the audit-count sabotage went GREEN because no test existed; `Blob.text()` runs the spec's UTF-8 decode and STRIPS the BOM, so a character-level assertion was green either way (switched to `arrayBuffer()`); and the webhook test claimed to cover the producer while being handed an already-redacted summary, so the producer got its own real-pipeline test. It also initially swept `\t` out of CSV cells, which IS the OWASP mitigation, and corrected to sweep executable leaders only. |

| D-117 ⚠COMPLIANCE | **A lead ad fill that never asked permission was refused at the front door and dialled by the button on the leads screen an hour later** | `ingest.service._record_dial_consent_declined` writes a `consent_ledger` row on both refusal branches — `purpose='callback'`, `status='declined'`, `consent_source='web_form_optin'` — keyed on the PHONE. `check_dispatch` grows a `no_consent` rule reading the LATEST row for `(tenant_id, phone_e164, 'callback')` and refusing on any status outside `granted`, via `DIAL_REFUSING_CONSENT_STATUSES` derived from the ledger's own vocabulary. | **The front door was already right and it did not matter.** `ingest_lead` correctly saves and refuses to dial a Meta lead-ad fill on a form with no opt-in question — but the refusal lived only in a `lead_events` row of type `note`, which the timeline renders as prose for a human and which no dial path reads. So `POST /v1/leads/{id}/call` dialled that person with the ENTIRE compliance gate passing, because the gate had nothing per-person to ask. The CRM slice proved it by test before recording it, which is why it is a fix and not a hypothesis. **The write is keyed on the phone, not the lead**, because the ledger's unit is a person and the same person arrives twice as two lead rows from two forms — a refusal attached to lead #1 would be silently undone by lead #2. **ABSENCE IS NOT A REFUSAL**, and that asymmetry is the whole design: most dialable numbers have no ledger row at all (typed in by staff, imported from a CSV, a caller who rang US), so reading silence as `declined` would refuse every one of them and be met as a total outbound outage rather than as a rule. **The status set is subtractive** — everything except `granted` blocks — so a status added to `CONSENT_STATUSES` tomorrow refuses by default and has to be argued INTO the allowed set, which is the safe direction on a compliance gate. **`callback`, not `marketing`**: this gate governs whether we may telephone this person, and `messaging` has its own gate on the WhatsApp path — a person may accept a call and refuse a message, and both answers are theirs. **The schema pushed back and was right**: `ck_consent_ledger_granted_consent_carries_evidence` refused a bare `granted` row in the supersession test, so a grant must say how it was obtained while a decline needs no evidence beyond the absence the timeline already records. **A methodological note worth more than the fix**: the first sabotage round reported the same two failures for four different breaks, and one of those breaks had not even applied — the source files had been reverted underneath the run, so the baseline was never green. Four identical results from four different causes is the signature of a test suite proving nothing, and it was caught only by noticing the uniformity. The round was redone with each edit's application verified by grep, a green baseline asserted either side, and the four breaks now produce four distinct signatures. |
| D-118 | **The onboarding wizard could hand over an account whose agent had never reached the engine, and when it did reach it, it could carry nine words of English** | `admin/intake.py` stops carrying its own copy of the `prompt_versions` INSERT and calls `prompts.insert_prompt_version` with `apply_live = not script_staged`; `IntakeOut` gains a required `staged_behind_script`. Publishing an agent with no script is `agent_has_no_script` (422) and writes nothing. A "Voice platform" panel performs the FIRST publish. Both client-realm invitation refusals move into `admin.service.create_invitation`, with admin-realm list/revoke added so the refusal cannot lock an operator out. `inviteLink()` is the one spelling of `/invite`. | **Four defects, and three of them made the product LIE rather than fail.** `agents.live_prompt_id` was never set by the wizard even though `agents/service.py` states the invariant "`live_prompt_id IS NULL` can only mean the two pointers agree" and NAMES `admin/intake.py` as a writer upholding it. Consequences in opposite directions: a freshly onboarded, freshly published agent told the CLIENT "Script v1 is waiting to go live" **forever**, about the script the engine was actually running — because `publish_agent` sends `COALESCE(live_prompt_id, system_prompt_id)`; and re-submitting the intake over a staged edit minted the new version as a DRAFT and then published the version the applied pointer still named, so an operator updating a client's prices saw `prompt_version: 3` while the engine kept v1. **Publishing an agent with no script shipped `"You are a helpful receptionist."`** — the wizard mints the agent row at step 1 before any prompt exists, and `_to_config` fell back to a hardcoded English string. The route answered **200 `status: live`**, wrote the routing row, and put nine words of English with no hours, no prices and no business name on a Telugu clinic's line. **Nothing anywhere performed the first publish.** The route was mounted and tested and had zero callers in either realm; every other publish path is a RE-publish guarded on the agent already being live (`apply_to_live` → `if row.is_live`, `set_call_cap`/`recompile_t0` → `status == 'live' AND engine_agent_ref`). A founder could run the entire wizard, invite the owner and hand over an account whose agent had never reached the engine — the half-wired-feature defect at the scale of a whole customer. **The admin invite path enforced neither of the client realm's two refusals**, so pressing Create-invite twice minted two live owner credentials for one address with only one revocable; the fix moves both into the single statement that mints the row, so both realms inherit them. That fix then created a 72-hour lockout for an operator whose token was lost — the only revoke was client-realm and the owner cannot sign in yet — which is why admin-realm list/revoke landed in the same change rather than as a follow-up. **Two sabotages found defects in the agent's own tests**: one asserted only a button's absence, which an empty card also satisfies (fixed to assert a skeleton), and the `/invite` path had nothing pinning it at all — the very defect that made every invite link a 404 for eight days. **Two sabotages could not be made red from app code and are recorded honestly rather than dropped**: `calevate_app` is NOBYPASSRLS and cannot `SET ROLE`, so those tests document a database-enforced guarantee rather than an application one. |
| D-119 ⚠SECURITY | **A blank impersonation header was an undesigned third state that set `impersonating` without a grant, a permission check or an audit row — and the RBAC registry passed vacuously on a permission that does not exist** | One `_impersonation_slug()` shared by all three dependencies; blank or whitespace is `422 impersonate_org_blank` naming the header; `Principal.impersonating` is derived from the RESOLVED TENANT, so it cannot be true without an authorised, audited entry. `assert_policy_registry_complete` validates every declared string against `get_args(Permission)` and against `GRANTED_PERMISSIONS`. `current_principal` refuses a request carrying the impersonation header with `403 impersonation_not_available_here`. | **`X-Impersonate-Org: ` with an empty value satisfied `header is not None`**, so `current_admin` set `impersonating=True` while the falsy slug skipped the `admin:impersonate` check, the grant requirement AND the `admin.impersonation_read` row. `Principal.impersonating` is what every mutating dependency, `can_mutate` and the console's grey-out logic read as "a grant was verified and this read was audited" — and it could be true when none of that had happened. `current_any` disagreed with `current_admin` about the same header and fell through to the CLIENT verifier, so a header problem answered "This token is not valid for this realm"; whitespace reached the directory lookup and answered `404 Organization not found`, a 404 about a client for a defect in a header. **The registry defect is the more instructive one**: `permission_meta("agents:reed")` beside `requires("agents:reed")` satisfied every existing check, because **two copies of one typo agree with each other** — declared equals enforced, an identity resolves. `role_has` then answered False for every role the database enums allow, so the route was a 403 for the entire population: a dead endpoint that reads as a guarded one, with no happy-path test to go red. mypy catches the literal spelling but does not run at boot, cannot see a permission built from a variable, and never looks at the route table. **Three client-realm mutations refused a valid view-as session with the wrong sentence** — correct refusal, wrong explanation, handed to someone whose credential is fine, escalating to whoever owns Clerk instead of whoever owns the console; the new check is on the HEADER rather than the token, because deciding "this is an admin's token" would create a second definition of which realm a request belongs to. **The mutation sweep is the test that would have caught all of it**: every one of the 66 routes declaring a mutating permission, driven with a real minted grant, each required to refuse with one of exactly two known codes — 63 by D-22's read-only rule and 3 by the realm boundary — with a floor assertion so the split cannot drift toward "D-22 is enforced by accident of which realm a route happens to declare". **Guarantees named as database-enforced rather than application-enforced**: `ROLE_PERMISSIONS` is one flat dict and nothing in Python stops a client `owner` and an admin role sharing a name and merging their permission sets — what prevents it is `ck_memberships_role_enum` and `ck_admin_users_role_enum`, so a colliding role cannot be STORED, and the test says plainly that its sabotage is an `ALTER TABLE … DROP CONSTRAINT` rather than a `.py` edit. Seven sabotages, seven distinct signatures, each verified landed by grep with a green baseline asserted either side. |
| D-120 ⚠TELUGU-FIRST | **Every non-Latin business name became `/c/client`, a closed account could be handed a key that burned itself on redemption, and the wizard's audit row was a second transaction** | `slugify` returns `""` and `derive_slug` refuses with `slug_not_derivable` (422) naming the field, on the wizard and on self-serve signup, with both screens asking before the POST. `admin.service.assert_account_open` is asked at BOTH the mint and the burn of an invitation — 404 for an absent tenant, 409 `account_closed` for churned or soft-deleted — and the refused redemption rolls back so the link stays redeemable. The lifecycle switch answers 404 for a soft-deleted client via the one `service.tenant_exists` definition. The wizard's audit row, the invite mint and the revoke all move into the birth transaction through the `on_created` hook self-serve signup already used. | **The slug defect is the one that should never have survived on a Telugu-first product.** `slugify` folded Telugu and Devanagari to the empty string and substituted a constant, so the FIRST such client silently took the immutable slug `client` and the second got `slug_taken` naming a slug nobody had typed. That is the default path for this product's market, not an edge case. Transliteration was considered and rejected for a stated reason rather than an aesthetic one: no ASCII-folding library is installed, and adding one to the tenant-birth path is a hard-rule-9 supply-chain decision — so the product ASKS. **The invitation defect is a complete broken journey rather than a bad status code**: inviting into a churned or soft-deleted tenant answered 201, the invitee accepted with 200, burned their single-use token, received a `memberships` row, and was then refused "You are not a member of this account" on their first request, because `core/auth.py` filters `deleted_at IS NULL AND status <> 'churned'`. Every step succeeded and the outcome was a person with a spent credential and no account. **`suspended` is deliberately still invitable** and pinned by a test: suspension stops outbound dialling, not access, and it is exactly when you add the person who will pay. **The audit gap is the quiet one**: a client account — or a live owner credential — could exist with nothing recording who created it, because the wizard wrote its audit row in a second transaction while self-serve signup had already solved this with the `on_created` hook. One mechanism now, not two. **Two of the agent's own tests were flaky and were found because the baseline was re-asserted between sabotages** — a shared signup-quota source IP and a non-unique 39-character slug — which is the argument for that discipline rather than for trusting a green run. Nine breaks, nine distinct signatures. **Two gaps recorded rather than fixed, neither vendor-blocked**: `organizations.deleted_at` has seven readers and no writer anywhere in `apps/`, so tenant-level erasure has no execution path (`deletion_requests` is per data subject); and `current_identity` answers 401 to a verified Clerk token whose Svix mirror has not yet landed — the ordinary race on signup and invite-accept — where a browser reads 401 as "sign in again", which does not help. |
| D-121 | **`publish_agent` never called `get_agent`, so "live" was four claims about the engine all derived from one fact about ourselves: our HTTP call returned without raising** | Migration `c1f6a94d2b07` adds `agents.live_verify_state` (four values, CHECK) and `live_verified_at`. New `agents/verification.py` — `judge()` / `verify_publish()` with verdicts `applied` / `not_applied` / `unreadable` / `unreachable`, scoring the disclosure line SEPARATELY from the script. `publish_agent` takes a row lock, reads back after the write, REFUSES on a proven mismatch (`engine_publish_not_applied`, transaction rolls back), records unproven states without rounding them up, and carries `AND deleted_at IS NULL` with a rowcount refusal. Same for `publish_variant`. `engine_drift_for()` + `GET /v1/agents/{agent_id}/engine-state`. A conformance clause over all four adapters. | **D-64 built the entire verification surface and nothing called it.** `get_agent` went onto the Protocol carrying the sentence "a 2xx says the vendor took the bytes, not that the agent is running them", along with `carries_prompt_marker`, `holds_speech` and three `*_readable` tri-states — and no production code path ever invoked any of them. So `status='live'`, `engine_agent_ref`, `live_prompt_id` and `live_tts_voice` were four assertions about the vendor derived from one fact about us. **The disclosure line is scored separately from the script** because an engine that keeps the script and drops the greeting passes a script-only check while breaking hard rule 5 — the one property here with a legal consequence — and a dictated-TTS engine is not scored on a voice it never took, because a check that fails on every publish is a check operators learn to ignore. **The orphan turned out to be manufactured by the ordinary case**: `publish_agent` was a read-then-write over `engine_agent_ref` with a vendor round trip in the middle and no lock, so two concurrent publishes both saw "no ref", both created, and one vendor agent became unaddressable and undeletable — proved with a double minting a fresh id per create (FakeEngine's deterministic ref hides it) plus 50ms of vendor latency to make the coroutines genuinely interleave. **The publish UPDATE named the id alone**, so a delete landing between `_load_agent`'s filter and that statement resurrected a soft-deleted agent to `live` AND gave it an `engine_agent_routes` row — the same class as the finding that `AND a.deleted_at IS NULL` in `_load_agent` was the only thing between a deleted agent and a caller, one statement further down. **`unreadable` and `unreachable` deliberately do NOT block**, argued at the module head rather than assumed: on a create the vendor write has already happened, so refusing there trades a recorded uncertainty for a guaranteed orphan plus the same uncertainty. **Reconciliation is a READ**: it re-publishes nothing, because overwriting someone's emergency console edit is a decision with a blast radius, and it is the only instrument that can see the two drifts a publish-time check structurally cannot — a vendor-dashboard edit, and a publish that failed on our side AFTER the vendor committed. **Two of the seven sabotages forced test fixes rather than passing**: the concurrency break was GREEN first time because `asyncio.gather` with no yield inside the vendor window ran each transaction in turn and measured nothing, and the resurrection break reported only `DID NOT RAISE` until the assertions were moved out of `pytest.raises` so they say what the missing refusal actually left behind. **Three gaps recorded with live probes**: `create_agent` is not idempotent on a lost response (the remedy is an idempotency key, and whether Bolna's `POST /v2/agent` honours one is unknown from here — a guessed header shipped as a guarantee is the D-31/D-32 mistake); there is no `delete_agent` on the Protocol, so an orphan cannot be compensated; and drift reconciliation is on-demand only, which is NOT vendor-blocked and closes with one ARQ cron. |
| D-122 | **`organizations.deleted_at` had nine readers and no writer anywhere, so tenant-level erasure — the last step of offboarding — had no execution path at all** | Migration f3a71c9e26b4 adds `tenant_erasure_requests` (RLS'd, one open request per tenant), the CHECK `ck_organizations_deleted_implies_churned`, and an exclusive second owner on `recording_erasure_holds` (`num_nonnulls(request_id, tenant_erasure_id) = 1`). New `compliance/tenant_erasure.py` + `tenant_erasure_routes.py`: `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{id}/erasure`, `admin:tenants` declared, `ops:manage` checked and a step-up bound to the tenant id, filing the row, the outbox job and the audit entry in ONE transaction. `workers/retention.execute_tenant_erasure` reuses `_erase_recordings`, `_erase_delivery_bodies` and the per-subject statements over a keyset walk of the tenant, and writes `deleted_at` LAST, beside the certificate. | **The column was load-bearing and unreachable**: `core/auth.py` (membership resolution AND the impersonation slug lookup), the dial gate, `tenant_exists`, `assert_account_open`, the admin directory, the health board, `quality/service.py`, `quality/sampling_routes.py` and `workers/qa_sampling.py` all branch on it, and nothing in `apps/` could set it — a whole column of behaviour that read as shipped. **The readers disagreed with each other and the fix was an invariant, not a vote**: some filter `deleted_at` alone, some `status` alone, some both, which is only safe if `deleted_at IS NOT NULL => status = 'churned'`. Without it an 'erased' but `active` tenant would be in `core/auth.py`'s refusal set and the health board's LIVE set at the same instant. Enforced in the DATABASE because a writer is a property of this week's code — the test says so and names `ALTER TABLE … DROP CONSTRAINT` as its only real sabotage. **A second erasure MECHANISM was refused and a second SUBJECT was insisted on**: `deletion_requests` is one data principal's DPDP §12 right, keyed by `phone_e164`, client-realm, with a CHECK forbidding an open row without a number — so tenant rows there would have shown up in a client's own register, and the fan-out alternative would have minted one §12 certificate per caller for a request nobody made. The worker, the object-store helpers, the outbox and the hold schedule are shared; only the table, the precondition and the tenant walk are new. **`recording_erasure_holds` needed a second owner or the bug it was built for came straight back**: clearing `calls.recording_url` destroys the only handle on the audio and the sweep selects `WHERE recording_url IS NOT NULL`, so a tenant erasure without a hold row makes the recording permanently undeletable — the exact defect migration 9c1d3e7a05f4 closed on the other path. **Hard rule 4 was a design constraint, not an obstacle**: every append-only ledger survives byte for byte, including the `consent_ledger` rows that carry the caller's number, and the certificate SAYS so rather than letting a document that lists what it cleared imply it cleared everything. **The certificate had to outlive its subject**: every other admin route filters `deleted_at IS NULL`, so a status read built the ordinary way would 404 at the instant the erasure it certifies succeeded — none of these routes goes through `tenant_exists`. Six sabotages, six distinct signatures, green baseline re-asserted either side; the seventh guarantee (the invariant) and the eighth (RLS) are database-enforced and say so in the test. |
| D-123 | **D-121 recorded three gaps and called two of them vendor-blocked; only one was. `delete_agent` was a published Bolna endpoint nobody had looked up, and the drift sweep was one cron** | `VoiceEngine.delete_agent` on the Protocol, IDEMPOTENT BY CONTRACT, implemented in all four adapters (`absent_is_success` on `_request` converts a 404 into the satisfied postcondition) with a conformance clause that deletes one of two agents, proves it gone through `get_agent`, proves the other survived, and deletes twice. `agents/service.py::_orphaned` became `await _reclaim_orphan(...)`: the compensator now DELETES the vendor object inline before the publish raises, and falls back to the old ERROR line naming the ref when the delete itself fails. Migration `d4b8e1c73f05` adds `drift_state` / `drift_checked_at` / `drift_detected_at` to `engine_agent_routes` with a five-value CHECK and a partial `(engine, drift_checked_at NULLS FIRST) WHERE active` index. New `agents/reconciliation.py` (`claim_drift_batch` / `record_drift` / `read_engine_drift`) and `workers/engine_reconciliation.py`'s `sweep_engine_drift`, an ARQ cron at :07/:37 with `max_tries=WORKER_MAX_TRIES`. `GET /v1/ops/platform` carries `engine_drift`; the admin ops console renders it as a read-only panel. OPERATIONS §2 gate 2 grew a `delete_agent` sub-check that runs on a THROWAWAY agent, and `runbooks/agent-engine-drift.md` is the alarm's procedure. | **A GAP CALLED VENDOR-BLOCKED SURVIVES BECAUSE NOBODY RE-CHECKS IT.** `no_delete_agent_on_the_protocol` said an unverified vendor behaviour is not a capability (D-31/D-32) and named a Bolna account as what closes it — and Bolna publishes `DELETE /v2/agent/{agent_id}` in its reference, 200 `{"message":"success","state":"deleted"}`, with a note that it destroys the agent's batches and executions. One search closed most of it. What genuinely remains unknown is narrower than the entry claimed: what a REPEAT delete answers. That is a MARKED ASSUMPTION in both real adapters (assumed 404, falsified by a 400) plus gate 2's third sub-check, not a silent premise. **Cartesia's is weaker and says so** — no public agent-delete documentation exists, which is itself the finding. **THE COMPENSATION IS INLINE AND NOT THROUGH THE OUTBOX**, because the ref lives only in the frame: an outbox row would roll back with the transaction that is failing and take the last copy of the id with it. **A HUMAN'S SOFT-DELETE DELIBERATELY DOES NOT REACH `delete_agent`** — the vendor's delete destroys call history that is a DPDP retention obligation, and the orphan's subject is an agent minted seconds ago that has never taken a call. **THE SWEEP'S RECORD LIVES ON `engine_agent_routes`, NOT ON `agents`**, and that is structural: `agents` is FORCE-RLS'd, so a staleness-ordered global work queue and a cross-tenant ops summary are both unaskable from a tenant session — the alternative was the first RLS exemption on `agents`, versus reusing the routing bridge's existing one, and the three columns hold a five-value verdict and two timestamps with no prose (hard rule 6). **IT SKIPS THE REDIS LEASE `campaign_dispatch` NEEDS**, because a 120s budget plus one read inside a 1,800s interval cannot overlap — and that licence is checked at import (`_assert_the_tick_fits_its_interval`) rather than asserted in a comment. **`unreadable`/`unreachable` ARE NOT THE ALARM**: an alert that fires when a vendor is briefly slow is muted before it ever catches a real dashboard edit, so the console publishes them as a separate count. **`oldest_checked_at` IS ON THE PAYLOAD BECAUSE THE PANEL WOULD OTHERWISE LIE BY OMISSION** — if the cron dies every count freezes and `out_of_sync: 0` reads as all-clear forever. **Two tests were green for the wrong reason first**: the lost-response drift wrote its new prompt in a committed transaction of its own, so our row and the engine ended up agreeing and the sweep correctly reported `applied` — it only became a divergence once the setup went through `apply_to_live`, which pushes to the engine INSIDE the transaction that moves the pointer; and every `checked=N` count was whatever routing rows previous suites had left in the shared database until the sweep's scope was pinned with `active`. **`create_agent` idempotency stays OPEN and is the one real vendor block**: whether `POST /v2/agent` honours an idempotency key is unknown from here, and a guessed header shipped as a guarantee is exactly the D-31/D-32 mistake. |
| D-124 ⚠SECURITY | **A verified Clerk token whose Svix mirror had not landed yet was answered 401 "This account is not provisioned.", so signup and invite-accept — the first thirty seconds of every client's and every colleague's life in the product — put people in a sign-in loop with no exit** | New `apps/api/core/clerk_identity.py` owns the ONE resolution of "which user is this token": `resolve_mirrored_user`, called by both `current_identity` and `_load_client_principal`. On a mirror miss it reconciles from Clerk's Backend API (`GET /v1/users/{id}`) through `mirror_clerk_user` — the SAME upsert the Svix webhook now delegates to — and only when Clerk cannot be reached does it raise the transient `identity_mirror_pending` (503, `retryable: true`, `Retry-After: 2`). `apps/web`'s transport waits that code out with bounded backoff (0.5/1/2/4s) instead of bouncing to sign-in. A deactivated row is 401 before any reconcile; a Clerk 404 is 401, not an endless 503; the admin realm is never reconciled. FLOWS §2's "the mirror must land first" ordering claim is deleted. | **Clerk's own guidance is the evidence and it names our design as the anti-pattern**: "Webhooks are asynchronous and eventually consistent… Do NOT rely on webhook delivery as part of a synchronous flow such as onboarding… For data the user just created, read it from the Clerk session token or call the Backend API directly" (github.com/clerk/skills, `features/clerk-webhooks/SKILL.md`, read 2026-08-15; clerk.com is unreachable from this build host). **Minting the row from the token's CLAIMS was rejected as a security defect, and that is the whole reason the Backend API is used instead**: `accept_invitation` binds an invitation to `users.email`, so that column is an authorization input — a row built from an `email` claim whose verification status the token does not state would let someone who added a colleague's address to their own Clerk account redeem the colleague's key. Pulling Clerk's User object places EXACTLY the trust the webhook already places and no more, which is why the two paths share one function rather than resembling each other. No new table, no migration and no fourth reliability mechanism: the upsert's `ON CONFLICT (clerk_user_id)` is what makes a late webhook and two concurrent reconciles collapse to one identity — proven by a test whose stubbed Clerk call blocks on a barrier until both callers are inside it, and which times out rather than passing if the pair is serialised. |
| D-125 | **Two events a client could subscribe to and one lead a client could see were all unproducible — `campaign.completed` had no producer, and a Meta lead refused for want of a token stopped being recoverable the moment Meta gave up retrying** | `campaign_dispatch.emit_campaign_completed` fans `campaign.completed` out IN the transaction `complete_or_rearm` writes the terminal status in, with aggregates only (`campaign_id`, `name`, `contacts_total`, `contacts_reached`, `completed_at`) and a matching `DEFAULT_SHEET_COLUMNS` layout; a re-armed repeat produces nothing. `POST /v1/lead-sources/{webhook_id}/meta/redrive` (`org:manage`, audited) selects that source's `webhook_inbox_events` rows failed on `meta.REDRIVABLE_REASONS` and calls the SAME `_absorb_leadgen` with a `LeadNotification` rebuilt from the row and the ROW'S OWN `payload_hash`, so the claim, the capability selector, the consent branch and the compliance gate are production's. `IngestActivityItemOut` gains `event_key`, `lead_source_id` and a server-derived `recoverable`; the Meta setup card counts what is waiting and offers the button. Both entries in `tests/crm_egress_known_gaps_test.py` deleted, both probes kept. | **The registry's own equality is what made this a fix rather than a rediscovery** — the entries could not survive their defects, and the kept probes now fail by the original names if either regresses. Three things the design work had not foreseen and the build found. **(1)** A `LeadNotification` rebuilt from an inbox row carries the `leadgen_id` and no provenance, so hashing it presents a different digest for the same key and `claim_inbox_event` correctly refuses it as a doctored replay — the row's stored hash has to be passed, and the price is that a recovered lead's `leads.data` names the id that fetched it and not the ad it came from, because the reliability inbox stores no vendor payload and hard rule 2 says it must not start. **(2)** The re-drive could STRAND the lead it exists to rescue: a Graph timeout mid-re-drive is recorded as `meta_graph_unavailable`, which is correct on the live path where Meta's own ladder brings the notification back, and fatal here because the recorded reason IS the candidate predicate — so `_restore_redrivable_reason` puts the original refusal back, guarded on `status = 'failed'`. **(3)** `campaign.completed` was the specimen two Sheets tests used for "an event with no column order", so giving it a producer left that refusal reachable by no event at all; the tests now monkeypatch the layout away and a third asserts the stronger claim — every event in `EVENT_TYPES` has one. A frontend sabotage also caught a test of mine passing for the wrong reason: the waiting-lead count was proved only against rows that were all recoverable, so the flag was not load-bearing until a non-recoverable row for the same source joined the fixture. |
| D-126 | **The debug archive for raw vendor payloads was a personal-data store no erasure could enumerate — fixed before its first caller, not after** | `storage.payload_key` now writes `engine-payloads/{tenant}/{call}/{engine}-{execution}.json` instead of `engine-payloads/{engine}/{date}/{execution}.json`; `archive_payload` takes the tenant and call as REQUIRED arguments and states the write order it needs (commit `calls.engine_payload_ref` FIRST, then PUT); `retention._erase_engine_payloads` deletes every object under the erased calls' prefixes and clears the refs on both the per-subject and the tenant erasure path, counted in each proof's `actions`; `storage.delivery_body_keys_for` became `keys_under` so one listing serves both stores. No migration and no schema change — the column already existed and nothing writes it — and NO change to `infra/object-lifecycle/policy.json`: the rule's `engine-payloads/` prefix is unchanged, which `tests/object_lifecycle_test.py` asserts rather than assumes, so nothing in `infra/` (which has never been applied to any bucket, README §5) was touched or needed to be. The registry entry in `tests/dpdp_known_gaps_test.py` is DELETED, which its equality assertion required. | The archive carried the caller's number and the transcript under a key naming neither tenant nor subject, so a DPDP §12 request could not reach it and no `retention_policies` category expires it — and it was safe only because the function had no caller. **Remedy (b) over remedy (a):** deleting the capability was the cheaper change but the wrong one, because TRD §5, SURFACES §5 (persist-then-ack), DEPLOYMENT §1 and DATA-MODEL §3 all describe archiving raw payloads as the deliberate escape valve for hard rule 2 — vendor payloads live in object storage precisely so they never land in typed columns — and Bolna is unsigned and at-most-once with the poller as truth (D-31), which is exactly the reconciliation an operator needs the vendor's own document for. Deleting a documented capability is a product decision, and the two-step of hard rule 8 does not license the drop just because step one is already true. The arm is gated on a call carrying a reference, following `_erase_delivery_bodies`' own argument that no erasure should depend on the object store being up when there is nothing to enumerate; the gate is sound only because the write order above makes an object-without-reference impossible, which is why that order is a contract in the docstring and not a preference. **What remains open and is nobody's to code around:** the archive still has no retention category (the enum is `recording|transcript|lead|consent_log`), so anyone who does NOT file an erasure is covered only by the bucket's 90-day rule — giving it a category is a DPA commitment plus a documented-enum change, reserved in SECURITY-COMPLIANCE §4 beside the backup clause. |
| D-127 | **Dashboard AI runs on Gemini through Vertex AI `asia-south1` and NOWHERE ELSE — supersedes D-36's LLM leg for the post-call, USER-TRIGGERED surface only; D-36's in-call stack (Saaras STT · Sarvam 105B · Bulbul v3) stands, and so does Sarvam on the first extraction pass** | Records the founder's decisions of 15 Aug 2026 (PLAN-HARDENING-AND-GEMINI §1, G-1…G-8) as one binding row. **G-1 — the endpoint, and it is the whole decision**: `asia-south1-aiplatform.googleapis.com` with `locations/asia-south1` in the path; the AI Studio / Gemini Developer API (`generativelanguage.googleapis.com`, which is what `apps/workers/extraction.py:55` reaches today) is DISQUALIFIED, not deprioritised. **G-2**: the dashboard assistant is given tenant-authored config plus REDACTED call data — `text_redacted`, masked summaries — and never raw PII. **G-7**: the FIRST post-call extraction stays Sarvam, permanently, for the reason under "why" below; Gemini serves only what a user asks for AFTERWARDS (re-summarise, reshape, ask-about) over the redacted copy. **G-3/G-4/G-5**: one Calevate-owned GCP credential, cost absorbed, metered per tenant into `usage_events` with its own unit types, overage charged as one `credit_ledger` row with reason `usage` past an included monthly quota — and at the ceiling the feature BLOCKS and opens a modal naming the cost, debiting nothing until a person accepts, that acceptance being itself auditable. **G-6**: on failure — unconfigured, over quota, provider down — one place decides, and it either falls back to Sarvam or refuses with a message the user can act on; a fallback is ALWAYS disclosed in the response and on the screen, because a silent one changes output quality with nobody told. **G-8**: autofill is a suggestion, never an act — human approval (client or operator) is required and it never reaches the publish path unapproved. Enforced before any Vertex code exists by `scripts/check_model_residency.py` in `make guardrails` (PLAN Part 12): no global Google model host in any URL literal under `apps/`, `packages/`, `scripts/`; every `*-aiplatform.googleapis.com` literal carrying `asia-south1`; the region resolvable only from a `Final` constant and never from a console-editable `Settings` field; and the `locations/…` segment interpolating that constant and nothing else. SECURITY-COMPLIANCE §4's cross-border row is rewritten around that guardrail and names Google Cloud (`asia-south1`) as a sub-processor in the client DPA. | **The founder's instruction — "Gemini for everything outside the live call" — reads at a glance as moving extraction, and moving extraction would have shipped raw caller PII to a second processor.** Verified at source before this row was written, not taken from the plan: `apps/workers/pipeline.py:749` computes `redacted = redact(turn.text)` and `:750` appends `turn.text` — the RAW turn — to the string `_persist_transcript` returns at `:773`, which `:541` binds and `:572` hands to `extract_call`. One line apart, deliberately, because a CRM "callback number" field needs the actual digits and an extractor reading `[REDACTED]` returns nothing worth storing. So the raw-PII pass stays sovereign and everything downstream of it runs on Gemini over the redacted copy — G-7 is not a compromise, it is the only split that keeps both halves true. **The guarantee D-36 bought survives; its ARGUMENT does not, and pretending otherwise is how a residency posture rots.** D-36 reasoned "Sarvam is sovereign, therefore no transcript text leaves India". That sentence stops being available the moment a Google endpoint is in the product, and the replacement is narrower and has to be enforced rather than asserted: **the endpoint is regional**. Which is precisely why the guardrail lands BEFORE the client that could violate it — a region is one string, the difference between `asia-south1` and `us-central1` is nine characters, and nothing in a code review reliably catches nine characters. **The AI Studio API is disqualified on two independent grounds, either alone sufficient**: it offers no data-residency guarantee (global host, no region in the URL, `locations/global` on the Vertex side of the same door), and on the free tier Google states it uses submitted prompts, uploads and responses to improve its products, **with human reviewers able to read them** — which for a Processor holding an Indian SMB's callers' transcripts is not a tradeoff, it is a disclosure we could not make. Vertex `asia-south1` (Mumbai) processes and stores in-region for GA generative features and does not train on paid usage, and serves the Gemini Flash / Flash-Lite class we need. ⚠ **Evidence standing is REPORTED, NOT READ and says so here rather than in a footnote**: `docs.cloud.google.com`, `modelavailability.com` and `docs.litellm.ai` are all refused by this environment's egress proxy, so the endpoint format, the regional model availability and the AI Studio terms rest on multiple independent search summaries agreeing — never a page fetched from this repository. It is the D-105 posture for the same reason: a wrong host fails LOUD (a 404 or a 401 from a host that does not serve our project), which is the safe direction, and confirming it is a five-minute task on the day the GCP project exists — it is an OPERATIONS §2-class vendor gate, not an engineering unknown. **The region is deliberately NOT a `Settings` field**, and that is the half a reviewer would wave through: `platform_config.managed_fields()` computes the console's editable set from `Settings.model_fields` minus the bootstrap keys minus credential-shaped names, so a field called `vertex_location` would be console-editable the day it is declared, and a residency posture invertible from a web form at 3am is not a posture. Same doctrine `check_bootstrap_keys` applies to `APP_ENV` (D-95 §4), enforced the same way. **What this row does NOT decide**: which model extracts better. The public benchmarks answer a different question (OCR, Hindi GEC) and nobody has published Telugu code-mixed call transcript → structured CRM fields; this repo owns the right instrument in the golden-transcript fixtures and `scripts/eval.py`, and that run is blocked outside this repo on a Sarvam key and egress, named as such rather than scheduled. |
| D-128 | **The readiness probe stopped naming the credentials this deployment has not installed, and the OpenAPI surface stopped being served in prod** | `core/health.py` splits the VERDICT from the DETAIL: `/healthz` and `/healthz/ready` answer `{status, service}` with an unchanged status code to everybody, and disclose `degradation_mode`, `checks`, `queue` and `fields[]` only to a caller who proves `ops:manage` through the existing `core.auth.requires` ladder — injected by `create_app` as `build_health_router(service, detail_gate=...)` rather than imported, so `core.health` stays inside voice-runtime's pinned import surface. What is withheld from the wire is written to a `health_not_ready` WARNING instead. `create_app` sets `docs_url`/`redoc_url`/`openapi_url` to `None` when `app_env == "prod"`, and `PUBLIC_PREFIXES` loses `/openapi.json`, `/docs` and `/redoc`. `tests/health_disclosure_test.py` drives both directions of every pair. | **`fields[].field` was `runtime_config_missing_keys` verbatim** — the NAMES of the credentials not yet installed (`BOLNA_API_KEY`, `CLERK_ADMIN_SECRET_KEY`, `AUDIT_CHAIN_SECRET`), unauthenticated, exempt from the in-app rate limiter, and proxied from both `api.` and `hooks.` — a targeting oracle that is loudest during exactly the window before real keys land. **The origin lock is not the alternative it looks like**: `calevate-origin.conf` is already included by the api vhost and what it admits is every Cloudflare edge range, i.e. the whole internet on a proxied zone, so the endpoint was ALREADY behind it — no nginx template was changed, because an edge `deny` would also refuse the authorised operator. **`/healthz` published the identical `checks` dict**, so gating readiness alone would have been a mitigation defeated by a second URL — the same shape as the one-route `/docs` workaround in `integrations/routes.py` that this change's other half removes. **Deleting the detail was not an option**: during `db_down` nobody can authenticate, so `ops:manage` reaches no one and the log line is the operator's only copy; it joins the key names into a string because `logging.redact_mapping` renders a list extra as `[N items]`, which would have logged the COUNT of missing credentials and none of their names. **voice-runtime deliberately gets no gate at all** — sabotage proved that wiring one drags `httpx` and `apps.api.compliance` onto a live-call service's boot graph and fails the import-surface guard (hard rule 3), so its readiness detail goes to the log and nowhere else. **"nothing loses a capability" was verified, not trusted**: `gen:api` reads the committed `apps/web/src/lib/api/openapi.json` off disk and `check_openapi_fresh` calls `app.openapi()`, which `openapi_url=None` does not remove — both asserted, and `check_openapi_fresh` stayed green at 145 paths because the response annotation is still `dict[str, Any]`, the honest declaration for a body whose keys depend on the caller's permission. Twelve sabotages, each with its own signature; the one that mattered most was a test asserting the log line off the LogRecord rather than through `JsonFormatter`, which would have passed for a line shipping `[1 items]` — found and fixed rather than reported. |
| D-129 | **The outbound webhook URL was a tenant-controlled SSRF into our own network, and registering one was the only unaudited egress act** | New `apps/api/integrations/egress_guard.py`: `assert_public_http_url` RESOLVES the host and refuses loopback, link-local, private, multicast, reserved, unspecified and non-globally-routable answers — unwrapping IPv4-mapped, 6to4 and Teredo and refusing the NAT64 prefixes outright — plus any port but 80/443. It runs at BOTH registration (`integrations/routes.py`) and again immediately before every POST (`service.deliver`), which returns the refusal as a PERMANENT `DeliveryResult` rather than raising, so it lands on the client's own delivery screen with our own code and never climbs the retry ladder. `APP_ENV=local` relaxes loopback ONLY, port rule included; RFC 1918 and link-local stay refused on a laptop. Both create routes now write `integration_endpoint.created` in the SAME transaction as the INSERT, recording the HOST (or the spreadsheet id), never the URL. | `routes.py:280` validated `HttpUrl` and nothing more while `service.py:534` POSTs a signed body carrying a lead's NAME AND PHONE NUMBER to whatever it names, so `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` was registrable and `GET /v1/integrations/deliveries` published the status code and error type back — an internal port scanner with a UI, and a PII egress channel into the private network. RESOLVING rather than pattern-matching is what makes `http://2130706433/`, `0177.0.0.1` and `0x7f000001` refusals with no special case anywhere: glibc parses them with the same legacy rules the connection would. The connect-time re-check is the half that matters, because the endpoint row outlives the lookup and the tenant owns that name's DNS. Measured on this interpreter, `is_global` alone is NOT the test — IPv4 multicast, IPv6 multicast and the NAT64 well-known prefix all report `is_global == True` — and CVE-2024-4032 moved those properties in 3.12.4, so every category is asserted explicitly rather than delegated. IP PINNING was considered and deliberately NOT taken; the residual window (our `getaddrinfo` to httpx's) and the trigger to close it are stated in the module. Sixteen sabotages, each with its own signature; the sharpest was dropping `follow_redirects=False` from the REQUEST, which re-sent the signed lead to 169.254.169.254 carrying the guard's approval of the first hop. |
| D-130 | **The one admin-realm route in the client path space moved into it, and two guardrail allowlists that had drifted off the route table were pinned back to it** | `PATCH /v1/agents/{agent_id}/voice` becomes `PATCH /v1/admin/tenants/{tenant_id}/agents/{agent_id}/voice` — tenant out of the body and into the path, `SetVoiceIn` down to one field, web client and screen test moved in the same change, no alias left behind — and `route_shape_test` grows `test_no_admin_realm_route_lives_outside_the_admin_path_space`. The D-22 sweep's five exemptions in `authz_audit_test` become module-level `READS_SHAPED_AS_POSTS`, each with its reason, under the staleness assertion its sibling `impersonation_reads_test` already had. `loadshed.ALWAYS_ALLOWED_PREFIXES` drops `/v1/auth`; new `tests/loadshed_exemption_test.py` asserts the census — every exempt prefix names a live route and records which of three admissible reasons (observability, operator reachability, provider callback) it is exempt for, and no publicly reachable write survives a shed unless it is a callback. BACKEND-PATTERNS §6 and SURFACES §2c updated to match. | **All three are one defect: a rule matched by STRING against a route table nothing re-checked.** The voice write was `realm="admin"` sitting under `/v1/agents` with its tenant in the body — the only such route in the app, confirmed by walking the live table rather than by eye — so it took the client rate-limit profile instead of `/v1/admin`'s, its audit row could not be read from the path, and it was the shape the next author would copy with a rationale attached. Moved with **no deprecation window**, deliberately: hard rule 8's two-step doctrine is about columns and readers that deploy apart, and an admin-realm route has exactly one reachable caller — this repo's own console, generated from this schema and shipped with it — so an alias would only keep the copyable wrong shape alive on the wrong limiter, which is the defect rather than a migration path (`POST /v1/agents/{agent_id}/publish` was moved the same way for the same reason). The D-22 sweep skipped five paths by string with nothing checking they still existed: rename one and the entry stops matching silently, and the next route to land on that path inherits an exemption written about a different endpoint — the hole its sibling file had been guarded against for a year. And `ALWAYS_ALLOWED_PREFIXES` exempted `/v1/auth` "so sign-in survives maintenance", which named a route **this API does not have** — Clerk owns sessions — while the one route the prefix did cover was `POST /v1/auth/signup`, a four-table write that let a platform in `emergency` or `maintenance` keep manufacturing tenants it had just declared it could not serve. Nothing was observably broken, because `signup.py` carries its own mode check and refuses first; that is exactly why it survived review, and why the fix ships as a census rather than as a behaviour patch. `POST /v1/invitations/accept` — the other write a caller with no organization can reach — was never exempt, which is the consistency this restores. Six sabotages, each with its own signature; the sharpest was renaming an exempted route AND updating the allowlist to its new path while leaving the old entry behind, which is the realistic drift and which only the new staleness assertion catches. |
| D-131 | **One client IP for the whole platform, a limiter identity that survives a restart, and every route under a named limit with a tenant dimension and a cost weight** | `calevate_shared/client_address.py` is now the ONE `client_ip`/`is_trusted_peer`/`TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDRS` — promoted out of `apps/voice-runtime/engine_intake.py` with `app_env` as a PARAMETER, because the import-linter contract forbids `calevate_shared` importing `apps` and hard rule 3 forbids voice-runtime importing `apps/api`; the engine receiver, the in-call tool route, `core/auth.client_request_ip` (renamed from `_request_ip`) and `tenancy/signup_routes` all call that one function. `core/middleware.py` gains `PUT` in `allow_methods` and `If-Match` in `allow_headers`. New `core/ratelimit.py` owns named `LimitProfile`s, a `*`/`**` pattern table resolved by SPECIFICITY rather than declaration order (one matcher answers for a concrete path and its route template, so the pre-routing and post-auth halves cannot disagree about which limit applies), the ONE fixed-window counter — `consume(..., fail_open=)`, with the signup quota passing `fail_open=False` instead of keeping its own copy of the INCR/EXPIRE pair — and a `blake2s` identity fingerprint. `RateLimitMiddleware` keeps only the dimensions decidable before routing and authentication; the per-TENANT dimension is charged at the first instant the tenant is VERIFIED, in `core/auth.charge_tenant_quota`, memoised on the ASGI scope. `/hooks` splits into `webhook_ingest` (keyed on the path's `webhook_id`), `webhook_payment` and `webhook_identity`; 22 cost rules weight 24 routes as `costly`/`bulk_read`/`bulk_write`; all 171 routes resolve a named profile and `tests/rate_limit_census_test.py` fails both on a route with no rule and on a rule with no route. Four suites: `cors_contract`, `client_address`, `rate_limit_census`, `rate_limit_behaviour`. | **`PUT` was missing from `allow_methods`, so five routes were unreachable from any browser** — `PUT /v1/billing/caps`, `PUT …/feature-flags/{flag}`, `PUT /v1/ops/config/{key}`, `PUT /v1/ops/secrets/{key}` (the vendor-credential install path this whole plan exists to prepare for) and `DELETE /v1/ops/config/{key}`, which sends `If-Match` — and NEITHER suite could see it, because the web tests mock `fetch` and CORS is enforced only by a browser; the guard is therefore a walk of `iter_api_routes` against `allow_methods` plus a regex over `client.ts`'s own request headers, not a list somebody typed. **`_request_ip` was one of 83 copies of `request.client.host if request.client else None`**: the audit named one, the tree holds 83, and 80 of them still stamp the PROXY's address into `audit_log.ip` where SEC-COMP §5 asks for the caller's — a mechanical migration to `client_request_ip`, left undone in this change only because it spans ~30 files being edited concurrently, and recorded in that function's docstring with its grep so it cannot be mistaken for a design. **The signup quota's `SIGNUPS_PER_IP_PER_HOUR = 30` was a cap on the PLATFORM**, since behind Cloudflare + nginx every request's socket peer is our own edge. **`hash(auth)` is salted per process (PEP 456) and prod runs two workers**, so one token held N buckets, the effective limit was N× the declared one, it moved on every restart, and the 32-bit truncation put ~77k live tokens at even odds of spending a neighbour's budget. **`requires()` calls `current_principal`/`current_admin` DIRECTLY rather than through `Depends`**, so FastAPI's per-request dependency cache does not cover it and any route carrying both `requires(...)` and `tenant_of` resolves a principal twice — measured, not theorised: without the scope memo `GET /v1/agents` costs two units of tenant budget and the effective ceiling depends on which dependencies a route happens to declare. **The signup quota's fail-closed branch was documented and untested** and now has a test that fails when the policy flips. **A degraded edge is now alerted rather than silent**: a trusted peer with no `CF-Connecting-IP` outside `local` breaks nothing visibly — every `audit_log.ip` goes NULL and every anonymous caller
