Migration `c4b70e928a1f` puts a FORCEd write policy on `engine_agent_routes` (global `FOR SELECT USING (true)` kept, so the session-less webhook lookup is unchanged) and the registry's exemption reason now says it buys reads only. `05bba2f3c19c`'s downgrade drops the cluster-wide `calevate_app` role only when `pg_shdepend` shows no other database depends on it. New `tests/ledger_truncate_immutability_test.py` and `tests/migration_reversibility_test.py`; `rls_sweep_test` gains a cross-tenant INSERT probe over all 42 tenant tables, a self-row-hijack probe, and a pin on what an exemption buys. | Measured, not inferred, on a migrated database: `TRUNCATE audit_log` emptied the compliance hash chain and `SET session_replication_role = replica; DELETE FROM consent_ledger` emptied the consent ledger — a row trigger cannot see TRUNCATE, and an ORIGIN trigger stops firing under a plain SET that needs no DDL and leaves no schema diff, which is what `pg_restore --disable-triggers` emits. Separately a session scoped to tenant A could UPDATE, DELETE and RE-TENANT tenant B's `engine_agent_routes` row, silencing or stealing another client's inbound calls: the same bug `e4f2a86b13d7` had already fixed on `dnc_list`, on the only other table with the same shape. And `alembic downgrade base` could not complete at all on a cluster with a sibling database, because a per-database migration was dropping a cluster-wide role. What held: all 63 revisions walk head->base->head with a byte-identical schema, all 42 tenant-isolated tables refuse cross-tenant SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE and the self-row hijack, and `calevate_app` holds no TRUNCATE, no CREATE, no SET ROLE and cannot set `session_replication_role`. |
| D-152 | **The coverage ratchet charged for a `Protocol` declaration and called it a pragma** | `check_coverage_ratchet.Measurement.excluded` becomes `suppressed`, counted by `_suppressed_lines`, which reads the source and counts only lines carrying a no-cover comment; the failure detail says "suppressed by a no-cover comment" instead of naming a pragma that may not be in the file. D-29's doctrine is unchanged — a suppression still counts as uncovered, and the only routes down are still a test or a `RAISED_BUDGETS` waiver. Sharpening it took two areas below their floors and left four real suppressions in guarded surfaces, all four then REMOVED rather than accepted: two `.first()`-plus-unreachable-`None` reads became `.one()`, one opt-out validation and one post-CAS re-read got the tests they always could have had. `tier_usage` — a D-36 reporting surface D-150 pinned as having no caller — is nested under `tiers` on `GET /v1/admin/tenants/{id}/margin`, where it partitions that card's `cost_inr`, and the admin panel renders it. | coverage 7 excludes `...`-bodied stubs and `if TYPE_CHECKING:` blocks BY DEFAULT and sweeps the blank lines around each excluded clause in with them. Nobody wrote those exclusions, so charging for them made a four-line `Protocol` cost four units in a hard rule 3 surface — three of them whitespace — under a message that sent the reader hunting for a comment the file did not contain. `VoiceEngine` is written in exactly that shape, so the guard was pricing the repo's own typing idiom and the cheapest way to satisfy it was an untyped callable. The new tests measure coverage's real defaults from an actual run rather than from a changelog, because the behaviour is version-dependent and a guard built on a remembered version is a guard that silently changes meaning on upgrade. Mounting `tier_usage` also exposed why `pnpm -C apps/web typecheck` had not caught five stale margin fixtures: each carried `as Margin`, which asserts the shape rather than checking it — the casts are gone. |
| D-153 | **A recording link that expired before its own audio ended, and a player that could not say so** | `crm/routes.recording_link_ttl_s` derives the presigned window from the call's metered `duration_s` — twice the duration, floored at `RECORDING_LINK_FLOOR_S` (300s) and ceilinged at `RECORDING_LINK_CEILING_S` (2 x `CALL_CAP_MAX_S` + the floor) — rather than handing every recording the flat `PRESIGN_TTL_S`. `recording_key_for` becomes `recording_ref_for`, returning a `RecordingRef(key, duration_s)` read in ONE query so a retention sweep cannot land between the two. `RecordingLinkOut` gains `duration_s` (required on the wire, nullable in value). The client screen replaces `<audio controls>` with `components/callAudioPlayer`: speed, +/-10s, a range-input seek bar, and a single re-mint on `error` that restores the playback position; every transcript turn carrying `start_ms` becomes a seek target and the turn being spoken is highlighted, bounded by the NEXT turn's start. | **Measured, not inferred: every link was signed for 300 seconds and `CALL_CAP_MAX_S` is 3600.** A twenty-minute call played for five minutes and stopped. S3 answers an expired signature with a 403 whose body is XML, which a browser surfaces as a bare `MEDIA_ERR_NETWORK` on the element and nothing else — so the owner's reasonable conclusion was that we had recorded only the first five minutes of their call, and no log anywhere would have contradicted them. Raising `PRESIGN_TTL_S` was rejected: it is shared with every other presigned artefact and the signature IS the credential, so widening it globally hands a longer window to objects that need seconds. Deriving per call keeps the window proportional to the only thing that justifies it, and the ceiling is a named constant so the widest window this route can open is readable rather than derived. `start_ms` had been on every transcript turn since the pipeline was written and NOTHING read it — the column, the API field and the seek it exists for were three-quarters of a feature for as long as the screen has existed. **Retention is unchanged and is not ours to shorten**: TRAI's 90-day floor is enforced by a DB CHECK and by `RECORDING_FLOOR_DAYS`, so a shorter recording policy is unlawful rather than merely aggressive. |
| D-154 | **The frontend's test fixtures stop asserting what they are supposed to prove: 106 casts onto generated wire types removed, and a guard so the 107th cannot land** | `tests/wireFixtureGuard.test.ts` bans any type assertion whose target resolves to a declaration in `src/lib/api/schema.d.ts`, in `tests/` AND in `src/`, with `tests/fixtures/wireFixtureShapes.ts` as its negative control (four banned shapes, six safe look-alikes). `satisfies T` and a plain annotation are the sanctioned spellings; DOM casts, hand-written types like `ApiProblem`, `as const`, and a deliberately off-contract literal carried as `unknown` all stay legal. The sweep removed 106 assertions across 42 files (55 of them `as unknown as T`), and the compiler then reported 34 errors that had been sitting behind them: three `Agent` fixtures with **no `disclosure_line`** (hard rule 5's field, on the outbound agents the campaign screens dial with), `CampaignSummary.created_at` missing from four, `CampaignProgress.concurrency`/`launched_at`/`status` from five, twelve client-realm `Me` fixtures carrying an `organization.plan_tier` that `OrganizationOut` does not define while omitting the `status` it requires, and `Dashboard.minutes_used_month` — a decimal-as-string under hard rule 7 — set to `null`, which its schema forbids. Also closed: `admin/.../prompt`'s call-cap box, whose `min`/`max` come from `/v1/agents/lanes` and silently became unbounded when that read failed, now says so. | **The assertion did not make the fixture right, it made it UNCHECKED**, and an unchecked fixture drifts from the wire in silence because nothing else in the system ever compares the two. None of the 34 errors broke a test — that is why review never caught them, and it is the reason the count matters more than any single instance: a fixture that lies in the direction the screen ignores produces a green suite and a false belief about what the screen has been shown. **90 of the 106 sites already had a type annotation underneath the assertion**, cancelling it: `as T` makes the initialiser's type `T`, so the annotation beside it checks nothing. Stripping revealed a second failure mode the sweep had to fix by hand — 11 sites had NO annotation, so removing the cast traded a false check for no check, and that residue is exactly what this guard cannot see and says so. **`as unknown as T` is strictly worse than `as T`** and was the majority spelling: a single assertion still rejects a literal with no overlap, a double rejects nothing. Two narrowings were forced by the negative control rather than by review: an indexed access (`"x" as AttentionItem["kind"]`) resolves to a bare union with no symbol in `schema.d.ts` and needed a syntactic branch, and it is the highest-damage shape of all — it asserts that a closed union already contains the value whose ABSENCE is the premise of the test around it, and would keep compiling on the day a kind is deleted for real. **The reverse direction was investigated and is genuinely closed**: the API sets no `response_model_exclude_*`, so every field is always serialized, and openapi-typescript 7 emits `default`-bearing properties as REQUIRED — so a Pydantic default does not become an optional TS property, and the 25 remaining optional-but-non-nullable fields are all read through `??`/`?.`, which absorb the `null` the server actually sends. |
| D-155 | **The log stream, the error tracker and the alert fingerprint each carried a hard-rule-6 hole that the module's own docstring said was closed** | Five closures, one per hole. (1) `JsonFormatter` now redacts `record.getMessage()` and the traceback, not only the extras — a `%`-arg or f-string message went out verbatim, and `tests/pii_logging_sweep_test` pins BOTH the formatter and the tree-wide discipline that every log message is a static event token. (2) `redact_exception` replaces `redact_text` on the traceback: frames are kept in full, every exception MESSAGE is withheld, and the whole thing is bounded by line count instead of by a 200-character head cap. (3) `redact_mapping` fails closed on TYPE — a tuple, set, Pydantic model, dataclass or `bytes` used to skip every branch and be rendered by `json.dumps(default=str)`, i.e. its `repr()`. (4) `scrub_event` scrubs `exception.values[].value` (withheld, same marker), `logentry` and event-attached `breadcrumbs`, and drops `request.cookies`; `_redact_span` scrubs span LINK attributes. (5) `install_error_handlers` fingerprints the catch-all alarm on the exception CLASS (`unhandled_exception:ValueError`), and `alerting` NAMES the codes its token bucket dropped instead of only counting them. Plus an ISO-8601 date joins the uuid and the hex digest as an identifier shape held out of the phone pass. | **A docstring is not a control, and every one of these had a docstring.** `observability` dropped `exception.message` off every span while `scrub_event`, forty lines away, shipped the identical string to Sentry — and `str(exc)` is where `pydantic.ValidationError` renders `input_value=`, i.e. the extraction payload. **A length cap is not a control either**, which `route_shape_test` already said about this exact class: whether a traceback's message fell inside 200 characters depended on how deep the stack happened to be, so the old code leaked the message on short stacks and destroyed the diagnosis on long ones — a 334-character twelve-frame traceback was cut off inside frame three, leaving the only durable record of a 500 naming three frames of ASGI middleware. Withholding the message costs the constraint name on a DBAPI error; CPython renders the `raise`'s SOURCE line, which recovers most of it, and no redactor on this path can recognise a caller's NAME or a Telugu sentence — proved by writing the permissive version first and watching `Ravi` reach the log stream. **The type fall-through was latent, not live**: a probe over the whole suite saw 20,377 records with extras and zero non-scalars, which is exactly the state in which a hole is invisible until the first `extra={"turn": turn}`. **The catch-all fingerprint is D-147's defect as a class rather than an instance**: `_admit` suppresses `stage:code` for fifteen minutes, so one code shared by every crash meant the first class to fire silenced the rest — `ClientDisconnect` was caught at its one call site, and the next cheap exception would have done the same thing. **The bucket's blindness is the same shape**: `webhook_source_rejected`, `tool_source_rejected` and both bad-signature codes are unauthenticated and free, and while dedupe stops a stranger holding a fingerprint down, it does not stop them emptying the six-token burst at a moment of their choosing — "12 other alerts were dropped" gave the operator no way to learn that one of them was `postcall_pipeline_stalled`. |
| D-156 | **Key moments in a call — precomputed once at storage time, and NOT embedded in the audio** | `apps/workers/moments.py` derives markers from what the post-call pipeline already holds: each extracted value anchored to the EARLIEST transcript turn whose text contains it (`start_ms`, persisted since the pipeline was written and read by nothing until now), plus the opt-out detector's `turn_idx`. Stored as a nullable `moments` JSONB on `call_extractions` (migration `f8c1d47a90e3`) — a column, not a table, because moments are 1:1 with a call and `call_extractions` already enforces that, so they inherit its FORCEd `tenant_isolation` policy instead of needing a second one. All three erasure statements in `workers/retention.py` clear `moments` with `data`. `CallDetailOut.moments` is REQUIRED on the wire and its `label` moves on the same raw/redacted switch as the transcript. `merge_moments` reserves room for model-authored highlights and caps only the model half. | **The audio is the wrong place and the research says so.** ID3v2 `CHAP`/`CTOC` is a real standard that Apple Music, Spotify and foobar2000 all ignore, and browsers do not read at all; WebVTT `<track kind="chapters">` is spec'd but every major browser ignores `<track>` entirely on an `<audio>` element. So embedding buys nothing for our own player while costing a container rewrite, a stored copy that is no longer byte-faithful to the vendor's, and — worst — chapter titles quoting the caller INSIDE an object that can only be deleted, never redacted (hard rules 5/6). **Cost is genuinely zero, for a reason worth stating precisely**: D-35 verified from the live rate card that Sarvam's LLM is free per token and that *the binding constraint is RATE LIMIT, not price*, so the free design is one that adds no REQUEST — the derived half adds no model call at all, and the model half must ride the extraction call that already runs. **The derived half exists because it cannot be wrong**: `anchor_of` returns a turn's own offset or None, never an approximation, and no fuzzy matching — a panel whose timestamps are sometimes right teaches people to distrust the ones that are exact. There is exactly ONE model kind (`highlight`) rather than a taxonomy, because D-36 records Sarvam's Telugu extraction quality as UNMEASURED and rendering an unscored classification as fact is a claim we cannot support until task #87 runs. |
| D-157 | **Hard rule 2 was a claim about imports, and the archive its erasure arm guards had no writer — both are now measurements** | `ExecutionSnapshot.raw_document: bytes | None` carries the vendor's own document out of `get_execution` (`apps/api/engine/document.engine_document`, capped at `MAX_ENGINE_DOCUMENT_BYTES` = 1 MiB, refused rather than truncated), and `pipeline._archive_engine_document` is the production caller `storage.archive_payload` never had: it commits `calls.engine_payload_ref` and THEN PUTs, which is the order D-126 made a contract. `archive_payload` becomes `async` over `asyncio.to_thread` and takes `document: bytes` instead of `payload: dict`. `ExecutionListing.complete` loses its default and gains a validator: an incomplete listing must name a reason, a complete one may not carry one. The fake engine now reports `page_cap_reached` for a truncation it can PROVE. `tests/engine_audit_test.py` grows a repo-wide AST scan for vendor keys, a classify-every-adapter-key clause, a stale-ban clause, an allowlist for the receiver, and a driven hard-rule-6 measurement; the conformance suite grows the archive clause and two saboteurs; `tests/engine_payload_archive_test.py` is new. No migration and no schema change — the column has existed since `05bba2f3c19c`. | **The import-linter contract proves nothing about a dict key.** It forbids naming a vendor adapter in an import; `payload["telephony_data"]` needs no import, arrives in a worker, and is invisible to it. Measured instead: **no shipped module outside `apps/api/engine/` reads a vendor-only key today** — every hit in the tree is a test stub building a vendor payload on purpose — and the scan now keeps it that way. The previous check existed but was scoped to two receiver files and banned four words (`transcript`, `recording_url`, `from_number`, `to_number`) that are also OURS, so it could not be widened; the receiver gets a positive allowlist instead, which also catches `duration_s` — our own word, and just as wrong there. **The archive's producer is the seam D-126 left open by design** ("the arm is built before the producer, because after the producer the unreachable objects already do"), and the type is the whole design: a `dict` would have handed a worker the vendor's field names, so what crosses is bytes, `exclude=True` so no `model_dump` into a span or a job payload can carry a caller's number, `repr=False` so no exception message can. **`complete: bool = True` was the defect its own docstring warned about** — "never the fallback for 'we did not look'" — since `ExecutionListing(snapshots=rows)` asserted it by omission; `EngineCapabilities` had already settled that doctrine three fields at a time. **Twenty-one sabotages, twenty-one distinct signatures, and one of them found a hole in a clause I had just written**: the Cartesia adapter archiving its own `ExecutionSnapshot` dump passed the new conformance clause, because the document still parsed and still differed per call — only a structural check (its keys are not a subset of our model's fields) catches it, and that check had been in the pipeline test instead of the contract, where exactly one adapter was covered by it. Ten adapter-file sabotages across bolna/cartesia/fake were each caught by the suite. **Not done, and named**: the rest of `workers/storage.py` is still synchronous boto3 called from async workers (`store_delivery_body`, `keys_under`, `delete_objects`, `copy_recording`'s PUT) — a real defect, not this slice's, because moving it touches `retention`, `outbound_webhooks` and two route modules at once; and the archive still has no `retention_policies` category, which SECURITY-COMPLIANCE §4 reserves as a DPA commitment plus a documented-enum change. |
| D-158 | **Our knowledge base and Bolna's copy only ever agreed at publish time, and a knowledge base can go months between publishes** | `apps/workers/kb_reconciliation.sweep_kb_drift` — hourly at :23, 15 agents a tick, stalest first — reads `list_kb(agent_ref)` back for each live agent and compares it with `kb.service.recorded_handles_of_agent`, the SAME set the publish gate consults. Verdicts land on `engine_agent_routes.kb_drift_state/_checked_at/_detected_at` (migration a7c31e05b8d4) and ride `GET /v1/ops/platform` as `kb_drift`, beside `engine_drift`. Six values: `in_sync`, `unaccounted`, `missing`, `divergent`, `unreadable`, `unreachable`. | `_reconcile_engine_state` asks "is the engine holding a copy no row of ours names" ONLY on the publish path, and a KB is the object here with the longest gap between writes — a client pastes a price list once and does not touch it for months, which is exactly how long a vendor-console edit or a publish that committed at Bolna and rolled back here stayed invisible. **BOTH DIRECTIONS**: an engine copy we cannot account for (the agent reads unapproved text to callers under the client's own PE registration — the FLOWS §7 gate bypassed entirely) and a handle we recorded that the engine no longer lists (the client silently lost knowledge, and the next publish dies on `kb_detach_failed`). **IT IS A READ**, D-121's argument with more force: the repair a KB drift invites is `detach_kb`, an irreversible DELETE at the vendor of a document our tables by hypothesis cannot describe — unattended, that destroys the only copy of text somebody added by hand. Output is a record plus an alert; `test_the_sweep_only_ever_reads` pins the call inventory at `['list_kb']`. **THE HONEST LIMIT**: an empty listing is ambiguous between "the documents are gone" and "the vendor's KB list does not carry the `agent_id` the adapter filters on" — pilot gate 8's `kb_list_carries_agent_linkage`, OPEN, and an external blocker (a Bolna account nobody has used against `/knowledgebase/all`). So an empty listing for an agent we believe holds documents is `unreadable` unless another agent in the same tick listed something, which is the positive control `scripts/pilot/knowledge.py` uses; reporting it as `missing` would put a fleet-wide false alarm on a schedule. **BOUNDS** are 15/tick (not 25 — `bolna.list_kb` pulls the WHOLE account listing per agent), 180s of wall clock, and hourly rather than half-hourly, with the tick's worst case asserted at import to fit inside its interval so no Redis lease is needed. Separate columns from D-123's because `drift_checked_at` IS that sweep's work queue and sharing it would make each sweep starve the other. The sweep takes `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock(kb:publish:<agent>)` around a read-verify-read bracket: the detach-then-attach ordering guarantees a window where the engine holds fewer documents than our rows record, so without it every routine price-list update would be reported as a divergence. `max_tries` explicit at the `cron()` call site (arq defaults it to 1). |
| D-159 | **Every object-store call in the tree now leaves the event loop, and a scan keeps it that way** | `store_delivery_body`, `read_delivery_body`, `keys_under`, `delete_objects` and `copy_recording`'s upload all move onto `asyncio.to_thread`, the pattern `archive_payload` established in D-157. Nine call sites follow: `integrations/routes.py` (one), `workers/retention.py` (five), `workers/outbound_webhooks.py` (one, which makes `_retain_body` a coroutine), and two test fixtures. `keys_under` runs its WHOLE pagination in one hop rather than one hop per page, and `delete_objects` all its chunks, because both already contract to be re-driven as a unit on failure. `presigned_url` stays synchronous and is named in the guard's allowlist: S3 presigning is a local HMAC with no round trip. `tests/storage_event_loop_test.py` is new. | **D-157 named this as the defect it was not fixing** — "the rest of `workers/storage.py` is still synchronous boto3 called from async workers" — and the worst instance was not in a worker at all: `read_delivery_body` is reached from a FastAPI request handler, so a synchronous `get_object` froze the API loop for EVERY tenant's request, not one job. arq's worker is one loop across all jobs, so the rest stalled siblings. **The test file is two halves that fail for different reasons.** Five behavioural tests run a store that sleeps SYNCHRONOUSLY (an `asyncio.sleep` fake would yield and pass against the very bug under test) while a witness coroutine counts how often the loop got to run: a threaded call yields ~20 ticks, a blocking one yields 0. Two structural tests are AST scans — every `_client()` call must sit inside a function handed to `to_thread`, and every network-touching function must be `async def` — and they exist because the behavioural half can only cover functions somebody remembers to add, which is exactly how four functions kept the old shape after D-157 fixed one. **Three sabotages, three distinct signature sets**: reverting the read reddened both halves at `0 >= 5` and named the line; reverting `keys_under` reddened three clauses including "not `async def`, so a caller cannot tell from the call site that a network round trip happens here"; and calling an existing `_delete()` helper directly instead of through `to_thread` — the regression that LOOKS correct — was still caught by the scan. **Rejected: aioboto3.** A second object-store client library for a property `to_thread` already delivers, on a path measured in a handful of calls per call record. |
| D-160 | **Alert suppression and the alert budget were per PROCESS, and production has run multiple processes since D-55** | `apps/api/core/alert_admission.py` moves the whole admission decision — repeat window, per-fingerprint suppression counts, and the token bucket — into ONE atomic Redis Lua script keyed by SERVICE. `alerting`'s module globals stay as a per-process pre-filter whose only remaining job is keeping one process from flooding its own bounded queue. Called from `_drain`, never from `alert()`, so the caller's thread still does nothing but a log record and a `put_nowait`. `_forget` gains its shared twin. `tests/alert_multiprocess_test.py` proves the property with REAL subprocesses; `tests/voice_runtime_event_loop_test.py` pins the two deployment facts the ack budget rests on. | **This was shipped, not hypothetical.** `compose.prod.yml` runs `--workers=${VOICE_RUNTIME_WORKERS:-4}` and `--workers=${API_WORKERS:-2}` — uvicorn workers are PROCESSES — so production had four independent 15-minute windows and, worse, four independent token buckets: a deliberate `ALERT_BUDGET_PER_HOUR` of 20 was silently 80. The duplicate pages are the visible half; the multiplied budget is the half an inbox cannot diagnose, because the symptom is "the rate limit does not seem to work" rather than an error. **FAIL OPEN, ABSOLUTELY**, because `alerting`'s docstring makes a load-bearing promise that the alert path survives the failures it reports — half these codes fire when infrastructure breaks. Unreachable, slow or unreadable Redis all return send, so an outage costs deduplication and never an alarm; the docstring was corrected rather than left saying "touches no Redis". **The clock is the caller's wall clock, not `redis.call('TIME')`** — TIME is the right tool for skewed hosts and the wrong one here, where every sharer is a `--workers=N` sibling on one VPS (D-25/D-26) and TIME buys nothing while costing a window no test can hold still. The marker STORES its instant and the window is decided by comparing it; the TTL is garbage collection only, because deciding by `EXISTS` would govern one window with two clocks. **uvloop needed no install** — `uvicorn[standard]` already ships it and `--loop auto` selects it — but nothing asserted either, and D-55's 250 acks/s was measured WITH uvloop, so trimming the extra (which also pulls httptools/watchfiles/websockets this service does not want) would have silently invalidated the whole worker table. Five sabotages, five distinct signature sets; the per-process revert reddened with "4 of 4 worker processes delivered the same alert" and "10 distinct alerts against a service burst of 6", and dropping the shared `_forget` was caught by a test that already existed. |
| D-161 | **A marketing homepage with real motion, and not one new claim** | `app/page.tsx` is rebuilt as a full landing page — hero, animated call-to-row figure, capabilities, the Indian-compliance section, a data section and the two doors — with `components/marketing/motion.tsx` (Lenis smooth scroll + GSAP `useGSAP`/ScrollTrigger) and `components/marketing/callDemo.tsx`. Three dependencies added: `gsap@3.15.0`, `@gsap/react@2.1.2`, `lenis@1.3.26`. `globals.css` gains a `:has([data-marketing-root])` override so the document can scroll. | **The honesty doctrine survived the redesign, and its guard proved it three times.** `publicLanding.test.tsx` asserts almost entirely NEGATIVE properties — no price, no customer count, no uptime or accuracy figure, no self-serve door when the flag is off — and it caught three real regressions in the new page: a rupee figure in the demo dialogue, the word `free` in a line of dialogue, and, most instructively, MY OWN disclaimer caption "not a measurement of accuracy" tripping the ban on that word. All three were reworded; the guard was not widened. **MOTION IS AN ENHANCEMENT AND THE PAGE IS FINISHED WITHOUT IT**: content renders visible and GSAP animates FROM a displaced state, so a failed bundle, a crawler, or `prefers-reduced-motion` all yield the same readable page — the `opacity: 0`-in-CSS alternative ships a permanently blank page when JS fails. **Supply chain, against hard rule 9 and the July 2025 incident**: all three packages have ZERO install-time lifecycle scripts and ZERO runtime dependencies, so pnpm never prompts and `allowBuilds` is untouched — verified byte-for-byte, since the incident's kill chain was a `postinstall` that pnpm correctly blocked and a human then allowed. **The dashboard pays nothing**: measured at build, `/` is 228 kB first load and `/c/[slug]/calls` is unchanged at 187 kB, because the libraries are imported only by client components under `components/marketing`. **Scroll ownership** is the one global change: `html, body { overflow: hidden }` is load-bearing for the `fixed inset-0` shells, so rather than relax it the override is scoped by `:has()` on the marketing root and is structurally unable to reach /c or /admin. **A jsdom `matchMedia` stub was needed** for the reduced-motion read, and adding it made the axe sweep MORE capable — it immediately surfaced a genuine pre-existing `heading-order` defect (an `<h4>` inside a `Card` whose title is an `<h2>`) on the admin tenant page, fixed in markup rather than exempted. |
| D-162 | **`outbox_messages.queue` read as routing and routed nothing; step 1 of a two-step retirement** | `enqueue_outbox` loses its `queue=` keyword and writes the module constant `reliability.service.OUTBOX_FLEET` (`"default"`). Six call sites drop the argument; `TENANT_ERASURE_QUEUE` and `DELETION_QUEUE` — two constants whose only value was `"default"` — are deleted. The COLUMN stays (hard rule 8 forbids dropping in the release that stops writing it) and `claim_outbox_batch` still selects it, so a second fleet has something to filter on. | **It was a second encoding of a fact already on the row.** Callers passed `"notifications"` or `"default"` as a pure function of `job`, `dispatch_outbox` published WITHOUT the value, and `WorkerSettings` sets no `queue_name` — so every message landed on arq's single default queue whatever the column said. The hazard is comprehension, not capacity: a column that reads as routing makes an operator believe notifications are isolated from CRM deliveries when the two are sharing one worker's ten slots, and that belief costs most on the night it matters. **HONOURING IT WAS THE OTHER FORK AND IS REFUSED FOR NOW.** arq routes by `enqueue_job(_queue_name=...)` consumed by a worker whose `queue_name` matches, and one worker consumes exactly one queue — so honouring the column means a SECOND DEPLOYABLE (container, deploy step, CI job) for a platform with no clients, against "monolith module before new service", and passing `_queue_name` today with no worker on that queue would silently stop every notification. **What closes this entry**: either the column is dropped in a later release, or a second worker fleet arrives and `dispatch_outbox` starts passing the value it already reads. Whichever happens, the code no longer claims the second one is already true. |
| D-163 | **The AI disclosure and the recording notice stop sharing a column, and both become per-agent toggles — while the truthful ANSWER becomes unfalsifiable** | Migration `f4a1d0b6e29c` splits `agents.disclosure_line` into `ai_disclosure_line` + `recording_notice_line` (both NOT NULL, non-blank) and adds `ai_disclosure_enabled` + `recording_notice_enabled` (NOT NULL, default TRUE) on inbound and outbound agents alike. `calevate_shared.engine.compose_opening_line` is the ONE producer of `AgentConfig.opening_line`, which may now legitimately be EMPTY. **The half that is not negotiable moved out of reach at the same time**: `TRUTHFUL_ANSWER_DIRECTIVE` is a `Final` in the portability contract with no writer anywhere, `compose_engine_prompt` appends it AFTER the client's script on every adapter with an explicit precedence sentence, `verification.judge` scores `TRUTHFUL_ANSWER_MARKER` on the read-back and REFUSES a publish that lost it, the half-hourly drift sweep alarms if it disappears later, the conformance suite requires it of every adapter, and `check_compliance_invariants` §6 fails the build if it ever becomes a field, a parameter or a second binding. Client-facing switch is `PATCH /v1/agents/{agent_id}/disclosure` (`org:manage` — the client owner's, which no admin or impersonating session holds against a tenant, D-22), applying immediately with an in-transaction republish; every flip writes an `audit_log` row whose ACTION names the toggle and the direction, because `write_audit` does not persist summaries and "who switched this off" must survive in the hash-chained ledger. **No `consent_ledger` row**: that register holds a data principal's consent, and a Fiduciary changing its own notice practice is not a caller consenting to anything. | **WHY.** SEC-COMP §2 has always stated two invariants under two regimes — AI identification (TRAI/UCC) and a recording notice (DPDP §5/§6 notice-and-consent) — and one column made them one switch, so a client could have both or neither. The seeded Telugu line is the defect written down: `"Namaskaram, idi {business} AI assistant. Ee call record avutundi."` is two obligations in one `TEXT NOT NULL`. Competitively, `docs/evidence/outpero-teardown-aug2026.md:276` records that Outpero makes disclosure optional and pushes the legal obligation to the client (AUP §4); the founder's decision is to match the flexibility and NOT the honesty gap — ours is the only one of the two where the agent cannot be scripted into claiming to be human. **THE RISK ACCEPTED, WITH DATES AND SOURCES (searched 17 Aug 2026; the primary PDFs are unreachable from the build environment, so these are search-summary citations and are marked as such, per the `GEMINI_MODEL_CONFIRMED_IN_REGION` doctrine).** (a) TRAI has **not notified** an AI-disclosure mandate as of this date: the 13 Mar 2026 draft Third Amendment to the TCCCPR (`trai.gov.in/sites/default/files/2026-03/Draft_CP_13032026_0.pdf`) mandates AI/ML spam detection by ACCESS PROVIDERS, and a mandatory AI-disclosure-at-call-start for senders is the widely reported likely OUTCOME of the accompanying consultation, not current law. If and when it is notified, `ai_disclosure_enabled` must be forced TRUE for `promotional`/commercial traffic and this entry is superseded. (b) The "₹10 lakh penalty" figure that circulates with these rules is the TCCCPR financial disincentive on **access providers** for misreporting UCC counts (₹2/5/10 lakh by instance, Feb 2025 amendment, PIB PRID 2102413) — it is not a telemarketer penalty; a telemarketer's real exposure is TSP enforcement (usage suspension at 5 complaints in 10 days, blacklisting on repeat) and the client PE's own liability. (c) The recording notice is DPDP, not the IT Act: a participant recording their own call is the *R. M. Malkani* (1973) one-party baseline, so switching the in-call notice off moves the client's DPDP §5 notice obligation to their own privacy notice — it does not discharge it, and the DPA must say so. (d) MeitY's IT Amendment Rules 2026 (G.S.R. 120(E), notified 10 Feb 2026, in force 20 Feb 2026) require labelling of synthetically generated information but bind **intermediaries**, not a telemarketer placing a call — considered and not engaged. **WHAT IT COSTS.** "We disclose everywhere anyway" stops being true by construction, so the EU AI Act Art. 50 row and the DPA both become conditional on a client's setting; `calls.disclosure_played` reads NULL rather than `true` for agents with the notice off, which thins the QA evidence for those clients; and the legacy `agents.disclosure_line` is now written and not read on the publish path — **step 1 of a two-step deprecation (hard rule 8)**. **WHAT CLOSES / REVERSES IT.** Step 2 is dropping `disclosure_line` in a later release. The DECISION is reversed by TRAI notifying the mandate (force the AI toggle on for commercial traffic) or by counsel ruling that the in-call recording notice cannot be moved to the client's own notice. Neither touches §2.0: the truthful answer is not part of what is toggled and there is no version of this entry that makes it so. |

| D-164 | **The erasure certificate discloses the backup window, closing SEC-COMP §4's open question** | `ERASURE_LIMITATIONS` gains a ninth clause and `ERASURE_EXCEPTIONS` its index-aligned `backup` entry, with `BACKUP_WINDOW_DAYS = 35` as the single constant and a test pinning it across three places — `infra/backup/README.md` (where the number is actually decided), the published DPA at `/legal/dpa`, and the certificate. Outcome word is `expires_with_backup`, deliberately not `retained_as_record`. The clause states the window, says backups are never searched or edited to remove one person, and says the erasure is re-applied on restore, which is already a mandatory step in `runbooks/database-restore.md`. | **WHY.** SEC-COMP §4 had reserved this as a legal call — "adding a sentence to a notice that clients hand to data principals is a commitment, not a code change" — and that framing was right when it was written. It stopped being the live question the moment the client DPA published the 35-day window: the commitment was then already made IN WRITING to the controller, while the certificate forwarded to the *data principal* still omitted it. Disclosing to the client and withholding from the subject is the wrong way round, and the legal audit recorded the asymmetry as F-7 (`docs/LEGAL-SURFACE.md`). Closing it added no new promise — it matched one already given. **WHAT IT COSTS.** The certificate now names a number that must track the ops runbook; the test is what stops those drifting, and it fails until all three agree. The WORDING is still counsel's — the whole `/legal` set carries `{{PENDING LEGAL REVIEW}}` for the same reason — and if counsel rewrites the clause, this entry stands: what was decided is that the fact is disclosed, not the sentence that discloses it. **WHAT REVERSES IT.** Counsel advising that a backup-retention clause misstates our position under DPDP §8(7), or the backup window changing — the latter is a one-constant change plus the runbook, which the test enforces jointly. |
| D-165 ⚠SUPERSEDES D-37's authentication leg; D-37's "our Postgres is the system of record" half STANDS and is what makes this cheap | **Clerk is replaced by a first-party auth module we own — designed in full, proved by a slice, cut over behind a flag.** (The number skips two: D-163 and D-164 were both claimed by branches in flight while this was being designed, and two rows sharing one id make every citation of it ambiguous.) | `docs/AUTH-MIGRATION.md` is the design AND the acceptance criteria — a 26-row capability inventory (§1) naming every behaviour Clerk performs today, where it moves to, and the test that proves it. Shipped with it: migration `e9a4c1d70b52` creating `auth_credentials` + `auth_sessions`, both platform-level (identity crosses tenants) and both FORCE-RLS'd **deny-by-default** under `app.auth`, a GUC only `db/session.credential_session()` sets — so a tenant session, an admin session, a user session, an invite session and a bare untenanted session all see zero rows. `apps/api/authn/`: Argon2id (`m=19456,t=2,p=1`, OWASP's second listed configuration) over an HKDF-derived pepper, and opaque server-side sessions with issue / verify / rotate / revoke, family-wide reuse detection, and per-realm idle+absolute bounds. `verify_session` OWNS its transaction — reuse detection's family revocation is the write the refusal would otherwise roll back, and the second-session fix would be a pool-exhaustion lever on a branch an attacker reaches on demand. 49 tests at 100% statement and branch coverage of the new package, with a negative control per security property, the reuse-durability one driven red against a deliberate rollback before it was kept. **Mounted on no router**, deliberately. | **RESIDENCY IS THE DRIVER AND IT IS NOT A LEGAL VIOLATION BEING CLOSED — say the smaller true thing.** Hosting is moving to an Indian VPS, and SEC-COMP §4 can already claim every model endpoint is pinned to an Indian region because a guardrail fails the build otherwise. Clerk is the hole in that sentence: identity data — every operator's and every client user's email, name, sign-in events, addresses — sits outside India. DPDP §16 permits transfer except where restricted, so Clerk is lawful; what it is, is a named foreign sub-processor in every client DPA whose incident is our reportable incident, while the rest of the stack is being pinned. Removing it shortens the disclosure and makes a DPDP erasure one database instead of two. **THE LANGUAGE CONSTRAINT IS THE DESIGN CONSTRAINT.** The prior implementation to learn from (raghava-organics, ~8,200 lines) is Node/TypeScript and CLAUDE.md forbids a second backend language, so it is a REQUIREMENTS CHECKLIST, not a drop-in: its frontend hooks port nearly verbatim and its reuse-detection design is adopted, while its `bcryptjs` (two different cost factors, and bcrypt over 256-bit refresh tokens in a per-row loop) is refused with a citation rather than by reflex. **Opaque sessions over JWTs** because revocation must bite on the next request and a denylist is a session table with worse failure modes. **The pepper is HKDF'd from `PLATFORM_KEK`** because the two obvious homes both fail OWASP's "not stored with the hash" test — `platform_secrets` is the same database, and a ninth bootstrap env var breaks the stated rule of `.env.example`. **The realm boundary is the dangerous part** and gets four independent mechanisms, of which one is structural: the realm is inside the token's hash domain, so cross-realm confusion is arithmetic rather than a WHERE clause somebody remembers. **WHAT WOULD REVERSE THIS**: hosting NOT moving to India, or a Clerk India-resident data-residency tier under contract — either removes the driver and leaves only cost, which at zero tenants does not justify owning credential storage. **WHAT BLOCKS IT**: an email transport account (external), the founder's answers to AUTH-MIGRATION §9, and the nginx hostname split, which was "worth doing" under Clerk's JWKS separation and is a PREREQUISITE under cookies. **WHAT WE LOSE AND ARE NOT PRETENDING OTHERWISE**: bot detection, breached-password checks, device management and SOC 2 on the identity layer (§1 C-23…C-25, §7). |
| D-166 | **Restore-drill evidence EXPIRES, and the check that says so is structurally incapable of producing it** | `scripts/check_drill_freshness.py` reads `docs/evidence/restore-drill-<YYYY>-Q<N>.md` — runbook §9's quarterly record — and refuses when the newest one is more than `MAX_QUARTERS_BEHIND = 1` quarters old, is post-dated, carries the template's unfilled `**PASS \| PARTIAL \| FAIL**` line, has no verdict, or records **FAIL**. It is in `make guardrails` and in CI. **The second property is the finding this entry exists for and it is enforced three ways**: the module may import only `ALLOWED_IMPORTS` (no `os`, `shutil`, `subprocess`, `tempfile`, and no path to `scripts.restore_drill`); it may call nothing in `FORBIDDEN_CALLS`; and `check_this_module_cannot_write` walks the file's own AST on every run, with `tests/drill_freshness_guard_test.py` re-deriving the import rule INDEPENDENTLY, planting writers to prove the audit sees them, and snapshotting `docs/evidence/` around a real run. The freshness clock is read off the FILENAME, never an mtime. `restore-drill-local-*.md` — what `make restore-drill` writes into the same directory — is counted, named, and never evidence. Today's verdict is **NOT RUN** (exit 0): no quarterly record exists, so nothing has expired, and the check prints that sentence plus "OPERATIONS §8's backups verified is untick" on every single build rather than passing quietly. | **WHY.** OPERATIONS §6 schedules the drill quarterly and §8 makes a committed record the evidence behind "backups verified"; nothing read those records, so a Q1 PASS would have gone on reading as proof in Q4, about infrastructure since rebuilt and re-credentialed. **AND WHY THE SECOND PROPERTY OUTRANKS THE FIRST.** The reference implementation this idea came from (`docs/evidence/raghava-deploy-teardown.md`) has the expiry check and not the property: `dr-stale-drill-check.js` demands a "production-like" artifact, `dr-ephemeral-pack.js:35-45` writes `status: 'ok'` into that artifact while touching no database, and their CI runs generator then validator in one job — a gate that cannot fail, leaving behind a file that reads as proof a drill happened. Ours is the same idea with the loop cut: the producer (`scripts/restore_drill.py` + a human on real infrastructure) is a different program, and the validator is built so that making it the producer is a diff a reviewer sees. **WHAT IT COSTS.** A guardrail whose green state today is "NOT RUN", which is a state somebody has to keep reading; the mitigation is that the line is printed unconditionally and names the untick gate. And a filename convention is now load-bearing: a quarterly record misnamed is reported, not silently ignored. **WHAT CLOSES IT.** The first quarterly drill — blocked outside this repo on a scratch host, an R2/offsite credential and the age identity (runbook §1), which is the same blocker `infra/backup/README.md` §9 already carries. **WHAT REVERSES IT.** A change to the ops calendar's cadence, which moves one constant; or a decision that PARTIAL should not reset the clock, which moves `ACCEPTED_VERDICTS`. Neither may be resolved by letting the check write. |
| D-167 | **The deploy pipeline's four host-side gaps close together: a disk ladder, a certificate reload hook, a daily hygiene timer, and one argument-free root grant** | (1) `scripts/deploy/docker-reclaim.sh` replaces the deploy's single 3GB refusal with an escalation — tier 0 always (our compose project's stopped containers, dangling images, build cache capped at 3GB), then below `RECLAIM_PURGE_FLOOR_GB` = 8, in order and stopping at the first rung that clears the floor: whole build cache -> per-commit images beyond the newest -> every unreferenced image; below `RECLAIM_REFUSE_FLOOR_GB` = 3 it refuses, before anything is built, migrated or swapped. (2) `--deploy-hook "systemctl reload nginx"` moves into the `certonly` command itself in DEPLOYMENT §9.5a step 3, with step 5 stating why and two pass conditions. (3) `infra/hygiene/` + `scripts/deploy/host-hygiene.sh`: a systemd timer, unprivileged, idempotent, holding the deploy's `flock`. (4) `infra/privileged/`: `calevate ALL=(root) NOPASSWD: /usr/local/sbin/calevate-nginx-apply ""` and nothing else; `install_nginx` loses all six of its self-composed `sudo` commands and stages into a fixed directory the root script validates. DEPLOYMENT gains §11 and renumbers §4's sequence (a step was inserted at 5). | **Each one is a defect the reference host already paid for, read off `docs/evidence/raghava-deploy-teardown.md` §9 rows 3, 15, 21 and 22.** (1) Their own comment records the deadlock — a post-build prune only runs after a SUCCESSFUL build, so a near-full disk kills the build and wedges every later deploy; ours walked into it from the other side, refusing at 3GB and printing commands for a human. **The rung ORDER is the design**: their version reaches for `image prune --all` first, throwing away the rollback artefact to save a build cache, which is exactly backwards on the day it matters. 8 is kept from their host because it is the only one of the two floors ever observed against a real build; ours has been run on no host at all. (2) `certonly` never touches nginx — that is why §5 forbids `--nginx` — so without a hook a day-60 renewal writes a new certificate that nginx never reads, and the fuse ends 30 days later on a live edge. `--deploy-hook` rather than `renewal-hooks/deploy/` because it fires once per renewed lineage and we have exactly one lineage covering four names; **a plain `certbot renew --dry-run` does not run deploy hooks**, so the pass condition is `--run-deploy-hooks` (present in certbot 5.7.0, absent in 2.6.0 — the introducing release is NOT verified here) or a hand reload. (3) Their cleanup runs from `/etc/cron.daily`, deletes the runner's `_work` under a job in flight, and prunes host-globally on a multi-project host. Ours does none of the three, and the lock is `flock` rather than a pid file because the pid file's cleanup is precisely what does not happen when an OOM takes `next build`. The journal is CAPPED in journald rather than vacuumed daily, which is what removes the last privileged step and lets the timer run as the deploy account. (4) Their grant `NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/rm -rf /var/lib/docker/containers/*` is unrestricted root deletion of any path, because sudo matches arguments as one concatenated string and the wildcard spans `/` and words — held by the account that runs every merged pull request. Inverting it cost the deploy its six privileged commands: the whole backup/test/restore dance moved into the root script, where it belongs, and the deploy's `sudo -n true` probe had to become `sudo -n -l <path>` because a correct policy REFUSES `true` — the probe would have failed a deploy for being configured properly. **WHAT IS UNVERIFIED**: none of this has run on a host (external blockers: a Hostinger VPS, root on it, a domain and issued certificates — teardown §9.1 items 1, 2, 4, 9), `systemd-analyze verify` and `visudo -c` have never been run against these files, and the `--reserved-space` / `--keep-storage` question is answered by asking the binary rather than by pinning a flag that is wrong on some engine either way (moby/moby#50120). **WHAT REVERSES IT**: a host with a different disk moves the two floors, which are environment-overridable constants; nothing else here is a preference. |
| D-168 | **A deploy can now be refused for what its environment SAYS, not only for which keys it has** | `scripts/check_deploy_env.py` is a new preflight that reads the environment the new image will actually run with and reports EVERY problem at once, never a value: the two DSNs must name one database on one host through TWO ROLES; `REDIS_URL` and both DSNs must name hosts a container can reach (`host.docker.internal` per D-26, `redis` by service name); `PLATFORM_KEK` is checked by calling `envelope.build_ring`; a retired key equal to its active generation is refused; the three HMAC secrets must be pairwise distinct and at least `MIN_HMAC_KEY_BYTES`; `AUDIT_CHAIN_SECRET` may not be the constant this repository publishes (D-81); and any value still byte-identical to `.env.example`'s, or carrying placeholder text, is refused. Console-managed keys declared in the environment WARN rather than refuse. Wired at `vps-deploy.sh::verify_bootstrap_env`, in the new image, before migrations and before any swap. `check_env_parity` gains a fifth direction (`preflight_contract_failures`) so the gate can never demand a variable nothing reads, and the Dockerfile finally copies `scripts/` and `.env.example` into the image. | **WHY.** `docs/evidence/raghava-deploy-teardown.md` §2.3 found this to be the one place the other project is genuinely ahead of us: it refuses on `DATABASE_URL` vs `POSTGRES_DB`, on `REDIS_URL` not embedding `REDIS_PASSWORD`, on `JWT_REFRESH_SECRET === JWT_SECRET` and on leftover placeholder text, and we checked PRESENCE and nothing else. Every one of those failures produces a deploy that swaps cleanly and breaks afterwards, which is the class the ordering in §4 exists to prevent and the one thing that ordering could not see. **THEIR CHECKS ARE NOT TRANSLITERATED.** We have no `POSTGRES_DB` and no `REDIS_PASSWORD`; what we have is `DATABASE_URL`/`ALEMBIC_DATABASE_URL` (one database, two roles — hard rule 1's RLS rests on the app role being NOSUPERUSER NOBYPASSRLS) and a redis with no published port, so the equivalent refusals are agreement between the DSNs and reachability from inside a container. Their half-configured-integration refusal is deliberately NOT copied: ours live encrypted in the console store (D-95), so an absent vendor key is what a CORRECT host looks like and `/healthz/ready` already owns that question. **THE SEAM WAS BROKEN BEFORE THIS AND NOBODY COULD HAVE KNOWN**: `vps-deploy.sh` already invoked `python -m scripts.deploy_revision_check` and `python -m scripts.seed` against an image that has never contained `scripts/`, so both would have died with `No module named 'scripts'` on the first real deploy. **WHAT IT COSTS.** The host-side `grep`s in `preflight()` stay, because DEPLOYMENT §2 says Python is not on the host and a pre-build refusal is worth keeping — the split is by WHEN a question can be answered, not two implementations of one check, and both files say so. Every refusal is declared in `REFUSAL_CODES` and `tests/deploy_env_preflight_test.py` fails if one of them has no negative control, because a check that cannot fail is worse than no check. **WHAT REVERSES IT.** A topology change: containers reaching Postgres by some route other than `host.docker.internal`, or a redis published on a host port, would make the two reachability refusals wrong — and each is one named check with one test, not a rule spread through a shell script. **NOT VERIFIED IN AN IMAGE**: the container registry is unreachable from this build environment (403 on both ghcr.io and docker.io blobs), so the Dockerfile change is proven only to the extent that `.dockerignore` admits both paths, which was checked against a locally cached base image. |
| D-169 | **An observability readiness LADDER, and configuration validity is where it stops — reachability becomes OPERATIONS §2 gate 15** | `apps/api/core/observability.observability_readiness` returns a `ComponentReadiness` per component with three rungs — `skipped` (not configured: clean skip, printed rather than silent), `ready`, `misconfigured` (named, by field) — and `scripts/check_observability_ready.py` runs it in `make guardrails` and CI. **sentry**: DSN shape (scheme, public key, host, numeric project id, no query), `sentry_sdk.utils.Dsn` when the optional group is installed, the SDK's presence at all, and `RELEASE_VERSION` still `dev` off `local`. **otel**: endpoint absolute/http(s)/no query, not already ending in `/v1/traces` (the exporter appends it), sample ratio not 0.0, SDK + OTLP-HTTP exporter installed. **langfuse**: no settings field exists (D-49), so the rung asks the TREE — an import, a dependency or a `langfuse`-shaped field — and fails on arrival, because the v3 SDK is its own OpenTelemetry pipeline and a direct client is a second, UNFILTERED exporter carrying the extraction prompt. On top, two syntax-decidable rungs hold hard rule 6's export filters in place: `sentry_sdk.init`'s `before_send`/`before_breadcrumb`/`send_default_pii`/`max_request_body_size` and the absence of `traces_sample_rate`, and every span processor in `init_tracing` being fed `_RedactingSpanExporter`. The same predicates run at BOOT (`observability_component_misconfigured`, codes only, never values), and `init_observability` now survives a malformed DSN instead of dying on `BadDsn`. Exit 2 = refused, when no `Settings` can be built at all. | **WHY.** Sentry initialised whenever a DSN was present and the ops console accepts one (D-95/D-101, `sentry_dsn` is `on_restart`), but nothing anywhere verified that error reporting reached anything: a typo'd project id, a `sentry-sdk` left in its dependency GROUP, a collector endpoint pasted from the signal-specific env var, or a 0.0 sample ratio each produce a process that reports nothing and says nothing — and every one of them is type-valid, so `check_config_applies`' bounds cannot see them. The middle rung is the whole point; off is already loud and on-and-working needs nothing. Worse, a malformed DSN did not degrade at all: `sentry_sdk.init` raises `BadDsn`, only `ImportError` was caught, and one wrong character in a console field would have stopped voice-runtime booting — contradicting this module's own docstring on the machine whose job is answering a telephone. **WHY THE LINE IS DRAWN AT CONFIGURATION.** The reference's `otel-readiness-check.js` probes the collector with a HEAD request; we cannot and must not fake that from a build container, and a check that "verified" a vendor by parsing a string is exactly the silent premise D-31/D-32 exist for. So delivery — a Sentry event accepted, a collector answering, a human seeing either — is **gate 15** in OPERATIONS §2, beside gate 14, on our own accounts. **WHAT IT COSTS.** Two rungs read `observability.py` by AST, so moving that code moves this check with it; both say so loudly instead of passing when they cannot find their subject. And a green run means "nothing here can be shown to be broken", never "errors are arriving" — the check prints that distinction every run. **WHAT REVERSES IT.** Gate 15 passing does not retire the ladder (configuration drifts after a gate is ticked); restoring Langfuse needs its own entry and the OTLP export path, which is what the langfuse rung refuses without. |
| D-170 ⚠SUPERSEDES D-165's cutover framing and its TOTP leg | **The first-party auth module is finished, mounted and is now the ONLY authentication this product has — and its second factor is an emailed one-time code, not TOTP.** | Migration `b3d9f6a2c815` adds `auth_email_tokens` (verification / password-reset / invite / admin-bootstrap, purpose inside the hash domain) and `auth_otp_challenges` (six digits, ten minutes, five guesses on the row), both FORCE-RLS'd deny-by-default under `app.auth` exactly as `e9a4c1d70b52`'s pair; plus `users.email_verified_at`, `admin_users.email` (unique on `lower(email)`) and NOT NULL dropped from both `clerk_user_id` columns. `apps/api/authn/` gains `codes` (keyed hashing + code minting), `otp`, `tokens`, `subjects`, `throttle`, `cookies`, `service`, `invitations`, `bootstrap` and `routes` — **24 endpoints mounted** across `/v1/auth/admin/**` and `/v1/auth/client/**`, two independent routers from one factory whose realm is a closure constant rather than a runtime branch. `Settings.first_party_auth_enabled` defaults **TRUE** and is a kill switch, not a cutover flag. `apps/workers/auth_email.py` delivers every one-time secret through the existing outbox + transport, registered in `FUNCTIONS`. | **WHAT "MFA" MEANS HERE, because a reader will otherwise go looking for an authenticator app: a six-digit code emailed to the address on file, and nothing else.** TOTP, recovery codes, an encrypted `auth_mfa_secrets` table and a KEK-wrapped seed were all BUILT and then REMOVED on the founder's decision that the second factor should be "nothing more than entering an OTP" — removed rather than left unmounted, because a table nothing writes and a module nothing imports are the half-wired defect `check_wiring` exists to catch. **The honest cost, stated rather than discovered:** the strength of the admin realm's second factor is now the strength of the operator's mailbox. It stops a stolen password and does not stop a compromised email account, which a TOTP secret would. **What it buys:** one mechanism instead of three (secret storage, enrolment, recovery), no device to lose, no enrolment step between a bootstrap and a usable console — and it closes task #66, whose complaint was that admin MFA is called mandatory in two documents and exists nowhere. `MFA_REQUIRED_REALMS` is asserted equal to `core/auth.py`'s copy so the sign-in path and the verifier cannot disagree. **CLERK IS NOT A PEER AND IS NOT COEXISTING** — D-165 designed a flag-gated cutover beside a live vendor; the founder has since decided Clerk is removed entirely, so this is built as the system of record and the flag's remaining job is an incident switch. Clerk code paths are untouched and still green in this change; deleting them is the next slice, and what becomes dead is named in that slice's brief. **The five defects of the reference implementation are each designed out with a named test** (`tests/authn_codes_test.py`, `authn_enumeration_test.py`): bcrypt's 72-byte truncation (Argon2id, proved by two 128-character passwords sharing a 72-byte prefix), unsalted SHA-256 over a 6-digit OTP (HMAC under a `PLATFORM_KEK`-derived key that is not in this database, proved against a built rainbow table), `randomInt`'s exclusive maximum (the generator's image is asserted to include `999999` rather than sampled), a reset token outliving its account (one `Subject | None` resolver, no code path reads `users` directly), and `check-identifier`'s enumeration oracle (uniform status, body AND measured timing, with an unknown address consuming a real throttle budget against a derived pseudo-subject). **Two real bugs were found BY these tests and are worth recording**: `str.isdigit()` accepts non-ASCII digits, so a Devanagari code reached `hmac.compare_digest` and raised an unhandled 500 on an authentication route — in a Telugu-first product sold in India; and `created` is a reserved `LogRecord` attribute, so the bootstrap's own log line crashed it. **WHAT WOULD REVERSE THE OTP DECISION**: a client or a regulator requiring a phishing-resistant factor on the operator console, at which point TOTP (or WebAuthn, which is the better answer if it is being built fresh) is a migration written then. **WHAT IS STILL NOT BUILT**: the signed double-submit CSRF token (half of it is `apps/web`, out of scope; `Sec-Fetch-Site` + `Origin` + `SameSite=Strict` ship), breached-password and bot checks (D-165 §7 accepted these), and the frontend. |
| D-171 | **The first administrator arrives by emailed invite, and the bootstrap refuses to be a back door** | `scripts/bootstrap_admin.py` is rewritten: `--clerk-user-id` is DELETED and it takes `--email`, minting an `auth_email_tokens` row of purpose `admin_bootstrap` and mailing a single-use setup link through the existing transport. `apps/api/authn/bootstrap.py` holds the logic; `POST /v1/auth/admin/bootstrap/confirm` is the redemption half, declared on the admin router only so the client realm answers 404 rather than refusing. **TTL is 60 minutes**, a named constant in `tokens.TOKEN_LIFETIMES`. | **WHY A SCRIPT AND NOT A ROUTE, still**: a bootstrap ENDPOINT is a route that exists to be unauthenticated exactly once — a window an attacker races on a public host, and permanent code whose whole value is being unreachable. Whoever runs the deploy already holds database credentials. The REDEMPTION half is a route and is not the same hazard: it needs a 256-bit single-use token mailed to an address the deployer named. **THE TTL IS ARGUED, NOT INHERITED.** The reference implementation's `admin-newuser.mjs` uses `INVITE_TTL_MS = 10 * 60 * 1000`; ten minutes is defensible for an invite handed to somebody watching their inbox and wrong for a deployment bootstrap, where an expired link means NO WAY INTO THE PLATFORM at all — every admin route 403s until an operator exists. Sixty minutes covers a deploy window plus greylisting's classic 15-minute retry, matches what a password reset gets (both set a password on an existing account), and is bounded by the fact that an unconsumed link is the only way in, so one that outlives the deploy is a live path to the widest authority in the system. Re-running is the recovery and is safe by design. **IDEMPOTENCY IS "REFUSE", AND THERE IS DELIBERATELY NO `--force`.** Three states: no operator → create and mail; the named operator exists with no password → mail a FRESH link, retiring the previous one (a resend, not a second account — the reference's reactivation case); any operator anywhere already holds a password → refuse with `already_bootstrapped` (409). A flag that mints an unattached administrator from a shell is exactly the back door this is not, and it would be reachable by anyone who has ever held database credentials — a contractor, a restored backup, a compromised CI runner. The check and the insert share one transaction under `LOCK TABLE admin_users IN SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE MODE`, because the invariant is "zero rows match" and there is no row to compare-and-swap on. **NO PASSWORD IS PRINTED, GENERATED OR DEFAULTED.** The reference's `seed-admin.mjs` logs `Admin created: ${admin.email} / ${PASSWORD}` — a hard rule 6 violation on its face that `check_redaction_exposure` would fail the build on, and a fixed password in a repository is a backdoor with a changelog entry. What is printed is the LINK; locally `ConsoleTransport` also logs the mail, so a developer needs no special path and mail delivery failing is NOT fatal (the mail credentials are themselves stored by an operator, in the console — a bootstrap that required SMTP would be a chicken-and-egg). **AUDITED**: `auth.admin_bootstrapped` at mint (naming the address, through `redact_mapping`) and `auth.admin_bootstrap_completed` at redemption — the most privileged act in a deployment's life leaves a record of when and to whom. The redemption endpoint additionally refuses an account that ALREADY has a password, so a leaked link from a finished deploy opens nothing even before it expires. **WHAT CLOSES THE REMAINING GAP**: an email transport account (external blocker, named in AUTH-MIGRATION §9 Q2) — until one exists the link is delivered by the operator reading it off their own terminal. |
| D-172 | **Raw SQL must be assembled from text we typed, and where a fragment is passed in the CALLERS are read** | `scripts/check_raw_sql.py` (in `make guardrails` and CI) walks every `text(...)` in `apps/` and `packages/` — 493 statements — and requires the expression to be *literal-derived*: a constant, a name resolving to constants across module and package boundaries, an f-string/`%`/`.format()`/`.join()`/comprehension whose parts all are, a call to one of our own functions whose every `return` is, or a call to a declared identifier validator (`_identifier`). When it reaches a function PARAMETER it does not give up and does not wave it through: it finds every call site of that function in the tree and applies the same rule to the argument, recursively. `exec_driver_sql` and `literal_column` are refused by name so a second door cannot open. `tests/` and `alembic/` are out of scope, stated in the docstring with the reason. Negative controls in `tests/raw_sql_guard_test.py`, including a two-step control that pins what the validator buys: a runtime table name is clean WITH `_identifier()` and reported WITHOUT it. | **The premise was written down and enforced by nobody.** `billing/plans.py::plan_in_effect_sql` interpolates its `columns` and `at` arguments straight into a SELECT and its own docstring says *"a caller that passes a user string is writing an injection, and no caller does"* — a true sentence about today's six callers and a promise about every future one, kept by review alone. `db/transition.py` interpolates a table name and a status column, which is correct because it validates them, and nothing checked that the validation stayed. **The blast radius is what makes this worth an AST walk rather than a grep**: hand-written SQL is how most tenant-scoped access here is expressed, and it executes as a role that is `NOSUPERUSER NOBYPASSRLS`, so a successful injection sits inside a session whose `tenant_id` GUC decides visibility — one `SET LOCAL` from every tenant's leads. That is hard rule 1 failing, not one account leaking. **WHAT IT COST.** Four resolver features the tree actually needed — relative imports, tuple destructuring, `enumerate` indexes, and separating the ITERATION question from the INDEXING question on a dict, because `transition_status` builds `{":__t_from0": state}` and splices the keys while binding the values, and a single verdict for that dict is wrong in one direction or the other. Two call-name indexes cut the run from 23s to 2.5s. **WHAT IT DOES NOT DO**: no taint tracking from request models — the rule here is *stricter*, refusing all runtime values including safe ones, and a narrower rule would have to be right about where data comes from while this one only has to be right about what a literal is. `SPLICE_ALLOWANCES` is EMPTY and is meant to stay so; an entry is a standing promise to re-read every future caller, which is the promise this file exists because nobody keeps. |
| D-173 | **The unauthenticated surface is ENUMERATED, because a path prefix is default-open** | `scripts/check_public_routes.py` (in `make guardrails` and CI) takes the routes `core/rbac.assert_policy_registry_complete` skips — the ones matching a `PUBLIC_PREFIXES` entry — and asks five questions of the live app: every one is declared in `UNAUTHENTICATED_ROUTES` with a reason; every declaration matches a live exempt route (the registry may only shrink); every `PUBLIC_PREFIXES` entry covers at least one live route; every MUTATING exempt route names the credential it verifies instead of a session, and that symbol is present in the module serving it; and no exempt route carries `x-calevate-permission`, which would read as protection in the OpenAPI schema and the generated client while the registry never looks at it. Ten routes today. Negative controls in `tests/public_routes_guard_test.py`, including one that mounts a route on the REAL app under `/v1/auth/` and takes it away again, plus a partition test proving the guarded and public sets cover the route table exactly once each. | **The strongest check in the repo opens with `continue`.** The RBAC boot assertion is genuinely excellent — declared AND enforced, the permission must be real and granted to somebody, and it refuses to pass if it checked zero routes — and the first thing it does is skip everything under a prefix. That is fine for a prefix holding one route and dangerous for one about to hold a module: the first-party auth module — designed and landing in a parallel slice, with Clerk still the live authenticator in `apps/api/core/auth.py` on this branch — adds login, refresh, logout, password reset, OTP request/verify and invite setup under `/v1/auth/`, and every one of them would have been exempt on the day it was mounted with nothing in the tree able to notice. **The evidence for that being the real trajectory is in `docs/evidence/raghava-platform-teardown.md` §2**: the reference repo's `route-discipline-check.js` has exactly this shape and its exemption list reached twelve hand-commented entries, none of them ever re-validated against a live route, beside a customer-side rule that is default-OPEN by prefix on the half holding customer PII. **WHAT IT COSTS.** Opening a route now costs a reviewed line instead of nothing, which is the entire point and is also the only thing anyone will feel. **WHAT IT DELIBERATELY IS NOT**: not a second implementation of the RBAC rule (one way per problem — the two partition the table and the test above proves it), not a judgement on whether a credential is STRONG (that is the module's own security tests; a static check answering from a name would be answering from a name), and not applied to `apps/voice-runtime`, whose whole surface is unauthenticated-by-design under hard rule 3. |
| D-174 | **The first-party auth SCREENS, and the nine defects of the reference implementation designed out one by one** | `apps/web/src/lib/authn/` — the realm factory (single-flight rotation, 3s result cache, rotation barrier), the cookie transport and its 429 rung, the RFC-9457 refusal vocabulary, and restore-on-mount with audiences, a 15s deadline, soft-vs-hard failure and a generation counter; `src/components/authn/` — sign-in + emailed code, set-password, reset-request, email verification, the shared session gate, the admin idle modal; and **ten screens** under `src/app/(auth)/auth/` — both realms' sign-in, forgot-password, reset-password and session/account pages, plus client invitation acceptance and the D-171 admin bootstrap. Two module-scoped instances, `adminAuthn` and `clientAuthn`, constructed once each with a literal. | **THE FACTORY IS WRITTEN ONCE AND THAT IS NOT REALM SHARING.** CLAUDE.md forbids the realms sharing session logic and AUTH-MIGRATION §3 says why — "a `realm` parameter on one shared module is one bad conditional away from presenting an admin credential on a client surface". `createRealmAuthn(realm)` has no such conditional: the realm is a **closure constant** fixed at import, a literal in every path, with no request-time input able to move a call between realms — the identical argument `apps/api/authn/routes.py::_realm_router` makes on the server side, and what comes out is two objects with two caches, two restore runtimes and two generation counters. The two SESSION modules are duplicated, neither importing the other, and `tests/authnSourceGuards.test.ts` pins all of it: the factory is called exactly twice, with literals, and each realm's module names exactly one instance. **A ROTATION BARRIER, WHICH THE REFERENCE DOES NOT HAVE.** §5.2's single-flight plus result cache is necessary and here insufficient: their refresh is a token exchange, ours calls `rotate_session`, which supersedes under CAS with **no concurrency grace window**, on the stated premise that rotation is "never a burst of background fetches". Presenting the superseded token is not staleness — it is `reuse_detected` and the whole family is revoked. So every call in a realm waits on an in-flight rotation before dispatching (bounded at three turns; a hang is worse than an ordering risk), and the transport deliberately has **no refresh-on-401 rung**: ours would be a second, uninvited rotation caller whose failure mode is every session the person holds revoked as theft. **THE NINE §5.7 DEFECTS, EACH WITH A TEST ASSERTING THE OPPOSITE.** (1) the restore deadline's `setTimeout` is cleared in a `finally` and touches no rotation state at all — theirs fires 15s later and nulls `refreshInFlight`, reintroducing the double-consume the single-flight exists to prevent; (2) **no enumeration oracle** — the server equalises status, body and wall-clock time, and the sign-in screens render a FIXED sentence chosen by problem code, so two different upstream bodies produce character-identical DOM and the typed address is never echoed; (3) no `devOtp` field, no `process.env` branch and nothing auto-filled, enforced by reading this directory's source; (4) one `useCountdown` keyed on an absolute deadline, and no hand-rolled interval survives anywhere in the surface; (5) the password is cleared the instant step one succeeds, which our backend makes possible because `/login` sets the cookie on the `otp_required` branch and `/login/otp/resend` therefore takes NO BODY — the cookie IS the short-lived challenge §5.7 asks for; (6) no credential is compared in the browser (the set-password confirmation compares two strings one person just typed and authenticates nothing); (7) no token is decoded and no claim is trusted — we hold none, and the idle modal accepts an extension only on the server's freshly re-read `SessionOut`; (8) `MIN/MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS` are read back out of `apps/api/authn/hashing.py` by a test and the hasher is asserted to be Argon2id; (9) unreachable and signed-out are different screens with different remedies, `status: 0` carrying the distinction. **TWO DEFECTS FOUND IN OUR OWN FIRST DRAFT, AND WORTH RECORDING.** The idle hook dismissed its warning on ANY activity, and `click`/`mousemove` are activity — so the pointer moving towards "Stay signed in" closed the dialog before it could be pressed, and pressing it closed the dialog on the same click that began the extension, hiding the outcome including the failure an operator most needs to see. The warning is now sticky and an explicit `resume()` restarts the clock: the standard shape (react-idle-timer's `promptBeforeIdle`, design.cms.gov's idle-timeout), right for the durable reason that extending a session is a decision and a stray mouse movement is not. And `signInMessage` indexed its copy table with a wire string, walking the prototype chain — routed through `lib/lookup.ts`, this repo's one answer to that class. **WHAT IS NOT BUILT, NAMED RATHER THAN DISCOVERED.** `Idempotency-Key` is sent by both reset forms and is **inert today**, because `/v1/auth/**` does not take the reliability dependency; the signed `X-CSRF-Token` has no server half to answer to (AUTH-MIGRATION §11). Both are backend changes this slice does not own, and the client-side one-in-flight-submit guard is what protects a user meanwhile. The screens stand BESIDE Clerk rather than replacing it: `apps/api/core/auth.py` does not yet read the first-party cookie, so `/admin` and `/c/<slug>` still authenticate through the vendor while these pages sign in, hold, extend and end a first-party session on their own. The day the verifier reads the cookie, the two providers move up into the two layouts and these become the session-management screens they already are. |
| D-175 | **An idempotency scope may never be attacker-choosable — audited, found CLEAN, and made checkable rather than left as a habit** | New `scripts/check_idempotency_scope.py` in `make guardrails` AND in CI, with 18 negative controls in `tests/idempotency_scope_guard_test.py`. Four properties: `reliability.scope_key` keeps its keyword-only `tenant_id: UUID \| None` / `user_id: UUID \| None` signature; `scope_key` is the only producer of a `claim_idempotency(scope=)` (through at most one local binding, which `billing/payment_routes.py` needs); its arguments are plain id references, never a call, a subscript or a literal; and no socket peer reaches ANY replay key — `claim_idempotency(scope=)`, `claim_inbox_event(provider=, event_key=)` or `enqueue_outbox_once(dedupe_key=)`. `check_audit_ip._is_peer_read` is promoted to `is_peer_read` and IMPORTED rather than restated. `enqueue_outbox_once` added to `reliability.service.__all__`. NO code path changed. | **THE FINDING WAS SOMEBODY ELSE'S, AND THE ANSWER HERE IS NO.** A teardown of a reference platform whose idempotency design is ours line for line — same `(scope, route, method, key)` tuple, same body hash, same 24h TTL — found its scope falling back to `anon:{fingerprint(request.ip)}` for an unauthenticated caller. Behind nginx or a CDN that is the EDGE's address unless proxy trust is exactly right, and the payoff is not a bad log field: it is caller A being served caller B's stored `response_payload`, before any tenant-scoped query runs, so no RLS policy is in a position to stop it. **We do not have it, and the evidence is exhaustive rather than reassuring**: `scope_key` has no fallback branch at all, and all four `claim_idempotency` call sites (`crm/routes.py` ×3, `billing/payment_routes.py` ×1) pass ids off a verified `Principal` or a tenant id resolved from a signature-verified payment.
