The other replay namespaces were audited to the same standard and are provider-issued, not caller-chosen: the inbox keys on Clerk's svix-id, Razorpay's payment id, Meta's leadgen id under a per-webhook-id provider, and the engine's `execution_id:raw_status`; the outbox's three `dedupe_key` spaces are built from uuid7 ids inside workers that never see a request. **THE DELIVERABLE IS THE GUARD, NOT A FIX** — CLAUDE.md's rule for an audit that finds nothing, and the reference project's fallback was also absent once. **THE TYPE IS THE FIRST LINE OF DEFENCE and the check's job is to keep it**: because both parameters are `UUID`, mypy strict already refuses `scope_key(tenant_id=request.headers.get(...))` at every call site in the repo — a header is a `str` — so widening one annotation would retire the guarantee everywhere at once with no call site changing, which is precisely the edit the signature property fails on. **WIDER SCOPE THAN `check_audit_ip` ON PURPOSE**: that guard asks "who is the caller?" over `apps/api`; this one asks "what may a replay key be derived from?" and must reach `apps/voice-runtime` and `apps/workers`, which hold inbox and outbox call sites it deliberately does not scan. **The `Idempotency-Key` header is deliberately NOT guarded** — it is the caller's to choose by contract, and is safe only because the scope beside it is not; a control pins that a guard banning it outright would be banning the feature. **What would reverse it**: an unauthenticated write that legitimately needs idempotency (a password reset, an OTP request) has no principal to scope by, and the answer is a server-derived subject, never an address — the guard refuses the address either way. |
| D-176 | **Our own twenty guardrails were audited against the rule D-166 wrote, and six of them could pass while seeing nothing** | `docs/evidence/our-gates-audit.md` asks three questions of every `scripts/check_*.py` — what makes it fail, whether anything on its own run path produces the evidence it reads, and whether it can pass vacuously — and records the answer per check with `file:line`. **Nothing in this repo has the reference's closed loop**: no check reads an artefact its own pipeline wrote (`check_openapi_fresh` compares a COMMITTED snapshot against a freshly built app, and `--write` is run by a human and by no gate; `pnpm -C apps/web gen:api` READS that file and never writes it). SIX vacuous passes were found and closed. `check_metadata_columns` printed `OK (61 tables agree in both directions)` against an EMPTY database, because `compare_metadata` reports an absent table as one `add_table` op rather than one `add_column` per column — it now REFUSES (exit 2) naming every table the models declare and the database lacks. `check_wiring`'s router half reported no offenders when `declared_routers()` found none, so a moved `apps/api` would print `OK (0 routers all mounted)`. `check_env_parity`'s third direction is an `rglob` search that yields nothing, without raising, over a directory that has been renamed. `check_config_applies` iterates a set DERIVED from `Settings` by exclusion, and four checks over an empty set are four green ones. All four now open with a `blind_spots()` section in the shape `check_docs_drift` and `check_web_env_parity` already had. **Two more were found by VERIFYING that list rather than trusting it**, and both had been cleared by READING in the first pass: `check_ledger_immutability`'s check 1 — hard rule 4's code half — returns `[]` from `check_sources(root=<empty dir>)` and prints `no mutating statements in app code` having read no code, anchored only by an exception registry whose correct end state is empty; and `check_observability_ready`'s langfuse rung asserts an ABSENCE while swallowing a missing scan root with `continue` and a missing manifest with `if manifest.exists()`, so a renamed `apps/` turns “no langfuse import anywhere” into “none anywhere I looked”. The first now has its own `blind_spots()`; the second raises `ObservabilityBlindError` and exits 2 REFUSED. And `test_every_guardrail_script_runs_in_both_gates` — the test that claims both gates run every check — was a substring search over the WHOLE Makefile and the WHOLE workflow, both of them heavily commented and several comments naming the script beside them; it now reads only recipe lines and only parsed `run:` scalars, with a control proving the reduction drops comments. An eighth finding of the same species: ENGINEERING-PRACTICES §2 — the page a reader goes to for what guards what — had a row for TWELVE of the twenty, and `test_every_guardrail_script_is_named_in_the_catalogue` now globs the directory so it cannot fall behind again. | **WHY.** D-166 landed the constraint — a check must be able to name what would make it fail, and that thing must not be produced by the run — and enforced it on the one check it shipped with. The reference repo's four instances (`docs/evidence/raghava-platform-teardown.md` §1.11, §3.1; `raghava-deploy-teardown.md` §5.3) are a PATTERN, not an accident, so the honest next move was to turn the rule on the other nineteen rather than to trust that ours were different. **WHAT THE AUDIT ACTUALLY FOUND.** Not the loop — the second shape, a gate whose evidence can be ABSENT and read as agreement. `check_metadata_columns` is the clean example: pointed at `sqlite://`, a database with nothing in it, `column_differences` returned `[]` and the check printed OK. That is the same failure as theirs from the other side, and it is the one a repo with committed, human-produced evidence is actually exposed to. **WHAT IT COSTS.** Four more sections that can fail for a reason that is not a violation, each with a floor (10 routers, 100 columns, 5 managed settings) that a real deletion could one day cross; the floors are an order of magnitude below today's counts for that reason, and every one names what it thinks went wrong. **WHAT CLOSES IT.** Nothing — it is a property, and the fourteen checks cleared are listed in the audit by name, each with the probe that cleared it, so the next reader inherits the evidence rather than the verdict. **THE PART THAT IS STILL INHERITED**: `check_coverage_ratchet` was read and not run (its plugin refuses a run whose stores were not empty), and `check_rls_coverage`'s empty-database refusal is reasoned rather than observed because the Postgres is shared — and findings 5 and 6 are exactly what reading-without-running costs, since both were cleared by reading before being run. **WHAT REVERSES IT.** A guardrail that legitimately has nothing to scan, which would move a floor to zero; that is a decision-log entry, never an edit. |
| D-177 ⚠SUPERSEDES D-37's authentication leg entirely; D-124 and the identity-mirror race are DELETED rather than superseded | **Clerk is gone. `core/auth.py` verifies our own session cookie, and the first-party module stops standing beside the vendor and starts being the authenticator.** | `core/auth.py` reads the realm's `__Host-` cookie (`authn/cookies.read_token`) and proves it with `authn/sessions.verify_session`; `VerifiedToken` becomes `VerifiedCaller` carrying `subject_id` — `users.id` on the client realm, `admin_users.id` on the admin realm — so both principal loaders look up by primary key and the vendor-subject translation disappears. **Deleted:** `core/clerk_identity.py`, `tenancy/clerk_webhooks.py`, `POST /hooks/v1/clerk` and its `webhook_identity` rate profile, the six `CLERK_*` settings and their `platform_config` rules, `jwks_url` / `missing_realm_separation_keys` / `_jwk_clients` / the `fva` claim reader, `Principal.clerk_user_id`, the Clerk-era `POST /v1/invitations/accept`, `tests/clerk_mirror_security_test.py`, `tests/identity_mirror_race_test.py`, `apps/web/src/lib/auth/**`, the three `(auth)` Clerk route groups and the `@clerk/nextjs` dependency. **Web:** both realm layouts mount D-174's providers and gates, `lib/api/client.ts` sends `credentials: "include"` and makes the bearer OPTIONAL, `lib/auth/mode.ts` survives as `lib/authn/mode.ts` with `clerk` renamed `session`, and `lib/authn/realmSessions.ts` is the one branch on that mode. `PyJWT` stays: `core/impersonation.py` signs the D-22 grant with it. | **THE DEV PATH KEEPS TWO INDEPENDENT GUARDS AND THE SECOND ONE IS NEW.** AUTH-MIGRATION C-21 requires them to survive; one of them was "this realm has no Clerk secret configured", which cannot. Its successor is `PLATFORM_KEK` being unset, and it is the SAME statement — *this deployment holds no real credential material* — but wider: the KEK peppers every password (`authn/hashing.py`), a prod host cannot omit it, and `/healthz/ready` reports it. So one mis-set `APP_ENV` can no longer switch the dev path on, which was the entire point of there being two. The subject also stops being caller-chosen: `dev:<realm>:<subject-uuid>`, and `dev:admin:anyone` no longer parses. **WHAT CREATES AND UPDATES `users` AND `admin_users` NOW, because deleting a mirror deletes a writer.** `users`: `authn/invitations._find_or_create_user`, on invitation redemption, in the transaction that also sets the password and creates the membership. `admin_users`: `scripts/bootstrap_admin.py` → `authn/bootstrap.py` for the first operator, and the ops console for the rest. `organizations` NEVER had a Clerk writer — the webhook answered `ignored` to every org event (D-10, admin-driven onboarding) — so nothing there moved. **The one writer with no successor is `user.deleted` → `users.deactivated_at`**, and that is correct rather than orphaned: an account is ours to end now, `POST /v1/members/{user_id}` removal and the operator surfaces set the column, and `authn/subjects.py` reads it as the client realm's whole liveness rule. **TWO ENDPOINTS BECAME ONE, and the collapse changed a security property rather than merely tidying.** `POST /v1/invitations/accept` compared the invitation's address against the address the caller had signed in with, and refused a mismatch (`invitation_wrong_recipient`). The first-party route takes the address FROM the invitation, so there is no comparison to get wrong — and no requirement that the redeemer already control a verified mailbox at that address. A forwarded link now yields an account for the invited address to whoever opens it first; what it buys is that an honest invitee whose mailbox differs from their old vendor account can no longer be locked out by a refusal they cannot act on. Single-use, hash-at-rest, 72h and CAS-burned are what bound the window, and they are untouched. `tests/member_invitations_test.py` carries that trade in the test that used to assert the refusal. **`mfa_claim_missing` AND `mfa_required` COLLAPSE INTO `second_factor_required`** — the code `authn/routes.py` already raised for the identical state. The pair existed because a custom JWT template could silently drop `fva`, so "we cannot tell" and "you did not" were genuinely different facts; `auth_sessions.mfa_verified_at` has no third state. It is `401 auth`, not `403 permission`: a half-MFA session is half-AUTHENTICATED, and the difference is what sends an operator to finish signing in rather than to ask somebody for a role. **`clerk_user_id` IS NOT DROPPED** (hard rule 8): both columns stop being written and stop being read — the ops console's "who set this key" fallback moves to the admin id, which is present for every operator rather than only Clerk-era ones — and both are recorded in `check_wiring.UNWIRED_BASELINE` with the DROP migration named as what closes them. **WHAT THIS CHANGE MAKES UNTRUE AND DOES NOT FIX**: `apps/web/src/lib/legal/{cookies,privacy,subprocessors}.ts` and the published DPA name Clerk as a sub-processor and as where passwords are held. Both are now false and both are DISCLOSURES — removing a named sub-processor from a client DPA is counsel's call, not a refactor, and the whole `/legal` set already carries `{{PENDING LEGAL REVIEW}}`. Named here so it is a deferral with an owner rather than a stale sentence. **WHAT WOULD REVERSE IT**: nothing short of a decision to buy authentication again; the Clerk applications' sessions are dead and a rollback is a restore, not a rollback (AUTH-MIGRATION §5 step 6 said so before it was run). |
| D-178 | **AUTH-MIGRATION §11's four named gaps, closed — three built, one retired, and one decided against with the guard left intact** | Migration `c7a1e93d40b8` (parent `b3d9f6a2c815`): `uq_users_email_lower` — `UNIQUE (lower(email)) WHERE deactivated_at IS NULL` on `users`, with a pre-flight that refuses BEFORE writing and prints colliding row ids and never addresses (hard rule 6) — plus a third `auth_otp_challenges.purpose`, `step_up`. New `apps/api/authn/stepup.py`; `core/stepup.py` becomes a `Depends(step_up_gate)` resolving a frozen `StepUp` whose synchronous `require` demands BOTH halves at all 15 call sites — a dependency rather than an inline await, because two of those sites hold an open `tenant_session` and `db/session.py`'s `max_overflow=0` is safe only while no path holds two. Two new endpoints, `POST /v1/auth/admin/step-up{,/verify}`, admin-realm-only by declaration. `authn/cookies.enforce_same_origin` stops exiting early on `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-site`; `core/middleware.CookieCsrfMiddleware` applies the same rule to every mutating request carrying a session cookie. `authn/invitations._find_or_create_user` moves to `ON CONFLICT`. Four new test files (49 cases); AUTH-MIGRATION §1 C-09/C-22, §2.2, §10.5 and §11 rewritten. | **STEP-UP (C-09) — BUILT, BECAUSE THE OBJECTION EXPIRED.** `core/auth.py` records why freshness was not enforced on the Clerk credential: Clerk models it as "reverification", this repo had no browser flow to raise the prompt, and "gating an incident lever on a flow that does not exist would be a control that gets switched off". D-170's emailed OTP IS that flow. So a dangerous mutation now needs INTENT (the `X-Confirm-Action` echo, which a stolen cookie satisfies trivially — the refusal prints the string on purpose) and PRESENCE (`mfa_verified_at` under five minutes). Five minutes because an operator working a runbook must not be challenged twice inside one task, and because a window at or above the 30-minute idle bound would be satisfied by every live session — a control that never fires. A request bearing no first-party session is NOT refused, and that is not the "passes when absent" hole: freshness is a property of a CREDENTIAL, the Clerk token has its own gates, and deleting Clerk leaves no other credential, so the branch becomes unreachable rather than permissive. **CSRF DOUBLE-SUBMIT — RETIRED, AND IT UNCOVERED A REAL HOLE.** OWASP's cheat sheet names five conditions for origin verification plus `SameSite` being sufficient without a token; we met four, and the one we did not — no shared registrable domain — was live: three consoles under `calevate.tech` mean any `*.calevate.tech` host issues `same-site` requests carrying the cookie, and `enforce_same_origin` returned before consulting the allowlist. `SameSite=Strict` does not stop it and `__Host-` stops a sibling SETTING the cookie, not the browser sending it. Checking `Origin` unconditionally is the same defence a token would have given against the same attacker, with no secret to distribute and no "absent" branch. The middleware exists so the sentence stays true of the whole API the day cookies authenticate the whole API. **`Idempotency-Key` ON `/v1/auth/**` — DECIDED AGAINST.** D-175's guard requires a scope derived from a principal or a tenant; the auth routes a double submit could hurt are the unauthenticated ones, which have no principal, so the only candidate scopes are the typed address and the socket peer — the second being D-175's finding verbatim. Weakening the guard to fit was not on the table. Each route's own single-use CAS is what answers the double submit, and it is a stronger statement than a replay cache: exactly one of two concurrent submissions wins at the database. The 422 the loser gets is correct — nothing can tell a double-click from a replayed leaked link. **`users.password_migrated_at` — VESTIGIAL.** D-170 makes first-party auth the only authenticator; there is no population of Clerk-era hashes to migrate and `auth_credentials.password_set_at` already carries the fact. **THE INDEX'S PREDICATE IS THE ONE DEPARTURE FROM `admin_users`** and it is what makes the two express one rule: "at most one LIVE account per address" is what both readers already assumed, and `admin_users` has no soft-delete to predicate on. Without it an owner who left and came back could never be re-onboarded. **WHAT REVERSES ANY OF IT**: a same-site attacker who can forge `Origin` (browsers do not permit it) would re-open the token question; an unauthenticated auth route that acquires a server-derived principal would re-open the idempotency one. |
| D-179 | **Four DPDP findings that were ours: the two stores with no retention clock, the breach procedure behind the 48-hour promise, and the caller notice a client cannot write without us** | Migration `c4d1f7b83e26` widens `retention_policies.data_category` to six with `engine_payload` (90 d, the number `infra/object-lifecycle/policy.json` already assigned that prefix) and `kb` (365 d, the transcript default). Two sweep arms: archived vendor documents page expired calls into the SAME `_erase_engine_payloads` the erasure uses; superseded and rejected knowledge versions are deleted, never the live one and never one still carrying an engine handle. `execute_deletion_request` now SEARCHES the knowledge base for the subject's number, digits-normalised, and reports the count — so `KB_OUTCOME` narrows from `not_searched` to `searched_not_erased` and both halves of the index-aligned register move with it. `runbooks/data-breach-notification.md` + `apps/api/compliance/breach.py` + `scripts/breach_notice.py` are the Rule 7 procedure and its renderer. `GET /v1/compliance/caller-notice` generates a client's caller-facing notice draft from their own extraction schemas, retention rows and D-163 announcement switches. | **WHY.** `docs/LEGAL-SURFACE.md` F-2, F-3, F-6 and F-8 are the four findings of that audit that needed nothing from outside this repository. F-2 and F-3 are DPDP §8(7) exposures that err in the dangerous direction — a copy of tenant personal data outside every policy a tenant can set, expiring only for the handful of people who file a §12 request. F-6 was a clock in a signed contract with no procedure behind it. F-8 is the one that is a product feature as much as a compliance fix: the itemised list Rule 3 demands IS the client's extraction schema, which only we hold, so "the notice duty is yours" was true and unhelpable. **WHAT IT COSTS.** Two more per-tenant numbers to honour, and a narrowing of the erasure certificate — which is the direction SEC-COMP §4 does not permit lightly, so it is earned by two mechanisms rather than by rewording. The KB search reads client content on every erasure, which is why nothing about it is logged. The caller-notice draft is a document a client publishes: it is marked DRAFT in the envelope AND in the text, every blank only they can fill is a visible `{{...}}`, and an agent with its AI announcement switched off changes what the draft says rather than being absorbed. **WHAT IS DELIBERATELY NOT DONE.** `campaign_contact` is still not a category — how long a client's own uploaded list is kept is a DPA commitment whose number is the founder's, and `tests/dpdp_known_gaps_test.py` keeps probing this constraint to hold that gap open. The erasure does not EDIT knowledge content: editing a live price list changes what the agent says on the next call and the platform holds its own copy, so the certificate names a manual step on both. The breach tooling sends nothing. **WHAT CLOSES THE REST.** The Board's own reporting channel (a lookup, nobody's blocker but nobody's yet) and counsel's review of the notice wording — both stated in the runbook's §7 rather than left to be discovered at 4am. |
| D-180 | **The host is a Hostinger India VPS — a founder decision, and the first thing that could make a residency claim true rather than aspirational** | Hosting for the site stack (web, api, workers, voice-runtime, Postgres, Redis) is a Hostinger VPS in India. It supersedes D-25's "Hetzner-class, India co-location NOT required" as to the PROVIDER and the REGION; it does not supersede D-25's reasoning, which was that co-location is not *required* for a stack outside the live-call path — that stays true, and this decision is the founder buying something D-25 said was optional. **Nothing about it is provisioned**: `infra/` still contains no host, no network and no DNS, and `terraform validate` has never run (`infra/README.md` §5). | **WHY.** F-1 is the founder decision the whole residency question hangs on, and it was open in both directions: the landing page's residency sentence had to be removed because nothing supported it, and `docs/BRD.md` went on telling sales to make the same claim. Choosing an Indian host is what turns that from a claim nobody can make into a claim that needs verifying. **WHAT IT DOES NOT SETTLE, and this is the part that matters more than the choice.** Our VPS is one of at least five places tenant personal data lives. Cloudflare R2 runs `AWS_REGION=auto` and offers no India-only jurisdiction; Bolna's recordings were OBSERVED on S3 `us-east-1` and its residency posture is still unpinned in the contract; Sarvam is all-India by D-36; Gemini is Vertex `asia-south1` by D-127; Resend and Sentry are operated outside India. So **the host being Indian does not make the data plane India-resident**, and until every one of those is answered in writing nothing in this repository may assert residency — the ban in LEGAL-SURFACE F-1 survives this decision rather than being lifted by it. What IS sellable today is unchanged: we name every sub-processor and where it runs, before signature. **WHAT IT COSTS.** `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` §0's provider prose and its Hetzner-class latency worked example are now describing a machine we are not renting, and they are the next thing to correct; the ~150ms figure was an argument about European hosting and does not survive the move. |
| D-181 | **A dial gets a call row BEFORE the vendor can seize a line; an `Idempotency-Key` claim stops dying with the side effect it guards; and the recording audio and the leads search leave `calls:read` and the URL** | (1) `dispatch_call` commits an INTENT row in its OWN transaction before `start_outbound_call`, keyed on an `engine_call_id` we mint (`UNCONFIRMED_ENGINE_CALL_PREFIX = "local:"`), stamps the vendor's handle over it on a clean return, and raises `DialUnconfirmedError` when the answer is lost. The campaign dispatcher writes `campaign_contacts.last_call_id` inside that same transaction (`on_reserved`), settles an unconfirmed dial TERMINALLY rather than on the ladder, and `_reap_stuck_dialing` does the same for the case that outruns an `except` (a `CancelledError` through the dial). (2) The two CRM routes that spend money — `POST /v1/calls/{id}/assist` and `POST /v1/leads/{id}/call`, plus `/callback` which had the same shape — take `billing/payment_routes._create_order_once`'s three-transaction form: claim committed before the paid call, `fail_idempotency` on the arm where nothing was paid for, meter+audit+complete committed together afterwards. (3) `GET /v1/calls/{id}/recording` moves to `calls:read_raw`. (4) `search` moves off the query string to `POST /v1/leads/search` and `POST /v1/leads/export.csv` (and into `LeadBulkIn.search`); the GET shapes REFUSE it. (5) The campaign retry ladder is computed with `now() + make_interval(mins => :minutes)` like its two sibling writers. No migration: the calls table is unchanged. | **(1) The docstring promised the ordering the code could not have** — the INSERT bound `engine_call_id` to the value the engine returns, so the row could not precede the call. A lost response (read timeout at 10s, reset, proxy 502 after the vendor committed) left a ringing phone with no row: no metering, no wallet debit, and a campaign contact returned to `pending` with no `last_call_id` for `resolve_campaign_contact` to match — **a real person dialled a second time**. A namespaced local id was chosen over making `engine_call_id` nullable because NULL would mean both 'not yet' and 'lost', and every reader that joins on the column would need a branch it cannot resolve. The conservative classification is deliberate: only failures that PROVE no line was seized (`DIAL_NOT_PLACED_CODES` — 429 and the pre-flight refusals) keep the retry ladder, because the adapters collapse 4xx and 5xx into one `engine_rejected` and a wrongly-retried dial costs a second unsolicited call while a wrongly-abandoned one costs a contact a human looks at. A vendor-side idempotency key on `POST /call` would let us do better and is UNVERIFIED for Bolna, so it is not assumed. `lead_id` is stamped through the caller's session after the dial, not in the intent row: `ingest/service.py` creates the lead in the same uncommitted transaction it dials from, and an FK from another connection would wait on the transaction that is waiting on us. **(2)** `core/deps.db` rolls the request back on any exception, so a raise after `run_assist` erased the claim, the `usage_events` rows and the audit row while Google had been paid — and the retry with the same key paid again. This repo had already solved it in `billing/payment_routes.py` and argued why; adopting that shape removes the second way of doing it. On `DialUnconfirmedError` the claim is left `processing` ON PURPOSE: releasing it invites a second call to somebody whose phone may be ringing. **(3)** The audio is the SOURCE of the text the whole redaction apparatus protects; the route had the audit half of hard rule 5 and not the role half, while the raw transcript and the CSV export beside it were already `calls:read_raw`. **(4)** `search` is matched against `phone_e164`, and nginx's default `combined` format logs the request line — the hazard `POST /v1/dnc/check` is already a POST for. REFUSED rather than ignored on the GETs, because FastAPI drops an undeclared parameter silently and a dropped filter WIDENS the set, which on `export.csv` is the whole contact list leaving with full numbers. **(5)** The worker clock wrote a column the database clock reads. **Cost accepted**: a dial now briefly holds two pooled connections (the caller's plus a short-lived one, released before the engine round trip), which the audit's separate no-overflow-pool finding already describes for two other paths. |

## 7. Explicitly Deferred (do not build until pulled by a client or a gate)

White-label/reseller tiers · payments-in-call · voice biometrics ·
multi-agent teams · non-India telephony · GPU inference · Temporal · separate analytics DB.

**No longer deferred: self-serve signup.** D-34/D-39 pulled it in and it ships —
`POST /v1/auth/signup`, plus the top-up intent, the server-side order route and the
payment webhook (SURFACES §2c). Server-side order creation **is** built (D-98); this line
said it was not, for a wave after it landed. What holds a client back is the credential,
not the code: the intake flag `self_serve_signup_enabled` defaults to OFF and no Razorpay
API secret is configured on any deployment, so `PaymentCapability.creates_orders` answers
False with reason `no_api_secret`. "Shipped" here means the surface and the adapter exist,
not that a client can pay today.
| D-182 | **The connection pool is sized against the nesting the code actually has, no transaction is held across a vendor round trip, and the three waits that could not end now end** | Five things, one theme: a bound that was justified by an untrue claim, and four places a wait had no end. (1) `db/session.py` justified `max_overflow=0` with "no code path here holds two sessions at once (checked across apps/api and apps/workers)". Fifty functions hold two: FastAPI's `Depends(deps.db)` holds the request session for the whole handler, so `check_dispatch` → `get_platform_status` → `_read_durable` on a cold 5s memo is a second connection inside the first; every admin route holds the directory session outside and a `tenant_session` inside; and `dispatch_outbox` → `claim_outbox_batch` takes a second connection on purpose. `max_overflow` becomes `MAX_NESTED_CONNECTIONS - 1` = 1, and `scripts/check_session_nesting.py` walks call chains THROUGH THE IMPORTS (understanding `Annotated[..., Depends(db)]`) and fails CI at depth 3, in `make guardrails`, CI and the guardrail catalogue. (2) `outbound_webhooks.deliver_outbound_webhook` splits into read / vendor / write, so neither the POST to the client's CRM nor the object-store PUT happens inside a transaction. (3) `compose.prod.yml` gives `api` `stop_grace_period: 30s`, and `tests/worker_reliability_test.py` pins drain < grace for all three long-lived services rather than for `workers` alone. (4) `core/health.py` bounds both dependency probes and the queue read at 2s and answers `db_down`/`redis_down`. (5) `dispatch_outbox`'s catch-all narrows to `POISON_PAYLOAD` (`arq.jobs.SerializationError`, `TypeError`, `ValueError`), and `platform_config.refresh` moves its whole body inside the `try` its docstring already promised. | **The pool comment is the finding, not the number.** A capacity decision justified by a property the code does not have is worse than an unjustified one: with no overflow, sixteen depth-2 tasks hold all sixteen connections and each waits `_POOL_TIMEOUT_S` for a seventeenth — a self-deadlock, and the memo hides it until Redis is down, which is when the API is busiest with retries. Rejected: restoring the old invariant. It would mean resolving the platform status before every session opens and changing `check_dispatch`'s signature across six callers, to buy back a rule the framework itself breaks on every route — so the honest move is to state the real invariant and enforce it, which is why the guard and the pool now hold ONE number in two files with a test asserting they agree. One slot is not the single-use-connection treadmill `get_engine` measured either: that needed 186 fresh backends sustained above `pool_size`, and one slot cannot sustain anything. **(2) is the same fault seen twice** — correctness 4 and R-5 found it independently. Ten slow deliveries park ten of the worker's sixteen connections, the campaign dispatch tick starves, and the alarm that fires is `dispatch_tick_overrun`: the operator is pointed at the dispatcher for one client's slow CRM. `pipeline._copy_recording_once` and `campaign_dispatch`'s claim/dial split already reason about this; the delivery worker did not. **(3) was measurable from the file**: Docker's documented default grace is 10s and the api declared none, so a 25-second drain met SIGKILL at ten on every deploy and the lifespan `finally` D-101 restored never ran — the fix undone one layer up by a key nobody wrote. **(4) A health endpoint that hangs is worse than one that fails**: an orchestrator cannot act on "slow", and each hung probe held a connection, so probing during a blackhole exhausted the pool and made everything else 503 for the wrong reason. **(5) both are alarms that fail towards silence**: the dispatcher's catch-all answered a database error by issuing another statement on the session that error had poisoned, so up to fifty messages were charged an attempt per tick and eventually dead-lettered as poison they never were; and `refresh`'s promise held by the current contents of `_resolve` and `apply_platform_overrides` rather than by its own shape, with both of its alerts inside the `except` that did not cover the raising half. **Not fixed, and named**: `campaign_dispatch._dispatch_for_campaign` holds the identical vendor-call-inside-a-transaction shape as (2) and belongs to another agent's fence this pass — it is bounded to one dial at a time by the tick lease, and it is the next thing to restructure. |
| D-183 | **A promised alarm that cannot fire is worse than an absent one: the client spend cap announces itself, the poller's sweep survives one tenant, and the metric stream stops pretending to be telemetry** | Four things, one principle. (1) `billing/caps.py` gains `cap_fullness` + `announce_cap_headroom` (`CAP_WARN_AT = 0.80`, the same constant and the same argument as D-140's `PLATFORM_BRAKE_WARN_AT`), fired from the meter's `spend_state` upsert — which now RETURNs the new totals and the ceilings from the same `caps` CTE the flag was judged by, so "under before, over after" is decided from the writing statement's own return value with no second table. Codes `tenant_spend_cap_approaching` and `tenant_spend_capped`, most-severe-first with a break, ids and a percentage only. The other two writers of the flag (the client's own stop button, the ops recompute) deliberately stay silent: both are a human acting on purpose with a response body that already says `capped`. (2) `core/alerting.py`'s recorder section says at its head that every `record_*` is a log line with NO consumer and that **no alarm may depend on one** — alarms travel `alert()`'s email path (D-49) and announce at the producing write. (3) `reconcile_executions` gets per-execution `try/except` + an `unreached` counter on the job result and in a new `reconciliation_probe_incomplete` alert, and its cron gets `max_tries=WORKER_MAX_TRIES`. (4) `integrations.subscribed_endpoint_sql(alias)` becomes the ONE spelling of "which endpoints get this event", read by `enqueue_events` and by the poller's `crm_fanout_owed` probe. Plus: `outbox_messages.queue`'s inertness is now stated at the column declaration and on `OutboxMessageRow.queue` (D-162 unchanged, no router built), and `integrations/service.py` records what `webhook_deliveries` can and cannot answer — the OUT direction is complete, the IN direction holds only CLAIMED deliveries, so an intrusion investigation must be pointed at the alert stream instead. | OPERATIONS §4 listed "cap approaching (80%)/breached" as a live trigger and `caps.py` contained no `alert(` at all: a capped tenant's campaign stopped dialling, the console still read "running", and the only trace was a `record_compliance_block(rule="spend_cap")` log line that nothing reads. D-140 having built exactly this alarm for the platform's OWN AI spend is what makes it an omission rather than a decision, so it is fixed by following that implementation rather than inventing a second alarm shape. The same principle explains (2): a recorder that looks like telemetry and reaches no consumer is CLAUDE.md's "half-wired feature", and the honest fix while DEPLOYMENT §8 defers the metrics endpoint is to say so and to forbid alarms built on it, not to pretend. (3) is the guarantee of record failing towards silence — TRD §5 makes the poller, not webhook delivery, what makes call records trustworthy, and one tenant's probe error ended the sweep for every execution behind it, failed the job on its first attempt and alerted nothing; `report_stalled_pipeline` was fixed for this exact shape and its argument is quoted where the new `try` sits. (4) is unreachable today only because the CHECK constraint happens to allow exactly `DELIVERABLE_KINDS` — a third kind landing ahead of its worker would make every completed call for that tenant a permanent `unfinished_pipeline` re-driven hourly WITH A BILLED EXTRACTION, and two spellings of one rule is a defect while they still agree. **Not fixed, and named rather than left implied**: complaint-spike, engine-5xx-spike and cert-expiry are still on OPERATIONS §4's trigger list with no call site anywhere — the first two are rates over counters (they need the deferred metrics pipeline or a producer in files this pass did not own) and cert expiry is a systemd timer beside the backup units using `notify.sh`. §4's list still names triggers nothing implements. |
| D-184 | **Two mechanisms for one compliance rule is a defect even while both work — and the console had four of them** | Nine frontend findings, one principle each. (1) The unredacted transcript is a `useMutation`, not a `useQuery` with `staleTime: Infinity`: `GET /v1/calls/{id}/transcript/raw` writes an `audit_log` row in its read transaction, so a hide-then-show — or a return inside `gcTime` — used to re-expose the text with no request and NO SECOND ROW, and `reset()` on Hide now drops the personal data instead of parking it in the heap. (2) `ScrollRegion` (`components/ui.tsx`) replaces all eighteen bare `overflow-x-auto` wrappers, including the one site that had argued the case inline. (3) `SkipLink` + `MAIN_CONTENT_ID` in both shells, with `tabIndex={-1}` on `<main>`. (4) `lib/nav.currentNavItem` is the ONE 'you are here' rule both shells read for the header title AND the sidebar highlight. (5) The leads lens travels in a POST body — `POST /v1/leads/search` and `POST /v1/leads/export.csv` — and `lensQuery` now THROWS on a search term, so the URL cannot carry one. (6) `INLINE_EDIT` carries `touch:min-h-11`; four more `py-0.5` buttons widened. (7) `tests/responsive.test.ts` gains the keyboard-reachability and hairline-control rules. (8) Delivery-log rows key on identity plus occurrence. (9) `callback-check`, `campaign-check` and `campaign` gain the org slug, with `tests/queryKeys.test.ts` scanning every `useQuery` in `src/` for the next one. | **WHY IT IS ONE ROW.** Every finding is the same shape: a rule the repo had already decided, implemented a second time and worse. The raw transcript is the sharpest — `useRecordingLink` sits eleven lines below it on the same screen, correctly a mutation for exactly the same reason, so hard rule 5's audit row was lost to a mechanism the file itself had already replaced. `document.tsx` had the focusable scroll container with axe's `scrollable-region-focusable` cited in a comment, and seventeen tables did not. Both shells computed the title by longest prefix and the highlight by exact match, four lines apart, so no element in the document carried `aria-current` on ANY detail route. `POST /v1/dnc/check` and the messaging-consent lookup both take a phone number in a body and both say why in their own files; the leads box — 'Name or last digits', matched against `phone_e164` — was the busiest screen left out of it. **GUARDS THAT CAN ACTUALLY FAIL.** axe cannot fire `bypass` or `scrollable-region-focusable` under jsdom (no layout, detached container — `tests/a11y.ts` says so itself), so adding those rules would have been a green tick on a check that never runs. Each fix instead ships a test asserting the mechanism where it is observable: request COUNTS at the network seam for the audit row, markup for the skip link, the source tree for the scroll containers and the query keys. Every one was run red against the pre-fix tree before being kept. **NOT CLOSED, and named rather than implied.** `GET /v1/leads/facets` has no POST shape, so the facet rail is sent NO search term at all and its counts describe the wider set under an active search — the table states its own narrowing beside it, and over-counting offers a filter that returns rows where under-counting would hide leads. Closed by a `POST /v1/leads/facets` taking the same `LeadLensIn`, which is `apps/api`'s. And F-8's real fix is one field: `ingest/routes.py` maps both the `ingest:` and `meta:` keyspaces onto one `lead_source_id` and discards the provider on the way out, so the console is handed an identity it cannot disambiguate; the occurrence counter is unique by construction but is a client-side answer to a server-side omission. |
| D-185 | **An unverified address could carry a membership into somebody else's organisation, and a lifted DNC suppression left no record of the number** | Two seams from the 17 Aug audits (S-2, S-4), plus the four docs-truth findings that were ours (F-8, F-9, F-10, F-12). **S-2 is NOT closed by D-177**, which is the finding's own claim and it is wrong: the report located "`users.email` is an authorization input with no verification check" in `core/clerk_identity.py`, D-177 deleted that file, and the PROPERTY the vendor was assumed to supply was never replaced. `authn/invitations._find_or_create_user` wrote `email_verified_at = now()` on the premise that possession of the emailed token proves the mailbox — but `InvitationCreatedOut.token` hands the raw token to the INVITER, because the client realm has no invitation mailer. So: an owner of any tenant invites an address they do not control, reads the token out of their own 201, redeems it, and holds the only `users` row that address can ever have (`uq_users_email_lower`) with a password they chose. When the real organisation later invites the real person, redemption reused that row, correctly declined to overwrite the password, and attached the membership — up to and including `owner`. The client realm has no second factor (`MFA_REQUIRED_REALMS` is `{'admin'}`), so a password is the whole credential. **Fixed**: the address starts UNVERIFIED (an `email_verify` OTP round trip is what fills the column, and that path was already built and already mailed), and an account that already carries a credential may only take on a NEW membership once its address is proven. A first-time invitee is untouched; the refusal lands before the CAS so the token stays redeemable afterwards. `tests/authn_invitation_verification_test.py` drives all four steps. **S-4**: `DELETE /v1/dnc/{id}` hard-deleted, and the audit row correctly carried no number, so nothing anywhere recorded which number stopped being suppressed. `remove_entry`/`remove_global_entry` now return a `Removal` carrying `compliance.export.subject_ref` — the same one-way handle `deletion_requests.subject_ref` stores — and the routes put it in the audit summary. **A tombstoned ROW was rejected and the reasons are in `dnc.Removal`**: it keeps a number that no longer suppresses anybody (`ERASURE_LIMITATIONS` justifies retaining a DNC entry solely because removing it would make the person callable again, which is false of a released row — so it would be retained personal data with no purpose, DPDP §8(7)); `add_numbers`' dedupe would read the tombstone and report a re-add as `already_suppressed`, which is the one direction hard rule 5 cannot fail in; and it would require the `(tenant_id, phone_e164)` unique key that `check_compliance_invariants` treats as an invariant to become partial. **Docs**: SEC-COMP §4 stops claiming a live client-facing retention misstatement (the DPA delegates to `/legal/privacy` §9, which publishes the seed's numbers, which is what the sweep enforces — §4's own table is the only outlier, and the founder decision stays open); DEV-SETUP names test files that exist; FLOWS §6 states the recording copy's real 30s/120s ladder; and D-95's "`.env` drops to 6" is corrected to a count of `BOOTSTRAP_KEYS` rather than of lines. | **WHY THE SMALLER FIX.** The complete answer to S-2 is to stop returning the invitation token to its issuer and email it instead, which restores the premise and closes the squat as well as the hijack. That needs the invitation mail template and the owner-facing screen that displays the token — `apps/workers/` and `apps/web/`, both outside this change — so what ships here turns an ACCOUNT TAKEOVER into a DENIAL OF SERVICE and says so rather than claiming a closure. `tests/authn_invitation_verification_test.py::test_the_squat_itself_is_still_possible_and_that_is_recorded_here` pins the residual and fails when it goes, which is what closes this entry. **WHY NOT A SIGN-IN GATE.** Refusing a session to an unverified client subject is the more obvious control and is unreachable from here: the verify endpoint needs a session, so it would lock out the very people it is for, and the gate itself belongs in `core/auth.py`. The membership boundary is the one place the escalation actually crosses. **WHAT IS ASSUMED, NOT VERIFIED**: that no path other than invitation redemption writes `users` — proven by grep (one `INSERT INTO users` in `apps/`, `scripts/` and `alembic/` outside test fixtures) and by `POST /v1/auth/signup` requiring `current_identity`, not by an exhaustive audit of every future writer. **COST**: one person joining a second client business now has an extra step, and it is a screen that already exists. |
| D-186 | **A metered call could be counted into the wrong IST month, a late call from a closed month wiped the open month's spend counters, the margin panel priced the whole self-serve motion as a pure loss, and the live spend counter priced a month by a different rule than the invoice** | Five money defects from a deep dive aimed away from the core `docs/evidence/audit-correctness.md` already cleared (`docs/evidence/deepdive-money.md` has the reproductions). **(1) A SECOND SPELLING OF THE BILLING MONTH.** `apps/workers/pipeline.py::_ist_month` was `(moment + timedelta(hours=5, minutes=30)).strftime("%Y-%m")`, correct only for a moment expressed in UTC — `strftime` renders the value's own naive fields rather than converting — and NOTHING guarantees UTC: both adapters parse `ended_at` with `datetime.fromisoformat` and PRESERVE the offset the vendor sent (`engine/bolna.py::_parse_dt`; its `replace(tzinfo=UTC)` covers naive values only), and the vendor is an Indian voice platform. Measured: a call stamped `2026-08-31T23:00+05:30` metered into `2026-09` while its own `usage_events` rows — read back through `billing.service._IST_MONTH`, which goes via `timestamptz` and is correct — sat in `2026-08`. `billing/plans.py::ist_billing_month` was already the stated home of that shift, converts properly for any aware instant and REFUSES a naive one; `_ist_month` is deleted and `_meter` calls it. `tests/one_billing_month_spelling_test.py` is an AST scan asserting that exactly ONE module in `apps/`, `packages/shared/src/` and `scripts/` turns an instant into a `%Y-%m` string. **(2) A CLOSED MONTH'S CALL RESET THE OPEN MONTH.** The `spend_state` upsert's accumulator was `CASE WHEN month = EXCLUDED.month THEN accumulate ELSE EXCLUDED.value END` — right forwards, destructive backwards. A call that settles late (the reconciliation poller's 30-minute window straddling midnight IST on the 1st, an ARQ retry ladder crossing it, a vendor that prices a call minutes after disconnect) arrives with last month's stamp and REPLACED this month's minutes, supplier spend, billed rupees and `capped` flag with its own, stamping `month` backwards — after which `compliance.spend_capped` reads the stale month as no cap at all and the client's panel reads ₹0.00 spent beside minutes correctly read from the ledger. The three near-identical accumulator strings collapse into one `_accumulate(column)` with a three-way rule (same month accumulates, later replaces, EARLIER leaves the counters alone) plus `month = GREATEST(...)`. The call's money is not lost: `usage_events` is the ledger every invoice reads; what a closed month's call may not do is move a ceiling for a month it does not belong to, and `spend_state` holds exactly one month by construction. **(3) THE MARGIN PANEL DID NOT KNOW WHAT A PREPAID CLIENT PAYS.** `margin_for_tenant` computed `revenue = monthly_fee + overage_cost`, which is the managed invoice's subtotal and is ₹0.00 for a `self_serve`/`trial` tenant, who has no `plans` row at all — so the one screen that answers "is this client making us money" reported ₹0.00 revenue against the real supplier cost for the entire self-serve motion, with `margin_pct` suppressed to `None` because that branch reads `revenue > 0`. New `calling_revenue_inr` is the one home of "this period's calling, priced to the client"; `_spend_used`'s prepaid branch (which was right) moved into it and the margin panel reads it. **(4) THE COUNTER AND THE INVOICE PRICED A MONTH DIFFERENTLY.** `usage_summary` spends the included allowance on the DEARER rung first (`split_overage`, deliberately client-favourable); `_billed_for_this_call` spent it in ARRIVAL order at each call's own rung. The two agree for a plan quoting ONE rate, which is every plan today, and diverge as soon as `plans.overage_rate_value` is quoted — measured at ₹880.00 against ₹520.00 on a two-rung month whose cheap minutes arrived first. `/c/<slug>/usage` prints BOTH figures in adjacent cards for one month, and the client's own spend cap is compared against the larger, so their stop button stops them before their bill justifies it. New `billing.service.priced_overage` is the one month-pricing rule (`usage_summary` and `build_invoice` already shared its parts); `overage_increment_inr` charges a call the difference it makes to it, from the per-rung SECONDS the ledger holds. `client_billed_inr` loses its managed branch and becomes `prepaid_billed_inr`; its two managed arguments move to `priced_overage` with the branch. **(5) THE SQL MONTH DEPENDED ON THE SESSION'S `TimeZone`.** `billing.service._IST_MONTH` — interpolated into every query that buckets `usage_events` by month, `ai_quota`'s two included — was `to_char(occurred_at + interval '5 hours 30 minutes', ...)`, and `to_char` on a `timestamptz` renders in the SESSION's `TimeZone`: shifting first and formatting second is the IST month only while that setting is UTC. Measured on one instant (23:00 IST on 31 August) across three zones, `+ interval` answers 2026-08 / **2026-09** / 2026-08 while `AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata'` answers 2026-08 three times. Now the latter, with the named zone rather than a literal `+05:30` for the reason the Python side documents its fixed offset. | **THE INCREMENT IS A DIFFERENCE OF TWO MONTH TOTALS, NOT A FORMULA, AND THAT IS THE INVENTION THIS ROW HAS TO DEFEND.** Two properties follow that nothing cheaper has: the increments TELESCOPE (each call's `before` is the previous call's `after`, both computed by one function from one raw-seconds state), so `spend_state.billed_inr` is exactly the month's own overage cost however many calls meter and in whatever order; and a rung's delta may legitimately be NEGATIVE (adding premium minutes moves the allowance onto the premium rung and INCREASES the value rung's overage), so only the month total is a meaningful quantity — pricing the rungs' deltas separately would have had to drop or clamp the negative one and would have been wrong by that amount. It reads SECONDS rather than the quantized minutes because `allocate_paise` distributes a remainder across the whole set and is not linear in any one bucket, so subtracting from minutes would not reproduce the state the previous call priced against. **COST, STATED**: one extra grouped aggregate over the month's `usage_events` per metered call, under a lock `_meter` already holds. **WHY THE ALARM CHANGED WITH (2)**: D-183's crossing arithmetic derives `before` as `after - this call's delta`, and a call that moved nothing has a delta of zero — so the statement had to say whether it applied. `EXCLUDED` is NOT referencable from a `RETURNING` clause (measured against this pg16: `invalid reference to FROM-clause entry for table "excluded"`, not recalled), so `RETURNING` carries the row's `month` instead and, because `GREATEST` never moves it backwards, `returned_month == :month` IS "this call's totals went in" — one rule, in the statement, read back from the statement. A skipped call gets an INFO line naming both months rather than an `alert()`: around a month boundary this is the expected outcome and an alarm that fires every rollover is a muted one. **PROVEN vs REASONED**: all five reproduce by execution against this tree and this database, each with a test that fails on the pre-fix tree (verified by reverting `apps/workers/pipeline.py` and re-running) — `tests/billing_month_ordering_test.py`, `tests/margin_prepaid_revenue_test.py`, `tests/two_rung_counter_agrees_test.py`. **NOT FIXED, AND WHY**: the client usage card's "Total so far" is `monthly_fee + overage_cost` and omits both one-time charges (the onboarding setup fee, which the invoice prints on that month) and GST, so it is neither the pre-tax usage subtotal nor the invoice total — what it should be is a founder decision about that card, not a refactor, and the invoice is one click away on the adjacent screen. A PREPAID tenant's invoice also shows no line for the minutes their wallet was debited for, because `build_invoice` derives everything from `plans` columns they do not have; what a prepaid statement is (top-ups received? consumption? where GST attaches on a prepaid credit) is a founder-and-accountant decision, and the named external blocker is the same GST registration Rule 46(b) is already waiting on. |
| D-187 | **Two silent premises at the engine seam: the poller believed a summary, and `end_call` had no contract to disagree with** | Three fixes from the voice-path deep dive (`docs/evidence/deepdive-voice.md`). **(1) `_pipeline_settled` read what a call was OWED off a `list_executions` row.** `_expected_artifacts` turns an absence in the engine's record into "nothing was owed" — sound for `get_execution`, unproven for a listing row, which the contract itself calls a summary (`VoiceEngine.get_execution`: "the poller's listing rows are summaries"). Bolna publishes no OpenAPI spec, so whether `GET /executions` rows are as rich as `GET /executions/{id}` is UNVERIFIED, and the conformance suite cannot fail on it either: `FakeEngine.list_executions` builds rows with the same `_snapshot_from` as its fetch, and the Bolna stub returned whole `BOLNA_COMPLETED` documents. Under the premise being wrong the failure is silent and total for one population — a completed call whose pipeline died, on an agent with no extraction schema and a tenant with no CRM endpoint, implies nothing, reads `settled` forever, and is never transcribed, metered or invoiced with no alert anywhere. The probe now confirms an EMPTY expectation set against the authenticated read before believing it, bounded by three conditions (`not expected` — the row carried neither cost nor transcript; `not any(present)` — the database holds nothing either, so a call whose pipeline ran is answered from its own artefacts with no vendor request; `late` — past `PIPELINE_STALL_AFTER`), which costs a genuinely silent call a handful of reads over its whole life rather than one per tick. A failing read PROPAGATES into `reconciliation_probe_incomplete`, which already reports repairs as a floor. **(2) `VoiceEngine.end_call` had no docstring, no stated semantics and no conformance clause, and the adapters disagreed underneath it**: `BolnaEngine` POSTs `/executions/{id}/stop` and `CartesiaEngine` POSTs `/agents/calls/{id}/end`, both surfacing the vendor's 404 as `engine_rejected`, while `FakeEngine` shrugged — so the offline pipeline (DEV-SETUP §3) reported a hang-up nobody performed. The contract now states the answer (RAISE — symmetric with `transfer`, deliberately not with `delete_agent`, because this method's caller is a control plane whose one observable failure is claiming to have stopped a call it did not), `FakeEngine` raises, and a clause pins it for all four subjects. Both vendor stubs' stop/end routes are now stateful for the reason their GET routes already are — a 200 for every id made the clause unfailable — and each carries a MARKED ASSUMPTION that the 404 is inferred, not captured (pilot gate 2). **(3) `_persist_transcript`'s upsert was partial**: `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE SET text, text_redacted` left `speaker`, `lang`, `start_ms`, `end_ms` at the first run's values and left the tail of a longer earlier reading in place, so a re-drive produced rows nobody spoke. Reachable through OUR parser, not only a fickle vendor: `bolna.parse_transcript` indexes by POSITION and drops a leading line it cannot attribute, so two fetches disagreeing about the opening line are off by one for the whole call and every turn inherits the other reading's speaker — and `speaker` drives the speaker-aware extractor (the reason `SAMPLE_TURNS` has the CALLER ask to book), the client's transcript view and the QA sample. The upsert now refreshes every column and deletes what this reading does not claim, matched on the indices written rather than on a length (contiguity is a property of today's parsers, not of `TranscriptTurn`). | **Each was a premise nothing could see.** (1) and (2) are the same defect class the conformance suite exists to catch, hiding in the two places the suite does not look: a method with no clause, and a field both adapters happen to fill. CLAUDE.md's rule is that an unverified vendor behaviour is a gate or a marked assumption, never a silent premise — the poller's richness assumption was a silent premise carrying D-31's entire guarantee of record, and `end_call`'s was one carrying the only observable failure a control-plane hang-up has. (3) is the "half-written row" class: an upsert that refreshes some columns of a row it is replacing is a fabrication that type-checks. **WHAT IT COSTS:** (1) adds at most one `get_execution` per bare completed call per tick past the grace window, for the ~3 ticks the 30-minute listing window admits it; the conditions are written so a healthy call never reaches the vendor at all. **WHAT CLOSES THE REMAINING UNKNOWNS, by name:** OPERATIONS §2 gate 6 records what a real Bolna listing row actually carries — if it is fetch-quality, (1) becomes belt-and-braces and stays; gate 2 records whether the vendor refuses a stop for an execution it is not running, which is what turns the stubs' marked assumption into a captured contract. Both need a Bolna account, which is the external blocker. |
| D-188 | **The production image had an empty virtualenv, the `.env` the deploy demands was one `Settings()` could not load, and the nginx floor was two releases below what the config needs — a first-deploy readiness audit, executed rather than read** | Five findings from walking the whole first-deploy path with the commands actually run, and their fixes. **(1) `Dockerfile` called `uv sync` without `--all-packages` in both phases.** The workspace root is `package = false` with `dependencies = []`, so uv resolved the root's empty list, installed nothing and exited 0: the shipped image's `site-packages` held THREE entries and not one third-party package. No service could start, `alembic upgrade head` could not run, and all three `scripts.*` modules `vps-deploy.sh` invokes through `compose run` would have died on their first import. Measured both ways: 3 entries before, 127 after. `--group errors` is added in the same line, because DEPLOYMENT §8's per-host `uv sync --all-packages --group errors` is an instruction the containerized architecture cannot obey (§2 puts no Python on the host; the venv is an image layer owned by a non-root user), so `sentry-sdk` was unreachable and `SENTRY_DSN` could never be more than a setting. The uv image is also pinned by digest, closing §4d item 1. **(2) `Settings` refused the prescribed `.env`.** `model_config` is `env_file='.env', extra='forbid'`, and pydantic-settings applies `forbid` to keys read from the DOTENV FILE — so the three object-store credentials that DEPLOYMENT §6 requires, that `vps-deploy.sh::preflight` ABORTS without, and that `check_deploy_env` validates, made `Settings()` raise three `extra_forbidden` errors for every process whose cwd is the deploy root. `calevate_shared.config` gains `SDK_OWNED_ENV_KEYS` and a `DotEnvSettingsSource` subclass that drops exactly those keys; `check_deploy_env.OBJECT_STORE_CREDENTIALS` aliases the constant and `check_env_parity.SDK_ENV_KEYS` asserts against it. `tests/deploy_env_file_loadable_test.py` is the regression, run red first. **(3) nginx.** `infra/nginx/` uses the standalone `http2 on;` directive, which appeared in nginx **1.25.1**; §2 said `nginx >= 1.24` and §1 says Ubuntu 22.04 (1.18) — on 1.24 `nginx -t` answers `unknown directive "http2"` and NO server block loads. §2 now says 1.25.1 with the reason, and `preflight_plan` refuses below `NGINX_MIN_VERSION` before anything is built. **(4) §2's "Python is NOT needed on the host" is false** for `scripts/backup/app-python.sh` — `notify.sh` defaults its sink to `alert-to-app.sh`, which exits 78 without the host venv, so every backup alarm reaches journald and nobody else — and for `scripts/bootstrap_admin.py`, whose documented `uv run` form cannot execute on the VPS at all. Both corrected; the bootstrap gains its container invocation. **(5) `AUDIT_CHAIN_SECRET` is a bootstrap ordering**: the first admin's audit row cannot be signed without it, it is normally console-managed, and the console needs the admin — so the first run takes it from the environment. Documented at the command. Plus `docs/evidence/deploy-readiness.md` (the executed audit, VERIFIED/BROKEN/ABSENT per step) and `runbooks/first-deploy.md` (the operator procedure, every command in it run). | **WHY ONE ROW.** Every finding is the same defect and DEPLOYMENT §4d names it: nothing here had ever been run. Each artefact is individually careful — the preflight's refusals are excellent, the migration ordering argument is right, the nginx config is the modern spelling rather than the deprecated one — and each was wrong in the one way that only execution reveals. **The empty venv is the proof of the class**: a `uv sync` that installs nothing exits 0 and looks exactly like a cache hit, the repository's own README, CI workflow and DEPLOYMENT §3 all say `--all-packages`, and the single file that builds the production artefact did not. **WHAT WAS VERIFIED BY RUNNING IT, because that is the whole value of this row**: the builder stage builds and every COPY resolves; `alembic upgrade head` from an EMPTY database reaches a single head (`c7a1e93d40b8`, 62 tables, 48 FORCE-RLS'd) and `check_rls_coverage` passes against it; the api boots under `APP_ENV=prod` and `/healthz/live` 200 / `/healthz` 200 / `/healthz/ready` 503 is honest; `check_deploy_env` correctly refused a loopback DSN and went green when fixed; `vps-deploy.sh --dry-run` reaches its plan for api/voice-runtime/workers/nginx; `bootstrap_admin` runs end to end BOTH on the host and through the image, and with no mail provider it prints the setup link to stdout and says `email sent: NO` — so **email delivery does NOT block the first login**, which was the standing assumption and is wrong; and the rendered nginx set is `test is successful` on 1.27 and `[emerg]` on 1.24. **WHAT IS STILL UNVERIFIED AND IS SAID PLAINLY**: the runtime image stage never built here (`deb.debian.org` and ghcr's blob CDN are refused by this environment's egress policy), so `curl`, the non-root user and the compose healthchecks are unexercised; nothing has touched a VPS, a domain, Cloudflare or R2; `terraform validate` still has never run; and the backup drill OPERATIONS §8 ticks still has never run — `bash -n` passes on all twelve shell scripts and `systemd-analyze verify` finds no directive errors, which is syntax and not a restore. **THE VERDICT THIS ROW EXISTS TO RECORD: not deployable before these fixes; deployable to a first attended deploy after them, and the remaining blockers are external** — a provisioned VPS, DNS, a Cloudflare zone, an R2 bucket with a backup-scoped token, a non-Cloudflare offsite target, a verified Resend sender domain and a Sentry project. |
| D-189 | **An erased account went on collecting callers, a caller's opt-out was the client's to delete, and the owner of a closed account was told they were a stranger to it** | The five end-to-end journeys walked as a person rather than module by module, hunting the seams BETWEEN steps (`docs/evidence/deepdive-journeys.md`). Three defects fixed, each proven by execution before the fix. **(1) Hard rule 5, on the most ordinary path there is.** `compliance.service.add_to_dnc` wrote `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, which is right for the row and wrong for `source`: somebody at the client pastes a number into the do-not-call page (`manual`, deletable, and it has to be — a mistyped digit must be fixable), that same person later rings in and says "stop calling me", and `record_call_optout` finds the row already there and changes nothing. The entry keeps `source = 'manual'`, `dnc.is_removable` returns True, the screen offers the delete button and `remove_entry` honours it — so the caller's opt-out is deleted by the account it was made against, with a `consent_ledger` row still saying they withdrew. That is "can never be removed by anyone" failing, and TCCCPR's ninety-day bar on re-soliciting an opted-out subscriber with it. The conflict now UPGRADES, monotonically: a client-deletable source is rewritten to an incoming non-deletable one and nothing else ever is, so a `manual` add can never weaken a `call_optout` row. `added_at` is untouched — the suppression has been continuous. `DNC_REMOVABLE_SOURCES` moves to `compliance/models.py` because `service.py` cannot import `dnc.py` (`dnc` → `ingest.service` → `compliance.service`); `dnc.REMOVABLE_SOURCES` re-exports it, so there is still one definition. **(2) The tenant erasure stopped what the account HELD and not what it could ACQUIRE.** `engine_agent_routes` is the one bridge from the voice platform's id space into ours, the vendor's agent is still configured and the DID still points at it — so the first inbound call after the certificate re-created a `calls` row, its transcript, its extraction, its lead and an archived engine payload, under a tenant whose certificate says it is erased and whose own people are locked out. Personal data with no purpose and no reader is DPDP §8(7) on its own; a certificate false within minutes of being signed is worse. The erasure now withdraws the routing in its own transaction, counts it on the certificate, and the certificate states in words that the agents remain configured at the voice platform and the numbers still route to them. `engine_agent_route_withdrawn` distinguishes that silence from a mis-provisioned agent, because they are different jobs for whoever is paged. **(3) `core/auth.py` told the owner of a closed account they were not a member of it** — false, on their own account, in a product whose offboarding flow hands them an export of the very data that screen shows. Zero rows is two facts; the closed one now answers `account_closed` with a next step, and a genuine stranger still gets the neutral refusal. Plus: FLOWS §5's "Recurrence is NOT built" (it is, and routed), and consent provenance whose summary, docstring and refusal said "draft only" while the statement accepts `scheduled`. | **WHY `delete_agent` WAS NOT CALLED FROM THE ERASURE.** It exists and is idempotent by contract, and it is a third-party round trip inside the one transaction that must not half-commit — a slow vendor would become a rolled-back erasure. The vendor-side deletion stays `unconfirmed_pending_vendor_api`, which is what the certificate has always claimed and all it can. **WHY CHURN WAS LEFT ALONE.** Churn also leaves the agent live and the number never released (FLOWS §9's "number released or ported" has no implementation anywhere), and the two states differ in what a wrong answer costs: an erasure carries a document asserting destruction, so continuing to collect makes it false; churn ends a commercial relationship, the data stays for its retention period by design, and withdrawing the routing there would leave a caller talking to an AI with no record kept — a worse position, and a founder's call rather than mine. **Externally blocked** on a telephony-provider account and a voice-platform account (OPERATIONS §2), which is what turns "release the number" into an API call an offboarding job can make. **CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED**: the retention sweep and the outbox dispatcher enumerate tenants unfiltered on `active`, so the countdown FLOWS §9 promises survives the withdrawal; the drift sweeps do stop, which is right for agents being abandoned. **LEFT OPEN AND NAMED**: `publish_agent` asks nothing about the organisation's lifecycle, so an operator with a hand-typed uuid can put a churned or erased client's agent back on the phone. The honest fix moves `admin.service.assert_account_open` to a module both realms already depend on and migrates its two callers; it was not done here because reaching it from `agents/service.py` today risks an import cycle through `admin/`, and a second copy of the predicate is the drift this repo pays for. |
| D-190 | **The invitation token is emailed to the invitee and returned to nobody — D-185's residual, closed** | `POST /v1/invitations` enqueues `invite_password` through `authn.service.enqueue_invitation_email` in the request's OWN transaction (one fate for the row and its mail), and `InvitationCreatedOut.token` is replaced by `delivery`. The team screen shows "Invitation sent to p••••@…" instead of printing a link; `auth_email._body` now points at `/auth/accept-invitation` rather than the legacy `/invite` redirect, because this template is what MINTS links now and D-177's rule is that new links name the surviving page. Every test that used to read the token off the 201 reads it out of the queued email instead — the same path a real invitee's link travels, which makes those redemptions end-to-end checks of delivery rather than of a field the API echoed back. | **WHY.** D-185 found that an owner of any tenant could invite an address they do not control, read the token out of their own 201 and redeem it — taking the one global `users` row for that address. D-185 stopped the ESCALATION (the real person's redemption is refused rather than hijacked) and could not stop the SQUAT, because the squat lives for exactly as long as anyone but the invitee can see the token. It named the closure precisely: email the token instead of returning it. `InvitationCreatedOut.token`'s own docstring had said the same thing since it was written — *"when that job exists this field should stop being returned, because a token that is displayed is a token that can be pasted into the wrong window"* — and the job (`deliver_auth_email`, D-170) had existed for two slices with an `invite_password` kind already in its subject table and nothing sending it. **WHAT IT COSTS.** An invitation now depends on email delivery, which is an external blocker (OPERATIONS §2, Resend domain verification) — the same one the first administrator already depends on, so it widens no surface that was not already there, and until a transport exists the link is read out of the outbox exactly as the bootstrap link is read off the terminal. **THE TEST THAT REPLACED THE RESIDUAL IS WRITTEN FROM THE ATTACKER'S SIDE ON PURPOSE**: the shared `_invite` helper now reads the outbox, which is a database privilege no caller has, so a squat test built on it would prove nothing — it would grant itself the very access the fix removes. The closure test calls the route and inspects only the response, and additionally refuses any credential-shaped value under a different key, so a future field cannot smuggle the secret back. |
| D-191 | **The facet rail took the same lens as the table it describes, so the two stopped counting different populations** | `POST /v1/leads/facets` takes `LeadLensIn` — the same body the table and the export take, minus the page — and the GET now REFUSES `search` (422 `search_must_be_posted`) rather than ignoring it. `useLeadFacets` sends the term and keys on the body via `lensKey`. Registered in `tests/authz_audit_test.READS_SHAPED_AS_POSTS` beside `/v1/leads/search` with the same reason. | **WHY.** D-181 moved the Leads table's search into a body because the term is matched against `phone_e164` and a query string is written to nginx's access log (hard rule 6). It left the rail on the GET, so the only safe client dropped the term — and a rail counting the WIDER set is two numbers on one screen describing different populations: the table shows 1 hot lead matching "9876" and the rail says 12. That is the kind of thing a client reports as a bug in their data rather than in our UI, and it was recorded honestly as a residual rather than hidden, which is why it was still findable. **WHY REFUSING BEATS IGNORING** on the GET: ignoring a filter WIDENS the set, and this repo already made that asymmetry a rule for dropped column filters — a widened lead set is a mailed contact list. |
| D-192 | **Two rules the repo had written down and the database was never told, and a migration that took a table down to install one of them** | Three findings in the schema itself, plus the lock discipline the third one taught. **(1) `dnc_list`: a tenant session could destroy a platform-wide suppression in two statements.** `e4f2a86b13d7` closed DELETE and CLEARED update in writing — "UPDATE was already safe and was checked rather than assumed" — having probed only `SET source = 'hijacked'`, which leaves the row GLOBAL and so fails `WITH CHECK` for a tenant session and proves nothing about the update that MOVES it. `SET tenant_id = <me>, scope = 'tenant'` satisfies `WITH CHECK` exactly, because the new row IS a legitimate row for that tenant; the row is then yours to delete. Measured as `calevate_app` against a scratch database migrated base→head: `DELETE 0` (e4f2a86b13d7 working), `UPDATE 1`, `DELETE 1`, suppression gone. Migration `e7b45c19a308` adds `dnc_list_update_scope`, RESTRICTIVE FOR UPDATE, predicate character-for-character its DELETE sibling's, `WITH CHECK` omitted so PG16 uses `USING` for both. **(2) `TenantMixin` claimed a foreign key it never created.** Its comment said "FK by name … ondelete RESTRICT: offboarding is an explicit workflow (FLOWS §9), never a cascade" and the `mapped_column()` call named no `ForeignKey`; 41 tenant tables hand-write the column and were fine, the two that trusted the mixin (`qa_reports`, `qa_call_samples`) had none, so the next `--autogenerate` proposed DROPPING `fk_qa_*_tenant_id_organizations`. Its `index=True` was the mirror error — three indexes the models declared and the schema has never had, proposed for CREATion against a budget DATA-MODEL §10 decides by measurement. **(3) `organizations.plan_tier` had a `CheckConstraint` in the model since D-39 and no constraint in the table**: `f170dbce6f47` added the column as a bare `sa.String()`, and a model `CheckConstraint` is a DDL instruction SQLAlchemy never evaluates, so `INSERT … plan_tier = 'enterprise_platinum'` succeeded. Installed in the same migration; `billing/models.PLAN_TIERS`, a dead identical copy nothing imported, is deleted. **(4)** Both statements in that migration take `AccessExclusiveLock`, so it carries `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '5s'`. Tests: `tests/dnc_global_scope_rls_test.py` (7, two red pre-fix and five controls green), `tests/orm_schema_fidelity_test.py` (4, three red pre-fix). | **WHY NOTHING CAUGHT ANY OF THEM, which is the same sentence three times.** `rls_sweep_test` asks "can tenant A touch tenant B's rows", and a global row belongs to NO tenant — `tenant_id IS NULL` is outside every probe there by construction, and `dnc_list` is the only table in this schema whose `USING` mentions it. `check_metadata_columns` judges `add_column`/`remove_column` only, and argues that scope well, so `add_index` and `remove_fk` had no reader. And `compare_metadata` does not diff CHECK constraints in EITHER direction, so an ORM check that was never migrated is invisible to every guard in the tree. Each gap is a guard whose stated scope was honest and whose complement nobody owned; `tests/orm_schema_fidelity_test.py` now owns the complement, narrowly — only the `add_*` direction, because the mirror is the legitimate half `check_metadata_columns` describes, and it pins that the mirror stays non-empty so the file cannot quietly widen into the guard nobody keeps. **(4) WAS FOUND BY CAUSING IT.** The first version of `e7b45c19a308` ran a bare `ALTER TABLE … ADD CONSTRAINT` against the shared development database; two sibling sessions sat `idle in transaction` holding `AccessShare` on `organizations` from an ordinary SELECT, the ALTER queued for `AccessExclusive`, and because PostgreSQL's lock queue is FIFO every statement behind it queued too — eleven observed in `pg_stat_activity`, including the compliance gate's own `SELECT plan_tier FROM organizations WHERE id = $1`. Lock levels then measured from `pg_locks` on 16.15: `ADD CONSTRAINT` and `ADD CONSTRAINT … NOT VALID` both `AccessExclusiveLock`, `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` `ShareUpdateExclusiveLock`. **Rejected: the NOT VALID/VALIDATE split** — the window it shrinks is the SCAN, the scan on this table is microseconds, and inside one transaction the lock `NOT VALID` takes is held to COMMIT anyway, so it only pays off across an `autocommit_block()` that would trade atomicity for nothing. `lock_timeout` is the fix that matches the actual hazard, and it proved itself on the retry: `LockNotAvailable` after 5s, `alembic_version` untouched, nobody blocked. **NOT FIXED, NAMED**: no other migration in the tree sets `lock_timeout`, and several take `AccessExclusive` on `calls`, `leads` and `organizations` — retrofitting 72 applied revisions changes nothing about databases already migrated, so the value is in the next one and the pattern now exists with its measurement attached. Also named: 33 FK child columns have no index leading with that column, six of them children of `calls`, which is unreachable today because nothing hard-deletes a call (`workers/retention.py` says so at line 662) and is re-opened by the first path that does. **CLEARED BY EXECUTION, not reading**: the whole chain base→head→base→head produces a schema byte-identical to a pristine migrate (`pg_dump -s` diff of 0 lines including ACLs), `downgrade base` leaves no table, function or enum type behind, all 8 `APPEND_ONLY_TABLES` carry `ENABLE ALWAYS` triggers, `compare_type`/`compare_server_default` report zero type and zero nullability divergences, no naive timestamp exists and every UUID primary key defaults to `uuid7`. |
| D-193 | **A tenant could NAME a neighbour's row in something it wrote — RLS does not cover a foreign key, because PostgreSQL checks those with row security bypassed** | `apps/api/db/ownership.py` is one primitive, `assert_visible(session, ref, row_id)`, that resolves a caller-supplied id under the caller's OWN RLS session before anything is stored and answers 404 (never 403). Four write paths use it: `campaigns.create_campaign` (agent, phone_number, dlt_template), `compliance.consent.record_messaging_consent` (call), and the two that already had a local copy of the same guard — `kb.submit_source` and `ingest.create_lead_source` — which MOVED onto it in the same change rather than becoming the third and fourth spelling. Reference kinds are a `Literal` and the SQL is a dict of literal strings, never a table-name parameter (`scripts/check_raw_sql.py`). `None` is a deliberate no-op: `number_id` and `dlt_template_id` are nullable and a half-filled draft must still save. `tests/cross_tenant_reference_test.py` drives all three attacks over HTTP and pins the two non-vacuity halves; `tests/adversarial_pass_test.py`'s IDOR sweep gained the six `{id}` routes it did not address, and a per-route `headers` element so the two that demand an `Idempotency-Key` reach the tenant check instead of asserting 400. | **WHY.** A policy's `WITH CHECK` enforces the `tenant_id` OF THE ROW BEING WRITTEN, so tenant B cannot forge a row into tenant A — but the foreign key inside B's own row is validated against the whole table, so B's row may point at A's agent, A's DLT-registered calling number, A's approved voice template or A's call, and the INSERT succeeds.
