Proven over HTTP with a valid tenant-B owner session: all three answered `201 Created`, and the foreign-agent campaign was then visible in B's own list and answered 200 on its detail route while its launch-check answered 404 "Campaign not found" (`_campaign_facts` INNER JOINs `agents`) — a row the client owns, can see and can never explain. **THE CONSENT ONE IS WHY THIS IS A FIX AND NOT A NOTE**: `consent_ledger` is in `APPEND_ONLY_TABLES` (hard rule 4), so an opt-in evidenced by a conversation the tenant was never party to is a DPDP record that can never be corrected, only compensated by a second row that still cannot say the first lied about whose call it was. **WHAT IT WAS NOT**: no dial and no disclosure was reachable — the launch gate's joins run under the caller's own session (a foreign number reads back as `number_missing`) and `check_dispatch` scopes the agent with an explicit `AND tenant_id = :tid`, so every consumer failed closed. What survived was the STORED reference, one un-scoped `JOIN` away from disclosure — and `campaigns/service.py:1197` already carried a hand-written `AND p.tenant_id = c.tenant_id` on one such join, i.e. the hazard was being patched at the read side one query at a time instead of at the source. **WHY ONE MODULE.** The check existed twice already and `kb`'s docstring stated the PostgreSQL mechanism correctly; two more write paths shipped without it, which is how a third copy comes to be missing. Evidence and the exact requests: `docs/evidence/deepdive-attack.md`. |
| D-194 | **Publishing an agent asks whether the account is still open, and the predicate moved to where both realms can reach it** | `assert_account_open` moved from `admin/service.py` to a new `apps/api/tenancy/lifecycle.py` that imports nothing but `core` and `db`; the two admin call sites migrated in the same change rather than pointing at a re-export. `agents.service.publish_agent` now calls it FIRST — before the row lock and before the engine — so a refusal costs one indexed read and leaves no agent at the vendor with nothing pointing at it. `tests/publish_lifecycle_test.py`: churned and soft-deleted are refused `account_closed`, suspended and open still publish, and removing the one call line turns exactly the two refusals red. | **WHY.** D-189 found that `publish_agent` asked nothing about the organisation, so an operator with a hand-typed uuid could put a CHURNED or ERASED client's agent back on the phone — answering calls and writing fresh `calls` rows with raw caller numbers against a tenant on a retention clock, and, after an erasure, under a signed certificate saying that data is gone. It is the same defect D-189 fixed inside the erasure path, reached from the other end: erasure now withdraws the routing, and this stops anything putting it back. Every other key-minting surface already asked; this was the loudest and the one that did not. **WHY THE MOVE RATHER THAN AN IMPORT.** `tenancy/signup.py` already imports `admin.service`, so `agents/` → `admin/` would have added the arrow that closes the cycle; D-189 recorded exactly that as what blocked it. A module owning only the predicate has no such edge. **WHAT `suspended` DOES NOT DO**, asserted rather than implied: suspension stops outbound dialling and nothing else, so refusing it here would turn a billing stop into an access stop and take a paying client's inbound line down over an unpaid invoice. **TWO FIXTURE BUGS WORTH THE COMMENT THEY GOT**: the first draft wrote the lifecycle under `untenanted_session`, where `organizations`' own-id policy matches zero rows and reports success — the refusals then failed with DID NOT RAISE, which reads as the fix being absent when the fixture had written nothing. The second set `deleted_at` alone and hit `ck_organizations_deleted_implies_churned`: the database refusing a state the product cannot reach. |
| D-195 | **The liveness probe could 500 during a dependency outage, which is a restart loop caused by the check that exists to prevent one** | `LoadShedMiddleware` now asks `loadshed.is_always_allowed(path)` BEFORE `get_platform_status()` rather than inside `is_shed` after it. `is_always_allowed` is split out for that ordering; `is_shed` still calls it, so the meaning of shedding is unchanged and there is one definition. `tests/liveness_survives_a_dependency_outage_test.py` makes the status lookup raise and asserts `/healthz/live`, `/hooks/**` and `/openapi.json` still answer, plus a control asserting `/v1/leads` is NOT exempt — without which the fix could have been a global bypass that satisfied every other assertion. Removing the hoist turns exactly the two outage tests red. | **WHY.** `/healthz/live` returns a literal dict and touches nothing, by contract, because `compose.prod.yml` polls it to decide whether to KILL the container. The handler was innocent; the middleware was not — `get_platform_status` reads Redis (guarded) and falls through to `_read_durable`, a database read that is NOT guarded, so with both dependencies gone it raised before any exemption was consulted and every request 500'd. Failure mode: dependency wobbles → liveness 500s → orchestrator restarts the container → it returns into the same wobble → repeat, and the restart kills the process that would have recovered by itself once the dependency came back. **HOW IT WAS FOUND.** The D-188 deploy audit observed a 500 on `/healthz/live` with Redis unreachable, traced the instance it saw to the `Settings()` defect it was fixing, and said plainly it could not prove no other path produced it. This is the other path — found because that pass recorded an unexplained observation instead of rounding it to the cause it had already found. **WHY HOISTING BEATS WRAPPING `_read_durable` IN A `try`.** A swallowed failure there would pick a platform mode with no evidence for it: failing open accepts traffic the operator may have shed on purpose, failing closed sheds everything over a blip. The exempt paths' answer never depended on the status at all, so not asking is both correct and strictly cheaper on the busiest-polled routes in the deployment. |
| D-196 | **A page module exported a symbol that is not a route field, which `next build` refuses and no local gate ran** | `deliveryRowKeys` moved out of `app/c/[slug]/integrations/page.tsx` into `lib/leadSourceRows.ts`, and `apps/web/tests/routeModuleExports.test.ts` now scans every route module in the tree against a CLOSED `ROUTE_FIELDS` set — with a non-vacuity assertion (`modules.length > 20`) so a scan that stops matching fails instead of reporting a clean sweep of nothing. `make web-check` gained `next build`: its comment claimed the build "buys nothing this target does not already have", which this defect falsified. | **WHY IT REACHED CI.** Next.js 15's App Router type-checks each route module against a fixed export shape, so an extra export is a BUILD error and not a lint or type error in isolation — and `make web-check` ran typecheck, lint and tests, none of which compile the route manifest. The local gate could not see the failure class by construction, which is why the fix is a test that runs in `web-check` AND the build being added to it: the test catches it in a second, the build is what proves the test's premise. **THE SCAN IMMEDIATELY FOUND A SECOND OFFENDER** CI had not reached — `MFA_PROBLEM_CODES` exported from `app/admin/layout.tsx` — which is the argument for deriving the check rather than fixing the one file. **PREMISE LATER CORRECTED BY MEASUREMENT (D-222):** this entry's original wording claimed `next build` type-checks page and layout modules alike. It does for every page and for a SERVER layout, and not for a client-component layout — Next 15.5.21 emits no validator for those, measured by diffing `.next/types/app/**`. The guard already covered the case; only the stated reason was wrong, and a guard whose premise is wrong is one somebody deletes when the premise is disproved. |
| D-197 | **`_env_file=None` did not mean no dotenv, so a process that disabled its `.env` read it anyway** | `Settings.settings_customise_sources` builds its filtered `_AppDotEnvSource` from the dotenv source pydantic-settings HANDED it — copying `env_file`, encoding, case sensitivity, prefix, delimiter and the empty/none handling off that instance — rather than from `cls.model_config`. `tests/deploy_env_file_loadable_test.py` pins the property directly, and skips rather than passing when no repository `.env` exists, because a green assertion about an absent file proves nothing. | **WHY.** D-188 swapped the dotenv source so the prescribed `.env` would load (`SDK_OWNED_ENV_KEYS` had made it unloadable), and constructed the replacement from the CLASS default — which discards the caller's own `_env_file`. `_env_file=None` is the documented way for a process to say it has no dotenv, so the effect was that a production host with a leftover `.env` beside the binary would be read by a process that had explicitly disabled it. `APP_ENV` is exactly the kind of key such a file carries, and `app_env == 'local'` is one of the two facts under which `_verify_dev_token` accepts a credential (D-49) — so the blast radius is the authentication bypass D-49 exists to prevent, reintroduced through a different door. **HOW IT WAS FOUND.** `tests/app_env_required_test.py` was the only thing in the tree that describes a forgetful deploy as *`_env_file=None` plus this dict and nothing else*, and it went red in the full suite while every targeted run around D-188 stayed green — the argument for running the whole suite before believing a merge. Reverting the one line reproduces all three failures. |
| D-198 | **Three holes on the first-party authentication surface nobody had audited: an unprefixed cookie alias that gave back everything `__Host-` was bought for, a reset form that throttled only the addresses that do not exist, and the admin realm's invitation still handing an operator a live credential** | **(1) `authn/cookies.read_token` accepts ONE name per request**, chosen by `_is_secure(request)` — the same predicate `set_session_cookie` uses to decide which name to write — instead of trying `__Host-calevate_<realm>_session` and then the stripped `calevate_<realm>_session` on every request whatever the scheme. **(2) `authn/service.request_password_reset` picks its throttle key BEFORE the known/unknown branch** and spends `check` + `record_failure` identically on both, against a new `throttle.RESET_BUDGET` (5 per address per realm per 15 minutes) that is deliberately NOT `OTP_BUDGET`; `confirm_password_reset` clears it. **(3) `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{tenant_id}/invitations` mails the link** through the existing `authn.service.enqueue_invitation_email`, in the invitation's own transaction, and `InviteOut.token` becomes `delivery` — D-190's change, applied to the twin route it was not applied to. `tests/member_invitations_test.mailed_invitation_token` becomes the one way a test reads an invitation secret and `tenant_birth_test` / `onboarding_to_live_test` move onto it rather than keeping a second spelling; the admin wizard's `CreatedPanel` confirms an address instead of rendering a token. New: `tests/authn_cookie_prefix_test.py` (13), `tests/authn_reset_throttle_test.py` (4), `tests/admin_invitation_delivery_test.py` (3) — each sabotage-verified by reverting its fix and watching exactly the intended tests go red. | **WHY (1).** This module's own docstring says the prefix means "no sibling subdomain and no compromised `*.calevate.tech` host can set or overwrite it", and that was true of the prefixed name and of nothing else. A page on any `*.calevate.tech` host cannot overwrite `__Host-…`, but it can set `calevate_client_session=<its own token>; Domain=.calevate.tech; Path=/`, which the browser then attaches to `api.calevate.tech` — so the prefix stopped a sibling REPLACING the credential and did not stop it SUPPLYING one. PROVEN: over TLS, with only the unprefixed cookie present, `read_token` returned the attacker's value. End to end that is OWASP session fixation with no other defect anywhere — a signed-out visitor is silently signed in as the attacker and types their data into the attacker's tenant — and `clear_session_cookie` cannot undo it, because `delete_cookie(path="/")` expires a HOST-ONLY cookie and a `Domain=`-scoped one is a different cookie to the browser. Rotation on sign-in closes nothing here: the fixated session is never the victim's to rotate. **Rejected: preferring the prefixed name.** It leaves the stripped one a live credential exactly when there is no prefixed one to prefer, which is the signed-out visitor the attack targets. The cost is one re-login on a deployment that gains TLS mid-life. **WHY (2).** `throttle.pseudo_subject`'s docstring closes "does this eventually 429" on the sign-in path and the reset form had it in the mirror image, on the one surface whose entire input is a stranger's address. PROVEN: eight requests for a live address produced eight 202s and eight queued emails; six for an unknown one produced 429 `too_many_attempts`. The same asymmetry meant nothing bounded reset mail to a known address at all. **Rejected: reusing `OTP_BUDGET`** — that is the five guesses an operator has against their own emailed second factor, so charging it from an unauthenticated route would let five requests aimed at an operator's address lock that operator out of finishing a sign-in, turning a fix for an oracle into a remote denial of service against the admin realm's only second factor. **NOT FIXED, NAMED AND MEASURED**: the wall clock still differs — 8.1ms known against 2.4ms unknown, a 3.4x ratio and a 5.7ms gap, because the known path invalidates, inserts a token, enqueues mail and appends to the audit chain. The docstring used to claim the two paths did "the same quantity of work", which was never true and is the reason nobody measured; it now states the number. Equalising it would mean handing an unauthenticated caller a 20-30ms Argon2 verification they have no reason to earn, i.e. a DoS lever bought to close a gap smaller than ordinary internet jitter. **WHY (3).** D-185 stopped a squatted address becoming somebody else's account and said outright it could not stop the squat, naming the emailing of the token as what closes it; D-190 did that on the client realm and left the admin twin returning the raw token and mailing nothing. An operator is a narrower attacker than "any tenant owner" — which is why the first pass stopped there — and not no attacker: `admin:tenants` is held by every `operator`, an invitation may name any address, and redeeming one takes the GLOBAL `users` row for that address under `uq_users_email_lower` with a password the redeemer chose. The half nobody had called a defect at all is the seam: the invitee was mailed nothing, so the flow that onboards every client depended on a human copying a credential out of an API response. **WHAT THIS AUDIT DID NOT FIND**, recorded because a negative result from a real attempt is evidence: session lifecycle, rotation and reuse detection, the credential's entropy and storage, Argon2 parameters, the MFA gate and its two copies, the realm boundary, the invitation CAS and the frontend's fail-closed defaults are all sound as written — see `docs/evidence/deepdive-authn.md` for what was attempted on each and what is still open. |
| D-199 | **The background fleet is checked three ways, and the third way is the one nothing could catch at runtime** | `scripts/check_job_wiring.py` computes, off the tree and off the live `WorkerSettings`, the three-way agreement between job functions DEFINED under `apps/workers`, names REGISTERED with the worker, and names an `enqueue`/`enqueue_outbox`/`enqueue_outbox_once`/`job_id_for` call site actually says. Any of the three disagreeing fails the gate; so does a job argument the scan cannot resolve unless it is recorded in `DYNAMIC_ENQUEUE_SITES` with a reason (there is exactly one, the outbox drain), and so does a scan matching less than its floor — `check_wiring.blind_spots()` (D-176) is the precedent, followed rather than re-invented. Wired into `make guardrails` and CI. `tests/job_registration_test.py` is rewritten from a duplicate scan into the negative controls for this one. | **`check_wiring` declines ARQ by name** ("No ARQ cron check") and the old test asked ONE of the three questions off a hand-maintained regex over `*_JOB*` constants — its own docstring had already recorded three shapes it had silently stopped seeing. Two scans of one question is the defect CLAUDE.md names even when both work. **The third shape is the expensive one and it is invisible at runtime**: read from the installed `arq` 0.28.0, `Worker.run_job` answers an unknown `function_name` with `logger.warning('job %s, function %r not found')` and a terminal `job_failed(...)` — no retry, no alert, and nothing in `apps/` or `scripts/` reads an arq result key. The outbox row says `published`, every screen says queued, the side effect never happened. That is exactly how `notify_hot_lead_whatsapp` and `escalate_campaign_contact` shipped. The sweep found the tree CLEAN in all three columns; what did not exist was anything that would keep it clean. Sabotage-verified on the real tree for all three shapes. |
| D-200 | **arq's own two terminal failures reach an operator, and `deliver_auth_email` gets the retry ladder it claimed to have** | `apps/workers/settings.py` gains `ARQ_TERMINAL_MESSAGES` + `install_arq_terminal_alerter()`, a `logging.Handler` on the `arq.worker` logger that turns arq's `function %r not found` and `max retries %d exceeded` warnings into `alert("WORKER_TERMINAL", ...)`; installed in `startup()`. `apps/workers/auth_email.py` moves to the fleet's standard shape — `raise Retry(defer=...)` on a 10s/30s ladder, then `alert("WORKER_DELIVERY", "auth_email_exhausted")` on the last attempt. New: `tests/worker_terminal_alert_test.py` (incl. a real `arq.worker.Worker` burst on its own queue name) and `tests/auth_email_delivery_test.py`. | **`WorkerSettings` argued that the alert is a property of each JOB, not of the queue, and that is right wherever the job's code runs.** Read off `Worker.run_job`, two terminal paths return BEFORE `on_job_start` is awaited — an unregistered name, and `job_try > max_tries` — so on both of them no per-job `alert()` can exist. The second is not exotic: every job that raises `Retry` to its budget ends there, and so does any cron cancelled three times at `job_timeout`, i.e. `apply_retention` gone until tomorrow with a log line. It is the residue the nine explicit `max_tries=WORKER_MAX_TRIES` crons do NOT cover — they buy the ladder, and this is what happens when the ladder runs out. NOT a second DLQ (rejected for "one way per problem" and because a durable store beside the outbox is what P6.5 declined): one more call site of the one `alert()`, touching neither Postgres nor Redis so it still works when the queue is what broke. **`deliver_auth_email` was worse than unalarmed — it had no ladder at all**: it raised `RuntimeError`, and arq 0.28 retries for `Retry`/`RetryJob`/`CancelledError` and nothing else, so `max_tries = 3` never applied and a reset link lost to one slow minute at the mail provider was lost for good while the sign-in screen truthfully said an email was on its way. Its docstring claimed both the ladder and "the DLQ catches what is left". Ladder is 10s+30s because an OTP expires in ten minutes and somebody is watching the screen. |
| D-201 | **A published outbox row keeps no payload** | `reliability.service.mark_outbox_published` sets `payload = '{}'::jsonb` in the SAME statement as the status flip. `failed` rows are untouched — that is the outbox dead-letter queue and `POST /v1/ops/outbox/replay` re-publishes from it. `tests/outbox_payload_scrub_test.py` is the assertion; `tests/outbox_dispatcher_test.py` moves its cleanup from `payload->>'marker'` to ids, because a published row no longer has a marker. | **`authn/service._enqueue_auth_email` bounded a live credential's exposure on the sentence "the row is deleted on successful dispatch", and the sentence was false.** Publishing UPDATEs the row; nothing removed it until `retention.prune_reliability_tables` reached it at `RELIABILITY_PRUNE_AFTER` — NINETY DAYS with a plaintext password-reset token, invite link or OTP in a jsonb column. A security argument resting on a premise the code does not implement is worse than none, because it stops the next reader looking. **D-190 widened it while this was being written**: routing every member invitation through `_enqueue_auth_email` made that path the ONLY way an invitation reaches anybody, so the 72-hour invite token joined the reset token in the ninety-day column. The same statement discharges a wider obligation `retention.py` had already written down: that column also carries "a lead's name, phone number and call summary … outside every retention policy a tenant can set, and outside the DPDP erasure path", and pruning bounded the GROWTH without shortening the EXPOSURE. Scrubbing at publish takes it from ninety days to one dispatch tick, for every job, with no new table, sweep or schedule. What survives is what the 90-day floor exists to answer — `job`, `job_id`, `published_at`, `attempt_count` — because the CONTENT of a delivery is `webhook_deliveries`' business, which has a tenant retention arm. One statement and not two: a row updated twice can be published-but-unscrubbed if the process dies between them, which is the exposure again with extra steps. |
| D-202 | **The alarm vocabulary is derived and checked in both directions, because three §4 promises had no call site and 44 real alarms had no document** | New `scripts/check_alarm_wiring.py` (in `make guardrails` and CI) + `runbooks/alarm-index.md`, the index an operator reads when a code lands on their phone. The guard derives what the tree can RAISE from five shapes — a literal `alert()` code, an f-string's literal prefix, a `ProblemError` carrying a `failure_stage` (which `core/errors.py` relays verbatim), a `*_code` on a module constant or a frozen ack meter, and the host backup chain's shell alarms — parses the index, and fails on either half. It also enforces `core/alerting.py`'s own stated rule that metrics are named recorders: the three ad-hoc `metrics_log.info("metric", …)` emitters found in the tree (`speed_to_lead_seconds`, `dispatch_tick_seconds`, `campaign_dials`) became `record_*` functions and their call sites moved in the same change. Refuses when either scan matches nothing, on each road separately. Negative controls: `tests/alarm_wiring_guard_test.py`. | **WHY A GUARD AND NOT A FIX.** D-183 found three §4 alarms with no call site by READING, which is the second time a person had to; the mirror-image defect was larger and nobody had counted it — **113 codes can page a human and 44 of them appeared in no document at all**, so `retention_below_trai_floor` at 3am cost an operator a code search before they could start. Both are one defect: a promise and a capability drifting apart. **WHY THE INDEX IS A DOCUMENT AND NOT A GENERATED FILE.** A generated table cannot carry "what to do", which is the only column that matters at 3am; what is derived is the CHECK, so the prose can be wrong about the world for exactly one CI run. **WHAT IT COST TO GET RIGHT.** The dangling-name half (a backticked `snake_case` name in the operator docs that resolves nowhere) produced ten hits on its first run and eight were false: runbooks legitimately cite tests by name, and one legitimately refers to a SQL alias its own query defines. Both narrowings are in the code with the reason — `tests/` joins the corpus, a document's own fenced blocks resolve names for that document, and exactly one composed leading word may be stripped (`f"blocked_{rule}"` is a real string in a log and in no file). A guard that cries wolf about a column alias is muted long before it catches a real dangling alarm. |
| D-203 | **`campaign_complaint_spike`: five opt-outs and 10% of connected calls in 24h pauses the campaign and pages** | `apps/api/campaigns/complaint_spike.py`, asked once per running campaign per dispatch tick, in the claiming transaction beside the standing compliance gate. It counts `consent_ledger` withdrawals attributable to that campaign's completed calls, CASes `running → paused`, writes `campaign.paused` to `audit_log` with `actor_type=system`, and alerts with counts and ids only. New partial index `ix_calls_campaign_started` (migration `c4f70b1e28da`) — this is the first query in the repo to filter `calls` by campaign, and it runs every 30 seconds. Runbook: `runbooks/dnc-complaint.md` §9. | **THE THREE NUMBERS.** `MIN_OPTOUTS = 5` is TRAI's own number used as a CEILING rather than a target: five unique complaints in ten days obliges the access provider to suspend the client's outgoing service (TCCCPR Second Amendment, in force 12 Feb 2025, tightened from ten-in-seven — <https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2102413>), and everyone who files a complaint said it to the agent first, so the leading indicator is a strict superset. `RATE = 0.10` keeps the count honest at scale: a measured 10,794-call cold outbound study reports 4.1% of calls ending in a do-not-call request (<https://ccdocs.com/outbound-call-center/>), and our clients dial consented lists, so one in ten is about two and a half times worse than cold-calling strangers. `WINDOW_HOURS = 24` is one calling day (the platform window is 09:00–21:00 IST), so a spike stops today and tomorrow measures afresh. **BOTH CONDITIONS, NOT EITHER**: count alone pages a large healthy campaign, rate alone pages a campaign that dialled four people and lost one; the pair is the standard significance-floor-under-a-rate shape `ai_quota` already uses. **WHY IT PAUSES.** FLOWS §5 says "pause + notify" and the order is the point — an alert that arrives while the campaign keeps dialling watches the damage. Resuming re-pauses while the window still holds the spike, and that is intended: nothing about the campaign changed in the ten minutes after somebody pressed resume. |
| D-204 | **`engine_error_spike`: 10 failed engine requests in 5 minutes, counted in Postgres because a rate held in a process means something different per process** | `apps/api/engine/health.py` + `platform_engine_health` (migration `c4f70b1e28da`), one upserted row per (engine, minute), written from both adapters' `_request` on a 5xx and on a transport failure. `SPIKE_THRESHOLD = 3 * WORKER_MAX_TRIES + 1`. Runbook: `runbooks/calls-stopped.md` §9. | **THE THRESHOLD IS DERIVED, NOT CHOSEN.** One unlucky operation already produces `WORKER_MAX_TRIES` failures on its own, so anything at or below 3 pages for a single call having a bad afternoon; three distinct operations failing every attempt they have, plus one, is the first count a single request cannot explain. Writing it as arithmetic means widening the ladder moves the threshold with it. **WHY IT COUNTS UNREACHABLE TOO, against the doc's literal wording.** A platform that is entirely down refuses connections rather than answering 502, so a strict "5xx" reading would have been silent through the total outage and loud only through the partial one — the exact inversion an operator cannot afford. The two are separate columns (different first move) and one alarm (a dial does not care which). 429 is excluded: it is the vendor working as designed and has its own ladder. **WHY POSTGRES AND NOT REDIS.** Redis is already here and `alert_admission` already counts in it — and that counter may only ever SUPPRESS a page, while this one CREATES one. A counter that can invent a page must be as durable as the thing it reports on, and this deployment's Redis is one container with no replica. The upsert into a minute bucket, rather than a row per failure, makes the table's size a function of time (1,440 rows/engine/day, pruned to 7) instead of of how bad the outage is. |
| D-205 | **`tls_certificate_expiring` handshakes with the ORIGIN and reads the certificate nginx is actually serving — it is not a systemd timer** | `apps/workers/tls_expiry.py`, a daily ARQ cron at 04:05, connecting to `TLS_ORIGIN_ADDRESS` (new setting, default `host.docker.internal:443`) with the public hostname from `WEBHOOK_BASE_URL` in SNI, verification off, alerting at ≤ 21 days remaining and separately when the certificate cannot be read at all. Runbook: `runbooks/tls-expiry.md`. | **A DEVIATION FROM D-183, WHICH EXPECTED A SYSTEMD TIMER BESIDE THE BACKUP UNITS.** Three reasons, in order of weight. (1) A file check proves certbot WROTE a certificate; a handshake proves nginx is SERVING it, and those differ in exactly the failure `infra/nginx/README.md` §4.3 warns about — `certonly` never touches nginx, so a renewal without `--deploy-hook "systemctl reload nginx"` leaves a fresh file on disk and an expiring certificate on the wire. (2) Nothing in `infra/` has ever been installed anywhere, so a new unit there would be an alarm that has never run; a cron in an existing worker runs the day it deploys and is driven by tests in CI against a real TLS socket. (3) It needs no bind mount and no root-readable `/etc/letsencrypt/archive`. **WHY IT DOES NOT USE THE PUBLIC NAME.** Cloudflare proxies in Full (strict), so a handshake with `hooks.calevate.tech` returns CLOUDFLARE's edge certificate — which renews itself and stays valid for months after ours has died; the origin expiring shows up as a 526 while an outside-in check reports green. nginx applies its Cloudflare-only `deny all` at the HTTP layer, after the handshake, so the certificate is readable from inside the stack even though a request would be refused. **WHY VERIFICATION IS OFF.** A verifying checker raises on precisely the condition it exists to report and can then say only "something is wrong", never "it expired on the 14th". **THE THRESHOLD.** Certbot renews at a third of the lifetime remaining — 30 days for a 90-day Let's Encrypt certificate — and tries twice a day, so 21 days is about eighteen failed renewals: past any transient failure, three weeks of runway, and no later than the 20-day notice **Let's Encrypt stopped sending on 4 June 2025** (<https://letsencrypt.org/2025/01/22/ending-expiration-emails/>), which is why nothing else is watching. **DOMAIN-registration expiry stays EXTERNAL**: the registrar is the authority, the notice goes to the registrant, and the remedy is a payment no code can make. |
| D-206 | **A foreign-key child column earns an index when something SCANS it, not only when a parent cascades — five indexes, each argued and measured** | Migration `c9e2a7b41d63` adds five: `ix_usage_events_call_id (call_id) WHERE call_id IS NOT NULL`, `ix_usage_events_tenant_occurred (tenant_id, occurred_at)`, `ix_leads_tenant_recent (tenant_id, updated_at DESC, id DESC) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL`, `ix_calls_tenant_started (tenant_id, started_at DESC NULLS LAST, id DESC)`, `ix_campaign_contacts_last_call_id (last_call_id) WHERE last_call_id IS NOT NULL`. `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` in an `autocommit_block()`, reversible. Twenty-eight of the census's other columns are declined IN THE MIGRATION, by group and with reasons; `calls (from_e164)`/`(to_e164)` are declined with the number attached. `tests/hot_path_index_test.py` pins all five. | **The count was wrong and the ARGUMENT was wrong.** D-192 counted 33 unindexed FK child columns and added none, on the ground that the parent is never hard-deleted so the referential scan is unreachable — factually correct, and the wrong test. Re-derived here from `pg_constraint` + `pg_index.indkey[0]`: **34** columns, confirmed identically on a second, independently-built database, of which `leads.assigned_to` is a false positive (a PARTIAL index serves it, and a catalog census cannot see that a strict operator proves the predicate). The honest deficit is 33; four of the five taken are bought purely by application queries. **MEASURED** on 86,013 leads / 72,000 calls / 360,000 usage_events, as `calevate_app` with RLS in every plan: the post-call metering guard 25.794 ms / 3,617 buffers → **0.024 ms / 4**; `_pipeline_settled`'s EXISTS 27.812 → **0.080 ms**; the Leads page 28.454 ms and a top-N heapsort of 50,001 rows → **0.064 ms / 6 buffers with no sort node**; the CSV export 44.889 ms **with an 8,224 kB external merge to disk** → **7.843 ms, no sort**; the Calls page 38.953 → **0.096 ms**; the month rollup 33.788 → **2.058 ms**; `_settle_contact` — whose own docstring promised "one indexed lookup and stops" and reached the row through the tenant's whole contact list — 4.700 ms / 650 buffers → **0.020 ms / 2**. Three indexes lead with `tenant_id` so the isolation predicate stays an index CONDITION; the two child-key ones deliberately do not, because they probe a near-unique column and prefixing would buy no access path while making them useless to the RI check. |
| D-207 | **A tenant session could hard-DELETE its own `organizations` row; the tenancy anchor stops being tenant-writable** | Migration `d1b8f30c94a7` adds `organizations_delete_admin_only`, RESTRICTIVE FOR DELETE, `USING (current_setting('app.admin', true) = 'on')` — the same shape as `dnc_list`'s two, ANDed with the permissive policy so SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE are untouched. `tests/dispatch_scale_test.py`'s cleanup moves from `tenant_session` to `admin_session` and asserts `rowcount == 1`. `tests/organizations_delete_rls_test.py` pins both halves. | **`WITH CHECK` is not consulted on DELETE** — the third time that PostgreSQL fact has cost this schema a rule (`e4f2a86b13d7`, `e7b45c19a308`, now this). PROVEN as `calevate_app` on a childless org: `DELETE 1`. Every one of the 43 tenant tables carries `tenant_id REFERENCES organizations(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT`, so that row is what makes "this data belongs to somebody" enforceable; destroying it destroys the lifecycle record, the erasure certificate's subject and the retention countdown's anchor. It is not an incident today only because no route issues the statement and a real org has children behind RESTRICT — the protection is the FOREIGN KEYS, by accident, rather than the policy, by design, and that runs out the first time a tenant's last child is deletable through a route (several already are). **NOT `USING (false)`**: removing a mistyped prospect that never got children is a real operator task, and `admin_session()` is minted only after an admin-realm principal is verified. **The fixture moved, not the schema** — D-192 identified this hole and left it open because a test hard-deleted organizations from a tenant session; RLS filters rather than raises, so that cleanup would have gone on "succeeding" while leaking rows, which is why the rowcount is now asserted. |
| D-208 | **`make db-reset` drops the schema instead of walking the migration chain backwards** | `scripts/db_reset.py` runs `DROP SCHEMA public CASCADE; CREATE SCHEMA public` and re-grants what the drop removed; the Makefile target is now that plus `alembic upgrade head` plus the seed. Guarded on TWO independent facts — `APP_ENV=local` and a loopback DSN host — running as the owner role, with no `--force`. `tests/db_reset_test.py` pins the refusals and the recipe. | **A downgrade UNDOES revisions in order, so the DATA a database holds can refuse it**, and a developer's database always holds some. PROVEN by causing it: on a seeded database carrying one first-party operator — what `scripts/bootstrap_admin.py` writes — `alembic downgrade base` fails with `column "clerk_user_id" of relation "admin_users" contains null values` and stops MID-CHAIN, leaving `alembic_version` at `b3d9f6a2c815` with 62 tables still present. That stranded shape is not hypothetical: it is the state the shared development database was found in, where the version table and the schema disagreed and every later `upgrade head` failed on an object that already existed. `f9c2b41a8e57`'s unique index refuses the same way on a database carrying the double-credit residue. `b3d9f6a2c815`'s refusal is CORRECT and is untouched — past that revision a downgrade is a restore, not a rollback; what was wrong was routing a RESET through it. Dropping the schema is O(1) in revisions rather than O(74), cannot be refused by data, and cannot half-apply — and is what Django, Rails and Prisma all do. |
| D-209 | **The IST billing month becomes a half-open range, and two reads that grew with a tenant's data get bounded** | (1) `plans.ist_month_window(month)` returns `[start, next_start)` in UTC from the same named zone; `billing/service._IST_MONTH_WINDOW` binds it, and the three filtering call sites (`rung_seconds`, the usage-panel call count, `ai_quota._USAGE_SQL`) move to it. `_IST_MONTH` survives, narrowed to RENDERING a row's own month for `ai_quota._INSERT_USAGE`'s `RETURNING`; `ist_month_end` is derived from the new window rather than recomputing the rollover. (2) `crm.service.lead_facets` gains `LIMIT :facet_cap` with a DECLARED-FIRST ordering. (3) `agents.business_hours.count_after_hours_calls` reads the hours once instead of once per call row. | **(1)** `to_char(occurred_at AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata','YYYY-MM') = :month` is CORRECT — D-186 fixed a real timezone defect to arrive at it — and **STABLE, not IMMUTABLE**, so PostgreSQL will use it neither as an index condition nor as an index expression. No index could ever have helped it, and every money rollup read the tenant's entire metering history one row at a time: **84.021 ms / 3,829 buffers → 2.222 ms / 76** on 225,000 rows. The range keeps every sentence of D-186's argument (built in Python from the named zone, so the session's `TimeZone` still cannot reach the answer) and is half-open for the same SQL:2011 reason `plan_in_effect_sql` is. 481 money tests green. **(2)** `MAX_FACET_VALUES`' own comment says it bounds the undeclared values "which is otherwise unbounded" — it bounded what was RENDERED, one layer after the whole `GROUP BY` had been transported into a Python dict, up to eight times per page render. A facet is an enum field by DECLARATION only; the extractor writes what the model produced. The ordering is declared-first because a bare LIMIT would drop a declared value below the cap and the zero-fill would then report it as 0 — a filter claiming a value nobody has, which is worse than the unbounded query. **(3)** The tile joined `agents` to `calls` and selected the JSONB opening hours BESIDE EVERY CALL ROW, on an endpoint the dashboard polls (D-24). Two queries transport the same information with the blob sent once; what remains is one row per call in the window, bounded by `PLATFORM_LINES_TOTAL` rather than by tenant history — a ceiling with a number rather than none. |
| D-210 | **Entering a client's account takes a second factor; staying in one does not — the step-up gap D-198 named and left as a founder's call** | `POST /v1/admin/impersonation-grants` now takes `StepUpGate` + `X-Confirm-Action: view_as:<slug>` (`admin/routes.py::view_as_confirmation`), making it the 16th `step_up.require` call site and the first that guards a DOOR rather than a mutation. The grant carries a new `auth_time` claim (OIDC Core 1.0 §2) recording when the factor was proved, and `core/impersonation.renewable_grant` lets a console EXTEND a live view-as session by presenting the grant it already holds as `renew` in the mint body — inheriting `auth_time`, never restamping it — for up to `VIEW_AS_MAX_AGE` = 1 h. `admin.impersonation_started` gains `auth_time`, `renews` and `window_s`, so the ledger distinguishes an operator walking through the door from a session extending itself. Console: `lib/authn/stepUpPrompt.ts` (an external store, single-flighted) + `components/authn/stepUpPrompt.tsx` mounted once in `app/admin/layout.tsx`, `requestAdminStepUp`/`confirmAdminStepUp` in `adminAuthn.ts`, and `lib/api/admin.ts::mint` prompting and retrying once. New: `tests/impersonation_stepup_test.py` (15), `apps/web/tests/adminStepUp.test.tsx` (9) — each sabotage-verified by reverting its fix and watching exactly the intended tests go red. | **WHY THIS ROUTE.** It is the single choke point into a client's data: `_load_admin_principal` refuses every impersonated request without a grant, and grants exist only here. BACKEND-PATTERNS §7 names raw-transcript access a step-up action, and the route that serves it is CLIENT-realm (`GET /v1/calls/{id}/transcript/raw`), where `StepUp.present` is False for every caller — so the door is not merely a convenient place for the check, it is the only place it can live. One gate in front of every tenant-realm read an operator can reach, including the recording D-181 moved onto `calls:read_raw`. It does not gate a READ on a mutating permission: `admin:impersonate` stays out of `MUTATING_PERMISSIONS`, and step-up asks who is at the keyboard, not what they may do. **WHY RENEWAL RIDES, AND WHY THAT IS THE STANDARD RATHER THAN AN INVENTION.** `REAUTH_MAX_AGE` is 5 min and `GRANT_TTL` is 15, so demanding a fresh factor on EVERY mint means an emailed code roughly every fourteen minutes to stay inside one account — which is the failure `authn/stepup.py` already names in its own words, "a control that gets switched off", on the surface an operator uses for ordinary support work. The shape used instead is the one the industry converged on for assuming authority into another account's data: AWS STS `AssumeRole` takes the MFA code ONCE and returns a session credential with a bounded life, and a CHAINED role session is capped at one hour and cannot be extended past it (docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_credentials_temp_request.html; repost.aws/knowledge-center/iam-role-chaining-limit). `auth_time` is the registered OIDC claim for exactly this question, so the ceiling is enforced with no `impersonation_grants` table — the module docstring's argument against one is untouched. Effective ceiling is 1 h + `GRANT_TTL`: capping `exp` at `auth_time + VIEW_AS_MAX_AGE` would make the boundary exact and the last grant arbitrarily short, which the console reads as "stale, re-mint" — a mint loop against an INSERT-ONLY ledger (hard rule 4). **REJECTED: reusing `X-Impersonation-Grant` for the renewal input.** That header means "this request is being made INSIDE the named tenant" and `core/auth.py` reads it beside `X-Impersonate-Org`; a mint is made from the operator's own admin session and is inside nothing. RFC 8693 carries the token being exchanged in the body for the same reason. **REJECTED: splitting the gate's two halves at this call site** so the echo is demanded on renewals too — `core/stepup.py` demands them together precisely so no route ends up with one, and a live grant for this tenant is stronger evidence of both intent and presence than an echo a stolen cookie satisfies trivially. **FAIL-CLOSED BY CONSTRUCTION**: `renewable_grant` is TOTAL — absent, forged, expired, another operator's, another tenant's and an unreadable `auth_time` all return `None`, and `None` sends the caller to the STRICTER path. No input can make it return a grant `verify_grant` would refuse. **THE SEAM THAT WAS ALREADY OPEN**: the console had NO step-up prompt at all, so the fifteen pre-existing gated routes were reachable only inside the five minutes after a sign-in and an operator's only remedy was to sign out and back in. That is fixed here for all sixteen, not just for this one. **NOT FIXED, NAMED**: the ops console's writes still render a `reauthentication_required` refusal rather than raising the prompt — the transport cannot retry those POSTs, because `client.ts`'s rule is that a repeated POST needs proof the refusal executed nothing, and only this route has that proof (`step_up.require` runs before `mint_grant` and before `write_audit`; `tests/impersonation_stepup_test.py` asserts the absent start row on every refusing branch). Wiring each of those call sites to prompt-then-retry is per-route work with a per-route safety argument, not a transport rung. |
| D-211 | **BACKEND-PATTERNS §7 said raw-transcript access is a step-up action while the client realm has no second factor — the conflict is resolved by realm, not by deleting the sentence** | §7's list now reads "high-risk **admin-realm** actions" and the raw-transcript item splits in two, each true of the realm it names: on the CLIENT realm an owner reading their own transcript is `calls:read_raw` + a `transcript.read_raw` audit row in the same transaction, by design; on the ADMIN realm it is step-up, taken at the impersonation door (D-210). SECURITY-COMPLIANCE §5 gains both bullets. §7's heading and opening paragraph stop saying "Clerk owns sessions" (D-177 deleted the vendor), and the two other dangling vendor references in that document — the load-shed paragraph's `/v1/auth` aside and the ops-config bullet — are corrected to what is true. | **WHY THE DOC WAS THE WRONG HALF, AND ONLY HALF-WRONG.** `MFA_REQUIRED_REALMS` is `{"admin"}` (D-170 removed TOTP, enrolment and recovery codes), so `StepUp.present` is False for every client caller: declaring the gate on `/v1/calls/{id}/transcript/raw` would not tighten the route, it would DELETE it — a tenant owner reading their own call refused with a remediation pointing at `/v1/auth/admin/step-up`, an endpoint their realm does not have. **WHY ROLE + AUDIT IS THE RIGHT CONTROL THERE**, rather than a gap to be closed later: the threat models are not the same shape. On the admin realm the reader is an outsider to the data — our staff reading a customer's customers' conversations — and step-up defends the tenant against us and against a stolen operator credential. On the client realm the reader is the Data Fiduciary's own `owner` reading recordings of their own callers; that is the controller exercising controllership, and the control that matters is the one that makes it attributable, which is the audit row hard rule 5 already requires and `staff` never holding `calls:read_raw` (D-181 put the AUDIO on the same pair). **WHAT WOULD CHANGE THE ANSWER**: a client-realm second factor existing at all. That is a product decision about Indian SMB owners and email deliverability which D-170 already took, and reversing it is its own decision-log entry — not a consequence of this one, and not an engineering gap being deferred. **WHY THE SENTENCE WAS AMENDED RATHER THAN DELETED**: it was never wrong about the admin realm, and D-210 makes it true there for the first time; deleting it would have thrown away a correct requirement to resolve an ambiguity about which realm it addressed. |
| D-215 | **The dashboard's average call length gets a window, and the whole tile gets the index** | `crm.service.dashboard`'s counts statement moves its seven-day bound from three of its four columns onto the statement (`FROM calls WHERE started_at >= :since`), and `DashboardOut.avg_duration_s` becomes `avg_duration_s_7d`. The client dashboard's hint changes from "Completed calls only" to "Completed calls, last 7 days" — the only frontend change. New: `tests/dashboard_avg_duration_window_test.py` (5 tests). | **It was the one tile on that screen with no time bound at all**: `avg(duration_s) FILTER (WHERE status = 'completed')` over the account's whole history, on an endpoint the dashboard polls (D-24), against a table nothing ever deletes from. No index fixes an aggregate that must visit every row, which is why the db-scale pass correctly stopped at naming it — the fix changes a number the client reads. **SEVEN AND NOT THIRTY**, against the obvious default: every other bounded figure on this screen is seven days and `DashboardOut` already spells the window into `calls_7d`, `leads_new_7d`, `after_hours_captured_7d` and `daily_7d`, so a 30-day average would be a fourth window on a screen carrying three (today, 7 days, this month) and close enough to "this month" to be read as it. The thirty-day reading of the same statistic already exists on the performance tab, already windowed, with its window as a field on the response — two labelled answers on two screens rather than one unlabelled one. The rename is the client-facing half: a field that keeps its name while changing its meaning is the version of this fix that costs somebody a support call. **MEASURED** as `calevate_app` with RLS in the plan, 45,000 tenant calls: **27.165 ms / 1,017 buffers → 0.842 ms / 61 buffers**, `ix_calls_tenant_started` (D-206) turning from unusable into the access path once every term is inside the bound. Sabotage-verified. |
| D-216 | **The facet rail's ceiling is derived from its own budget, and D-209's LIMIT stops spilling 13 MB to disk** | `crm.service` gains `FACET_RAIL_BUDGET_MS = 200` and `FACET_QUERY_COST_MS = 50` (measured), and `MAX_FACET_FIELDS` becomes `FACET_RAIL_BUDGET_MS // FACET_QUERY_COST_MS` — four, from eight. `lead_facets`' aggregate moves into a `WITH counted AS MATERIALIZED (…)` with the declared-first `ORDER BY` and the `LIMIT` outside it. Three tests in `tests/lead_columns_test.py`. | **A ceiling with no enforcement is a promise.** D-209 measured one facet at 43.4 ms and recorded that eight facets is ~350 ms against a researched 200 ms budget, then left the cap at eight because no shipped template gets near it. **"Make it fit" was tried first and measured, not assumed away**: a `CROSS JOIN unnest(keys)` with a per-facet `row_number()` window is 209-221 ms with 12 MB spilled, and `GROUP BY GROUPING SETS ((1),…,(8))` is 223 ms — both ~2.8x better than eight sequential queries and **both still over budget**, because PostgreSQL has no `n_distinct` for `data ->> 'key'` on a runtime-bound jsonb key, estimates every row as its own group, and refuses to hash-aggregate. Forcing the hash shows a 111 ms floor that no shippable query can reach. So the count is bounded instead, and the refusal is one a client can act on and already renders: the first four facetable fields IN SCHEMA ORDER plus `omitted_field_count`, which the rail shows as "ask us to reorder your capture list". **The second half is a regression D-209 introduced while fixing a real unboundedness**: its `ORDER BY … LIMIT` made the planner sort FULL-WIDTH lead rows — `external merge  Disk: 13296kB` per facet per page render, 70.5-74.9 ms. Fenced off, the sort carries the extracted text, fits `work_mem`, and the same rows come back in **47.4-50.6 ms with zero temp blocks**. Sabotage-verified. |
| D-217 | **`outbox_messages.queue` stops being written — hard rule 8's first step towards dropping it** | Migration `b7e4c1a90d38` gives the column a server default; `enqueue_outbox` and `enqueue_outbox_once` stop naming it, `claim_outbox_batch` stops selecting it, and `OutboxMessageRow.queue` is deleted. `OUTBOX_FLEET` survives as the value the migration defaults to, pinned equal by test. `tests/outbox_queue_deprecation_test.py` rewritten (6 tests). **Step 2 is `ALTER TABLE outbox_messages DROP COLUMN queue`, next release, no code change beside it.** | **D-162 named exactly two ways its fork could close — the column is dropped, or a second worker fleet filters on it — and this takes the first.** The second is not a near miss: one arq worker consumes exactly one queue, so honouring the column means a second deployable for a platform with no clients (ROADMAP §6, "monolith module before new service"), and passing `_queue_name` with no worker on that queue would silently stop every notification. Nothing about that changed; what changed is that leaving a column nobody reads is not a third option. **WHY THE DEFAULT AND NOT THE DROP.** `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` §4b: `alembic upgrade head` runs against the service database BEFORE the containers are recreated, so the old image serves against the new schema for the length of the swap. With the default, old code (which names the column) and new code (which omits it) are both correct against this schema; a `DROP COLUMN` here would 500 every enqueue for those seconds, on the table carrying password-reset emails and CRM deliveries. That is the whole content of the two-step, and the second step waits on one deploy rather than on anything outside this repo. The test file's exemption for `reliability/service.py` — "the writer names the column in its INSERT; that is the point" — went with the write, so the sweep now covers the file most likely to re-introduce it. |
| D-218 | **The client directory stops asking the hold gates about accounts no gate applies to** | `admin.service.tenant_overview` selects `plan_tier` (already on the row it reads) and calls `read_tenant_holds` only for `SELF_SERVE_TIERS`, answering the new `admin.holds.NO_HOLDS` otherwise. The constant is IMPORTED from `compliance/service.py`, never re-spelled. New: `tests/admin_directory_holds_prefilter_test.py` (3 tests). | **The recorded deferral now has a number, and the largest term in it was work that could not change an answer.** 312 accounts, 1,027 ms warm — 3.3 ms each, broken down as connection checkout + `set_config` 0.90 ms, four counts 0.55 ms, **`holds` 0.95 ms**, `capped` 0.45 ms. Each blocker opens with `plan_tier_of(...) not in SELF_SERVE_TIERS -> None`, so for a `managed` account `read_tenant_holds` IS two `SELECT plan_tier FROM organizations` round trips and nothing else — re-reading, once per account, a column the directory row already carries, to reach an answer that could not have differed. **`admin/holds.py::held_tenants` — the work queue built from the same predicate — has always pre-filtered on exactly this line**, and argues at length why a filter on the CANDIDATE SET is not a second copy of the RULE; the directory did not, so two surfaces disagreed about which accounts can even be held. That is the older defect under the slow one. Measured after: **994.3 ms → 701.7 ms** on an all-managed directory (3.19 → 2.25 ms per account). **THE SHAPE IS NOT CLOSED AND THIS SAYS SO**: closing it needs either widening `calls`/`leads`/`kyc_records` for `app.admin` (hard rule 1, and `admin/holds.py` rejects it — a policy is table-scoped, so widening to COUNT rows also hands over the rows) or paging the response, which is an admin-console contract change rather than a query change. The materialized `tenant_health` table stays the named escape, now with a number to be judged against. Sabotage-verified. |
| D-219 | **The inbound webhook forensic gap stays open, on reasons D-204 did not falsify** | `integrations.service.INBOUND_REFUSAL_ALERTS` is the one list of the four refusal codes; OPERATIONS §7 and SEC-COMP §4 cite it instead of re-typing it, `WebhookDelivery`'s declaration carries the warning an investigator meets before any query, and the `duplicate` claim is corrected in all three places. New: `tests/webhook_inbound_forensics_test.py` (7 tests). No schema change. | **A deferral is only a decision while its reason is still true.** R-9 recorded that `webhook_deliveries` holds only the inbound deliveries we CLAIMED, and `integrations/service.py` deferred the fix on "a bounded, aggregated counter … needs the metrics pipeline `DEPLOYMENT.md` §8 defers". **D-204 falsified that while it stood**: `platform_engine_health` IS a bounded minute-bucket counter, in Postgres, shipped with no metrics pipeline. So the deferral was re-argued rather than repeated, and it survives on two reasons about THIS endpoint: (1) **hard rule 3** — all four refusals are on the receiver's ack path and three happen before a body is read at all, so a durable counter there is a DB write beyond the minimal event row; (2) **the write rate belongs to the caller** — `platform_engine_health` is written from OUR outbound failures at a rate we control, while on an unauthenticated public endpoint the same pattern hands a prober one write per POST. A minute bucket is a smaller ROW than a row per refusal and the same NUMBER of writes, so it does not answer the objection that rejected the row per refusal. What would answer it is in-process aggregation flushed on a timer, i.e. a stateful receiver whose state dies with every deploy (§4b). **Two corrections of record.** R-9 listed `duplicate` among the gaps and OPERATIONS called it "not evidence of anything": `claim_inbox_event` bumps `webhook_inbox_events.duplicate_count` on the transition's own row, so a replay burst is durable, queryable evidence — it is the one inbound outcome recorded without an alert. And `webhook_claim_timeout` was missing from both documents' refusal lists, which is why the list is now derived from code. **What re-opens it**: a signed engine (Bolna signs nothing today, D-31) makes the source check a signature check, at which point a refusal is attributable and worth a durable row. |
| D-220 | **A GET that writes an `audit_log` row is a MUTATION, and the last two that were still queries have been moved** | `useDeliveryPayload` (`/v1/integrations/deliveries/{id}/payload`) becomes a `useMutation`, matching `useRawTranscript` and `useRecordingLink`; the integrations screen `reset()`s it on close and on a switch between rows. `useQaSample` (`GET /v1/admin/qa-samples/{id}`) stays a `useQuery` — it is a screen's whole payload, not a panel — and gains `refetchOnMount: "always"` beside its `staleTime: Infinity`. Tests in `tests/integrationsPayload.test.tsx` and `tests/qaSampling.test.tsx` drive two opens and count the requests; the QA one renders through ONE `QueryClient` across two mounts, because a fresh client per render is a fresh cache and would pass whatever the hook did. | **WHY.** Hard rule 5 lets raw transcript text and unredacted delivery bodies out of the API only behind a role check AND an audit write, and SEC-COMP §5 says an admin read of a client's call is always audited. That makes the row the route's PURPOSE, not an incidental — so "who looked, and how often" has to be answerable from `audit_log` alone. No staleness policy a query offers is right for such a read: automatic refetching forges rows naming somebody who did not ask, and the `staleTime: Infinity` that stops it means the SECOND open is served from memory. The trail then records one look where there were two, and it understates in the direction that matters. **WHY THE TWO ANSWERS DIFFER.** A mutation is right where the read backs a PANEL the user opens and closes inside a screen — there is no mount to hang a policy on, and `reset()` gives the "do not replay the previous customer's data before the new answer lands" property for free. `refetchOnMount: "always"` is right where the read IS the screen: TanStack treats `true` as "refetch on mount if stale", which under an infinite staleTime is never, while `"always"` is per mount regardless — so a navigation costs a row and a background timer still costs none, which is the split the QA hook's own comment was reaching for. **WHAT IT COST TO FIND.** `useRawTranscript` was fixed in an earlier wave and its two siblings were not, one of them with a docstring that said "same shape, same reasoning, as `useRawTranscript`". A class fixed at one site and left at two is why the guard below exists. |
| D-221 | **The §52 guard gains rule 6: a mutation that is fired and whose failure has nowhere to go** | `tests/surfaceStatesGuard.test.ts` flags a `useMutation` envelope that is TRIGGERED in the function that declared it (`X.mutate`/`X.mutateAsync`) while nothing in that function consults `X.error`/`isError`/`status`/`failureReason`. Gated by the SAME `refusesSomewhere` rules 3–5 use, so "handed to a child" is tolerated by one code path rather than two. Three fixture shapes — banned, refused, handed on. Measured across `src/`: **zero live hits**, so it ships with an empty `EXEMPT`. | **WHY A RULE FOR THE OTHER ENVELOPE.** Rules 1–5 are all about a query, and that file says why: a mutation's `undefined` data honestly means "the user has not asked yet". The mutation's defect is on the other side of the click. `mutate()` is fire-and-forget — it never throws, never rejects, renders nothing — so a component that calls it and never reads `.error` shows a control that goes silent on a 403, a 409 and a timeout alike, and the user presses it again. For a dial, a top-up or a campaign launch that is the worst available outcome. It costs more here than in most products: `apps/api` answers every refusal with an RFC-9457 problem carrying a `title`, a `detail` and a `remediation`, written so the person on the other end knows what to do next, and `ProblemNotice` exists to render all three — an unread `error` discards that work at the last hop. **WHY SHIP A RULE WITH NO HITS.** Because the alternative is shipping it after the first one, and there are ~90 mutation call sites for the class to stop being clean in. It was still driven red twice: once on a planted fixture, and once by deleting the real `add.error` refusal from `/c/<slug>/do-not-call`, where it named the declaration and quoted the fix. |
| D-222 ⚠SUPERSEDES the `color-contrast` and page-level entries in `tests/a11y.ts` | **The a11y suite's two named blind spots are closed, and both were hiding real failures** | A browser-mode run (`next build` + `next start` + axe-core injected into real Chromium, nine documents) reported **twelve** violations the 1,159-test jsdom suite cannot see. Four structural: `SessionGate` replaces the whole shell, so a gated `/admin` had no `main`, no `<h1>` and content outside every landmark, and a gated `/c/<slug>` had a skip link pointing at a `#main-content` that did not exist. Eight contrast: `--text-faint` at `#94a3b8` is **2.56:1** on `--surface`, and fifteen call sites put white text on `bg-brand` (3.38:1). Fixes: a `landmark` prop on `SessionGate`, threaded through both realm gates, set by the two shells and off by default for the two auth pages that render the gate inside their own `<main>`; a palette move (light muted → slate-600, light faint → slate-500, dark faint → `#7c8a9c`); and every labelled control off `--brand` onto `--brand-strong`/`--brand-deep`. Re-run: **zero**. `tests/contrast.test.ts` is the durable half. | **WHY THE BROWSER RUN WAS THE ONLY WAY.** `tests/a11y.ts` disables `color-contrast` because jsdom has no layout and no canvas, and lists `region`/`bypass`/`landmark-one-main`/`page-has-heading-one`/`document-title` as inapplicable because the sweep scans a DETACHED CONTAINER. Both entries end "CLOSED BY: a browser-mode run", and both were right that the gap was real. **THE SKIP-LINK DEFECT IS THE ONE WORTH REMEMBERING**: the client shell renders `SkipLink` outside its session provider *on purpose*, so a reader can bypass 21 navigation links while the session resolves — and the target lives inside that provider, so in exactly the state the control was added for, it pointed at nothing. A correct-looking fix whose premise was never checked against a real document. Driven by keyboard in Chromium after the fix: first Tab lands on a visible "Skip to main content", Enter moves focus to `MAIN#main-content`. **WHY THE PALETTE MOVED TWO TOKENS AND NOT ONE.** The smallest passing value for `--text-faint` is exactly `--text-muted`, which would collapse two tokens into one — a hierarchy the design no longer has, restored by the next person picking a lighter grey and reintroducing this. So muted moves down the same slate ramp and faint takes its old value: same family, same relationship, both above 4.5:1. **WHY THE GATE IS AN ARITHMETIC TEST AND NOT THE BROWSER RUN.** A browser run needs a build, a port and a Chromium binary, none of which `make web-check` has, and a check that cannot be a gate is a check that stops happening. The ratio is a pure function of two hex values; `tests/contrast.test.ts` reads `globals.css` rather than restating it, so a palette edit cannot pass it by editing a copy. |
| D-223 | **A route module's export list is CONFIGURATION, and `next build` does not check it on a client-component layout** | `tests/routeExportsGuard.test.ts` walks the AST of every `page`/`layout`/`route`/`template`/`error`/`loading`/`not-found` module under `src/app` and refuses any export outside the App Router's own set (ignoring type-only exports, which are erased before Next sees the module). Two negative controls are the two escapes that reached CI verbatim. | **THE MEASUREMENT, because the premise is not obvious.** Comparing `src/app/**` with the validators Next emits into `.next/types/app/**` gives one for every `page.tsx` and one for `src/app/layout.tsx`, and NONE for `admin/layout.tsx` or `c/[slug]/layout.tsx` — the two that carry `"use client"`. Deleting that directive from `admin/layout.tsx` and rebuilding makes the validator appear. Next 15.5.21 emits no route-type validator for a client-component LAYOUT while emitting one for a client-component PAGE, so **the two files every screen in the product renders inside are precisely the ones the build will not check**. Sabotage in both directions: an extra export on `c/[slug]/attention/page.tsx` fails `next build` with '"sabotageRowKeys" is not a valid Page export field'; the same export on `c/[slug]/layout.tsx` builds green. `admin/layout.tsx` had `export const MFA_PROBLEM_CODES` four lines below a comment asserting that Next rejects exactly that — a premise stated as a fact, which is the thing nobody re-checks. **WHY A VITEST SOURCE SCAN AND NOT `scripts/check_*.py`.** The subject is `apps/web` source, the reader is a frontend author, and the gate that has to fail is `make web-check` — the one they run before pushing. `tests/responsive.test.ts` and `tests/surfaceStatesGuard.test.ts` are the precedent; a Python checker would put the answer in a different command from the question. |
| D-230 | **`close_queue` and `close_admission` were written, exported and called from nowhere, so no process ever closed the two Redis clients it opened** | The API lifespan closed `core/redis` and flushed spans; the worker's `on_shutdown` flushed spans and did nothing else — under a `job_completion_wait = 45` comment that explicitly budgets fifteen seconds for "the pool teardown". `close_admission`'s own docstring said it was "called from the same shutdown path as `close_redis`", which nothing had ever made true. Both are now called in `core/bootstrap`'s lifespan `finally` and in `apps/workers/settings.shutdown`, each under `suppress(Exception)` and ahead of `shutdown_tracing` — a drain is exactly when a socket is already half gone AND exactly when the spans matter, so one failing closer may not cost the flush. `tests/service_teardown_test.py` asserts the CALLS, because a teardown that imports a closer and never reaches it reads identically to one that does. Found by the whole-class sweep, not by a symptom: a leaked pool is invisible until a redeploy holds connections open. |
| D-231 | **`COHERE_API_KEY` was a `Settings` field, offered by the ops console, that no code path read** | Deleted, field and `AppliesRule` row together, rather than deprecated — the argument D-177 made four lines above it in the same file when it removed the Clerk six: "a setting nothing reads is a value an operator can still install and then believe in". D-28 makes retrieval a managed API service that owns its own embeddings, so no code has ever needed one; the comment justifying it ("only needed if the bake-off selects a store that does not") described a decision nobody had taken. `tests/platform_secrets_test.py` was its last consumer in the repository — as a FIXTURE key for the env-shadowing test, which is how a dead key survives an audit: the fixture looks like a use. Repointed to `cartesia_api_key`. If a future retrieval provider requires us to embed, the field returns in the same change as the code that reads it. |
| D-232 | **Three public functions whose only mention outside their own `def` was their own module's `__all__`** | `core/settings.fail_fast` (a second unhandled-startup mechanism beside `BootstrapError`, which is the one actually raised), `quality/sampling.get_sample` (the `get_`-raises twin of `find_sample`, which every caller uses with its own `not_found`), and `agents/voices.voice_selection_available` (a boolean wrapper over `voice_selection_capability().available`). Each is two-ways-per-problem with the second way at zero callers, so each is deleted rather than wired. Proof of death, not absence of grep hits: no reference in `apps/`, `packages/`, `scripts/`, `alembic/` or `tests/`; not decorated, so no FastAPI or pytest dispatch; not a string anywhere, so no `getattr` and no arq job name; and `apps/web` reaches the backend over HTTP, never by Python name. |
| D-233 | **`campaign_contacts.dedupe_hash` stored an unsalted truncated SHA-256 of a phone number that nothing has ever read** | Write-stopped in `campaigns.service.add_contacts`; step 1 of hard rule 8's two-step, and the DROP migration is what closes it. The upload dedupes within a batch on the normalized number (`seen`) and across batches on `UNIQUE (campaign_id, phone_e164)` — both on the number itself — so the column had no reader even during the import it was named for. Indian mobile E.164 is a ~10^9 space, so `sha256(phone)[:16]` is the number back in seconds of enumeration, sitting in the table whose whole erasure story is that the number goes: a DPDP minimisation defect, not a spare index. Retention's `_CAMPAIGN_CONTACT_ERASE_SQL` keeps NULLing the values older rows carry until the DROP. `tests/campaigns_test.py::test_a_contact_upload_stores_no_hash_of_the_number` is the live assertion, because the tempting fix for the guard that flags this column is to put the write back with a reader bolted on. |
| D-234 | **The inbox recorded when a webhook arrived, when its job was handed off and when it finished, and nothing ever subtracted two of them** | `webhook_inbox_events.enqueued_at` and `processed_at` were written by `mark_inbox_enqueued`/`mark_inbox_processed` and read by no query, response or metric — while the only latency number this system published was the ACK the caller waits for, which hard rule 3 caps at 500ms precisely BECAUSE the work is deferred. A fast ack over a stalled pipeline was therefore indistinguishable from a healthy one. Two named recorders now close it (BACKEND-PATTERNS §8): `record_inbox_handling_ms` from `mark_inbox_processed`'s own `RETURNING` — computed by the statement that writes it, so no second read and no window — and `record_inbox_lag` on the dispatcher tick beside `record_outbox_lag`, reporting the oldest row still parked at `enqueued`, which is a webhook whose arq job never came back. That failure is otherwise invisible: the reconciliation poller (D-31) repairs the call regardless, quietly. |
| D-235 | **Six audit waves fixed instances of "leave no half-wired feature"; nobody had swept for the class** | `scripts/check_half_wired.py`, in `make guardrails` and CI beside `check_wiring`, which names these shapes in its own docstring as out of scope. Six sections: a column something WRITES and nothing reads (SQL clause position, with a `CheckConstraint` counted as the reader it is — four live columns are written by one statement, selected by none, and are half of an integrity rule); a `Settings` field nothing consumes; a public function no file references, `__all__` and docstrings excluded because neither is a caller; a stub body outside a `Protocol` with no docstring saying what it stands in for; a broad `except` whose body does nothing AT ALL (not "logs nothing" — that rule reported four correct handlers, including `health.ready` setting `redis_ok = False`, where the failure IS the verdict); and a `TODO`/`FIXME` marker. TWO FILES rather than one because they fail differently: `check_wiring` asks whether a declaration is in the registry that gives it effect and needs no exemptions, this one judges POSITION and BODY and carries two shrink-only baselines. Exit 2 = REFUSED when a scan cannot see its subject. It blinded itself twice while being written — its own baseline strings counted as reads of the columns they exempt, and its negative controls counted as references to the functions they prove dead — so registry strings and docstrings are excluded from evidence and `tests/half_wired_guard_test.py` pins both. |
| D-240 | **One HTTP ladder for every vendor adapter, and conformance clauses that can fail a second one** | `apps/api/engine/vendor_http.py` holds the throttle backoff, the error vocabulary and the unreadable-success refusal; `BolnaEngine._request` and `CartesiaEngine._request` both delegate to it and keep only their own client, base URL, credential and version pin. Log codes carry an `engine` label instead of a vendor prefix (`cartesia_request_failed` and `engine_error` were one event under two greppable names). Five new conformance clauses cover the failure half — a retried throttle, an exhausted one, a 200 whose body will not parse, a vendor error body that must not reach our caller, and a transport that never answers — run against every adapter that speaks HTTP through a `TRANSPORT_RECIPES` table, plus a sixth clause deriving the HTTP-speaking set from the roster BY TYPE so a third vendor cannot join with its failure paths unmeasured. `tests/engine_audit_test.py::_DriftedLadder` keeps the pre-fix ladder as a permanent saboteur. | **WHY.** The conformance suite opens by promising that every adapter is held to identical checkable behaviour; that was true of the happy path and false of every failure path, because no fixture ever made a vendor misbehave. Measured on the shipped tree: a **429** was `engine_rate_limited` (`transient`, retryable, with backoff) on one adapter and `engine_rejected` (`dependency`, terminal, no backoff) on the other — and `workers.pipeline.TRANSIENT_ENGINE_CODES` and `agents/service.py` both dispatch on that code, so a throttled call was a terminal failure everywhere those two decide, which is exactly what the first adapter's own comment forbids. A **200 carrying a WAF challenge** raised `engine_bad_response` on one and became `{}` on the other, where `get_execution` then answered with a snapshot naming no call (`engine_call_id=''`), priced at nothing, holding no transcript, and archiving `{}` as the vendor's own document — a completed call written as failed, metered at zero, and read `settled` by the reconciliation poller forever. `VoiceEngine.get_agent` forbids that shape in words ("a conclusion drawn from nothing that looks like a measurement") and P2.2 had already found and closed it on ONE adapter. **SABOTAGE-VERIFIED**: restoring the old ladder fails exactly three of the new clauses and no others. |
| D-241 | **Hard rule 3's number had never been measured as a distribution, and the instrument for it already existed one file away** | `tests/ack_harness.py` holds the nearest-rank percentile, the discarded warm-up and the barrier release; `tool_endpoint_budget_test` (which had them) and `voice_runtime_ack_budget_test` (which did not) both use it. The receiver now reports sequential server/client distributions, a barrier-released concurrent one, and the REFUSAL path's — the shape a flood takes and the one an operator has never been given a scale for. No millisecond is asserted anywhere; `docs/evidence/deepdive-voice2.md` carries the numbers and the noise. | **WHY.** D-109 measured the in-call tool endpoint against TRD §6.2's 100ms and recorded a full table. The WEBHOOK receiver — whose budget is the hard rule, and whose vendor delivers at most once so a slow ack loses a call — had only round-trip counts and deadlines. Two copies of `percentile` would have made the two budgets incomparable, which is the point of sharing the instrument rather than writing a second one. |
| D-242 | **The reconciliation poller's window is anchored to when a call STARTED and its promise is about when a call ENDED** | `pipeline.reconcile_outstanding_calls`, a new half-hourly cron at :15/:45, asks the engine directly about every call row we hold that the pipeline has not finished — two shapes: a row that never reached a terminal status, and a `completed` row carrying neither a usage event nor a transcript turn. Terminal candidates are judged by `_pipeline_settled` rather than re-derived, so a call that owed nothing is not re-driven. Bounded four ways: a 10-minute floor, a 24-hour horizon, 50 rows per tenant and 200 vendor requests per sweep, the last alerting when it truncates. It also emits `calls_never_finished`. `callable_tenants` moved from `dispatcher` to `pipeline`, the layer dispatcher already imports. | **WHY.** `list_executions` sends `created_after=<now-30m>`. For a call that runs 40 minutes, `completed` lands ~43 minutes after creation, so the execution has fallen out of every listing before its terminal transition happens. Bolna's webhook is at-most-once with errors swallowed, so ONE lost delivery on a long call left it never transcribed, never metered, never invoiced — and invisible to everything else: `report_stalled_pipeline` asks `EXTRACTION_OWED_SQL`, which only sees calls already recorded `completed`, and the row a lost terminal webhook leaves is stuck at `in_progress`. Nothing in the tree ever said the guarantee of record was bounded by call DURATION — the silent premise D-31/D-32 exist to forbid, under the one mechanism appointed to be the backstop. **REJECTED: a wider `created_after`** — 25 hours would re-list a day of executions past `_LISTING_MAX_PAGES` for any real fleet, so the poller would report `reconciliation_listing_incomplete` on every healthy tick and train the operator to ignore the one alarm that says calls are unrecoverable; and it does nothing for a call that never completes. **REJECTED: an `updated_after` filter** — inventing a freshness parameter has `_next_link`'s exact failure mode ("a `?page=` the vendor ignores returns page one forever"); whether `GET /executions` accepts one stays OPERATIONS §2 gate 6. **WHY ITS OWN CRON**: the sweep is O(tenants) because `calls` is FORCE-RLS'd, and as a phase of the 10-minute tick it took the poller test file from 13s to 51s against 1,925 tenants — a third fleet-wide fan-out to buy a recovery latency nobody needs, since the common case is already covered by the listing within ten minutes. **NAMED, NOT FIXED**: an exhausted probe budget starves the same tail of the tenant ordering until the incident clears; a rotating offset needs durable state to be fair and makes the sweep non-deterministic, so the truncation ALERTS instead. **SABOTAGE-VERIFIED** in both directions: stubbing the sweep fails the recovery clauses; forcing every terminal candidate `settled` fails the dead-pipeline clause; forcing none of them settled fails the owed-nothing clause. |
| D-243 | **A stranger's engine name reached an alert field, bounded one function below** | `engine_intake.engine_label` is the ONE place the `{engine}` path segment is bounded to the engines this deployment answers for. `webhook_routes._refuse` (the metric label) and both source-rejection `alert()` call sites — the receiver's and the in-call tool endpoint's — use it. `tests/voice_runtime_security_test.py` drives both endpoints with 414 characters of hostile path segment and asserts the alert record carries `unknown` while keeping the reason string an operator needs. | **WHY.** `_refuse` spends a paragraph on why the value must be bounded before it becomes a metric label — "anyone who found the URL could mint unbounded label cardinality in the metrics pipeline, a cheap way to hurt the monitoring of the service they are already probing" — and the `alert()` twenty lines above it passed the raw segment through. Driven, not read: 414 characters of attacker-chosen text with an embedded newline on `calevate.alert`'s record, on EVERY request rather than once per suppression window, from any source address, while the metric label beside it correctly read `unknown`.
