Not PII and capped at 200 characters by the log redactor, so this is cardinality and operator-facing noise rather than disclosure — but it is the same value, the same argument, and one of two consumers protected. **SABOTAGE-VERIFIED**: restoring the raw `engine=` in either call site turns the clause red with the 414-character value quoted in the failure message. |
| D-250 | **The meter priced a late-settling call by TODAY's plan, where its own month's panel and invoice priced it by that month's** | `pipeline._meter` resolves `plan_in_effect_sql` at `plans.month_pricing_instant(month)` — the instant `usage_summary` and `billing/charges.py` already price a month at — rather than at `NOW_SQL`. `month` moved above the plan read to make that possible. The CEILING is deliberately still resolved at `now()` inside the upsert's `caps` CTE. `tests/late_call_prices_at_its_own_month_test.py` reproduces it and pins both directions. | **WHY.** A call whose `ended_at` falls in a CLOSED month reaches the meter routinely — `engine.py` records that the vendor may take minutes to price a call, the reconciliation poller's window straddles midnight IST on the 1st, and the ARQ retry ladder can cross it. Priced at `now()`, its contribution to `spend_state.billed_inr` used the terms in force today. Measured on this tree: one ten-minute call ending on the last day of a month whose ₹2/min terms were superseded by ₹20/min on the 1st was counted at **₹200.00** where that month's panel and invoice both say **₹20.00**. A RATE is a term of the month being priced; a CAP is a question about whether this tenant may dial right now, which is why the two instants differ on purpose. |
| D-251 | **A vendor-reported NEGATIVE call duration aborted metering entirely, and every retry hit the same wall** | `pipeline._billable_seconds` clamps a negative `ExecutionSnapshot.duration_s` to zero and fires `call_duration_negative` (`WORKER_TERMINAL`, ids plus the value). The call still meters: `_unit_price` keeps a leg whole at `qty <= 0`, so the supplier cost lands and the client is billed for no minutes. `tests/negative_duration_test.py`. | **WHY.** `duration_s` is `int | None` with no floor and both adapters build it as `int(duration) if isinstance(duration, int | float) else None`, so a `-1` "unknown" sentinel or a duration derived from two disagreeing clocks reaches the money path unfiltered. Taking seconds AWAY makes `month_increment`'s `after < before`, i.e. a NEGATIVE contribution to `spend_state.billed_inr` — the one input to that counter that is not monotone, which is why `ck_spend_state_billed_inr_nonnegative` had never been reachable. And a healthy accumulated total does not save it: PostgreSQL evaluates a CHECK against the row an `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` PROPOSES, before the conflict is arbitrated — measured against this pg16, not recalled (`INSERT INTO t VALUES (1, -5) ON CONFLICT (k) DO UPDATE SET v = t.v + EXCLUDED.v` fails with `Failing row contains (1, -5)` against a `v >= 0` check on a row already holding 100). So the whole metering transaction rolled back — no usage rows, no wallet debit, no counters — and the call could never settle, because every retry reproduced it. Clamping rather than refusing keeps P1.2's rule: a completed call with no usage artefact is one the reconciliation poller calls `settled` and never revisits. |
| D-252 | **An operator TIGHTENING a client's ceiling did not arm the gate, so the runaway it was typed to stop kept dialling** | `billing/terms.py::record_terms` takes `lock_tenant_spend_state` before the `plans` read its write depends on, and calls `caps.recompute_capped` in the same transaction as the insert. `TermsWriteResult.capped_now` reports whether the write stopped the client's calling, and the `plan.terms_recorded` audit row carries it. Three writers of `spend_state.capped`, one definition of over-cap. `tests/admin_cap_arms_the_gate_test.py`. | **WHY.** `plans.hard_cap_min` / `hard_cap_spend` are written by exactly one function and are half of the ceiling `over_cap_sql` enforces (`LEAST(hard, client)`), but the only thing that can stop a dial is `spend_state.capped`. `caps.apply_client_caps` had this right for the CLIENT's own stop button and states the argument in as many words — *"a cap accepted whose gate is not armed is a cap that does nothing until the next call meters, and for an outbound-only tenant the next call is exactly what the cap was supposed to stop"* — and the ADMIN half, the one an operator reaches for during an incident, did not do it. Measured before the fix: ₹480 already billed for the month, operator writes `hard_cap_spend = ₹100`, `capped` stays `false` and `check_dispatch` still returns `allowed=True`. The recompute sits AFTER the `_same_terms` early return so re-posting identical terms stays a true no-op. |
| D-253 | **Two spellings of "minutes used this month": the ceiling was judged against one and the client was shown the other** | `billing.service.month_increment` (was `overage_increment_inr`) returns a `MonthIncrement` carrying BOTH the minute and the rupee difference this call makes to its month, from one `rung_seconds` read. `pipeline._counter_increment` (was `_billed_for_this_call`) feeds the minute half into `spend_state.minutes_used` on every tier. `tests/counter_minutes_match_the_panel_test.py`. | **WHY.** `spend_state.minutes_used` accumulated the meter's own `duration_s / 60` one call at a time at the column's NUMERIC(14,4) scale, while `usage_summary.minutes_used` is the month's total SECONDS divided once and allocated to paise. `over_cap_sql` compares the first against `cap_min`; `minutes_left` is derived from the second. Measured on four calls of 3847/2913/611/137 seconds: **125.1333** against **125.13**. The drift is the sum of the per-call rounding errors and only grows within a month, so a busy tenant's two figures land either side of an integer ceiling and the panel promises minutes the gate has already refused. Because `rung_minutes` guarantees its parts sum to `to_paise(total_seconds / 60)`, the ledger-derived increments telescope to precisely the published figure and every increment is a two-decimal value the column stores without rounding at all. The PREPAID wallet debit deliberately did NOT move to this figure: a call is charged for its own length, and pricing it off a month's running remainder would charge two identical calls differently on a statement a client reads per entry. |
| D-254 | **The prepaid wallet and the prepaid panel price a month by two arithmetics, and which one is right is not an engineering question** | Measured and recorded on `billing.service.calling_revenue_inr` rather than changed. Ten calls of 7/7/7/13/41/59/101/137/211/307 seconds at ₹6.00: the wallet is debited **₹89.0000** (and `spend_state.billed_inr` equals it exactly, by construction — the meter hands both the same figure from `prepaid_billed_inr`), while the closed-month panel reports **₹88.98**, which is 14.83 min × ₹6.00. | **WHY IT IS NOT FIXED HERE.** The two answer different questions and cannot both be satisfied. The wallet is debited per call at scale 4, because a call is charged for its own length and that is the only rule a client can be shown per ledger entry. The panel multiplies out against the `minutes_used` printed beside it, which is the arithmetic a client actually performs — feeding it the exact seconds instead would close the gap against the wallet and open it against the panel, since ₹89.00 is not `14.83 × ₹6.00`. The gap is bounded by half a paisa of minutes times the rate (under ₹0.05 at any rate this product would quote) and is systematic rather than random. **WHAT CLOSES IT:** the founder decision `docs/evidence/deepdive-money.md` N-2 already names — what a prepaid statement IS (a receipt for top-ups received, a statement of consumption, or both). If the wallet is the statement this branch reads the ledger; if the panel is, the wallet's per-call rule is what moves. |
| D-255 | **The invoice stated a tax total its own tax lines were not required to add up to** | `build_invoice` computes `tax_components` through `gst.split_tax` FIRST and publishes `gst_inr` as their sum. `tests/invoice_tax_total_is_the_heads_test.py` pins that the total follows the heads by making the two differ. | **WHY.** The line was a second `to_paise(subtotal * GST_RATE_PCT / 100)` — character for character the expression `split_tax` opens with — so `split_tax`'s own promise that *"the two halves summing to the printed GST total is the property a hand-checker actually tests"* was held by two identical spellings agreeing rather than by one number being published once. They agreed; nothing made them. Rule 46(l)-(m) requires the heads to be stated separately and a recipient credits them to different ledgers, so the heads are what the document asserts and the total is a convenience over them — and the next rounding decision on this line (D-256) would otherwise have had to be made in two places or the document stops adding up. |
| D-256 | **GST on this document is stated in PAISE, and CGST s.170 says tax is rounded to the nearest RUPEE — OPEN, and not ours to settle** | Recorded beside `GST_RATE_PCT` in `billing/invoice.py` with the citation and the measurement, not implemented. On the worked example in `docs/evidence/deepdive-money2.md` it is the difference between a stated GST of **₹3,614.52** and a per-head rounding of **CGST ₹1,807 + SGST ₹1,807 = ₹3,614**, with the ₹0.52 appearing as a round-off line rather than vanishing. | **WHY IT IS NOT IMPLEMENTED. REPORTED, NOT READ** (`billing/payments.py`'s three-rung evidence ladder — cbic-gst.gov.in is not reachable from this network). Several independent secondaries quote s.170 identically: *tax, interest, penalty, fine, refund or any other sum shall be rounded off to the nearest rupee*, fifty paise or more rounding up; and they state it is applied per INVOICE and per HEAD (CGST, SGST/UTGST, IGST separately) rather than on a consolidated total. What none of them settles first-party is whether it binds the DOCUMENT or the RETURN, and whether the taxable value rounds with the tax — and this changes money on every invoice the platform would ever issue. Nothing is out of compliance today: `supplier.is_registered` is false in every deployment and the document says `proforma`, exactly the posture Rule 46(b)'s serial-number gap already sits in. **WHAT CLOSES IT:** the GST registration (ROADMAP M0) plus a first-party read or an accountant's confirmation of the per-invoice, per-head reading — at which point the round-off becomes its own invoice line so `subtotal + tax + round_off = total` still adds up by hand. |
| D-260 | **The Bolna adapter was reconciled against the vendor's own source, and three things it inferred were wrong** | `bolna-ai/bolna` — the open-source framework the hosted platform is built on — was **cloned and read in full** at commit `cd2e192`, rather than summarised. Three provable defects, each fixed with a test that fails without the fix (sabotage-verified): **(1) the agent body omitted `toolchain`**, which has no default in their `Task` model and is dereferenced with a bare subscript in two runtime paths — one of which is what declares the task consumes AUDIO. An omitted `toolchain` is a validation error at create time or a `KeyError` at dial time, i.e. an agent that cannot take a call. **(2) `parse_transcript` spliced non-dialogue lines into dialogue turns.** Their `format_messages` emits `system:`, `assistant_tool_call:` and `tool_response:` into the same string as the conversation; none matches our turn regex, so each fell into the *continuation* branch and was appended to the previous speaker's text — putting a serialized tool call, arguments and all, inside what the transcript said the agent said, which extraction then reads. **(3) `_agent_models` looked only under `agent_config`**, but their `GET /agent/{id}` returns the stored object with `tasks` at the ROOT, so a perfectly readable agent reported `models_readable=False` — the verdict that function's own docstring says must never come from looking in the wrong place. Its two siblings already had the fallback. **Three further findings were NOT acted on, deliberately**, because the evidence is about the OSS framework and not the hosted contract: `family` is read by nothing while `provider` selects the LLM client (gate 16); `voicemail` is reported as a flag rather than a status (gate 17); the "15-value status enum" in our comment had no source anywhere and was corrected to say so. Evidence and its three classes are captured in `docs/vendor/bolna/`; the assumption-by-assumption table is `docs/evidence/vendor-bolna-reconciliation.md`. |
| D-261 | **The cost unit is a marked assumption on the money path, and both sourced signals now argue against it** | `_to_inr` divided the vendor's figure by 100 under a docstring calling it "USD cents" — a bare literal, so nothing could disagree with it and no gate could score it. Two independent signals now argue it is **dollars**: their own cost function returns `round(total_cost, 5)` computed from per-token DOLLAR rates (VERIFIED-OSS), and every published Bolna price is quoted in dollars per minute (REPORTED-DOCS). **Behaviour deliberately unchanged.** The hosted billing figure is not that OSS function — it adds telephony and the platform fee and is computed by a service we cannot read — so changing a money constant on inference would risk a 100x error in the other direction, on the one path (hard rule 7) where that is unrecoverable. Instead the `/ 100` became `_ASSUMED_MINOR_UNITS_PER_MAJOR`, a named constant carrying the counter-evidence, what falsifies it, and what a wrong value costs: every `usage_event` under-values calls 100x, no spend cap ever arms, every margin panel reads ~₹0.00. **OPERATIONS §2 gate 7 was raised from soft to HARD** and rewritten around the single observation that settles it — one completed call's `total_cost` beside the same call's charge on their dashboard. Closed by: a Bolna account. |
| D-262 | **Bolna HAS transfer; it is not the shape our Protocol names — so the flag stays `False` and the reason changes** | `BOLNA_CAPABILITIES.transfer=False` was carried as "Bolna may well support it; nobody ran the gate". It does support it, read at source: `transfer_call` is a function the LLM invokes mid-conversation, latched so a re-firing model cannot transfer twice, with the destination supplied by CONFIG rather than by the model. `VoiceEngine.transfer(call_id, to, warm)` describes something else — an out-of-band instruction to an execution already in flight — and nothing sourced exposes that over REST. So the value is unchanged and is now a **statement about shape mismatch** rather than an absence of evidence, which matters because CLAUDE.md prefers configuring engine built-ins over rebuilding them and this is the case where the built-in exists but does not fit the seam. Adopting it is a design change, not a flag flip: a per-agent escalation number would become engine config set at publish time. OPERATIONS §2 gate 18 asks the two questions that decide it. Closed by: a Bolna account. |
| D-270 | **The `VoiceEngine` contract is Bolna-shaped, and Cartesia's own generated clients are what proved it — the second adapter looked finished because its stub was built from our own inferences** | `docs/vendor/cartesia/` records every fact harvested from Cartesia's published SDKs, at three evidence classes; `docs/evidence/vendor-cartesia-reconciliation.md` maps each of our 23 assumptions to what the evidence did to it. TRD §10.5 gains a second correction block and an **Agent hosting** row. OPERATIONS §2 gains **gate 16**, whose (a) is the port work rather than a question. The adapter keeps every method the port requires and adds a fourth evidence label, **CONTRADICTED**, for the ones the vendor demonstrably does not offer — because an inference that has been checked and failed is not the same animal as one nobody has checked, and it may not keep wearing the same label. | **HOW.** `docs.cartesia.ai` is still egress-blocked, but `github.com` is not, and Cartesia publishes **Stainless-generated** clients (`cartesia-python`, `cartesia-js`) whose every file says *"File generated from our OpenAPI spec by Stainless"*. A generated client is a machine translation of the spec — one method per operation — so for the first time a MISSING operation is evidence rather than silence, and Python agreeing with TypeScript is the spec speaking rather than a generator quirk. **WHAT IT SETTLED.** There is no `POST /agents`; `AgentSummary` carries no prompt, no greeting and no model but does carry `git_repository`/`git_deploy_branch`; `PATCH /agents/{id}` accepts exactly `{description, name, tts_language, tts_voice}`. On Line the agent IS a deployed repository and the prompt is per-CALL data (`CallRequest.agent.system_prompt`) or code. **WHY THAT MATTERS MORE THAN A FIELD NAME.** §10.5 opened by saying nobody had tested whether the port was vendor-neutral or merely Bolna-shaped, *"and those look identical while only one vendor exists"*. It is Bolna-shaped: `create_agent` and the prompt read-back have no Cartesia counterpart, `agents/verification.py` would score every publish `unreadable`, and hard rule 5 could not be enforced from this repository on that engine at all. **WHY THE PORT WAS NOT CHANGED IN THE SAME BREATH.** Saying "this engine does not host an agent of ours" needs a new `EngineCapabilities` member, a conformance clause that respects it, a `FakeEngine` profile and a publish path that degrades honestly — a large change to the one seam the whole engine story rests on, made against a vendor we hold no account with. It is named work (gate 16(a)) rather than a reassurance, which is the difference between a decision and a deferral. **WHY THE ADAPTER LOOKED HEALTHY.** Its conformance stub was hand-built from the same inferences the adapter made, so the suite could only ever confirm the two agreed. The stub now uses the verified shapes on every field where evidence exists, which is the only version of that fixture worth having. |
| D-271 | **Six wire-level facts in the Cartesia adapter were invented, and all six were SILENT — the version pin was a version of a different protocol** | Fixed with a sabotage-verified test each (`tests/engine_capability_test.py`): `API_VERSION` `2026-04-03` → **`2026-08-14`**; auth `X-API-Key` → **`Authorization: Bearer`**; call id `agent_call_id` → **`id`**; instants `started_at`/`ended_at` → **`start_time`/`end_time`**; numbers top-level → **nested `telephony_params.{from,to}`**; transcript utterance `content` → **`text`**, with `role == "system"` rows excluded as LOG entries. `duration_s` is derived from the two instants because their call object has no duration field. `language` → **`tts_language`** on the agent body, and the invented `webhook_url` key is gone. | **THE ONE WORTH NAMING TWICE.** `2026-04-03` came from `line/voice_agent_app.py:129`, and reading its only use settles what it is: the body OUR deployed agent returns to Cartesia's harness from `POST /chats`. It versions the in-call WEBSOCKET protocol, it travels agent→harness, and it is a body field. We were pinning it as the REST API's `Cartesia-Version` header — asking one system for a version of a different one. All three of Cartesia's current clients send `2026-08-14`. **WHY "SILENT" IS THE POINT.** Every one of these fails without an exception: a wrong field name yields `None`, so a real Cartesia payload would have produced a snapshot with no id, no timestamps and no phone numbers, reported as a healthy completed call — and the transcript would have parsed to nothing while `transcript_lines_unparsed` stayed at zero, because the parser counted only rows it recognised as broken. The `system`-role bug is the one a client would have seen: their own field documentation says `system` marks `log_event`/`log_metric` rows, and everything not the agent was being filed as the CALLER, so instrumentation would have appeared in a customer's transcript as something the caller said. **THE TESTS ARE SABOTAGE-VERIFIED**: each fix was reverted in place and the named clause failed, then restored. A test written after a fix that has never been seen to fail is a test of nothing. |
| D-272 | **`cancelled` was mapped and not TERMINAL, so a cancelled Cartesia call would have been re-polled for ever and never reached the post-call pipeline** | `cancelled`/`canceled` added to both `_STATUS_MAP` (→ `failed`) and `_TERMINAL_RAW` in `apps/api/engine/cartesia.py`, with a clause that fails if either half is removed. | Their status enum is exactly `active|completed|failed|cancelled` (READ AT SOURCE, `types/agents/agent_call.py`). `cancelled` already reached `failed` through the unmapped-degrades-to-failed default, so the STATUS looked right and the mapping table looked complete — which is why this survived. The defect was one set membership: a non-terminal terminal status is a call the reconciliation poller re-reads on every tick and the pipeline never processes, so the lead is never extracted and the CRM row never appears. `calevate_shared.events.CallStatus` has no `cancelled` member and `failed` is the right landing place — terminal, and never billable as a success — but the mapping is a lossy one and `raw_status` is what preserves it. |
| D-273 | **Cartesia has no per-call cost and no global call listing — one of those is now an answer rather than a gap, and the other was a request the vendor cannot serve** | `CartesiaEngine._cost` still returns `None`, but as a CONCLUSION with two citations rather than a pilot to-do. `list_executions` is rewritten to the published contract: fan out over `GET /agents` (`{"summaries": [...]}`), page each agent with `limit=100`/`starting_after`, send `expand=transcript`, and apply `since` client-side. `_LISTING_PAGE_SUSPECT` is deleted; completeness now comes from a real bounded walk with `page_cap_reached`/`next_link_no_progress`, the same shape `BolnaEngine` uses. | **COST.** `AgentCall` has no cost, price, credit or currency field, and usage is an ACCOUNT meter — `GET /usage/credits` (`cartesia-mcp/cartesia_mcp/extra_api.py:54-79`), finest interval a DAY, `group_by ∈ {capability, model, voice, api_key}` with no `call` or `agent` member. So there is no route from a Cartesia response to a per-call rupee figure at the granularity `usage_events` needs. Hard rule 7 has nothing to convert, and the hole is COMMERCIAL — a rate card, per D-94's $0.014/min Scale tier — not an endpoint anybody can go and find. Recording it as an answer stops the next session spending an afternoon looking. **LISTING.** `agent_id` is `Required[str]` and no time filter exists, so the previous global `GET /agents/calls?start_time=...` was missing the one required parameter and sending one that does not exist — it could never have returned a row, and D-31 makes this poller the guarantee of record. Their order is start-time DESC, which is what makes a client-side `since` cheap: the walk for an agent stops at the first row outside the window instead of reading its whole history. `expand=transcript` is not optional either — without it the vendor returns no transcript, and the poller is the path with no webhook behind it to supply one. **ONE PROPERTY WORTH KNOWING**: the listing is per agent, so a call placed against an agent the account no longer holds is invisible to reconciliation on this engine. That is the vendor's model, not our code, and it is why `get_execution`'s D-126 archive matters more here than on Bolna. |
| D-280 | **The `VoiceEngine` port grew the field it was missing: `EngineCapabilities.agent_hosting`, because "this engine will hold an agent of ours" was an ASSUMPTION and assumptions cannot be refused, declared or exercised** | `AgentHosting = Literal["control_plane", "external_deployment"]` in `packages/shared/src/calevate_shared/engine.py`, a required `agent_hosting` member on `EngineCapabilities` (no default, like every other field), a `hosts_agents()` accessor, `"agent_hosting"` added to `EngineCapabilityName` and to `has()`, and a remediation sentence in `apps/api/engine/capabilities._REMEDIATION`. All three adapters declare it: `bolna` and the default `fake` are `control_plane`, `cartesia` is `external_deployment`. `CallContext` grows `system_prompt` — the per-call home the second shape gives hard rule 5 (D-282). The conformance suite gains `test_agent_hosting_decides_where_the_truthful_answer_rule_lives`, which branches on the capability rather than on a name, and `test_every_agent_hosting_shape_is_exercised_by_the_roster`, which derives the shapes from the Literal so a third cannot be added to the port without a subject declaring it. | **WHAT D-270 COULD ONLY WRITE DOWN.** TRD §10.5 opened by asking whether this contract is vendor-neutral or merely Bolna-shaped — *"those look identical while only one vendor exists"* — and Cartesia's Stainless-generated clients answered it: no `POST /agents`, an `AgentSummary` carrying `git_repository`/`git_deploy_branch` and no prompt, greeting or model, and a `PATCH` that accepts exactly `{description, name, tts_language, tts_voice}`. D-270 relabelled `create_agent` and the prompt read-back CONTRADICTED and left them POSTing, because `EngineCapabilities` had no way to say *"this engine does not host an agent of ours"* and inventing one was a change to the seam the whole engine story rests on. **The current state was worse than the change**: Cartesia was selectable, and those calls would 404 mid-publish — indistinguishable from a vendor outage, so an operator retries a structural fact for ever. **WHY A CAPABILITY MEMBER AND NOT A BRANCH.** The other six members are each a refusal reason an operator reads, a metric label and something the conformance suite exercises; a branch in the publish path is none of those. **WHY TWO VALUES AND NOT THREE.** A third value for "externally deployed AND we cannot even put a prompt on the call" was drafted and rejected: it conflates where an agent COMES FROM with what one REQUEST can carry, and the second is already expressible — the adapter refuses the dial by name (D-282). Two values keep the member answering exactly one question. **WHY `hosts_agents()` RATHER THAN `== "control_plane"`.** The comparison is spelled in the publish path, two adapters, the conformance suite and the console pre-flight; a fifth caller writing `!= "external_deployment"` is correct today and wrong the day a third shape lands — `speech_control`'s argument. |
| D-281 | **Cartesia's three agent-write methods, the publish path and the LLM leg all describe endpoints that do not exist, so all five refuse by name instead** | `CartesiaEngine.create_agent`, `update_agent` and `get_agent` raise `engine_lacks("agent_hosting")` before any request is built; `_agent_body` is deleted with them. `publish_agent` asks `require_capability("agent_hosting")` FIRST — before the row lock and before the vendor — so nothing is written and no `engine_agent_ref` can exist. `verify_publish` re-raises the same refusal rather than converting it to `unreachable`. `engine_drift_for` reports `not_published`, which is true and needs no new state and no migration. `VerificationState`/`VerificationOut`/`schema.d.ts` gain `publishable`, derived from `engine_capabilities().hosts_agents()`, and the admin prompt screen renders the sentence and hides Publish. `CARTESIA_CAPABILITIES.llm` moves `ours` → `engine`. | **WHY REFUSING BEATS A 404.** `publish_agent` writes our half of the marriage in one transaction after the vendor call, so a 404 arriving there is a structural fact wearing an outage's clothes. A named refusal is the same answer given before the transaction opens, with a remediation, and — because the console asks the same capability — before the button is offered at all, which is the divergence D-93 exists to remove. **WHY `get_agent` REFUSES RATHER THAN ANSWERING `readable=False`**, which looks like the honest option: the three `AgentSnapshot._readable` tri-states mean *the adapter could not FIND the field*, which is a reason to go and look at the adapter. On this platform the field is not there, for everyone, permanently — reporting a platform fact through the lookup-failure channel sends every future reader after a bug that is not there and makes `verification.judge` score `unreadable` for ever, a verdict that is not wrong, not actionable and indistinguishable from an adapter somebody should fix. **WHY NOT ADOPT INSTEAD OF REFUSE.** `GET /agents` is real and `name` is documented unique, so "adopt the agent whose name matches" is implementable — and it would put an agent live running a prompt we did not write and cannot read back, i.e. hard rule 5 resting on a repository nobody in this deployment can see. That is a product decision with a compliance consequence, gated at 19(a), not a rename. **THE LLM LEG IS DERIVED, NOT WEAKENED TO PASS.** `SpeechControl`'s own docstring defines `ours` as *"our provider and model strings REACH THE VENDOR"*; on Line `LlmAgent(model=...)` is a constructor call inside the deployed program, `AgentSummary` has no `model`, and `AgentUpdateParams` is four fields none of which is one. It is `transfer`'s argument applied to a speech leg — the VENDOR fact (Sarvam runs on Line through LiteLLM) is unchanged and TRD §10.5's table still says so. With the three write methods refusing, `require_speech_leg` no longer runs on this adapter, so `llm="ours"` would have become a claim nothing could contradict — the unfalsifiable descriptor entry `test_an_engine_side_campaign_object_is_not_claimable_yet` refuses outright. It reverts the day a model reaches a call (gate 19(b)). **NOT gate 16**, which is Bolna's `family`-versus-`provider` and where no correct value is derivable; here the value is derived from the same VERIFIED-SDK absence and makes the descriptor say LESS. |
| D-282 | **On an engine whose agent record cannot hold a prompt, hard rule 5 rides the per-call payload — and an adapter that cannot carry it there refuses to dial rather than dialling with a weaker floor** | `CallContext.system_prompt` is the vehicle; `agents/service._call_prompt_for` is its one production writer, composing through `_to_config` + `compose_engine_prompt` so no second rendering of our intent exists. `apps/api/engine/capabilities.require_call_compliance_floor(engine=, prompt_on_the_wire=)` is the one guard, called inside every adapter's `start_outbound_call`; `calevate_shared.engine.carries_truthful_answer_floor` is the one predicate. `CartesiaEngine` passes `prompt_on_the_wire=None` and therefore refuses **every** dial, with `engine_compliance_floor_absent` (a member of `DIAL_NOT_PLACED_CODES`: no line was seized). `fake.EXTERNAL_DEPLOYMENT_CAPABILITIES` is the profile that carries it, stores it on the call and answers `call_prompt()` — so the alternative contract is exercised in CI with no account. `scripts/check_compliance_invariants` §6 now asks each adapter what its DECLARED hosting owes: `control_plane` must call `compose_engine_prompt`, `external_deployment` must call the floor guard inside `start_outbound_call`, and the single dial site must build its `CallContext` with `system_prompt=`. | **THE LINE THAT COULD NOT BE CROSSED.** "Degrades honestly" must never mean the compliance floor is quietly dropped for one vendor: an agent that can be scripted into claiming it is human is the single failure hard rule 5 exists to make impossible, and it is undetectable from every screen we own. So the port offers exactly two outcomes for an externally-deployed engine — carry the rule on the call, or refuse the call — and the conformance suite asserts BOTH branches on every subject. **WHY THE GUARD TAKES WHAT THE ADAPTER SENDS, NOT THE CONTEXT IT RECEIVED.** An adapter handed `ctx.system_prompt` with no request field to put it in would satisfy a context-shaped check and dial anyway, dropping the floor silently — verbatim the failure `require_speech_leg` exists to stop one layer down, where an engine that dictates its voice must REFUSE ours rather than drop it. Each adapter passes the expression it puts in its body; Cartesia passes `None` and is refused every time, which is what `POST /agents/calls` (REPORTED-DOCS, four keys, no prompt) actually supports. The per-call prompt that IS read at source — `agent: {system_prompt, introduction}` on a `start` event — belongs to the WebSocket Calls API, which this adapter does not speak, and guessing a field would be D-31/D-32's rule broken on the compliance path. **WHY A NEGATIVE PROBE IS THE FALSIFIER.** `start_outbound_call` returns a handle and offers no read-back, so "it carried our prompt" and "it dropped our prompt" are the same observation from the port — `transfer`'s problem exactly, and the suite uses `transfer`'s answer: an adapter that can really carry the floor is required to REFUSE a dial that has none. The POSITIVE round trip is observed where it can be, on the fixture that is its own vendor (`tests/engine_capability_test.py`), and enforced statically everywhere else by the guardrail. **WHY THE GUARDRAIL HAD TO LEARN A SECOND SHAPE.** §6 asked every adapter for `compose_engine_prompt`, which was right while every engine was Bolna-shaped and wrong the day one was not: `cartesia.py` composes nothing because it writes no agent, and a check that could only see the composer would have forced a dead call to it — a guardrail teaching the code to lie to it, which is worse than no guardrail. |
| D-283 | **Bolna's `max_tokens` and `temperature` were not sent, so every agent on the platform ran with a 100-token ceiling on each reply that nobody had chosen** | `_agent_body`'s `llm_agent` block now sends `max_tokens: 400` and `temperature: 0.1` explicitly, with the reasoning and the citation at the line. | **WHAT WAS INHERITED.** VERIFIED-OSS at `bolna-ai/bolna@cd2e192`, `bolna/models.py`: `Llm.max_tokens` defaults to **100** and `Llm.temperature` to **0.1**, and `task_manager.__setup_llm` reads both with bare subscripts off `llm_agent_config`. Our body omitted them, the stored `agent_config.model_dump()` filled the defaults, and a 100-token cap on every agent reply is a product knob — not a bug, but not a decision anybody had taken either. **WHY 400.** A cap is a safety valve against a runaway generation, not a style control: brevity is the script's job, and a ceiling that bites mid-sentence does not shorten a reply, it truncates one — the TTS then speaks a fragment and stops. 100 is close enough to a normal turn to bite, and worse in our language than the number suggests: token fertility is ~2.1–2.3 for Telugu against ~1.2–1.4 for English on general tokenizers (Sarvam's own reaches 1.4–2.1 for Indic), so 100 tokens is roughly 45 Telugu words and a receptionist reading back three appointment slots passes it. 400 clears every legitimate turn while still bounding a monologue a caller would have to sit through, and it costs nothing on the money path because the LLM leg is Sarvam 105B, free per token (D-35/D-36, TRD §10); `max_call_duration_s` bounds the other side. Fertility figures are REPORTED, NOT READ (searched 18 Aug 2026, FLORES-200 tokenizer comparisons) and do not need to be read: the decision is "leave headroom" and the direction is not in doubt. **WHY 0.1 — THE VENDOR'S DEFAULT IS RIGHT, AND IS SENT ANYWAY.** The agent reads a client's script and carries `TRUTHFUL_ANSWER_DIRECTIVE` underneath it; the failure that matters is the model paraphrasing away a compliance sentence or improvising a price, and low temperature makes that rarest. Raising it buys "sounds more natural", which is a prompt-and-voice problem on a phone call rather than a sampling one. It is written down rather than inherited because a vendor default is somebody else's release note, and a compliance-bearing prompt is not a thing to leave on a number that can change without our deploying. **WHAT THIS DOES NOT TOUCH:** gate 16's `family`-versus-`provider` question is unchanged and still unanswerable from here; gate 16's read-back now also records what these two came back as. |
| D-290 | **The deploy workflow and the deploy script are two descriptions of one procedure, and CI now refuses to let them drift** | `scripts/check_deploy_workflow.py`, in `make guardrails` and CI, with negative controls in `tests/deploy_workflow_guard_test.py`. Six syntax-decidable questions over the parsed workflow and the script's own argument loop: the workflow INVOKES `scripts/vps-deploy.sh` in command position; no executed command builds an image, runs compose, runs alembic, moves the git checkout, touches pm2 or reloads nginx; every `--flag` it passes is one the script's `case` arms parse; every `secrets.*`/`vars.*` it reads is named in `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`; every `run:` block passes `bash -n`; and the four safety properties (kill switch, `workflow_run.conclusion == 'success'`, `head_branch == 'main'`, `cancel-in-progress: false`) are still in the file. Exit 2 = REFUSED on an absent workflow, no `run:` steps, an unparseable argument loop, or no secrets/vars at all. | **WHY.** Every property this deployment rests on lives in the script — migrations strictly before the swap, the health-gated swap, one image per commit, `--no-deps` so an `api` deploy never restarts `voice-runtime` (hard rule 3). The workflow is the copy nobody runs by hand, so the moment it does any of that itself there are two orderings and the unattended one is the unreviewed one. **The reference implementation is the evidence, not a hypothetical**: `raghava-organics-site`'s `deploy.yml` carries its own `git fetch`/`checkout`/`pull --ff-only` block, duplicated verbatim across two jobs, in a file its own comment records as NOT covered by the sync mechanism that keeps every other file in step — hand-carried on a dated line. **WHY THE FLAG CHECK IS THE ONE THAT EARNS THE FILE**: the other five catch deliberate acts; renaming an option in a 1200-line bash script and not editing a YAML file two directories away is the ordinary, well-intentioned change, and its symptom is a deploy that exits 1 on the production host at the last possible moment. **WHY `echo`/`printf` LINES ARE EXCLUDED from the reimplementation scan**: the deploy step legitimately PRINTS `git ... checkout main` as the operator's way back from a rollback, and a check that forbade that would push workflows towards giving no instructions — the control for it is a test of its own. **WHY exit 2 EXISTS**: a scan that matches nothing and prints OK is the failure this whole pack is written against (D-176, D-172). |
| D-291 | **A rollback that needs an SSH session is not a rollback the deploy path has** | `scripts/vps-deploy.sh --checkout <sha>` fetches, verifies the object, and moves the deploy checkout to that commit DETACHED, then deploys it; the pull path refuses to run from a detached HEAD and names both ways out. `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` gained a `commit_sha` `workflow_dispatch` input that passes it. It replaces the two-command form (`git checkout <sha>` then `--all --no-pull`) everywhere it was written — the summary banner, `runbooks/deploy-failed.md` §4, `scripts/deploy_revision_check.py`, DEPLOYMENT §3b/§4a. `tests/deploy_checkout_flag_test.py` drives all four behaviours against a real git clone with a stubbed `docker`. | **WHY A FLAG AND NOT TWO STEPS IN THE WORKFLOW.** The workflow is a caller now, and a caller that knows how to move a deploy checkout is a second copy of that knowledge in the file least likely to be run by hand — the exact drift D-290 refuses. One flag, one place. **WHY IT REFUSES A REF.** `--checkout main` deploys whatever the branch points at when the line runs, which is the ambiguity `--expected-sha` exists to remove; the caller reaching for this flag is choosing one artefact on purpose, so 7–40 hex only, which also makes the value incapable of being an option or a path. **WHY DETACHED, AND WHY THE PULL PATH THEN REFUSES.** Landing on `main` at an older commit is a tree the next `git pull --ff-only` silently fast-forwards: the first green CI after a rollback would redeploy the release just rolled away from, unattended, with nobody deciding it. Detaching makes that impossible and the refusal makes it legible — bare `git pull` from a detached HEAD fails with a message about an upstream, which reads as a git misconfiguration rather than as "this host is deliberately rolled back". The cost is that automatic deploys stay stuck until a human runs `git checkout main`, and that is the intended price. **WHY NOT `--expected-sha` AS WELL**: the flag that chooses the commit and the flag that verifies it would be one value checking itself, which reads like a gate and is not one. **NOT RUN**: the git behaviour is tested, the deploy it triggers has never executed on a host (DEPLOYMENT §4d step 7). |
| D-292 | **The runner is written down as a numbered procedure, because it is the one component here that holds a credential to this repository** | DEPLOYMENT §3a: which user (the `calevate` deploy user, never root — the runner executes whatever a workflow says, so a root runner turns every merge into root on the production host), the exact `config.sh` invocation with the literal label, `svc.sh install calevate` plus the two facts that must be checked in the unit (`User=`, `enabled`), the full Secrets/Variables table, the prove-it-with-`dry_run`-first order, how to turn the kill switch off in an incident, and how to REVOKE — including that deleting the runner in GitHub settings is what actually revokes the credential and that a wiped disk is not a substitute. §4d gained a rollback drill with a pass condition. | **WHY THE LABEL IS LITERAL AND NOT A REPO VARIABLE.** The reference reads it from `vars.VPS_RUNNER_LABEL` with a fallback to bare `self-hosted`, which is right for a template synced across many client repos and wrong here: the fallback is a misroute waiting for the day a second runner registers anywhere in the org, and this repo has one VPS. A literal label makes a typo show up as a job that queues forever — loud — rather than as a deploy landing on someone else's host. **WHY IT IS A CHECKLIST AND NOT A CLAIM.** There is no VPS, no runner and no repo Secret; every line of §3a is a step a human executes, and writing it as prose about how CD works would have made an unrun path read as a running one. **WHY THE KILL SWITCH DOES NOT CANCEL A RUN IN FLIGHT.** Same reason `cancel-in-progress` is `false`: a deploy stopped between the migration and the swap is the half-migrated state the script is built to avoid, so the incident answer is a rollback (§3b) first and the switch second. |
| D-300 | **The most reachable failures on the HTTP surface were the ones with no next step in them** | `core/errors.py` carries `_DEFAULT_REMEDIATION`, one sentence per `ErrorKind`, applied in `as_problem` when the raise site wrote none — so `remediation` is now the fifth extension of the BACKEND-PATTERNS §3 ladder that CANNOT be absent, alongside `kind`, `retryable`, `title` and `detail`. The framework `HTTPException` handler additionally carries its own sentences for 404 and 405, whose kinds' floors are wrong for them. `tests/error_ladder_test.py` proves both halves: totality off `get_args(ErrorKind)`, and seven REACHABLE failures driven over HTTP (401, 403, router-404, row-404, 405, 422, and the 400 that demands an `Idempotency-Key`), each asserted to carry the whole ladder and to leak no traceback, SQL or module path. | **WHY.** `remediation` was an optional keyword: 104 raise sites passed it and 66 did not, and the ones that did not were the reachable ones. `ProblemError.forbidden()` and `.unauthorized()` are what `auth.requires()` raises on EVERY 403 and EVERY 401 in the product; the framework handlers answer every unknown path, every wrong method and every schema failure; the catch-all answers everything nobody caught. So a rare business rule three modules deep had a sentence a screen could render and the refusal every user meets had none — and `apps/web` renders `remediation` when it is there, so the gap was visible on the screens with the most traffic. **WHY A FLOOR PER KIND RATHER THAN 66 EDITS.** The failure mode is copy-from-the-neighbour, and the neighbour that forgot is the cheaper example to copy; a default closes the class, an edit closes an instance. It also reaches `apps/api/engine/`, whose vendor failures surface as 502s that no sentence in this repo was going to be written for. An explicit `remediation=` still wins, so all 104 existing sentences are unchanged — asserted, not assumed. **WHAT THE FLOOR MAY SAY**: what the CALLER can do. "Quote the trace id to support" is an action a person can take; "the team has been alerted" is not, and is why the generic 500's `detail` is not its remediation. |
| D-301 | **The IDOR sweep was checked in one direction only, so the route it never drove was the one with the most to lose** | `tests/adversarial_pass_test.py` gains `test_every_id_route_in_the_client_space_is_swept`, which walks the LIVE route table and fails for any client-realm `{id}` route not in `_IDOR_ROUTES`, with a `_NOT_AN_OBJECT_REFERENCE` registry (empty today) for ids that name nothing a neighbour owns. The one route it found — `GET /v1/integrations/deliveries/{delivery_id}/payload` — is now driven with a real neighbour's delivery, seeded against a real endpoint row. | **WHY.** The sweep is a hand-written list of forty entries with a non-vacuity test that every entry names a live route. Nothing asked the converse, so a route added afterwards was simply not swept and nothing anywhere went red — the same shape as the `PUBLIC_PREFIXES` blind spot D-173 closed, in a test rather than in a guardrail. **WHY THAT ROUTE MATTERS MOST.** It returns the unredacted body we POSTed to a client's CRM — a lead's name and number in whatever form their own opt-in produced — and `webhook_deliveries` is the one table in that module with NO RLS policy, because engine webhooks land before a tenant is resolved. The only thing between tenant B and tenant A's delivered payload is the `endpoint_id IN (SELECT id FROM outbound_webhooks)` subquery in `integrations.service.delivery_body_ref`: a clause a person has to keep writing, where every other row in the sweep is protected by a policy the database enforces. **THE RESULT WAS CLEAN** — it answers 404 exactly as it should, proven over HTTP, and deleting the subquery turns the sweep red. It was never broken; it was unwatched, which is the state the next refactor decides. |
| D-302 | **A response's LENGTH was nobody's invariant, so twenty-five routes returned as many rows as a client had** | `scripts/check_list_bounds.py` (in `make guardrails` and CI): every route whose declared response can contain a list — transitively, so `LeadListOut.items` counts — must either take a `limit` the SCHEMA bounds (`le=`, not merely `ge=`) or hold a `BOUNDED_LISTS` entry naming what bounds it instead. Fifteen routes gained the house page (`limit: int = Query(…, ge=1, le=200)`) with the `LIMIT` pushed into the query: members, invitations (both realms), agents, campaigns, knowledge sources, lead sources, outbound endpoints and prompt history. `GET /v1/performance`'s unbounded `days` gained `ge=1, le=365`. `tests/list_bounds_guard_test.py` mounts four negative controls on the REAL app and pins that an UNBOUNDED `limit` does not count. | **WHY IT IS A GUARDRAIL AND NOT TWENTY-FIVE FIXES.** Every unbounded list here was written by somebody who knew the list was short, and every one of them was right on the day they wrote it — a picker, two agents, a handful of uploaded documents. "Short today" is a fact about the FIRST tenant, and it is checked exactly once, in a review, against an empty database; the whole result is materialised in Python and serialised, so the client who finds out is the first one who succeeds at using the product. **THE DISTINCTION THE REGISTRY ENCODES**, which is the part that had to be argued rather than automated: what needs a ceiling is a list whose length is CALLER-CONTROLLED — rows a tenant mints, or ones that accumulate per tenant with nobody minting them (`prompt_versions` is append-only, so it is the one admin list that grows on its own). What does not is a list bounded by something we provision or declare: the voice catalogue, a role's permission set, the config registry, seven days, one call's turns under `CALL_CAP_MAX_S`, or the number of CLIENTS we have signed. Truncating that second kind is the worse failure — a `LIMIT` on `/v1/admin/client-health` would cut the list BEFORE the worst-first triage sort and hide the account most in trouble — so those 54 entries name the other bound and `admin/health.py`'s `client_health_walk_over_budget` alarm is what watches them. **WHY IT DOES NOT READ SQL.** `GET /v1/campaigns` already had `LIMIT 100`, as a literal buried in a service, where no caller could see it and no caller could get past it; a ceiling the contract does not state is indistinguishable from a client who really has that many rows. The fix was to make the number a parameter, not to teach the check to find it. **NOT A COMPLETENESS CLAIM ABOUT SUBJECT ACCESS**: `POST /v1/compliance/subject-export` and the erasure proofs stay unbounded on purpose and say so — a truncated DPDP access document is a non-compliant one. |
| D-303 | **The response-model sweep was a list of eleven operations, so the five that answered `dict[str, str]` were never on it** | `tests/response_shape_test.py` gains `test_no_success_response_in_the_whole_app_is_an_undeclared_shape`, which walks EVERY 2xx in the live OpenAPI spec and refuses a free-form object, with a `_UNMODELLED_SUCCESS` registry of six reasoned exemptions (the three probe endpoints, the two CSV byte streams, Meta's plain-text handshake) plus one for `POST /v1/numbers/purchase`, whose handler is `NoReturn` and has no success body to model. Five admin routes gained declared models — `KbReviewOut`, `NumberDltStatusOut`, `TemplateRegisteredOut`, `TemplateStatusOut` — and the seven empty 202s in `authn/routes.py` gained `response_class=Response`, so their documented body matches the body they send. | **WHY.** D-118's sweep fixed the panels it named and left the rule as a list, which can only ever be behind the app: `.../kb/{source_id}/approve`, `/reject`, `.../numbers/{number_id}/dlt-status` and the two DLT template writes shipped bare mappings the entire time that test was green. A mapping is not a shape — it is the absence of one, and three things read a response model: `check_redaction_exposure` walks them and is structurally blind to a route that declares none, the generated TypeScript client renders one as an index signature so the frontend hand-writes a mirror that drifts, and BACKEND-PATTERNS §1's "the response model IS the output whitelist" has nothing to whitelist. **NOTHING LEAKED** — every one of these returns a literal written three lines above the `return` — which is exactly the point: the guardrail could not have told us that. **THE EMPTY 202s ARE THE SAME DEFECT SPELLED DIFFERENTLY**: a handler returning a bare `Response(status_code=202)` was documented as `application/json` with a `{}` schema, i.e. a media type it never sends, and that is indistinguishable in the schema from a genuinely undeclared body. `response_class=Response` is the one-word fix and FastAPI then documents the 202 with no content at all. |
| D-310 | **An erasure certificate went false minutes after it was signed: a call still in flight when the erasure ran wrote the transcript, the summary and a lead carrying the number back afterwards** | `compliance/deletion.refile_erasure_for_late_records` runs as STEP 9 of the post-call pipeline, in the transaction that holds the pipeline's own last writes: if a COMPLETED erasure covers this call — `completed_at >= COALESCE(calls.started_at, calls.created_at)`, one statement over two RLS-scoped tables — it files a fresh `deletion_requests` row through the existing producer, with its outbox job. Migration `c1e9a4f7d302` adds `calls.erased_subject_ref`, the one-way handle (same construction as `deletion_requests.subject_ref`) the erasure leaves when it clears `from_e164`/`to_e164`, and `execute_deletion_request`'s call lookup reads it. New: `tests/erasure_late_arrival_test.py` (2), sabotage-verified three ways. | **MEASURED, NOT REASONED.** With a call `in_progress` and a §12 request executed against it, the certificate was signed over a `calls` row and nothing else; the ordinary pipeline then wrote `transcript_turns` verbatim (`naa peru Ravi`), `calls.summary`, the extraction, the recording pointer, the archived vendor document and a `leads` row holding the erased number. No replay, no poller, no adversary — two concurrent processes, one of which could not reach forward. The moment people actually ask to be forgotten is while they are talking to the business, so this is the ordinary case rather than the exotic one. **WHY THE BOUNDARY IS THE CALL'S OWN START.** `request_erasure` already argues that erasure is not terminal for a phone number — the same person may ring next month and generate fresh, lawful data — so a rule keyed on the subject alone would destroy records nobody asked us to destroy. A call that had already STARTED when the erasure executed was inside the instruction whenever its records land; one that starts afterwards is not. `tests/…::test_a_later_call_from_the_same_person_is_not_re_erased` is that half, and it goes red if the clause is dropped. **WHY RE-FILE RATHER THAN ERASE INLINE.** A second erasure of one subject is exactly what `deletion_requests` is: re-filing reuses the worker, the object-store arms and the recording floor holds instead of a second, thinner copy of the erase statements (CLAUDE.md: one way per problem), and it produces the artefact the data principal is owed — a second proof. **REJECTED: amending the first certificate.** It has already been handed on; evidence that changes after the fact is not evidence. **WHY THE COLUMN.** The erasure found the person's LEAD and not their CALL, because it locates calls by the two phone columns it then CLEARS — so an erased call is orphaned from its subject forever and no later erasure can reach anything that arrives for it. That is a standing defect the race merely exposed. **RESIDUAL, NAMED:** the CRM fan-out (step 8) still posts the call summary to the client's own endpoint before step 9 runs. The client is the Fiduciary who received the instruction and already holds the person's record, so this is their copy going back to them, and suppressing the fan-out here would leave `crm_notified_at` NULL and make the poller re-drive the call forever. |
| D-311 | **The calling window was inclusive at 21:00, so a dial placed at 21:00:00 IST was inside the gate and outside the law** | `compliance.service.within_calling_hours` and `campaigns.service.campaign_window_open` are half-open at the top: `start <= t < end`. New: `tests/calling_window_boundary_test.py` (9). | **THE RULE IS A PROHIBITION.** TCCCPR 2018 forbids a commercial communication "between 2100 hours and 0900 hours" (TRAI's framing, carried from TCCCPR 2010 reg. 12 and unchanged by the Second Amendment of 12 Feb 2025; searched 18 Aug 2026 — trai.gov.in/sites/default/files/2025-02/Regulation_12022025.pdf remains unreachable from this environment, so the wording is read through the search index, as `compliance/optout.py` already records for the same source). 21:00:00 is therefore the first instant of the forbidden band, not the last of the permitted one, and the lower bound stays inclusive for the mirror-image reason. **IT ALSO ENDS A TWO-CONVENTION DRIFT**: `agents/business_hours.is_after_hours` has always used `opens <= t < closes`, so this repo held one concept in two spellings that disagreed in the unsafe direction. **SIZE, STATED HONESTLY:** one second a day per window. It is fixed because the cost is a comparison operator and the artefact of getting it wrong is a subscriber complaint timestamped 21:00, which is a violation on its face whatever our operator thought. |
| D-312 | **`audit_log.ip` was the only one of SEC-COMP §5's four fields the hash chain did not sign, so an operator's source address could be rewritten with every hash still verifying** | `write_audit` puts `ip` in the hashed payload; `_matching_generation` tries the current shape and then the pre-D-312 shape, so entries written before it still verify. New: `tests/audit_chain_ip_test.py` (5), each sabotage-verified. | **WHY THIS FIELD.** `scripts/check_audit_ip.py` exists because "where did this act come from" is the question an impersonation dispute (D-22/D-210) or a breach timeline turns on, and D-131/D-139 spent a sweep making the column true. Tamper-EVIDENCE that covers actor, tenant and action but not the address is evidence with a hole exactly where somebody would want one: the table's `ENABLE ALWAYS` trigger stops the app role, but the threat model for a hash chain is the party who can get past that. **WHY THE OLD SHAPE STAYS ADMISSIBLE, AND WHY THAT IS NOT A DOWNGRADE.** `audit_log` is append-only (hard rule 4) so existing rows cannot be re-signed, and a change that turned the whole log red would be indistinguishable from tampering on the day it deployed — the argument `_key_ring` already makes for keys. Accepting the old shape lets an attacker KEEP a row that omits the ip; it does not let them PRODUCE one, because either shape needs the secret. The generation floor that bounds the public generation-0 key is untouched: both shapes are tried under the same admissible keys. **WHY `at` IS STILL OUTSIDE**, stated rather than left as an omission: it is stamped by `clock_timestamp()` inside the chain lock and does not exist until the INSERT, and computing it in Python to hash it would move the chain's ordering off the database clock and onto whichever app instance served the request. An edited `at` still surfaces, as a `link` break in the replay order; an edited `ip` surfaced as nothing. |
| D-313 | **A national-DND scrub's own suppressions made room for exactly that many unscrubbed contacts, and the gate stayed green** | `national_dnd_blocker` asks whether any pending contact was `created_at > scrubbed_at` and keeps the count comparison as a backstop. New: `tests/national_dnd_test.py::test_the_scrubs_own_suppressions_do_not_make_room_for_unscrubbed_contacts`; the existing growth test now adds its contacts in a separate transaction, which is the only shape an upload has in production. | **MEASURED:** a scrub over ten contacts that suppressed three admitted three brand-new numbers the register never saw, and `national_dnd_blocker` answered `None`. The cause is that `submitted_count` is counted BEFORE the run's blocked numbers are marked `dnc_blocked`, so live pending sits `suppressed_count` below the number it was compared against — and every other way pending falls (a dial consuming one, the launch DNC pass, an erasure) widens the same gap. SEC-COMP §3 already states the rule the code was approximating: `national_dnd_scrub_incomplete` is for "contacts were added after it ran". **WHY `scrubbed_at` RATHER THAN `recorded_at`:** the provider's verdict describes the instant the provider fixed the list, so a contact added while an operator was still typing the reference into the console is one the register never scored. **WHY THE COUNT RULE IS KEPT:** `created_at` is `now()`, i.e. transaction-start time, so an upload whose transaction opened before the scrub and committed after it back-dates itself past the timestamp — a window of seconds no timestamp comparison closes. Growth past the submitted size catches it, costs one indexed count, and can only ever refuse a list bigger than the one the provider scored. |
| D-320 | **`authn`'s "only the newest credential works" rules were retire-then-issue with nothing serializing them, so two overlapping requests left two live ones** | New `apps/api/authn/locks.py`: `lock_subject_credentials` takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtextextended('authn:subject:{realm}:{subject}', 0))`, called at the top of `otp.issue_challenge` and `tokens.invalidate_outstanding` — the two statements that own the retire half. Migration `f1c8b7d5a903` adds `ux_auth_otp_challenges_live`, a partial unique index on `(realm, subject_id, purpose) WHERE consumed_at IS NULL`, as the structural statement of the invariant against a future writer that forgets the lock. `tests/authn_credential_race_test.py` forces the overlap (the first writer holds its transaction open behind an `asyncio.Event`, the second starts once the lock is taken) and asserts BOTH that one credential survives and that the second writer WAITED; two negative controls remove the lock and assert the same harness breaks. | **WHY.** `otp.py` opens with a section titled ONE LIVE CHALLENGE PER (SUBJECT, PURPOSE) and rests the whole guess budget on it — "without that rule, 'resend the code' becomes an attempt-budget reset" — and `service.request_password_reset` says "only the newest link works. Without this, 'click forgot password three times' leaves three live keys in a mailbox for an hour". Both were implemented as an `UPDATE ... WHERE <live> IS NULL` followed by an `INSERT`, two statements in one transaction, which under READ COMMITTED interleave with no conflict for the database to detect. **MEASURED, before the fix**, two `issue_challenge` calls for one admin with the second opening while the first was uncommitted: `live challenges: 2` and *both* codes authenticated. Reachable by a double-tap on "resend". **WHY A LOCK AND NOT ONLY AN INDEX.** The loser of an index-only race meets a unique violation, so a person who pressed "resend" twice gets a 500 — `kb/service._lock_agent_publishes` rejects an index-only fix in almost the same words. **WHY `auth_email_tokens` GETS NO INDEX.** "One live token" is true of `password_reset` and deliberately false of `email_verify`, which nothing invalidates; a unique index there would refuse a resend that is allowed. **WHY THE KEY IS THE SUBJECT, NOT (SUBJECT, PURPOSE).** The reset path spans two tables and a password change, and a per-purpose key would let a reset request slip a new link in between `set_password` and the `invalidate_outstanding` meant to kill it. |
| D-321 | **The reliability triad's terminal transitions were blind UPDATEs, so an abandoned holder's late report reopened work somebody else had finished** | `complete_idempotency` and `fail_idempotency` CAS on `status = 'processing'`; `mark_inbox_processed` and `mark_inbox_failed` CAS on `status IN ('processing','enqueued')`. A lost CAS is a WARNING (`_late_report`) rather than a raise — the reporting attempt has nothing left to do and its request is already answered, but an attempt outliving its lease is the signal that `CLAIM_LEASE` is shorter than something real. `tests/reliability_late_report_test.py` drives the documented recovery (age `updated_at` past the lease, re-claim, second holder resolves, first holder reports) for both tables and both directions. | **WHY.** `claim_idempotency` and `claim_inbox_event` hand a lapsed claim to a SECOND holder ON PURPOSE — "a crashed attempt must not own the key until the TTL sweep"; "an at-most-once engine event whose key says duplicate is a silently dropped call" — so the design itself creates a state where two callers hold one row id and the first is by definition the late one. `mark_outbox_failed` already argues its own guard is "load-bearing rather than decorative" because "a late failure report drags a message that has ALREADY been published back to pending"; the four writers here were the same shape with no guard and no dedupe behind them. A reopened idempotency record answers the client's next retry `fresh` instead of replaying — on `POST /v1/leads/{lead_id}/call` that is a SECOND REAL PHONE CALL to a member of the public, and on `POST /v1/calls/{call_id}/assist` a second paid model run. A reopened inbox row makes the vendor's next retry re-drive the whole post-call pipeline for a call already metered, extracted and notified on. **ONE WAY PER PROBLEM:** the outbox's guard was the precedent, and this makes the triad answer "may I still report this outcome?" the same way in all three of its mechanisms. |
| D-322 | **Two deadlines were written by the application's clock and judged by the database's** | `idempotency_records.expires_at` and `invitations.expires_at` are now written as `now() + make_interval(secs => :ttl_s)`, the same clock every reader compares them with. `tests/reliability_late_report_test.py::test_the_idempotency_ttl_is_measured_by_one_clock` and `tests/admin_security_test.py::test_the_invitation_deadline_is_measured_by_one_clock` age the transaction with a `pg_sleep` and assert `expires_at - created_at` is EXACTLY the TTL. | **WHY.** Both columns were `datetime.now(UTC) + TTL` while both readers use the database clock — `sweep_idempotency`'s `expires_at < now()`, and `expires_at > now()` in the pending-invitation probe and in `accept_invitation`'s burn. That is wrong twice. By the host skew, which is unbounded and invisible. And, on every deployment regardless of skew, by the AGE OF THE TRANSACTION: Postgres `now()` is transaction START time while the Python expression is evaluated when the statement is built, so a row minted after other work in the same transaction quietly outlives its stated TTL by however long that work took — measured at **+501ms on a 24h key and +506ms on a 72h invitation** with a single 500ms statement ahead of it, and `create_invitation` really does run after three reads. The repo already made this correction once, for the outbox's retry rung (`_BACKOFF_INTERVAL_SQL`); these are the two places it had not reached. Every other deadline in the tree was checked and is self-consistent: the whole of `authn` writes and compares with the app clock (`now=` is injectable for testability), and `consent`/`preference_scrub` do the same. |
| D-330 | **The same cookie-alias hole as D-198, found a second time by an independent red-team pass — recorded because HOW it was found is the part D-198 does not carry** | No code change: `authn/cookies.read_token` was already fixed by D-198, and the red-team branch had forked before that landed. What survives the merge is the EVIDENCE — `tests/authn_cookie_fixation_e2e_test.py`, which drives the attack over HTTP against the real ASGI app with a real, MFA-complete, live session on both realms, where D-198's `tests/authn_cookie_prefix_test.py` asserts the property on `read_token` itself with no database at all. Both files are kept: a unit test that stayed green while some middleware or dependency read the cookie by another route would be a green light over a live hole. | **WHY IT IS WORTH A ROW.** Two passes reaching the same defect from opposite directions — one reading the module docstring's claim, one setting a `Domain=.calevate.tech` cookie against a booted app — says the defect was structural rather than obscure, and it tells the next reader that the unit assertion alone was not what caught it. The red-team variant of the fix ALSO accepted the prefixed name on a plain-HTTP request; D-198's does not, and D-198's is what shipped, because a browser will not send a `__Host-` cookie over cleartext and a name that cannot legitimately arrive should not be read. |
| D-331 | **The fifth D-193 write path, and it was on the admin router — a provisioned number could name another tenant's agent** | `agents.service.provision_number` calls `db.ownership.assert_visible(session, "agent", agent_id)` before the INSERT and before any lock, which is the same primitive and the same position as D-193's four. `tests/cross_tenant_reference_test.py` gains the attack (404, `not_found`, and a count assertion that no row was written — without which a guard that refused the RESPONSE after writing would pass) and two non-vacuity controls: the tenant's OWN agent still provisions, and a `null` agent still provisions, because a number recorded before its agent exists is the ordinary onboarding order. | **WHY D-193 MISSED IT.** That pass swept the paths where "a client names a neighbour's row", and this one takes its tenant from the PATH on the admin router rather than from a session — so it is not shaped like the population that was searched, and `POST /v1/admin/tenants/{tenant_id}/numbers` reads as an operator acting on one client rather than as a cross-tenant reference. The mechanism is identical: `phone_numbers.agent_id` is a foreign key, PostgreSQL validates foreign keys with row security bypassed, and the tenant-scoped session's `WITH CHECK` only constrains the `tenant_id` OF THE ROW. **MEASURED**: with a valid `superadmin` credential, `POST /v1/admin/tenants/<B>/numbers {"e164": …, "series": "160", "agent_id": "<tenant A's agent>"}` answered `201 Created`, and `SELECT … FROM phone_numbers p JOIN agents a ON a.id = p.agent_id WHERE p.tenant_id <> a.tenant_id` returned the row. **WHAT IT WAS NOT**: no dial and no disclosure. `phone_numbers.agent_id` is written and read by nothing today — the campaign paths join `phone_numbers` on `number_id` and scope the agent separately — so what survived is the STORED reference, which is the same residue D-193 recorded for its campaign cases and the same reason it fixed them rather than noting them: it is one un-scoped `JOIN` away from a client's own screen naming a neighbour's agent, and the column exists precisely so that something will read it. **HOW THE SEARCH WAS DONE, so the next pass can repeat it rather than re-derive it**: every foreign key in the schema whose referent is a tenant-scoped table was enumerated from `pg_constraint`, and each was traced to whether its value can arrive in a request body. Six candidates reached a body; five resolve their reference under RLS before use (`_assert_assignable` on `leads.assigned_to`, `_version_id` on the experiment variants, the path-param agent on prompts and extraction schemas, the four D-193 sites) and this one did not. |
| D-340 | **The step-up prompt existed and reached exactly one caller: twelve confirmed admin writes across six screens had no way to answer "confirm it is still you"** | D-210 built the mechanism — a module-level single-flight store (`lib/authn/stepUpPrompt`) with one `StepUpPrompt` mounted in `admin/layout.tsx` — and wired it to `lib/api/admin.ts::mint` alone. Every other `reauthentication_required` refusal, including the big red switch, the platform-wide DNC list, a credit adjustment and a tenant erasure, rendered the SERVER's `remediation` verbatim through `ProblemNotice`: two curl calls and a header, on a screen, to an operator mid-incident. `WriteFailure` moves out of `ops/` (where two screens used it) to `app/admin/writeFailure.tsx` and is now every confirmed write's renderer on all six screens; its `reauthentication_required` branch offers a button that calls the EXISTING `requireStepUp`. | **WHY IT ROUTES INTO THE EXISTING ASKER RATHER THAN MOUNTING A PROMPT.** The branch this came from mounted its own `StepUpPrompt` per failure, which would be a second asker: `service.request_step_up` retires the previous challenge on issue, so six queries refused together would email six codes and invalidate five — an operator typing a code that is already dead. The store is single-flight precisely for that, and a confirmed write is its second caller, not a second mechanism. **The button deliberately does not replay the write.** Proving a factor says who is at the keyboard; it does not restate an intent to halt a platform or adjust a client's credits, and re-firing one on the back of an emailed code would turn an identity check into a confirmation. |
| D-341 | **`text-brand` on `bg-brand-soft` is 3.08:1, and it was the "completed" pill on the calls screen** | Two sites — the call-status pill and one medallion on the client home — wrote `text-brand` on a `bg-brand-soft` ground while THIRTY others wrote `text-brand-strong` (6.01:1 on the same ground). Both join the convention. Two guards, at two levels, because neither could have caught it alone: `tests/contrastTokens.test.ts` parses `globals.css` and asserts the PAIRING is sound (plus every ink tier on every surface, and the dark palette nothing toggles yet), and `tests/contrast.test.ts` gains a SOURCE scan for the pairing itself. | **WHY `--brand` WAS NOT RAISED.** The pass that found this arrived with a test demanding 4.5:1 of `--brand` itself, on the premise that `bg-brand` + `text-white` is every primary button. That was true of its base and is not true here: D-222 already split the token — `PRIMARY_BUTTON` rests on `--brand-strong`, and `contrast.test.ts` refuses white text on an unsuffixed `bg-brand`. `--brand` is what medallions, bars and icons are painted in, which SC 1.4.11 holds to 3:1, so raising it would have darkened the brand across ~104 sites to satisfy a threshold that does not govern it. The assertion was rewritten to say what each token is FOR. **The palette guard alone was not enough**, and that is measured rather than assumed: reintroducing `text-brand` at the call site leaves all 24 palette tests green, which is why the source scan exists and is sabotage-verified separately. |
| D-390 | **`/healthz/ready` said `ready` against a database the running code had migrations for** | `core/health.py` grew `_check_schema_current`, a fourth question beside DB, Redis and credentials, and a `schema_behind` member on `DegradationMode` ordered directly after `db_down`. The image's revision graph comes from alembic's own `ScriptDirectory` (the `check_wiring`/`deploy_revision_check` idiom), resolved once per process and cached, imported inside the function so it stays off voice-runtime's boot graph. Three answers, not two: stored revision IS a head → ready; stored revision is NOT IN this image's chain → the database is AHEAD, i.e. a rollback, and stays ready; stored revision is in the chain and is not a head → **not ready**. An unreadable versions tree or an unreadable `alembic_version` abstains and logs rather than accusing; an EMPTY `alembic_version` is not ready, because a database nothing has ever migrated cannot serve. `tests/health_schema_revision_test.py` pins all four. | **WHY.** Readiness answered every question about this deployment's credentials and none about its schema. `vps-deploy.sh` migrates before it swaps, so its own ordering is safe — but nothing in the running process re-asked afterwards, so a container started outside that script (a bare `docker compose up -d api` in the deploy directory, a partial `--no-pull` deploy, a migrate step that failed while the swap went ahead) served against whatever schema it found: 500 on every request touching a column the release added, `ready` on the probe, and the orchestrator keeping it in rotation. That is D-49's shape — unfit AND silent — with a step nobody ran in place of a variable nobody set. **WHY NOT `stored != head`.** That one-liner would refuse the release you roll back TO, during the incident you are rolling back FOR: migrations are expand-only (hard rule 8), `deploy_revision_check` skips migrations for exactly this case by exit code, and the previous image running on the newer schema is the supported path. The question is "does this image have migrations the database has not applied", not "do the two strings match". |
| D-391 | **The restore drill's `verify()` stage had never finished, so three of its eight checks had never once executed** | Three fixes in `scripts/restore_drill.py`, each with a test in `tests/restore_drill_test.py`. (1) `_check_audit_chain` passed the verifier's JSON as `**verdict`, and that JSON has an `ok` key while `check`'s second POSITIONAL parameter is also `ok` — `TypeError: check() got multiple values for argument 'ok'`, unconditionally, aborting the stage. The verdict now rides under `chain_ok` with `ok` filtered out of the facts. (2) The append-only probe wrote `SET tenant_id = tenant_id` on every ledger: it does not parse on `platform_secrets` (no tenant column) and `_raises` reads that `UndefinedColumn` as "the database did not refuse", and a no-op write is invisible to the two triggers that compare NEW to OLD. It now attempts a REAL change per ledger (`tenant_id = gen_random_uuid()`, `last_four = last_four \|\| 'x'` for the D-97 one). (3) `_seed` populated five of the eight `APPEND_ONLY_TABLES`; a `FOR EACH ROW` trigger cannot fire against an empty table, so the drill reported the other three as NOT ENFORCED against a database whose triggers were entirely correct. The seed covers all eight, an empty ledger is now reported as UNTESTABLE in its own sentence rather than as unprotected, and `row_counts` covers the three new ledgers. `pg_dump_under_rls` — which runs after the crash and had therefore also never executed — required the string `row-level security` in stderr and goes red on a correct database: `c5a9e34b71d0` grants the app role USAGE and not SELECT on the one sequence in the schema, so pg_dump dies on `permission denied for sequence platform_settings_revision_seq` first. It now requires a non-zero exit and RECORDS which refusal arrived. | **WHY THIS MATTERED MORE THAN ITS LINE COUNT.** The drill is the only thing that would tell anyone whether a backup restores, and it aborted with "unexpected TypeError" — which reads as an environment problem rather than as a defect in the harness, which is how it survived. `--sabotage=tamper-audit-row` existed to prove the audit chain check and could not: a tampered ledger and an intact one produced the same crash. All four sabotage modes and the green path were re-run after the fix; all four go red for their own reason and the green path is GREEN (local scope) for the first time. **STILL NOT A QUARTERLY DRILL** — the ten `NOT_COVERED` ids are unchanged and every one of them needs a provisioned host: wal-g, the object store, the offsite provider, the age identity, the systemd schedule, alert delivery and the external dead man. |
